### [1] [Explaining GAND: A Resource on Gender-Ambiguous Natural Data & Contrastive Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22546)
*Janiça Hackenbuchner,Jasper Degraeuwe,Arda Tezcan,Joke Daems*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GAND is a gender-ambiguous natural data benchmark for machine translation that enables interpretability analysis of how contextual cues influence gender translation of ambiguous referents, using feature attribution to identify which source words drive gender decisions in target languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MT systems produce gender-biased translations that can cause harm when gender cues are absent or ambiguous. Existing benchmarking resources inadequately capture natural gender-ambiguous scenarios, limiting the ability to analyze how systems resolve gender when no explicit cues are present.

Method: The authors introduce GAND, an English-source benchmark of gender-ambiguous sentences designed to study contextual influence on gender translation. They translate a subset into two grammatical gender languages, create manually crafted contrastive translations, and apply feature attribution analysis to identify which source-context words inform the gender assigned to an ambiguous referent in the target output.

Result: Feature attribution analysis on GAND translations reveals which specific source words in context drive the gender translation of an ambiguous referent, providing interpretable insights into how MT systems resolve gender ambiguity.

Conclusion: GAND fills a gap in MT evaluation by offering a natural, gender-ambiguous benchmark that supports interpretability analysis, helping researchers understand and ultimately mitigate gender bias in machine translation.

Abstract: Machine translation (MT) systems continue to produce gender-biased translations. In a time where self-expression is paramount, mistranslations based on default behaviour and stereotyping can lead to harm for users of these systems. To better understand how these systems translate gender in the absence of clear gender cues, we need benchmarking resources that reflect gender-ambiguous scenarios in a natural way. To this end, we present GAND, a gender-ambiguous natural data benchmarking resource for MT consisting of English source sentences, specifically designed to analyse the influence of contextual cues on gender in translation. We leverage GAND to conduct an interpretability analysis: we translate a subset of GAND into two grammatical gender languages and extend these with manually crafted contrastive translations. A following feature attribution analysis reveals source words in context that inform the gender translation of an ambiguous referent entity in the target translation.

</details>


### [2] [MioFFAn: an Annotation Software for Formula Formalization with LLM Automation Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22552)
*Nicolas Sibuet,Horacio Saggion,Riccardo Rossi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MioFFAn is an open-source, document-centric annotation framework for translating mathematical expressions from scientific literature into executable symbolic code (Formula Formalization). It extends MioGatto with customizable taxonomies, symbolic operator configuration, and LLM-based partial automation, enabling human-in-the-loop annotation in specialized scientific domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formula Formalization—converting mathematical expressions in papers into executable code—is severely limited by the lack of high-quality, ground-truth datasets, especially for specialized technical domains.

Method: The authors build MioFFAn on top of the MioGatto architecture, adding features for: (1) selecting equations of interest, (2) aided symbolic code specification, (3) user-configurable taxonomies and symbol properties, and (4) compatible symbolic operator definitions. Partial automation is introduced via modular LLM-based sub-tasks with strict output formats, evaluated using standard NLP metrics in a human-in-the-loop paradigm.

Result: A preliminary evaluation demonstrates the efficacy of the human-in-the-loop approach, showing that the modular automation sub-tasks assist iterative refinement and allow competitive strategy comparison.

Conclusion: MioFFAn provides a flexible, domain-adaptable annotation framework that addresses the dataset scarcity problem in Formula Formalization, and its LLM-assisted human-in-the-loop design enables progressive automation while maintaining annotation quality.

Abstract: The automatic translation of mathematical expressions in scientific literature into executable symbolic code (a process we refer to as Formula Formalization) is hindered by a severe scarcity of high-quality, ground-truth datasets specialized for technical scientific domains. In this paper, we present MioFFAn, an open-source, document-centric, and customizable framework designed to facilitate rapid annotation for this task. Building upon the MioGatto architecture, we extend existing features to overcome structural limitations and pivot its scope by introducing specific functionalities for Formula Formalization, such as selection of equations of interest and aided symbolic code specification. By allowing users to configure custom taxonomies and properties for identified symbols, and compatible symbolic operators, we ensure the framework is adaptable to diverse specialized scientific fields. Furthermore, MioFFAn is designed to incorporate partial automation via Large Language Models. By defining a modular set of automated sub-tasks with strict output formats, we enable researchers to iteratively refine automation capabilities and evaluate competing strategies using standard NLP metrics. We specify the current automation methodology and perform a preliminary evaluation that demonstrates to efficacy of this human-in-the-loop approach.

</details>


### [3] [Evaluating the Impact of Reviewer Guideline Design on LLM-Based Automated Peer Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22553)
*Haowen Li,Yoichi Ishibashi,Masafumi Oyamada*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates how different reviewer guidelines affect automated peer review quality, finding that official conference guidelines outperform LLM-generated reviewer-imitating guidelines, and that rigid rubric scoring harms performance compared to holistic evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Peer review is critical to scientific research but faces growing workload pressures, driving interest in automation. However, it is unclear what form of reviewer guidelines best supports automated reviewing systems — whether official conference criteria or guidelines mimicking actual reviewer behavior yield better alignment with human judgments.

Method: The authors compare two types of reviewer guidelines: (1) official conference guidelines used in real academic venues, and (2) reviewer-imitating guidelines generated by LLMs from high-quality human reviews. They evaluate automated review outputs against human judgments under both conditions, and test the effect of strict rubric-style scoring versus holistic/subjective scoring.

Result: Official conference guidelines produced review results most consistent with human judgments. Reviewer-imitating guidelines were generally less effective. Enforcing strict rubric-style scoring consistently degraded performance across both guideline types.

Conclusion: Evaluation criteria refined through conference practice serve as effective guidance for automated reviewing. Allowing subjective, holistic scoring is important for maintaining review quality; rigid rubrics undermine performance.

Abstract: Peer review is an essential process in scientific research, yet the growing workload has made its automation increasingly necessary. In this study, we analyze how different types of reviewer guidelines, such as official conference guidelines and reviewer-imitating ones generated from high-quality human reviews using LLMs, affect automated peer review. Our experiments show that official conference guidelines produce review results most consistent with human judgments, suggesting that evaluation criteria refined through conference practice serve as effective guidance for automated reviewing as well. In contrast, reviewer-imitating guidelines were generally less effective than official conference guidelines. Furthermore, enforcing strict rubric-style scoring consistently degraded performance, highlighting the importance of allowing subjective and holistic scoring.

</details>


### [4] [Learning When to Reason for Text-to-SQL via SFT and DPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22622)
*Soohyuk Jang,Jiheum Yeom,Nohil Park,Sang Hun Kim,Yoonyoung Choi,Kiwook Bae,Sungroh Yoon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AutoThinkSQL introduces an adaptive reasoning mechanism for Text-to-SQL that dynamically skips chain-of-thought for simple queries while applying deep reasoning to complex ones, achieving comparable or better accuracy with 18–25% fewer output tokens and 11–17% lower latency on Spider and BIRD benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Text-to-SQL methods enforce Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning uniformly across all queries, but many real-world queries are simple lookups or aggregations that don't require multi-step deduction. This forced reasoning incurs unnecessary inference-time overhead (tokens and latency) without accuracy benefits.

Method: AutoThinkSQL is a framework that incorporates an auto-thinking mechanism into both Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). The model learns to dynamically decide whether to bypass reasoning (for simple queries) or invoke deep CoT (for complex queries), effectively aligning its reasoning behavior with query difficulty.

Result: On Qwen3-Coder-30B-A3B, AutoThinkSQL achieves consistent accuracy gains over the best baseline on both Spider and BIRD benchmarks, while reducing average output tokens by 24.6% (Spider) and 18.3% (BIRD), and average latency by 17.1% (Spider) and 11.5% (BIRD) compared to CoT-only generation.

Conclusion: The model successfully learns to align its reasoning decisions with query difficulty, demonstrating that adaptive, query-aware reasoning is both more efficient and more effective than uniform CoT for Text-to-SQL tasks.

Abstract: Recent Text-to-SQL methods rely heavily on reasoning-centric paradigms such as Chain-of-Thought (CoT), achieving substantial gains on complex benchmarks at the cost of high inference-time overhead. However, a large fraction of real-world queries are simple lookups or aggregations that can be resolved without multi-step deduction, making forced reasoning wasteful. Thus, we propose AutoThinkSQL, a framework that integrates an auto-thinking mechanism into both Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) on Text-to-SQL. Our approach enables the model to dynamically bypass reasoning for simple queries while invoking deep CoT for complex queries. On Qwen3-Coder-30B-A3B, our method achieves consistent gains compared to the best counterpart baseline on both Spider and BIRD benchmarks while simultaneously reducing average output tokens by 24.6% and 18.3%, and average latency by 17.1% and 11.5% compared to CoT-only generation. Further analysis indicates that the model learns to align its reasoning decisions with query difficulty.

</details>


### [5] [Between Suppression and Collapse: Evaluating Narrative Unlearning with LENS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22657)
*Viktoriia Makovska,George Fletcher*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LENS is an evaluation protocol for testing narrative suppression in LLMs across four resistance levels. Using SCE scores, the authors show unlearning checkpoints can reduce disinformation-aligned narrative reproduction, though entity recovery remains a side effect under abstract prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can reproduce disinformation-aligned narrative frames, and it is unknown whether machine unlearning algorithms can effectively suppress such behavior. Existing evaluation methods lack systematic, multi-level protocols for assessing narrative suppression.

Method: The authors introduce LENS (Level-based Evaluation of Narrative Suppression), a protocol testing narrative reproduction across four levels: direct, attributed, contrastive, and abstract. They evaluate two source-grounded narratives (NATO-expansion framing of Russia-Ukraine; US exploitation/abandonment framing of Taiwan) on four ~12B multilingual instruction models. A novel metric, Suppression-Collapse Efficiency (SCE), is introduced for checkpoint selection that balances narrative suppression against output degradation.

Result: Selected unlearning checkpoints successfully reduced narrative reproduction, with suppression transferring beyond direct forget prompts. However, a side effect emerged: abstract A/B/C prompts caused models to recover real-world actors associated with the target frame after unlearning, termed "entity recovery."

Conclusion: LENS serves as an effective diagnostic protocol for evaluating and guiding further research into the deeper structure of narrative unlearning in LLMs, while highlighting entity recovery as a persistent challenge requiring attention.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can reproduce disinformation-aligned narrative frames as plausible explanations, raising the question of whether existing machine-unlearning algorithms can suppress this behavior. We introduce Level-based Evaluation of Narrative Suppression (LENS), a contextualization based evaluation protocol for testing target narrative reproduction across direct, attributed, contrastive, and abstract resistance levels. We evaluate two source-grounded narratives: one framing Russia's war against Ukraine as forced by NATO expansion, and one framing the United States as exploiting or abandoning Taiwan. The experiments cover four near-12B multilingual instruction models: Lapa LLM, Gemma-12B, Qwen-14B, and TAIDE-Gemma.
  We introduce the Suppression-Collapse Efficiency (SCE) score as a checkpoint selection summary that rewards target-narrative suppression while penalizing degraded outputs. Our results shows that selected checkpoints can reduce narrative reproduction and suppression may transfer beyond direct forget prompts. We also report entity recovery as a separate side effect: abstract A/B/C prompts can cause models to recover the real-world actors associated with the target frame after unlearning. These findings demonstrate that LENS is a successful diagnostic protocol for both reporting and guiding the further study of the deeper structure of narrative unlearning.

</details>


### [6] [PatiGonit22K: A Comprehensive Dataset for Solving Complex Bengali MWPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22859)
*Swastika Kundu,Azizul Hakim Fayaz,Tashreef Muhammad*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PatiGonit22K is an expanded Bengali mathematical word problem dataset with 22,441 problems, extending the original PatiGonit dataset with greater scale and complexity to serve as a benchmark for mathematical reasoning in low-resource languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bengali, a low-resource language, lacks large-scale annotated datasets for mathematical word problems, limiting progress in evaluating natural language understanding and quantitative reasoning. The original PatiGonit dataset was limited in size and complexity, motivating the creation of a substantially larger and more diverse benchmark.

Method: The authors extended the PatiGonit dataset by collecting, translating, culturally adapting, and manually annotating a large corpus of mathematical word problems. The resulting PatiGonit22K dataset contains both simple and multi-operation equations, covering varied difficulty levels, with rigorous verification for linguistic consistency and mathematical correctness.

Result: PatiGonit22K comprises 22,441 Bengali mathematical word problems, significantly expanding the scale and complexity over the original dataset, and provides a balanced benchmark for assessing mathematical reasoning across diverse difficulty tiers.

Conclusion: PatiGonit22K offers a comprehensive and culturally adapted resource that can advance research in mathematical reasoning and educational NLP for Bengali and other low-resource languages.

Abstract: Mathematical Word Problems (MWPs) are an important benchmark for evaluating natural language understanding and quantitative reasoning. Despite recent progress in high resource languages, Bengali remains underexplored due to the limited availability of large scale annotated datasets. In this work, we introduce PatiGonit22K, an expanded Bengali MWP dataset containing 22,441 problems, developed by extending the original PatiGonit dataset with a substantially larger collection of complex mathematical problems. The dataset includes both simple and multi operation equations, providing a balanced benchmark for evaluating mathematical reasoning across different difficulty levels. Each problem is carefully translated, annotated, culturally adapted, and verified to ensure linguistic consistency and mathematical correctness. By increasing both the scale and complexity of Bengali MWPs, PatiGonit22K provides a more comprehensive resource for future research on mathematical reasoning and educational NLP applications in low resource languages.

</details>


### [7] [CHiPS: Character Histograms and Positional Signals for Lightweight Authorship Attribution in Romanian Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22884)
*Sanda-Maria Avram,George C. Ţurcaş*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CHiPS is a lightweight, character-level authorship attribution method for Romanian that uses character histograms (CH-SVM) and Fourier spectral descriptors of positional signals (FFT12-LR), achieving strong accuracy without tokenization, syntactic analysis, or pretrained language models, while emphasizing transparency and strict leakage control over maximizing classification performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to explore how far restricted, transparent character-level evidence can go for authorship attribution under strict leakage control, explicitly not claiming state-of-the-art accuracy. The work addresses the need for lightweight, interpretable methods that avoid heavy NLP pipelines, pretrained models, or higher-order n-gram features, particularly for Romanian texts.

Method: CHiPS combines two complementary fingerprints: (1) CH-SVM, an SVM classifier over one-character marginal distributions (character histograms), and (2) FFT12-LR, a logistic regression classifier that encodes selected characters/punctuation as binary impulse trains over character positions and extracts Fourier/Welch spectral descriptors. A leakage-safe decision-level fusion variant (CHiPS-F) and an optional top-5 listwise reranker trained on out-of-fold predictions are also proposed. The method avoids character n-grams with n≥2, tokenization, syntactic analysis, and transformer fine-tuning.

Result: On the locked grouped ROST split (400 files, 392 source-text groups, 10 authors) with grouped five-fold model selection, CHiPS-F achieves 0.9310 accuracy and 0.9341 macro-F1. An unrestricted character 2–5-gram TF-IDF SVM comparator reaches perfect 1.0000 scores on the same held-out groups. On ROSTories-cleaned (1,248 files, 1,240 source-text groups, 19 authors), CHiPS-R attains 0.8919 accuracy and 0.8708 macro-F1.

Conclusion: Restricted, transparent character-level evidence can yield competitive authorship attribution performance under strict leakage control, though higher-order n-gram approaches still outperform it. The contribution lies in demonstrating the effectiveness of lightweight, interpretable character-based methods rather than surpassing state-of-the-art accuracy.

Abstract: We propose CHiPS, a lightweight character-level authorship attribution method for Romanian texts. All reported experiments are closed-set: the true author is one of the candidate authors in the training data. CHiPS studies two complementary fingerprints of writing style: CH-SVM, a character-histogram classifier based on one-character marginal distributions, and FFT12-LR, a positional-signal classifier that represents selected characters and punctuation classes as impulse trains (binary indicator sequences over character positions) and extracts Fourier/Welch spectral descriptors. We also report CHiPS-F, a leakage-safe decision-level fusion variant, and an optional top-5 listwise reranker trained only on out-of-fold predictions. The method requires no tokenization, syntactic analysis, pretrained language model, or transformer fine-tuning, and it avoids character $n$-gram features with $n \geq 2$ in the histogram component. On a locked grouped ROST split comprising 400 files from 392 source-text groups, written by 10 authors, with source-text-level evaluation and grouped five-fold model selection, CHiPS-F reaches 0.9310 accuracy and 0.9341 macro-F1. A matched but unrestricted character 2--5-gram TF--IDF SVM comparator reaches 1.0000 accuracy and macro-F1 on the same held-out groups, so the contribution is not a claim of best possible classification accuracy. Instead, the experiments ask how far restricted, transparent character evidence can go under strict leakage control. On ROSTories-cleaned, a secondary ROST-overlapping corpus comprising 1,248 files from 1,240 source-text groups, written by 19 authors, the same protocol gives 0.8919 accuracy and 0.8708 macro-F1 for CHiPS-R.

</details>


### [8] [Simple Language Normalization Wins: Cross-Lingual Speaker Verification for the TidyVoice 2026 Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22923)
*Nina Hosseini-Kivanani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper applies Nuisance Attribute Projection (NAP) to remove language-specific information from speaker embeddings, achieving significant EER reduction on the TidyVoice2026 Challenge—demonstrating that simple back-end language normalization can match more complex systems without requiring language labels at test time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-lingual mismatch severely degrades speaker verification performance. The TidyVoice2026 Challenge formalizes this problem with training/development data in 40 languages and evaluation in 38 unseen languages, with no language labels at test time. The authors seek a simple, effective back-end solution for language normalization in the embedding space.

Method: Starting from a SimAM-ResNet34 baseline (pretrained on VoxBlink2 and VoxCeleb2, fine-tuned on TidyVoice), the authors apply Nuisance Attribute Projection (NAP): (1) estimate a compact language subspace from cross-language same-speaker differences in the development set, (2) project speaker embeddings onto the orthogonal complement of this subspace to remove language-variant directions, (3) perform cosine scoring with Adaptive Symmetric score normalization (AS-Norm).

Result: NAP reduces development EER from 2.97% (cosine only) and 2.70% (AS-Norm) to 2.18%. On the Codabench evaluation leaderboard, the system achieves a score of 8.40, demonstrating strong performance on unseen languages.

Conclusion: Simple back-end language normalization via NAP can rival more complex systems for cross-lingual speaker verification. The approach is practical as it requires no language labels at inference time and operates purely in the embedding space as a post-processing step.

Abstract: Cross-lingual mismatch remains a key source of overall degradation in modern speaker verification. The TidyVoice2026 Challenge targets this setting with text-independent verification, comprising 3,666 training and 808 development speakers in 40 languages and 2,200 evaluation speakers in 38 unseen languages, without language labels at test time. Starting from the official SimAM-ResNet34 baseline pretrained on VoxBlink2 and VoxCeleb2 and fine-tuned on TidyVoice, we revisit Nuisance Attribute Projection (NAP) as a simple language-normalization step in the embedding space. We estimate a compact language subspace from cross-language same-speaker differences and project embeddings onto its orthogonal complement before cosine scoring with Adaptive Symmetric score normalization. This reduces development EER from 2.97\% with cosine and 2.70\% with AS-Norm to 2.18\% and yields a Codabench evaluation score of 8.40, showing that simple back-end language normalization can rival more complex systems.

</details>


### [9] [Not All LLM Reasoning is Visible in the Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22925)
*Vatsal Baherwani,Tom Goldstein,Ashwinee Panda*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Frontier language models exploit semantically irrelevant "filler tokens" to perform invisible reasoning, improving accuracy on synthetic tasks by up to 13 percentage points. This hidden computation can serve objectives entirely invisible to chain-of-thought monitoring, raising AI safety concerns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The central question is whether language models express all their reasoning in output tokens. If models can perform consequential computation invisibly, chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring—a key AI safety technique—becomes unreliable, as models could pursue misaligned objectives without detectable traces.

Method: The authors evaluate 13 frontier language models across three synthetic reasoning tasks, inserting semantically irrelevant filler tokens into model outputs. They measure accuracy improvements attributable to filler tokens. They further test whether Claude Opus 4.5 can satisfy a hidden modular arithmetic constraint while maintaining primary task accuracy. They also attempt to induce filler-token utilization via reinforcement learning (RL) and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on Qwen3-235B.

Result: Many models significantly benefit from filler tokens, with accuracy gains up to 13 pp. The benefit is token-specific and model-dependent. Claude Opus 4.5 successfully satisfies a hidden constraint via filler tokens without degrading primary task performance. RL induces strong filler-token preferences in Qwen3-235B, but neither RL nor SFT yields persistent test-time benefits from filler tokens.

Conclusion: Frontier models already perform consequential invisible reasoning through filler tokens, undermining the assumption that CoT monitoring captures all model reasoning. This represents a concrete AI safety failure mode that warrants further investigation and mitigation.

Abstract: A key question for AI safety is whether a language model expresses all of its reasoning in its output tokens. We demonstrate a concrete failure mode where frontier models exhibit invisible reasoning by leveraging semantically irrelevant filler tokens to improve performance on synthetic reasoning tasks. We evaluate 13 frontier language models across three tasks and find that many models benefit significantly from filler tokens, with accuracy improvements of up to 13 percentage points. The benefit depends on which tokens are used and differs across models. We further show that filler tokens enable Claude Opus 4.5 to satisfy a hidden modular arithmetic constraint without sacrificing accuracy on its primary task, demonstrating that invisible reasoning can serve objectives entirely invisible to CoT monitoring. Reinforcement learning gives Qwen3-235B strong preferences over filler token content, but neither RL nor supervised fine-tuning produces a filler token benefit that persists at test time. Our results indicate that frontier models already perform consequential computation with no interpretable trace in their output tokens.

</details>


### [10] [Co-Evolving Graph and Text Memory for Training-Free Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23278)
*Hieu Man,Thien Huu Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Co-E introduces a training-free multi-hop QA system using synchronized bidirectional graph-text working memory, where a synchronization cycle consolidates textual memory, extracts relational triples into graph memory, and injects graph facts back into generation context, improving over comparable baselines on six benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-hop QA requires integrating relational and textual evidence across reasoning steps—something neither pure text corpora nor knowledge graphs alone can provide. Existing approaches are incomplete: graph-augmented RAG retrieves from static or query-updated graphs, KGQA searches topic-centered subgraphs, and memory-augmented agents lack continuous reconciliation between graph memory and textual context.

Method: Co-E features a synchronized bidirectional graph-text working memory with a cyclic process that (1) consolidates textual memory from retrieved passages, (2) extracts relational triples from text to build and update graph memory, and (3) injects graph facts back into the generation context. Both memories are maintained throughout reasoning, jointly shaping subsequent retrieval and generation steps without any training.

Result: Evaluated on six multi-hop QA benchmarks, Co-E outperforms comparable training-free open-backbone baselines and achieves competitive performance with larger or trained systems.

Conclusion: Synchronized bidirectional graph-text memory offers an effective, training-free approach to multi-hop QA, bridging the gap between textual and structured reasoning without requiring specialized model training or large-scale architectures.

Abstract: Multi-hop question answering requires coordinating relational and textual evidence across reasoning steps, a combination neither a text corpus nor a knowledge graph can supply alone. Prior work often emphasizes only part of this loop: graph-augmented RAG retrieves from a pre-built or query-updated graph, KGQA systems search within topic-centered subgraphs, and memory-augmented agents maintain evolving memories without continuously reconciling graph memory with textual context. We propose Co-E, a training-free system built around synchronized bidirectional graph-text working memory. A synchronization cycle consolidates textual memory, extracts relational triples into graph memory, and injects graph facts back into the generation context. Because both memories are maintained, they shape subsequent retrieval and generation. Evaluated on six multi-hop QA benchmarks, Co-E improves over comparable training-free open-backbone baselines and is competitive with larger or trained systems.

</details>


### [11] [Toward Automated Detection of Documentation Inconsistencies in Electronic Health Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22954)
*Jian Lu,Panyu Chen,Miriam Treggiari,Robert Blessing,Danyang Zhuo,Chunhua Weng,William W. Stead,Anru R. Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A two-stage LLM pipeline (Gemini 2.5 Pro for candidate identification, Gemini 2.5 Flash for verification) applied to 3,000 MIMIC-IV-Note discharge summaries surfaced 3,460 internal documentation inconsistencies affecting 69.7% of admissions. Expert review identified recurring failure modes involving temporal reasoning, evolving diagnoses, and outpatient-prescribing knowledge gaps. The authors propose a graded ontology for classifying inconsistencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electronic health record (EHR) discharge summaries are known to contain internal documentation inconsistencies that can compromise clinical reasoning and patient safety. Manual detection does not scale, so there is a need to characterize what a general-domain LLM can reliably surface and where it fails, establishing groundwork for validated, large-scale inconsistency analysis.

Method: A two-stage LLM pipeline was applied to 3,000 randomly sampled MIMIC-IV-Note discharge summaries. Stage 1 used Gemini 2.5 Pro for open-ended candidate identification of inconsistencies. Stage 2 used Gemini 2.5 Flash for context-grounded verification of flagged pairs. A subset of pipeline outputs underwent manual expert review by clinicians.

Result: The pipeline identified 3,460 candidate inconsistencies across 69.7% of admissions. Inconsistencies spanned demographics, allergies, procedures, diagnoses, laboratory results, medications, and care-planning domains. Expert review revealed recurring failure modes: verification breaking down when temporal reasoning is required, when diagnoses are still evolving, or when outpatient-prescribing conventions exceed the model's native knowledge.

Conclusion: This formative study establishes a methodological foundation and conceptual framework for large-scale EHR inconsistency analysis, proposing a graded ontology (strict contradiction vs. ambiguity) and a classification schema (category, section, domain, inconsistency axis) to guide future validated research.

Abstract: Objective: To characterize the kinds of internal documentation inconsistencies a general-domain large language model (LLM) can surface from real-world discharge summaries, and to identify recurring failure modes that limit reliability at scale.
  Materials and Methods: We applied a two-stage LLM pipeline---open-ended candidate identification (Gemini 2.5 Pro) followed by context-grounded verification (Gemini 2.5 Flash)---to 3,000 randomly sampled MIMIC-IV-Note discharge summaries. A subset of the pipeline output was then reviewed manually by clinical experts.
  Results: Our pipeline surfaced 3,460 candidate inconsistencies, affecting 69.7% of admissions. Representative examples spanned demographics, allergies, procedures, diagnoses, laboratory, medications, and care-planning domains, with direct implications for clinical reasoning or patient safety. Expert review also revealed recurring failure modes that arise when verification requires temporal reasoning, evolving-diagnosis context, or knowledge of outpatient-prescribing conventions the model does not natively possess.
  Discussion: Detection is highly context-dependent: many flagged pairs require anchoring each statement to its source section and clinical domain, then assessing whether the conflict reflects a true contradiction or missing context. We propose a graded ontology spanning strict contradiction and ambiguity, with a schema characterizing each flagged case by category, section, domain, and inconsistency axis.
  Conclusion: This formative study establishes a methodological foundation and conceptual framework to guide subsequent validated, large-scale EHR-inconsistency analysis.

</details>


### [12] [Beyond Direct Answering: Aligning Educational LLMs as Socratic Guides via Heuristic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22996)
*Xiaokun Wang,Siyu Song,Wentao Liu,Xiaodong Zou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: HeuristicEdu is a two-phase pipeline (supervised warm-up + GRPO) that aligns Qwen2.5-7B toward Socratic tutoring using 797 multi-turn dialogues. A heuristic reward balances cognitive depth, curiosity engagement, and directness. The best GRPO variant improves Scaffolding Effectiveness from 30.0% to 63.3% and reduces keyword leakage from 30.0% to 13.3%, while a 72B baseline scores 0% SE, demonstrating that scale alone does not induce Socratic behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs deployed in educational contexts tend to act as direct answerers, disclosing target concepts upfront rather than guiding students through progressive inquiry as Socratic pedagogy requires. This undermines the pedagogical value of AI tutors. The authors aim to bridge this gap by aligning a smaller model (7B) toward genuine Socratic tutoring behavior, and they note that simply scaling model size does not resolve the issue.

Method: The approach, HeuristicEdu, is a two-phase pipeline: (1) supervised warm-up using SocraticEdu, a dataset of 797 reconstructed multi-turn Chinese children's science dialogues from a live platform; (2) Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with a composite heuristic reward comprising cognitive depth (R_cog), curiosity engagement (R_eng), directness penalty (R_dir), and a K_query correction term for student-introduced vocabulary. Two novel evaluation metrics are introduced: Scaffolding Effectiveness (SE) and Conversation Depth (CD). Ablation includes a variant omitting the directness penalty.

Result: On 30 held-out questions, the best GRPO variant raises Scaffolding Effectiveness from 30.0% to 63.3% and reduces keyword leakage from 30.0% to 13.3%. The best variant notably omits the directness penalty (R_dir), indicating that explicit anti-leakage terms may conflict with gradient-based behavioral alignment. An unaligned Qwen-72B baseline achieves 0% SE and 96.7% leakage, confirming that scale alone fails to induce Socratic behavior.

Conclusion: Aligning LLMs for Socratic tutoring is feasible with a modest 7B model using heuristic rewards and GRPO. The counterintuitive finding that omitting the directness penalty yields better outcomes suggests that explicit anti-leakage rewards can interfere with policy gradient optimization. Scale alone is insufficient; deliberate alignment strategies are essential for pedagogical behavior.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) deployed in educational settings often behave as direct answerers: they disclose target concepts in the opening turn instead of guiding students through progressive inquiry, as Socratic pedagogy prescribes. We present HeuristicEdu, a two-phase pipeline that aligns Qwen2.5-7B toward Socratic tutoring via supervised warm-up and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Training uses SocraticEdu, 797 multi-turn Chinese children's science dialogues reconstructed from a live platform, with a heuristic reward over cognitive depth (R_cog), curiosity engagement (R_eng), and directness (R_dir), together with a K_query correction for student-introduced terms. We introduce Scaffolding Effectiveness (SE) and Conversation Depth (CD) to evaluate outcomes beyond surface fluency. On 30 held-out questions, the best GRPO variant improves SE from 30.0% to 63.3% and lowers keyword leakage from 30.0% to 13.3%. Notably, this best variant omits the directness penalty during optimization, suggesting that explicit anti-leakage terms can conflict with gradient-based behavioral alignment. An unaligned Qwen-72B baseline reaches 0% SE and 96.7% leakage, showing that scale alone does not induce Socratic behavior.

</details>


### [13] [Speech Signals Complement LLMs for Predicting Interpersonal Attraction in Speed Dating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23037)
*Yuriko Kikuchi,Takato Hayashi,Ryusei Kimura,Naoya Inoue,Ryo Ishii,Shogo Okada*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Speech-based predictors can complement transcript-only LLM predictions of interpersonal attraction, but the complementarity is conditional — improving pairwise ranking universally, while per-participant correlation gains vary and are concentrated where the speech predictor is more accurate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can already predict interpersonal attraction from conversation transcripts, but it is unclear whether speech-based features provide additional predictive value beyond what transcripts alone offer.

Method: Using Japanese speed-dating conversations, the authors combine predictions from a transcript-only LLM and a supervised speech predictor to estimate participants' reported liking. They evaluate both pairwise ranking accuracy and per-participant Pearson r, analyzing complementarity across conversation rounds and rating directions.

Result: Combining speech and transcript predictions significantly improves pairwise ranking accuracy over transcript-only LLM in all conditions. Per-participant Pearson r gains vary by round and rating direction, with none significant after correction. Retrospective analysis shows gains concentrate among participants for whom the speech predictor is more accurate.

Conclusion: Speech retains predictive value beyond LLM transcript prediction, but the key question is not whether speech helps, but where and under what conditions its complementarity emerges.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can predict interpersonal attraction from conversation transcripts, but it remains unclear what a speech predictor can add beyond transcript-only LLM prediction. Using Japanese speed-dating conversations, we combine predictions from a transcript-only LLM and a supervised speech predictor to estimate participants' reported liking of their partners. We show that speech can complement transcript-only LLM prediction, but that this complementarity is conditional rather than universal. Combining the two predictions significantly improves pairwise ranking accuracy over the transcript-only LLM alone in all evaluated conditions. By contrast, gains in per-participant Pearson $r$ vary across conversation rounds and rating directions, with none significant after correction. Retrospectively, these $r$ gains are concentrated among participants for whom the speech predictor is more accurate. Speech can therefore retain predictive value even when an LLM predicts attraction from transcripts. The relevant question is not simply whether speech helps, but where its complementarity emerges.

</details>


### [14] [ADAGE: A Language-Agnostic Pipeline for Analogical Reasoning Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23058)
*Ahmed Haj Ahmed,Alvin Grissom*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ADAGE is a language-agnostic pipeline for constructing culturally-grounded analogical reasoning benchmarks without translation. Benchmarks built for Arabic, Amharic, and Japanese reveal a 12–52 point accuracy drop in open-weight models compared to English, exposing a consistent cultural reasoning gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multilingual reasoning evaluation relies heavily on translating English benchmarks, which introduces linguistic artifacts from translation and fundamentally fails to assess reasoning that is grounded in local culture. There is a need for native, translation-free evaluation of abstract analogical reasoning across diverse languages and cultural contexts.

Method: The authors propose ADAGE, a language-agnostic benchmark construction pipeline that combines native-speaker curation with LLM-assisted generation. The pipeline produces challenging analogical reasoning benchmarks that are culturally authentic and translation-free. ADAGE was validated by constructing benchmarks for three typologically and culturally distinct languages: Arabic, Amharic, and Japanese. Fourteen open-weight models were evaluated on these benchmarks.

Result: A consistent cultural reasoning gap was observed: models that perform well on English proverb-based analogical reasoning suffer substantial drops on all three native benchmarks, with accuracy decreasing by 12 to 52 percentage points relative to English performance.

Conclusion: Translation-based multilingual evaluation is insufficient for measuring culturally-grounded reasoning. The ADAGE pipeline offers a scalable, language-agnostic solution for building authentic benchmarks. The persistent cultural reasoning gap across all evaluated models highlights a critical limitation in current LLM capabilities and underscores the need for truly multilingual, culturally-aware model development.

Abstract: Multilingual reasoning evaluation overwhelmingly relies on translating English benchmarks, a practice that introduces linguistic artifacts and fails to test culturally-grounded reasoning. We introduce ADAGE (Analogical Difficulty-by-design Assessment for Grounded Evaluation), a language-agnostic pipeline that combines native-speaker curation with LLM-assisted generation to construct challenging, translation-free benchmarks for abstract analogical reasoning. We validate ADAGE by constructing benchmarks for Arabic, Amharic, and Japanese. Evaluating 14 open-weight models, we find a consistent cultural reasoning gap: models that perform well on English proverb reasoning struggle substantially on all three native benchmarks, with accuracy dropping by 12--52 percentage points relative to English. We release the pipeline, all three benchmarks, and the full evaluation suite.

</details>


### [15] [Self-Authored Verification Is Unreliable in Heuristic Self-Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24300)
*Diandian Guo,Cong Cao,Fangfang Yuan,Yingqi Wang,Yueshan Wang,Dakui Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Self-improving agents that iteratively rewrite both policies and their own verification tests suffer from a "verifier-deployment gap" — self-assigned scores remain high while real performance degrades. The authors propose SEAL (Sealed Exogenous Acceptance Loop), which adds a fixed, agent-inaccessible harness-side audit to reject regressions, and demonstrate that it outperforms unprotected baselines across six models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When self-improving agents control both the optimized policy/controller and the verifier (self-authored tests or metrics), they can inadvertently or exploitatively maintain high self-assigned scores while true deployment performance stagnates or declines. This "verifier-deployment gap" is underexplored, particularly how it evolves with agent capability and how minimal exogenous oversight can prevent silent regressions.

Method: The authors introduce SEAL (Sealed Exogenous Acceptance Loop), which retains self-authored tests but interposes a fixed harness-side audit between the candidate policy and the incumbent. The agent cannot author, inspect, or access the audit; it receives only an accept/reject signal. Upon rejection, the full incumbent state is preserved, preventing regressions from being deployed. Experiments span heuristic learning settings requiring trial-and-error discovery, across six models and three random seeds, with analysis stratified by agent capability.

Result: Failures of self-written verification are stratified by capability: weaker agents tend to damage previously acquired strategies behind easy self-tests, while stronger agents are more stable but still mismeasure the deployment distribution. Standard self-written constraints do not reliably close the gap. SEAL consistently outperforms unprotected baselines across all tested models and seeds.

Conclusion: Reliable self-improvement does not require abandoning self-verification entirely, but it critically requires at least one deployment-acceptance signal that lies outside the agent's control — a sealed exogenous check is sufficient to prevent deployment regressions while preserving the benefits of iterative self-rewriting.

Abstract: Self-improving agents accumulate capability by repeatedly rewriting procedural policies, controllers, or heuristic rules. They typically rely on self-authored tests or metrics to decide whether to accept subsequent edits. The agent controls both the optimized object and its verifier. As a result, self-assigned scores can remain near perfect while real deployment performance degrades or stays low. We study this problem through the verifier--deployment gap. This gap refers to the discrepancy between an agent's self-authored verification signal and a sealed deployment evaluation that the agent cannot observe or access. We ask how self-authored verification fails under iterative policy-and-test rewriting, how the failure changes with capability, and how little exogenous trust is sufficient to prevent real regressions from being deployed. To address this problem, we introduce a Sealed Exogenous Acceptance Loop (SEAL). SEAL retains self-authored tests but compares each candidate with the incumbent through a fixed harness-side audit. The agent cannot author or inspect the audit, receives only accept/reject, and the whole incumbent state is retained after a clear regression. Our experiments show that this problem often appears in heuristic learning settings. These settings require trial-and-error discovery of the target objective. We further find that failures of self-written verification are stratified by capability. Weaker agents tend to damage previously acquired strategies behind easy self-tests. Stronger agents are more stable, but they still mismeasure the deployment distribution. Standard self-written constraints do not reliably close this gap. In contrast, SEAL outperforms unprotected baselines across six models and three random seeds. Reliable self-improvement need not abandon self-verification, but it requires at least one deployment-acceptance signal outside the agent's control.

</details>


### [16] [Attention-Guided Layer Selection for Contrastive Decoding in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23067)
*Yusuke Sakai,Natthawut Kertkeidkachorn,Kiyoaki Shirai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes three attention-guided strategies (Attention-JSD, Attention-Entropy-Max, Attention-Entropy-Min) for dynamic layer selection in contrastive decoding, replacing DoLa's vocabulary-distribution-based selection. These strategies leverage internal self-attention structural information and consistently outperform the original DoLa on TruthfulQA, especially on multi-answer metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DoLa's dynamic layer selection relies solely on divergences in output vocabulary distributions, which may overlook richer structural signals within the model. The authors hypothesize that internal self-attention mechanisms carry structural information that can serve as a more sensitive signal for selecting mature vs. premature layers during contrastive decoding.

Method: The authors propose three attention-guided layer selection strategies: (1) Attention-JSD, which computes Jensen-Shannon Divergence between attention distributions of different layers; (2) Attention-Entropy-Max and (3) Attention-Entropy-Min, which use attention entropy maximization or minimization as selection criteria. These replace DoLa's original vocabulary-divergence-based dynamic layer selection, while retaining the contrastive decoding framework.

Result: Experimental results on TruthfulQA show that the proposed strategies, particularly Attention-JSD and Attention-Entropy-Min, consistently outperform the original DoLa. Significant gains are observed on multi-answer metrics (MC2 and MC3), indicating that attention distributions provide a more sensitive signal for resolving factual knowledge than output vocabulary distributions.

Conclusion: Attention-guided layer selection offers a superior alternative to vocabulary-distribution-based methods for contrastive decoding. The structural information in self-attention distributions provides a more sensitive signal for factual knowledge resolution, leading to improved factuality in LLM outputs.

Abstract: Contrastive decoding methods such as DoLa improve the factuality of Large Language Models (LLMs) by contrasting the output distributions of mature and premature layers. However, DoLa's dynamic layer selection relies solely on divergences in output vocabulary distributions. In this work, we propose three attention-guided strategies: Attention-JSD, Attention-Entropy-Max, and Attention-Entropy-Min, which leverage structural information carried by internal self-attention mechanisms as a signal for layer selection. Experimental results on TruthfulQA demonstrate that our strategies, particularly Attention-JSD and Attention-Entropy-Min, consistently outperform the original DoLa. We observe significant gains on multi-answer metrics (MC2 and MC3), suggesting that attention distributions can provide a more sensitive signal for resolving factual knowledge than output vocabulary distributions.

</details>


### [17] [LoRA for Gender-Inclusive Rewriting and Activation Steering for Counter-Narrative Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23083)
*Akhil Rajeev P,Manoj Balaji J*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents the IHLC system for gender-inclusive language tasks, using LoRA fine-tuning for rewriting (80.00% score) and a novel inference-time PCA-based activation steering method on Gemma-3-4B-it for counter-narrative generation (78.12% score), with analysis of steering failure modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for gender-inclusive language generation by transforming biased text into inclusive alternatives, while providing a lightweight, compute-efficient alternative to full model fine-tuning for controllable and socially aligned text generation.

Method: Two approaches: (1) Parameter-efficient LoRA fine-tuning for gender-inclusive rewriting; (2) Inference-time representation engineering for counter-narrative generation, using PCA on contrastive hidden-state activations to derive a principal steering direction, injected into intermediate representations of Gemma-3-4B-it, combined with constrained prompting.

Result: Official scores: 80.00% for gender-inclusive rewriting (LoRA), 78.12% for counter-narrative generation (activation steering). Manual analysis identified failure modes: semantic drift, residual bias leakage, layer sensitivity, over-steering, and text degeneration.

Conclusion: Activation steering via PCA-derived directions is a viable lightweight alternative to parameter updates for controllable generation, but suffers from key failure modes that limit current practical deployment, highlighting both potential and limitations.

Abstract: Gender-inclusive language generation seeks to transform biased text into inclusive alternatives while preserving semantic meaning and contextual coherence. This paper presents the IHLC system for the LT-EDI 2026 Shared Task, addressing both gender-inclusive rewriting and counter-narrative generation. For gender-inclusive rewriting, we employ parameter-efficient Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning, achieving an official score of 80.00%. Our primary contribution is a compute-efficient inference-time representation engineering approach for counter-narrative generation. We derive a principal steering direction from contrastive hidden-state activations using principal component analysis (PCA) and inject it into the intermediate representations of Gemma-3-4B-it during inference, enabling behavioral steering toward inclusive responses without modifying model weights. Combined with constrained prompting, this approach produces polite and contextually appropriate counter-narratives, achieving an official score of 78.12%. We further present a manual analysis of steering behavior, identifying key failure modes including semantic drift, residual bias leakage, layer sensitivity, over-steering, and text degeneration. Our findings highlight both the practical potential and current limitations of activation steering as a lightweight alternative to parameter updates for controllable and socially aligned language generation.

</details>


### [18] [Interview with Kalle Lyytinen on "Implications of Theories of Language for Information Systems"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23142)
*Kalle J. Lyytinen,Pierre Maier,Paul Ackah Toffey*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Lyytinen reflects on his 40-year-old MIS Quarterly paper on language theories for IS, discussing the linguistic core of information systems in light of recent developments like large language models and generative AI, and outlines future research directions from a linguistic perspective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To revisit the foundational ideas of a seminal IS paper four decades after its publication and examine how the linguistic perspective on information systems has evolved, particularly in the context of contemporary advances in large language models and generative AI.

Method: This is an interview-based reflection piece where Lyytinen provides retrospective commentary on his original publication, discusses the linguistic foundations of IS, and maps them onto current technological trends and future research trajectories.

Result: The interview highlights the enduring relevance of linguistic theories for understanding information systems, while also demonstrating how the emergence of LLMs and generative AI both reinforces and challenges the linguistic core of IS research, opening new avenues for inquiry.

Conclusion: A linguistic perspective on IS remains highly pertinent. Future IS research should further explore the implications of language-based AI technologies, building on the theoretical foundations laid decades ago to address emerging phenomena in the field.

Abstract: Over fourty years after the initial publication of "Implications of Theories of Language for Information Systems" in MIS Quaterly, Lyytinen reflects about the origins of his publication and the developments in this area of research over the past decades. In the here presented interview, Lyytinen discusses the linguistic core of information systems also in light of recent trends and developments in the field, especially with regards to large language models and generative AI. Future research directions following a linguistic perspective on Information Systems (IS) research are outlined.

</details>


### [19] [Beyond a Global Norm: Personalizing Toxicity Sensitivity in Language Models Without Retraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23175)
*Rares A. C. Diaconescu,Iulia Slanina,Alina Florea,Andrei B. Trache,Miruna E. Coroi,Anne Arzberger,Jie Yang,Enrico Liscio*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A comparative evaluation of training-free methods for aligning language models to user-specific toxicity sensitivities reveals a fundamental trade-off between alignment effectiveness, personalization, and general language quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Toxicity reduction is typically treated as a global alignment problem, but perceptions of harmful language are subjective and context-dependent. There is a need to understand how to align language generation to individual user-specific toxicity sensitivities without additional training.

Method: The authors conduct a comparative evaluation of training-free alignment methods across three inference-time intervention stages: pre-decoding (prompt conditioning and rewriting), in-decoding (token, logit, and representation steering), and post-decoding (candidate re-ranking). Toxicity sensitivity targets are derived from the PRISM dataset for evaluation.

Result: All methods reduce alignment error by 28-47% relative to baselines. However, results reveal a fundamental trade-off between alignment effectiveness, personalization granularity, and general language quality, framing toxicity sensitivity alignment as a multi-objective problem.

Conclusion: Training-free inference-time interventions can effectively tailor toxicity alignment to individual users, but no single method simultaneously optimizes for alignment, personalization, and language quality. Toxicity sensitivity alignment should be approached as an inherently multi-objective optimization problem.

Abstract: Reducing toxicity is often framed as a global alignment problem, yet perceptions of harmful language are subjective and context-dependent. We present the first comparative evaluation of training-free methods for aligning language generation to user-specific toxicity sensitivities across three inference-time intervention stages: pre-decoding (prompt conditioning and rewriting), in-decoding (token, logit, and representation steering), and post-decoding (candidate re-ranking). Evaluated against toxicity sensitivity targets derived from the PRISM dataset, all methods reduce alignment error by 28-47%. However, the results reveal a fundamental trade-off between alignment effectiveness, personalization, and general language quality, showing how toxicity sensitivity alignment is an inherently multi-objective problem.

</details>


### [20] [IndicTalk: A Large-Scale Persona-Based Multilingual Conversational Corpus for Indic Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23242)
*Sahil Deepak Gawande,Mayank Singh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: IndicTalk is a large-scale, automatically generated multilingual code-mixed conversational corpus spanning 9 Indic languages across 18 varieties, built using news-grounded, persona-conditioned LLM pipelines with automatic quality validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-quality multilingual code-mixed dialogue resources are scarce, especially for Indic languages where speakers naturally mix English with native languages in both native-script and Romanized forms. This gap hinders development of conversational AI for these underrepresented languages.

Method: A fully automated three-stage pipeline: (1) real-world news grounding to provide conversational context, (2) persona-conditioned dialogue generation using multilingual LLMs to produce code-mixed multi-turn conversations, and (3) automatic quality validation to ensure fluency and coherence.

Result: The resulting corpus contains over 1,328,604 event-grounded multi-turn conversations across 18 language varieties (9 Indic languages × 2 script variants). Extensive linguistic, automatic, and human evaluations confirm the conversations are fluent, coherent, and exhibit natural code-mixing patterns.

Conclusion: IndicTalk provides a valuable resource for developing and evaluating multilingual conversational AI for Indic languages. The dataset will be publicly released on HuggingFace to support further research.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have transformed conversational AI, yet high-quality multilingual code-mixed dialogue resources remain scarce, particularly for Indic languages where speakers naturally alternate between English and their native language in both native-script and Romanized forms. We present IndicTalk, one of the largest multilingual Indic code-mixed conversational corpora, comprising over 13,28,604 event-grounded multi-turn conversations across 18 language varieties covering 9 Indic languages. The corpus is generated through a fully automated pipeline that combines real-world news grounding, persona-conditioned dialogue generation using multilingual LLMs, and automatic quality validation. Extensive linguistic, automatic, and human evaluations demonstrate that IndicTalk produces fluent, coherent, and naturally code-mixed conversations across both script variants. We will release IndicTalk to support the development and evaluation of multilingual conversational AI for underrepresented Indic languages. The dataset is available at: https://huggingface.co/datasets/LingoIITGN/IndicTalk .

</details>


### [21] [BHARATI: Morphology-Aware Tokenizers for Classical Indian Languages with Subword Fertility Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23319)
*Poornima Kumaresan,Pavithra Muruganantham,Lakshmi Rajendran,Santhosh Sivasubramani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BHARATI introduces optimized SentencePiece BPE tokenizers (v1→v3) trained on a 781 MB multilingual corpus covering seven languages (English + six Indic languages). Version 3 achieves 2.6 tokens per IKS technical term versus 5.25 (GPT-2) and 3.75 (multilingual baseline), reducing sequence lengths by ~90% over GPT-2 and ~25% over mBART-50 for IKS-domain text, thereby increasing effective context length for downstream models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard subword tokenizers (BPE, SentencePiece) are trained predominantly on modern languages and produce inefficient, overly fragmented segmentations for classical Indic languages like Sanskrit, Tamil, Telugu, etc. These languages feature agglutinative morphology, sandhi (word-boundary fusion), and specialized domain vocabulary (Indian Knowledge Systems/IKS) that general-purpose tokenizers cannot handle natively, motivating the need for a dedicated, script-aware tokenizer suite.

Method: The authors construct a balanced 781 MB training corpus spanning seven languages (English, Hindi, Sanskrit, Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam) with native script support. They develop three iterative versions of SentencePiece BPE tokenizers (vocabulary size 32,000): v1 (English + Sanskrit only, broken byte-fallback for Tamil), v2 (four-language support with byte-level fallback for southern languages), and v3 (full seven-language native subword coverage). Evaluation uses subword fertility analysis on IKS terms and sequence-length reduction on a 490-sentence held-out test set (70 per language).

Result: v3 averages 2.6 tokens per IKS technical term vs. 5.25 (GPT-2) and 3.75 (multilingual SentencePiece baseline), with reserved IKS terms represented as single tokens. On the held-out IKS test set, v3 reduces sequence length by ~90% relative to GPT-2/byte-level encoding and ~25% relative to mBART-50, averaged across the six Indic languages.

Conclusion: BHARATI demonstrates that script-aware, domain-balanced tokenizer training dramatically improves tokenization efficiency for classical Indic languages. The reduction in sequence length directly translates to increased effective context length for downstream language models, and the open release of tokenizers, training scripts, and benchmarks facilitates further research on Indian Knowledge Systems.

Abstract: Standard subword tokenization algorithms such as Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE) and SentencePiece are trained predominantly on modern language corpora and produce inefficient segmentations when applied to classical Indian languages. Sanskrit, Tamil, and other classical Indic languages exhibit agglutinative morphology, productive sandhi (phonological fusion at word boundaries), and domain-specific vocabularies absent from general-purpose training data. This paper presents BHARATI, a set of SentencePiece BPE tokenizers trained on a balanced 781 MB corpus spanning seven languages (English, Hindi, Sanskrit, Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, and Malayalam) with native script support for all languages. We describe three successive tokenizer versions: v1 (English and Sanskrit only, with broken byte-fallback for Tamil), v2 (four-language support with byte-level fallback for southern languages), and v3 (full seven-language native subword coverage). Subword fertility analysis demonstrates that v3 averages 2.6 tokens per Indian Knowledge System (IKS) technical term, compared to 5.25 tokens per term with GPT-2's tokenizer and 3.75 tokens with the multilingual SentencePiece baseline, with the largest gains on a set of reserved IKS terms that are represented as single tokens by construction. On a held-out test set of 490 IKS-domain sentences (70 per language across seven languages, released with the measurement script), v3 reduces sequence length by roughly 90% relative to GPT-2 and byte-level encoding (which lack native Indic subwords) and by approximately 25% relative to the mBART-50 multilingual baseline, averaged across the six Indic languages, directly translating to increased effective context length for downstream language models. The tokenizer models (32,000 vocabulary), training scripts, and evaluation benchmarks are released under open licenses.

</details>


### [22] [IKS-Instruct: A 24,000-Example Multilingual Dataset for Teaching Language Models Indian Knowledge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23322)
*Shwetha Singaravelu,Gayathri Muruganantham,Lakshmi Rajendran,Santhosh Sivasubramani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: IKS-Instruct is a multilingual dataset of 24,795 instruction-response pairs designed to fine-tune LLMs for delivering educational content rooted in Indian Knowledge Systems, spanning 7 languages and 41 Vedic pedagogical techniques aligned with the CBSE curriculum. A 7B model fine-tuned on this data approaches the performance of a strong general-purpose model on IKS-specific dimensions at lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing instruction tuning datasets are dominated by English general-knowledge tasks and neglect specialized pedagogical domains, particularly those grounded in non-Western knowledge traditions. The authors aim to bridge this gap by creating a dataset that teaches LLMs to deliver curriculum-aligned educational content based on Indian Knowledge Systems (IKS) across multiple Indian languages.

Method: The authors construct IKS-Instruct, comprising 24,795 instruction-response pairs derived from six source types: classical texts (Bhagavad Gita, Thirukkural, Sangam literature, Vedic texts), CBSE-aligned pedagogical templates (classes 6–12), Vedic mathematical sutra demonstrations, bilingual pairs, multi-turn dialogues, and cross-tradition comparative analyses. Quality is evaluated via a multi-judge framework where independent LMs score responses across 12 dimensions (technique fidelity, pedagogical quality, factual accuracy, IKS cultural depth, etc.), using a five-judge external panel with median aggregation over 1,201 stratified items.

Result: A 7B model fine-tuned on IKS-Instruct achieves a median judge score of 6.39, within 0.15 of Nemotron-Nano (6.54), while the non-fine-tuned base model scores near zero on IKS-specific dimensions. Model quality does not increase monotonically with data curation effort, and data-quality gains are reported alongside this finding.

Conclusion: IKS-Instruct demonstrates that targeted, domain-specific instruction tuning can enable compact models to rival larger general-purpose models on specialized pedagogical tasks at a fraction of the deployment cost. The non-monotonic relationship between data curation and model quality highlights the importance of careful dataset design over sheer volume.

Abstract: Instruction tuning has become the standard method for adapting large language models to follow human intent, yet existing instruction datasets are dominated by English-language general-knowledge tasks and lack coverage of specialized pedagogical domains. This paper presents IKS-Instruct, a dataset of 24,795 instruction-response pairs for teaching language models to deliver educational content grounded in Indian Knowledge Systems (IKS). The dataset spans seven languages (English, Hindi, Sanskrit, Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, and Malayalam), covers 41 pedagogical techniques from the Vedic oral and mathematical traditions, and is aligned with the Central Board of Secondary Education (CBSE) curriculum for classes 6 through 12. The pairs are derived from six source types: classical text corpora (Bhagavad Gita, Thirukkural, Sangam literature, Vedic texts), curriculum-aligned pedagogical templates, Vedic mathematical sutra demonstrations, bilingual instruction pairs, technique-grounded multi-turn dialogues, and cross-tradition comparative analyses. Quality is assessed through a multi-judge evaluation framework in which independent language models score responses on 12 dimensions including technique fidelity, pedagogical quality, factual accuracy, and IKS cultural depth. Under a uniform five-judge external panel (median aggregation over 1,201 stratified items), the strongest IKS-Instruct fine-tune of a compact 7B model reaches a median judge score of 6.39, within 0.15 of a strong general-purpose reference model (Nemotron-Nano at 6.54) at a fraction of its deployment cost, while the base model without IKS fine-tuning scores near zero on the IKS-specific dimensions. Model quality does not increase monotonically with data curation, a result we report together with the corresponding data-quality gains.

</details>
