### [23] [GeoTEAM: A Geospatial Tangible User Interface for Exploration and Visual Analysis of Migration Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22825)
*Karen Penaranda Valdivia,Nujaimah Ahmed,Aswah Butt,Nao Nagashima,Sarah Hoyos-Hoyos,Krupa Shah,Elise van den Hoven,Robert McLeman,Roozbeh Manshaei,Gabby Resch,Jamy Li,Ali Mazalek*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: GeoTEAM is a geospatial tangible user interface (GTUI) that uses active tangible dials with touchscreens to enable collaborative, real-time exploration of migration data. Evaluated with nine pairs of researchers, the system promoted collaboration, intuitive sense-making, and user confidence through physical interaction and real-time feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Migration studies is interdisciplinary and requires collaboration among researchers with varying geospatial and quantitative data literacy. Existing GTUIs lack real-time visual feedback of data values to support collaborative trend identification, creating a gap for embodied, collaborative spatial data exploration.

Method: The authors co-designed GeoTEAM, a tangible system with active tangible dials featuring built-in touchscreens, deployed in a multi-surface environment (tabletop + wall displays). The dials control time and navigate map layers for net migration and environmental sub-drivers. The system was evaluated qualitatively with nine pairs of researchers possessing multi-expertise in geospatial data literacy.

Result: Qualitative findings demonstrated that GeoTEAM promotes collaboration, intuitive sense-making, and increased user confidence. These benefits were enabled specifically by physical interaction with tangibles and the real-time visual feedback they provide.

Conclusion: GeoTEAM successfully addresses the gap in GTUIs by integrating real-time feedback into active tangibles, demonstrating that physical interaction coupled with dynamic data visualization enhances collaborative spatial analysis in interdisciplinary contexts like migration studies.

Abstract: Migration studies is an interdisciplinary field, requiring collaboration between researchers with varying levels of geospatial and quantitative data literacy. Geospatial tangible user interfaces (GTUIs) offer promising opportunities for embodied and collaborative spatial exploration of migration data. Few GTUIs, however, provide real-time visual feedback of data values to help users collaboratively identify trends. To address this gap, we present GeoTEAM, a novel tangible system for real-time, dynamic regional exploration, co-designed with migration and HCI researchers. GeoTEAM features active tangible dials with built-in touchscreens designed to control time and navigate map layers for net migration and various environmental sub-drivers in a multi-surface environment with tabletop and wall displays. We evaluated this system with nine pairs of researchers possessing multi-expertise in geospatial data literacy. Qualitative findings show that our system promotes collaboration, intuitive sense-making, and increased user confidence, enabled by physical interaction and real-time feedback from the active tangibles.

</details>


### [24] [Validation of a Real-Time Manual Wheelchair Simulator through Biomechanical and Perceptual Measures: A Comparison with Overground Propulsion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22851)
*Ateayeh Bayat,Félix Chénier*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This study evaluated a real-time manual wheelchair simulator's ecological validity by comparing biomechanical (temporal, kinetic, waveform) and perceptual outcomes between overground and simulated propulsion in 13 participants. Temporal and waveform measures showed strong similarity, while kinetic variables differed more. Participants rated realism positively, supporting the simulator's partial ecological validity for training applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual wheelchair simulators offer safe, controlled environments for training and assessment, but their ecological validity must be empirically established to confirm that simulator-derived outcomes meaningfully transfer to real-world overground propulsion.

Method: Thirteen participants (10 able-bodied, 3 experienced manual wheelchair users) completed matched propulsion tasks — straight-line, acceleration, turning, ascending, and descending — both overground and on a real-time simulator. Instrumented wheels captured biomechanical data, analyzed via temporal, kinetic, and waveform-based comparisons. Perceptual data were gathered through task-specific realism ratings, a post-test questionnaire, and semi-structured interviews.

Result: Temporal variables showed relatively small between-environment differences, whereas kinetic variables exhibited larger discrepancies — consistent with prior simulator literature. Force, moment, and power waveforms demonstrated high similarity. Participants reported positive perceptions of realism, safety, and satisfaction, valuing the controlled practice environment.

Conclusion: The simulator demonstrated partial ecological validity, particularly for temporal and waveform-based propulsion outcomes. Accommodating the user's own wheelchair configuration likely preserves ergonomics and enhances realism, supporting the simulator's potential as an assistive technology tool for manual wheelchair propulsion training.

Abstract: Purpose: Manual wheelchair simulators can provide a safe and controlled environment for propulsion training, biofeedback, and biomechanical assessment. However, their ecological validity must be established to ensure that simulator-based outcomes reflect overground propulsion. This study aimed to evaluate the ecological validity of a real-time manual wheelchair simulator by comparing biomechanical and perceptual outcomes during matched overground and simulator tasks. Materials and Methods: Thirteen participants (10 able-bodied individuals, 3 experienced manual wheelchair users), performed propulsion tasks overground and on the simulator. Task segments included straight-line propulsion, acceleration, turning, ascending, and descending. Biomechanical outcomes were collected using instrumented wheels and compared between environments using temporal, kinetic, and waveform-based measures. Perceived realism and user experience were assessed using task-specific realism ratings, a post-test questionnaire, and semi-structured interviews. Results: Temporal variables showed relatively small differences between overground and simulator propulsion, whereas kinetic variables showed larger discrepancies, consistent with previous simulator comparisons. Waveform patterns for force, moment, and power demonstrated high similarity between environments. Participants generally reported positive perceptions of realism, safety, and satisfaction, and highlighted the value of practicing wheelchair skills in a controlled environment. Conclusion: The simulator demonstrated partial ecological validity, particularly for temporal and waveform-based propulsion outcomes. Its ability to accommodate the user's own wheelchair configuration may help preserve ergonomics and enhance realism, supporting its potential use as an assistive technology tool for manual wheelchair propulsion training.

</details>


### [25] [Reflections and Recommendations on AI Adoption Practice from a Mixed-Ability Research Group](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22886)
*Shalini Madan,Sreelakshmi Surabiyil Bindu,Veronica Pimenova,Ellie Seehorn,Venkatesh Potluri*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A qualitative study of a mixed-ability research team's experiences with generative AI, identifying five key themes (disability tax, identity homogenization, privacy risk, self-experimentation, and information seeking) and proposing lab-level recommendations for balanced, agency-preserving AI adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on AI adoption in research settings focuses primarily on collaboration and productivity, yet perceptions within mixed-ability teams remain divided and underexplored. The authors aim to surface the nuanced motivations, challenges, and practices of generative AI use within their own mixed-ability lab to inform more inclusive and balanced adoption strategies.

Method: The authors conducted a qualitative analysis of interviews with all five members of their own mixed-ability research team, employing reflective, first-person methods to elicit experiences and perceptions around generative AI tool use.

Result: Five thematic findings emerged: (1) managing disability tax and crip time, (2) homogenization of identity through AI outputs, (3) risks of disclosing private information, (4) self-experimentation and miscellaneous task use, and (5) AI for information seeking. These themes informed a set of lab-level AI practice recommendations.

Conclusion: The paper advocates for balanced, reflective AI integration in mixed-ability research settings, offering concrete recommendations that promote workflow adoption while safeguarding agency, disability identity, and privacy.

Abstract: Generative AI tools have recently been rapidly adopted by academics in mixed-ability research teams for both personal and professional tasks. While previous work on adoption of AI-based workflows has focused on collaboration and productivity, the perceptions of AI use within research teams remains divided. Through qualitative analysis of interviews of the five members of our mixed-ability research team, we discuss the motivations, challenges, and practices surrounding the use of generative AI in our lab. We reflect on experiences that shaped recommendations for balanced AI use that enable mixed-ability team workflows: (1) managing disability tax & crip time, (2) homogenizing identity, (3) risk disclosure of private information, (4) self-experimentation and miscellaneous tasks, and (5) information seeking. We build upon these themes to present AI practice recommendations we established for our lab to promote AI workflow adoption while preserving agency and disability identity.

</details>


### [26] ["Why SuaCode?": Understanding African Students' Motivations for Taking a Smartphone-Based Online Coding Course](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22940)
*Michael Addo,Nana Maryam Munagah,Victor Kumbol,Judith Uchidiuno,George Boateng*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A study of African students in a smartphone-based coding MOOC (SuaCode) found that 68.7% enrolled for intrinsic reasons—self-improvement, community learning, and solving societal problems—providing insights for better MOOC design targeting this demographic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Less than 1% of post-secondary African students leave secondary school with fundamental coding skills. Understanding what motivates African students to enroll in programming MOOCs can inform better content and assessment design, ultimately increasing the efficacy of these courses for underserved populations with limited access to quality CS instruction.

Method: The researchers administered an open-ended response survey to self-identified African students enrolled in SuaCode, a smartphone-based online coding course. They randomly sampled 450 out of 3,000 responses and analyzed them using a grounded theory approach to identify enrollment motivations.

Result: The majority of African students (68.7%) participated in SuaCode for intrinsic reasons, categorized into three main themes: self-improvement, learning alongside like-minded individuals, and acquiring skills to help address societal challenges.

Conclusion: Programming MOOCs targeted at African students should be designed with intrinsic motivation in mind—emphasizing personal growth, fostering community, and connecting coursework to real-world societal impact—to better engage and retain this learner population.

Abstract: Computer programming MOOCs are instrumental in providing students with high-quality instruction in areas where there is limited access. They are especially beneficial to post-secondary African students as less than 1% of them leave secondary school with fundamental coding skills. One strategy for increasing their efficacy for African students is to understand students' motivation for enrolling. These insights can inform the design of MOOC content and assessments to align with students' interests. We administered an open-ended response survey to (self-identified) Africans enrolled in a smartphone-based online coding course (SuaCode). We analyzed a random sample of 450 (of 3000) responses using a grounded theory approach. We found that most African students (68.7%) participated in SuaCode for intrinsic reasons such as improving themselves, learning with like-minded individuals, and gaining skills to help address societal issues. We discuss the implications of these findings in the design of programming MOOCs targeted at African students.

</details>


### [27] [The Help Ladder: Skill-Adaptive Peer Scaffolding for Real-Time Collaborative Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23031)
*Panayu Keelawat,Sriram Narlapati,Sangwook Lee,Darshan Nere,Griffin Ogura,Xinran Adeline Li,Sang Won Lee,Yan Chen*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Canary is an AI-powered system that scaffolds peer programming collaboration by decomposing complex problems into incremental, skill-appropriate steps — enabling students to contribute progressively rather than being overwhelmed by understanding an entire problem at once.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collaborative programming in classrooms is intended to foster peer scaffolding, but real-time collaboration frequently degrades into parallel solo work with minimal interaction. Formative studies identified that students are willing to collaborate but are inhibited by the cognitive burden of needing to comprehend a teammate's entire problem before contributing helpfully.

Method: The authors designed and built Canary, a system that uses AI to decompose programming problems into a graduated, step-by-step "ladder" — beginning with simple fixes and progressively advancing toward harder logic. Canary proactively alerts potential helpers to specific, accessible starting points matched to their skill level, enabling incremental contributions.

Result: Evaluation demonstrated that Canary's staged scaffolding approach reduced the perceived overwhelming nature of helping, resulting in more frequent and more effective peer collaboration compared to baseline conditions.

Conclusion: Decomposing programming obstacles into smaller, skill-matched steps lowers the barrier to entry for peer helpers, transforming collaboration from an all-or-nothing endeavor into a progressive, manageable process that yields measurable improvements in collaborative frequency and quality.

Abstract: Collaborative programming is a widely adopted classroom activity to encourage peer scaffolding, yet real-time collaboration often breaks down into parallel individual work with minimal interaction. Our formative studies reveal that even when students want to collaborate, they are held back by the effort required to understand a teammate's entire problem at once. We present Canary, a system that supports peer scaffolding by breaking down programming obstacles into smaller steps tailored to a student's skill level. Canary alerts potential helpers to specific places where they can start, using AI to turn complex problems into a step-by-step ladder that starts with easy fixes before moving toward harder logic. By providing this gradual ramp-up, Canary enables students to make quick contributions and progressively work toward solving their peers' problems. Our evaluation shows that this staged approach makes helping feel less overwhelming, leading to more frequent and effective collaboration among students.

</details>


### [28] [Touching or Chatting: The Utility of LLMs and Tactile Charts for Learning about Complex Chart Types by BLV Individuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23065)
*Tingying He,Maggie McCracken,Daniel Hajas,Sarah Creem-Regehr,Alexander Lex*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper investigates how tactile charts and LLM chatbots jointly support blind and low-vision (BLV) individuals in learning chart types, finding that tactile templates aid mental model formation and improve subsequent LLM-mediated data exploration, while text+LLM alone shows weaknesses for spatial reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BLV individuals lack adequate support for understanding chart types, which is essential for interpreting visualizations and collaborating with sighted peers. While LLMs are increasingly used for chart QA, prior work focuses on dataset exploration rather than chart-type learning, and users struggle with spatial concepts when mental models are weak. This study aims to understand how tactile learning and LLMs can be combined to address these gaps.

Method: An interview study with 12 BLV participants comparing two learning formats: (1) tactile chart + textual explanation + LLM chatbot, and (2) textual explanation + LLM chatbot only. The learning phase was followed by exploration of an unfamiliar dataset using alt text and an LLM. Thematic analysis was applied to the qualitative data.

Result: Tactile templates support BLV participants in forming chart-type mental models, which in turn scaffold subsequent LLM-mediated data exploration. The Text+LLM condition without tactile support exhibited clear weaknesses for spatial-reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Combining tactile charts with LLM-based interactive explanations offers a more effective approach than text+LLM alone for helping BLV individuals learn chart types and perform spatial reasoning during data exploration.

Abstract: Visualizations are central to communicating data, yet blind and low-vision (BLV) people often lack support for understanding chart types---knowledge that is essential for interpreting new visualizations and collaborating with sighted peers. Prior work found that BLV individuals viewed example tactile charts as more helpful than text-only approaches and preferred them for learning advanced chart types, particularly for understanding spatial layouts and shapes. Meanwhile, large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used by BLV individuals for chart explanation and question answering (QA), but have been studied primarily for dataset exploration rather than chart-type learning. Existing LLM-based chart QA also shows that users frequently ask about layout and structure, yet struggle with spatial concepts and misdirect questions when mental models are weak. We investigate how LLMs influence chart-type learning and whether tactile learning improves subsequent LLM-supported exploration. We extend our tactile chart learning tools with an LLM chatbot that provides interactive explanations and supports follow-up questions. In an interview study with 12 BLV participants, we compare two learning formats: (1) a tactile chart, a textual explanation, and an LLM chatbot; and (2) a textual explanation and an LLM chatbot. The learning phase was followed by exploration of an unfamiliar dataset using alt text and an LLM. Thematic analysis shows that tactile templates support BLV participants' formation of chart-type mental models, which scaffolds subsequent LLM-mediated data exploration. Text+LLM explanations without tactile support show weaknesses for spatial-reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [29] [From Vibe to Code -- and Back: Lexical Oscillation in the Formation of Design Intent with Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23126)
*Daisaku Sato*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Expert UI/UX designers using generative AI tools exhibit "lexical oscillation" — shifting bidirectionally between vague (L1 "vibe"), domain-specific (L2), and operational (L3) language — rather than moving linearly from intent to code. AI output mismatches prompt designers to reconsider their own intent, reframing the tool from instruction-follower to consultative interlocutor. Ambiguity emerges as a productive resource for design judgment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenges the prevailing assumption that natural-language prompts merely transmit pre-existing design intent. The authors argue this view overlooks how intent is dynamically formed through interaction with AI, and seek to reconceptualize the designer-AI relationship as situated and dialogic rather than unidirectional.

Method: Five expert UI/UX designers (11–20 years experience, M=15.4) designed landing-page hero sections using a generative AI tool. Data collected via think-aloud protocols and retrospective interviews. Analyzed through reflexive thematic analysis, using a three-tier lexical granularity framework (L1: vibe/atmospheric, L2: design-domain, L3: operational/code-level language) as a sensitizing lens.

Result: Designers exhibited "lexical oscillation" — movement back and forth across granularity levels, including returns from precise operational language (L3) to ambiguous "vibe" terms (L1). Mismatches between AI outputs and designer expectations were not treated as errors but as opportunities to re-examine design intent. Engagement consequently shifted from instructional to consultative. A single negative case (non-oscillating trajectory) suggested conceptual misalignment as a boundary condition.

Conclusion: Generative AI should be understood as a non-neutral "generative interlocutor" in the design process. Ambiguity, rather than being eliminated, serves as a resource for situated design judgment. The findings have implications for how prompt-based design tools are conceived and how design pedagogy addresses human-AI collaboration.

Abstract: Generative AI design tools make natural-language prompts a starting point for design, placing new articulation demands on designers. Rather than treating prompts as the transmission of pre-existing design intent, we ask how design intent is formed through situated interaction with AI. Five expert UI/UX designers (11-20 years' experience, M = 15.4) designed landing-page hero sections with a generative AI tool, recorded through think-aloud and retrospective interviews. Using reflexive thematic analysis, we used lexical granularity (L1 vibe, L2 design-domain, L3 operational language) as a sensitizing lens. Rather than moving from vibe to code unidirectionally, designers showed lexical oscillation, including returns from operational specificity to ambiguity. Mismatches with AI outputs were taken up as occasions for designers to reconsider what they meant, and engagement shifted from instruction to consultation. One non-oscillating trajectory -- a negative case -- suggested conceptual misalignment as a tentative boundary for future examination. We position AI as a non-neutral generative interlocutor and ambiguity as a resource for design judgment.

</details>


### [30] [Beyond Conversations: Spatially-Anchored Previews for Intent Disambiguation in LLM-Assisted Geometry Editing in Virtual Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23201)
*Junlong Chen,Amr Gomaa,Jens Grubert,Per Ola Kristensson*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper evaluates how combining traditional dialogue-based clarification with spatially-anchored graphical previews improves intent disambiguation in LLM-assisted VR editing workflows. A within-subjects study (N=24) shows the hybrid approach reduces conversation rounds and improves interaction stability and user experience compared to no-disambiguation baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Intent disambiguation is a key challenge in intelligent interactive systems, but research on incorporating it into LLM-assisted editing in immersive VR/AR environments is limited. Traditional dialogue-based disambiguation methods from 2D interfaces have not been systematically evaluated or augmented for immersive contexts.

Method: A within-subjects study with 24 participants completing complex geometry editing tasks in VR. Participants edited parameter-driven immersive scenes using LLM-assisted commands. The study compared a hybrid disambiguation approach (clarification questions + spatially-anchored graphical previews) against a baseline condition with no disambiguation support.

Result: The hybrid disambiguation approach yielded better interaction stability, fewer conversation rounds, and improved user experience compared to the no-disambiguation condition, as measured by both quantitative metrics and qualitative feedback.

Conclusion: The findings provide empirical evidence for the effectiveness of combining clarification questions with graphical previews for disambiguation in LLM-assisted immersive editing. The authors offer design guidelines for future integration of LLMs in advanced VR/AR systems.

Abstract: User intent disambiguation remains a key challenge in intelligent interactive systems. While they have been widely studied in dialogue systems in 2D interfaces, research on how intent disambiguation could be incorporated within Large Language Model (LLM) assisted editing workflows in immersive environments remains limited. Recent advances in LLMs create opportunities to leverage the immersive nature of virtual and augmented reality (VR/AR) environments to provide better disambiguation support. In this paper, we evaluate how traditional dialogue-based disambiguation can be augmented with spatially-anchored graphical previews to resolve ambiguous user commands in LLM-assisted parameter-driven editing workflows. A within-subjects study in which 24 participants completed complex geometry editing tasks in VR simulate scenarios where VR scenes are controlled by numerical parameters. Compared with the condition where disambiguation is not available, quantitative metrics and qualitative feedback indicate that a hybrid approach which combines clarification questions and graphical previews can support better interaction stability with fewer conversation rounds while improving user experience. These findings provide empirical evidence on the effectiveness of disambiguation methods in LLM-assisted editing of parameter-driven immersive scenes and inform design guidelines for future integration of LLMs in advanced VR/AR systems.

</details>


### [31] [A Taxonomy of Confabulations and the Perception-Reality Gap in LLM-Assisted Immersive Scene Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23213)
*Junlong Chen,Per Ola Kristensson*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper investigates confabulations (errors/hallucinations) produced by LLMs in immersive 3D scene editing, constructing a taxonomy of confabulation types through a user study with 24 non-experts, and proposing the "perception-reality gap" construct to describe the mismatch between actual and perceived error occurrence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly integrated into immersive 3D design workflows for rapid prototyping and accessibility, but their tendency to confabulate poses risks to user trust, satisfaction, and safety. This area of LLM errors in immersive 3D contexts remains underexplored.

Method: An exploratory user study with 24 non-expert participants performing LLM-assisted immersive 3D scene editing tasks. The authors catalogued and categorized observed confabulations to build a taxonomy, measured their prevalence and disruptiveness, and analyzed user awareness patterns.

Result: A taxonomy of LLM confabulation types in immersive 3D editing was constructed. Key findings include: quantifiable prevalence and disruptiveness of each confabulation type; the "perception-reality gap" — a discrepancy between actual confabulation occurrence and user perception; and a saturation effect where confabulation awareness diminishes under high cognitive load.

Conclusion: The paper concludes with design implications for mitigating confabulations in future LLM-assisted immersive 3D systems, emphasizing the need to address the perception-reality gap and the saturation of awareness under load to improve user trust and system reliability.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are being increasingly integrated into immersive environments and design workflows, providing application prospects in areas such as rapid scene prototyping for non-expert users and scene understanding capabilities for accessibility design. While many workflows that incorporate LLMs in immersive spaces are proposed, such systems can exhibit errors, potentially resulting in frustration, loss of user trust, and compromised user safety. This paper studies the underexplored area of LLM confabulations in immersive 3D scene editing contexts. Through an exploratory study with 24 non-expert users, we construct a taxonomy of the different types of confabulation observed in LLM-assisted immersive 3D scene editing. We report their prevalence and disruptiveness, and define the construct perception-reality gap to help understand the gap between the actual and perceived occurrence of confabulations. We highlight the observed saturation of confabulation awareness under load and conclude by discussing design implications for confabulation mitigation in future LLM-assisted systems in immersive 3D scenes.

</details>


### [32] [Cross Sensory Co-Design Tools and Interaction Qualities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23298)
*Albrecht Kurze,Klaus Stephan*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper presents two co-design tools—the Loaded Dice and the Wheel of Plush—developed over a decade to facilitate cross-sensory and multi-modal interaction design. These tools leverage technical synesthesia, allowing arbitrary sensor-to-actuator mappings, enabling intuitive exploration and ideation in affective computing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-sensory and multi-modal interactions hold promise for affective and emotional applications, but designing such interactions is difficult without appropriate co-design tools. The authors address this gap by providing tangible artifacts that make sensory mapping intuitive and accessible.

Method: The authors developed two physical co-design tools—Loaded Dice and Wheel of Plush—that embed multiple sensors and actuators. Both operate on the principle of technical synesthesia: any sensor (e.g., microphone, accelerometer) can be mapped to any actuator (e.g., LED, vibration motor) through simple, intuitive interaction schemes. These tools are deployed in co-design workshops for exploration, sensory sketching, and scenario-driven ideation.

Result: The tools enable participants to effortlessly create novel sensor-actuator combinations, fostering cross-modal creativity. Meaningful mappings between sensors and actuators were realized and validated through workshop use.

Conclusion: The Loaded Dice and Wheel of Plush effectively support co-design of cross-sensory interactions. The authors see further potential for the tools in broader affective and multi-modal interaction design contexts.

Abstract: What is the temperature of a loud cry? What is the color of a hand-clap? Cross-sensory and multi-modal interactions offer interesting possibilities in terms of inputs and outputs that might be leveraged for affective and emotional purposes. However, it is hard to design such interactions without the right co-design tools. We developed such tools in the last 10 years: the Loaded Dice and the Wheel of Plush. Both devices incorporate a multitude of sensors and actuators to demonstrate interactions and aid in co-design workshops. The tools incorporate the principle of technical synesthesia: the ability to map any of the included sensors to any of the included actuators. With intuitive simple interaction schemes it is possible to easily and effortlessly create new combinations. We discuss the core principles of the tools and how we realized a meaningful mapping between sensors and actuators. We further discuss how we use the tools for exploration, sensory sketching or scenario driven ideation, and where we see additional potential.

</details>


### [33] [Explainable AI through the Lens of Material Agency: Enabling Musical Interface Design with Neural Audio Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23309)
*Shuoyang Jasper Zheng,Anna Xambó Sedó,Nick Bryan-Kinns*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Proposes "material explainability" — transforming AI models into accessible, hackable design materials for artists through artifacts and activities, demonstrated via a case study in neural audio model exploration for musical interface design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional XAI focuses on understanding model decisions, but for artists and designers, the goal is different: they need to treat AI models as malleable design materials whose computational properties can be creatively explored. Existing explainability approaches fail to support this hands-on, exploratory, craft-oriented engagement.

Method: The authors introduce the concept of "material explainability" and illustrate it through a case study — building a resource repository that enables artists to explore neural audio models for NIME (New Interfaces for Musical Expression) design. They reflect on community-building experiences and a curated collection of musical interface designs co-developed with artists.

Result: Three actionable recommendations emerge for designing future XAI systems that support artistic practices: making AI models legible as craftable materials, fostering community-driven resource sharing, and enabling sustained creative exploration rather than one-shot explanations.

Conclusion: Material explainability reframes XAI for creative contexts by emphasizing accessibility, inclusivity, and hackability of AI models as design materials. Community-centered approaches are essential for enabling sustained artistic innovation with AI.

Abstract: Recent work in Human-Computer Interaction (HCI) increasingly treats AI models as design materials that have distinctive computational properties to shape design artifacts. Artists learn to work with the model "at play" to explore their emerging properties. The aim of explainability, in this view, is to make visible a crafting and hacking space to enable sustained creative practices with AI. In this chapter, we propose material explainability as a range of activities and artifacts that transform AI models into accessible and inclusive design materials in the workspace of artists, designers, and makers. We present a case study of building a repository of resources to enable artistic explorations of neural audio models in New Interfaces for Musical Expression (NIME) design. Reflecting on our community-building journey and the making of a collection of musical interface designs with a group of artists, we raise three recommendations on enabling the exploration of AI as materials in artistic practices to inspire future XAI design for artists.

</details>


### [34] [Visualizing in the Mind's Eye: Icon Design Shapes Mental Imagery of Fire Risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23369)
*Wen Xu,Anjana Arunkumar,Lace M. Padilla*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Abstract icons evoked more mental imagery than concrete icons, which in turn increased negative affect and led to risk-averse decisions — suggesting concrete visualizations can suppress affect-driven behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether and how data visualization design (specifically concreteness of icons) shapes mental imagery and subsequently influences decision-making, addressing a gap in understanding the cognitive mechanisms linking visualization design to behavioral outcomes.

Method: A preregistered study (n=400) comparing abstract geometric icons versus concrete house-on-fire icons for visualizing fire risk data, with mediation analysis examining the pathway from icon type → mental imagery → negative affect → risk-averse decisions.

Result: Abstract geometric icons evoked more, more diverse, and more personalized mental images than concrete house-on-fire icons. Mediation analysis revealed that increased mental imagery led to risk-averse decisions through the mediating role of negative affect.

Conclusion: Visualization concreteness influences decisions through a nuanced cognitive-affective mechanism: counterintuitively, concrete designs may suppress mental imagery and thereby dampen affect-driven risk aversion, whereas abstract designs promote richer mental simulation that heightens emotional response and cautious behavior.

Abstract: We introduce mental imagery, or seeing images in the "mind's eye," as a cognitive process that can be shaped by data visualization design and in turn impact decisions. We found in a preregistered study (n = 400) that abstract geometric icons visualizing fire risk data evoked more mental images than concrete house-on-fire icons, and also produced more diverse and personalized mental images. Mediation analysis showed that increased mental imagery subsequently led to risk-averse decisions through evoking negative affect. These findings reveal a nuanced mechanism through which visualization concreteness influences decisions: concrete designs may actually suppress affect-driven behavior by restricting mental imagery.

</details>


### [35] [Multimodal Data Comprehension: Understanding How Visual-Textual Chains of Information Influence Data Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23489)
*Arran Zeyu Wang,Fuling Sun,Danielle Albers Szafir*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper introduces an experimental paradigm to study how the order of presenting visualizations and text affects multimodal data comprehension. Using visualization-first and text-first sequential chains, the study finds that visualization-first promotes exploratory, hypothesis-driven understanding, while text-first leads to confirmatory comprehension where visuals reinforce textual claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the widespread combined use of visualizations and text in data communication, little is known about how these two modalities interact and influence people's comprehension of data. The authors aim to fill this gap by systematically investigating the interplay between visual and textual modalities.

Method: A novel experimental paradigm using two sequential presentation chains: a visualization-first chain (visualization → text) and a text-first chain (text → visualization). Participants describe the data iteratively at each step, across both crowdsourced and think-aloud settings. Comparison across the chains reveals how each modality contributes to and shapes comprehension.

Result: The visualization-first chain fosters exploratory comprehension characterized by hypothesis-driven discovery. In contrast, the text-first chain produces confirmatory comprehension, analogous to framing effects, where visualizations primarily reinforce and confirm observations already drawn from text.

Conclusion: The findings offer empirical evidence on multimodal information integration, highlighting how presentation order fundamentally shapes comprehension style. These insights carry implications for designing more effective data-driven communication strategies.

Abstract: Visualizations and text often work together to support effective data communication. Despite this common paradigm, we know little about how the interplay of these modalities affects people's data comprehension. We present a novel experimental paradigm to investigate multimodal data comprehension---the process of people comprehending information from multimodal visual and textual data---across both crowdsourced and think-aloud environments. Our methodology employs two sequential chains for presenting multimodal information---a visualization-first chain and a text-first chain---asking people to describe the data presented iteratively. By comparing how people's data comprehension changes across the chain, we can assess the information contribution of each modality and how they shape subsequent comprehension. We found that the visualization-first chain facilitates exploratory comprehension with hypothesis-driven discovery, whereas the text-first chain yields confirmatory comprehension akin to framing effects where visualizations serve to reinforce and confirm observations drawn from text. Our findings provide empirical insights into multimodal information integration, with implications for designing more effective data-driven communication.

</details>


### [36] [Plans Work in Mysterious Ways: Evaluating a Plan Mode for Spreadsheet Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23670)
*Aayush Kumar,Avik Dutta,Sumit Gulwani,Gustavo Soares,Advait Sarkar,Emerson Murphy-Hill*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A user study (N=24) comparing a Plan Mode prototype for spreadsheet programming against a non-planning baseline found that while task outcomes were similar, Plan Mode reduced refinement iterations and improved user perception of creativity support and human-AI collaboration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plan Modes are common in agentic programming tools, but it's unclear whether their benefits extend to end-user programming environments like spreadsheets, where users work iteratively and prioritize practicality over technical correctness.

Method: The authors built a Plan Mode prototype for spreadsheet programming and conducted a within-subjects user study with 24 participants, comparing it against a non-planning baseline.

Result: Both tools yielded similar task outcomes; however, Plan Mode reduced the amount of refinement users needed and improved their perception of the tool on dimensions of creativity support and human-machine collaboration.

Conclusion: Plan Modes offer meaningful benefits for end-user programming, particularly in improving user experience and collaborative feel, even when task performance is comparable. This has implications for designing future Plan Modes and understanding human-AI planning in end-user contexts.

Abstract: Plan Modes have become standard features in agentic programming tools, allowing users to gain transparency and control by working with the agent to develop a plan before task execution. However, it remains unclear whether the benefits of this feature translate to end-user programming environments such as spreadsheets. Since spreadsheet programmers tend to work iteratively and care less about technical correctness, upfront planning may not fit into their workflows as easily. In this paper, we build a prototype of a Plan Mode for spreadsheet programming and evaluate it against a non-planning baseline through a within-subjects user study (N=24). We found that despite similar task outcomes with both tools, using Plan Mode led to a reduction in refinement and a better perception of the tool across dimensions of creativity support and human-machine collaboration. We discuss the implications of these results for the future design of Plan Modes, and for the broader role of human-AI planning in end-user programming.

</details>


### [37] [Meandering Photo Lines: Fluid Multiscale Exploration of Photographic Archives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23769)
*Mark-Jan Bludau,Christian Tominski,Marian Dörk*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Meandering Photo Lines is a visualization that integrates spatial and temporal dimensions of photo collections into interactive, river-like pathways, enabling both analytical and serendipitous exploration across scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing photo browsing tools treat space and time as separate filtering/sorting dimensions, failing to represent the integrated spatiotemporal journey of a photographer's archive. This gap is especially significant for personal or documentary collections where the photographer's lifeline across space and time provides essential context and meaning.

Method: The authors propose two complementary views bridged by animated transitions: (1) a grid-based cluster overview for analytical browsing using spatiotemporal cluster signatures, and (2) meandering spatiotemporal curves inspired by rivers that facilitate reflective, serendipitous exploration. These views support multiscale navigation from high-level clusters down to chronological photo series and individual images.

Result: The approach was demonstrated on two case studies: a 100,000-photo documentary archive of Jewish diaspora life (1978–2021) and a 20,000-image personal smartphone collection. A think-aloud qualitative study indicated the system supports both analytical examination and curiosity-driven engagement.

Conclusion: Meandering Photo Lines successfully fills the gap for integrated spatiotemporal photo browsing, offering a novel visual metaphor that unifies overview and detail through seamless multiscale transitions, with qualitative evidence supporting its dual utility for analysis and reflective exploration.

Abstract: We introduce a visualization that interweaves spatial and temporal structures of photo collections into explorable pathways. Existing photo browsing interfaces typically use space and time separately for sorting, filtering, or mapping photos. What remains missing is an integrated visual form that represents the photographic journey itself. This is particularly relevant for the individual photographer's archive, where their unique lifeline is written into the collection, and it is this spatiotemporal trajectory that gives necessary context and meaning to the work. Inspired by the winding nature of rivers, fluid interaction, and un/foldable visualizations, Meandering Photo Lines visualizes adjustable pathways through a photo collection across space, time, and topics. Two complementary views enable visual exploration at different scales: A grid-based arrangement of photo clusters offers a more analytical overview, while meandering spatiotemporal curves facilitate more serendipitous and reflective exploration. Animated transformations connect these two views seamlessly for multiscale navigation from spatiotemporal cluster signatures down to chronological photo series and individual photographs. We demonstrate our approach through two case studies: an archive of approximately 100,000 photographs documenting the Jewish diaspora life worldwide from 1978 to 2021 and a personal smartphone collection of around 20,000 pictures. Qualitative feedback from a think-aloud study indicates that our approach supports both analytical examination and curiosity-driven engagement.

</details>


### [38] [Visible to the Court: How AI Is (and Isn't) Litigated in U.S. Federal Court Opinions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23888)
*Julie Yu,Rock Yuren Pang,Jevan Hutson,Katharina Reinecke*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A systematic review of 559 U.S. federal court opinions involving AI reveals seven recurring dispute areas, six AI technology categories, and four litigant types. The study finds courts rely on pre-existing legal doctrines rather than AI-specific laws, producing piecemeal governance that leaves certain AI harms unaddressed. Comparison with the AI Incident Database shows courts capture only part of the AI risk landscape.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI is being rapidly deployed in the U.S. under limited federal regulation, and courts are increasingly becoming the venue where AI-related practices are challenged. There is a gap in empirical understanding of the AI litigation landscape, which this study aims to fill.

Method: Systematic review and taxonomy-building analysis of 559 U.S. federal court opinions where AI plays a role in the parties' contentions. The authors taxonomized disputes by (1) common topics, (2) AI technologies implicated, and (3) parties involved (plaintiff and defendant types). They also compared their taxonomy to the AI Incident Database to assess coverage gaps.

Result: Seven recurring dispute areas, six categories of AI technologies, and four common litigant types were identified. Comparison with the AI Incident Database revealed substantial gaps in coverage, definitions, and prevalence between documented harms and litigated harms. Courts predominantly apply pre-existing legal doctrines rather than creating AI-specific law, resulting in piecemeal AI governance where outcomes depend on whether harms are cognizable under existing statutes.

Conclusion: Federal court outcomes are shaped less by where AI has caused harms and more by which harms are cognizable under existing statutes, leaving certain AI harms unresolved. The courts capture only part of the AI risk landscape, indicating a need for more comprehensive governance mechanisms.

Abstract: In the United States, artificial intelligence (AI) is rapidly deployed amid limited federal regulation. With courts become a recurring forum in which AI-related practices are scrutinized, it is important to empirically understand the AI litigation landscape to date. We address this gap through a systematic review of 559 U.S. federal court opinions in which AI plays a role in the parties' contentions, taxonomizing (1) common topics of dispute, (2) the AI technologies implicated, and (3) the parties involved, including common plaintiff and defendant types. We identify seven recurring dispute areas, six categories of AI technologies at the center of litigation, and four types of common litigants, alongside legal doctrines used by the litigants. A comparison of this taxonomy to the AI Incident Database revealed substantial gaps in coverage, definitions, and prevalence between documented and litigated harms, suggesting courts capture only part of the AI risk landscape. In addition, we found that court decisions primarily rely on pre-existing legal doctrines to manage AI rather than making new AI-specific laws, producing a form of "piecemeal" AI governance. As a result, federal court outcomes are shaped less by where AI has caused harms and more by which harms are cognizable under existing statutes, leading to certain AI harms remaining unresolved.

</details>


### [39] [Evaluating Closed-Loop EEG Feedback for Simulated Prosthetic Vision in Immersive VR: A Sham-Controlled Feasibility Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23889)
*Ruyi Cao,Lily M. Turkstra,Adyah Rastogi,Michael Beyeler*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A neuroadaptive training platform using EEG-contingent feedback was tested in immersive VR-simulated prosthetic vision. While both groups improved with practice, EEG-based feedback showed no reliable group-level advantage over sham, revealing limitations in the engagement index, single-session design, and post-trial feedback approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual prostheses users must navigate sparse, distorted artificial percepts via active search, and training efficiency is critical. The study aimed to determine whether EEG-guided neuroadaptive feedback — specifically an engagement index (β/(α+θ)) — could enhance perceptual learning and behavioral performance in simulated prosthetic vision compared to sham feedback.

Method: Twenty-two sighted participants performed an object-localization task in a low-resolution phosphene-simulated virtual desk scene using a VR head-mounted display with integrated dry-electrode EEG. Participants were allocated to either EEG-contingent feedback (engagement index) or visually matched sham (non-contingent) feedback delivered post-trial. Localization accuracy, completion time, workload, and EEG index modulation were compared between groups within a single session.

Result: Both groups exhibited comparable within-session performance improvements, attributable to practice, perceptual familiarization, or search strategy refinement. No significant group-level differences emerged in localization accuracy, completion time, subjective workload, or modulation of the β/(α+θ) index. Exploratory analyses revealed substantial inter-individual variability but failed to establish a feedback-specific relationship between the engagement index and behavioral outcomes.

Conclusion: Integrating EEG-contingent feedback with immersive simulated prosthetic vision is technically feasible, but the current approach — relying on the β/(α+θ) engagement index, a single-session protocol, and post-trial feedback — is insufficient to produce reliable training advantages. Future work must address these limitations to develop effective neuroadaptive training for visual prosthesis users.

Abstract: Visual prostheses require users to interpret sparse and distorted artificial percepts through active visual search. We developed an EEG-guided neuroadaptive training platform for simulated prosthetic vision in immersive virtual reality and evaluated its feasibility in a sham-controlled object-localization task. Twenty-two sighted participants searched a virtual desk scene rendered through a low-resolution phosphene simulation while EEG was recorded using a dry-electrode headset integrated with a head-mounted display. During training, participants received post-trial visual feedback based either on a commonly used EEG engagement index, $β/(α+θ)$, or on visually matched non-contingent sham values. Both groups showed comparable within-session improvements in localization performance, consistent with practice, increasing familiarity with the simulated percepts, or refinement of search strategies. EEG-contingent feedback did not produce reliable group-level benefits in localization accuracy, completion time, workload, or modulation of the targeted index. Exploratory analyses showed substantial individual variability, but did not establish a feedback-specific relationship between the engagement index and behavioral performance. These findings demonstrate the feasibility of integrating EEG-contingent feedback with immersive simulated prosthetic vision, while identifying important limitations of the EEG measure, single-session training protocol, and post-trial feedback design.

</details>


### [40] [SHARE: Towards Head-Mounted AR with User-Centric SLAM in Shared Human-Robot Workspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23901)
*Tianyuan Du,Tianyi Hu,Hanting Ye,Maria Gorlatova*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: SHARE is a user-centric SLAM system for human-robot collaboration that prioritizes AR user latency by adaptively scheduling edge resources and leveraging shared visual feature redundancy, achieving 43.3% latency reduction for AR users while maintaining sub-2cm robot tracking accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent SLAM systems for HRC treat all agents uniformly on edge servers, ignoring the heterogeneous latency requirements between robots and head-mounted AR users. This uniform allocation causes high lag for AR manipulation tasks, degrading user experience.

Method: SHARE introduces (1) a first-of-its-kind experience model capturing the distinct latency and accuracy needs of HRC agents (AR users vs. robots), (2) adaptive transmission priority scheduling based on this model, and (3) exploitation of redundant visual features across agents in shared workspaces to reduce edge computation time. The system was implemented on commercial AR headsets and a ground robot.

Result: Real-world deployment achieved 13.22 ms average latency for AR users (43.3% reduction from baseline), maintained sub-2-centimeter tracking accuracy for robots, and yielded statistically significant improvements in user perception in user studies.

Conclusion: Prioritizing AR user experience via adaptive, experience-aware resource allocation and leveraging cross-agent visual redundancy is an effective strategy for multi-agent SLAM in HRC, overcoming the limitations of uniform edge-based scheduling.

Abstract: Human-Robot Collaboration (HRC) in shared physical spaces using Augmented Reality (AR) interfaces is powered by Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM). Existing multi-agent SLAM systems rely on an edge server to combine visual findings of multiple resource-constrained agents, perform computation, and schedule updates to their local maps. However, the edge treats all agents uniformly and ignores the fundamentally different latency requirements of heterogeneous HRC agents: robots and head-mounted AR users. This uniform resource allocation often results in high lag for user manipulation, as it does not meet the stringent latency requirements of AR. In this work, we design, implement, and evaluate SHARE, a user-centric SLAM system that strategically prioritizes AR user experience while maintaining accurate tracking performance for robots. SHARE builds a first-of-its-kind experience model for HRC agents and adaptively adjusts transmission priorities to match it. To reduce end-to-end latency, SHARE leverages the redundancy of visual features acquired by agents in shared human-robot workspaces to reduce computation time induced by edge-based processing. Real-world deployment with commercial AR headsets and a ground robot achieves 13.22 ms average latency for AR users (43.3% reduction from baseline) while maintaining sub-2-centimeter tracking accuracy. User studies further reveal statistically significant improvements in user perception.

</details>


### [41] [A Sustainable Remote Access Architecture for Digital Inclusion through the Reuse of Discredited TV-BOX Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23935)
*Italo Thiago Felix dos Santos,Carlos Eduardo Correa Queiroz,Adevan Neves Santos,Edgard Luciano Oliveira da Silva*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A sustainable, low-cost Desktop Virtualization Infrastructure (VDI) is built by repurposing discarded TV Boxes as ARM thin clients and a refurbished Linux server, aligning with circular economy principles to support digital education.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Educational institutions with limited resources struggle to deploy and maintain computer labs. Simultaneously, digital inclusion and e-waste are pressing societal challenges. The authors aim to address both by reusing discarded electronics to create affordable computing environments for education.

Method: A VDI solution was implemented using a refurbished Linux server as the host. Confiscated TV Box devices (originally destined for disposal) were repurposed as ARM-based thin clients by installing a compatible Linux distribution. The system architecture follows circular economy principles of hardware reuse.

Result: The proposed approach yielded a functional, low-cost VDI suitable for educational settings. The TV Box-to-thin-client conversion proved viable, demonstrating that discarded hardware can be successfully repurposed to deliver desktop virtualization in resource-constrained institutions.

Conclusion: Repurposing e-waste into VDI thin clients represents a viable, sustainable alternative for supporting digital education initiatives, simultaneously addressing digital inclusion and electronic waste reduction.

Abstract: Digital inclusion and the volume of electronic waste (e-waste) are major challenges for society, with direct impacts on the educational context. Educational institutions, especially those with limited resources, face difficulties in expanding and maintaining computer laboratories. This paper presents the implementation of a sustainable, low-cost Desktop Virtualization Infrastructure (VDI) developed through the reuse of discarded electronic equipment. The proposed solution employs a refurbished Linux server and repurposes confiscated TV Box devices (originally intended for disposal) into functional ARM-based thin clients by installing a compatible Linux distribution. This approach is aligned with the principles of the circular economy by promoting hardware reuse and seeks to reduce the costs associated with deploying computing environments for education. The paper describes the system architecture, the device repurposing process, and the observed results, indicating that this approach represents a viable alternative to support digital education initiatives with a focus on sustainability.

</details>


### [42] [A Design Space for Quantum Circuit Visualizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24042)
*Hyeok Kim,Leilani Battle*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper presents a comprehensive design space for quantum circuit visualizations, derived from analyzing 194 cases (182 static, 12 interactive). It identifies encoding options, relates them to visualization principles, and proposes a unified grammar for cross-platform quantum circuit rendering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current quantum circuit visualization tools lack consistency due to idiosyncratic encoding choices across different quantum computing platforms and hardware providers. This fragmentation hinders sense-making and communication. A unified grammar is needed to enable consistent, cross-platform enhancement of quantum circuit visualizations.

Method: The authors systematically analyzed a corpus of 194 quantum circuit visualizations — 182 static cases from online tutorials, documentation, research publications, and public presentations, plus 12 interactive cases from prior systems. From this analysis, they constructed a design space that categorizes the encoding dimensions and variations observed across these sources.

Result: The resulting design space maps the full spectrum of encoding options used in quantum circuit visualization, revealing how existing practices relate to established visualization principles while also exhibiting unique, quantum-specific aspects not addressed by conventional guidelines.

Conclusion: The design space reveals opportunities for future systems in three areas: data structure design for circuit representations, cognitive support for quantum programmers, and integrability across platforms. A unified grammar based on this design space could enable consistent, scalable quantum circuit visualization.

Abstract: Quantum circuit visualizations play an essential role in supporting sense-making and communication of quantum programs. While several tools exist for rendering quantum circuits, they vary widely in encoding options due to idiosyncrasies among machine and platform providers. We observe an opportunity to coalesce these disparate rendering approaches under a single, unified grammar to enable consistent, cross-platform enhancement of quantum circuit visualizations. However, it is unclear how to design such a grammar to best support the quantum computing community. Towards this end, we contribute a design space of quantum circuit visualizations by analyzing 182 static and 12 interactive cases collected from online tutorials and documentations, research publications, public presentations, and prior systems. Based on our analysis, we discuss how our design space relates to existing visualization principles yet exhibits unique aspects. We conclude with opportunities for future systems regarding data structure, cognition, and integrability.

</details>


### [43] [The Effect of Photorealism Consistency between the Virtual Hands and Environment on the Sense of Body Ownership and Presence in Virtual Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24047)
*Nami Ogawa,Takuji Narumi*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This study examines how photorealistic rendering styles of virtual hands and environments affect body ownership illusion and presence in VR. A 3×3 mixed-design experiment (N=117) found that rendering style of virtual hands did not influence body ownership, but more photorealistic virtual environments increased presence. The results suggest different dimensions of virtual body realism differentially affect body ownership, calling for more nuanced classifications of realism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work has examined anthropomorphism's role in body ownership illusion, but the specific effect of photorealism (another key dimension of visual realism) on body ownership has not been systematically studied. Additionally, evidence regarding photorealism's effect on presence remains inconsistent. This gap motivates a controlled investigation of how the rendering style of virtual hands and environments jointly affect body ownership and presence.

Method: A 3×3 mixed-design remote VR experiment with 117 participants, manipulating two factors: (1) rendering style of the virtual hand (three levels of photorealism) and (2) rendering style of the virtual environment (three levels of photorealism). Participants experienced one of nine conditions combining hand and environment styles. Subjective measures of body ownership and presence were collected via questionnaires and analyzed.

Result: Neither the rendering style of virtual hands nor the consistency between hand and environment rendering styles significantly affected the sense of body ownership. However, more photorealistically rendered virtual environments led to significantly stronger presence. This suggests photorealism operates differently on body ownership versus presence, and that different dimensions of virtual body realism (e.g., anthropomorphism vs. photorealism) have distinct effects.

Conclusion: The findings reveal a dissociation: photorealism of the virtual environment enhances presence but not body ownership, while hand photorealism and visual consistency between body and environment affect neither. This underscores the importance of applying a suitable, multi-dimensional classification of realism when studying body ownership illusion in VR.

Abstract: Virtual reality (VR) technology allows users to feel a virtual body as if it was their own (i.e., body ownership illusion). Previous studies have explored how the visual realism of the user's virtual body influences the sense of body ownership in VR, focusing on the aspect of anthropomorphism. However, the effect of photorealism, another element that characterizes the visual realism of a virtual human, has not been systematically examined in the context of body ownership illusion. Therefore, we investigate the effect of the rendering style of virtual hands on the sense of body ownership, hypothesizing that the effect is affected by the rendering style of the virtual environment. In addition, we examine the effect of photorealism on presence (i.e., the sense of being there), as existing studies have offered inconsistent evidence. To this aim, we conducted a 3 x 3 mixed-design remote VR experiment (N=117) that factored in the rendering styles of both the virtual hand and the environment and analyzed the subjective data from the questionnaire. The results suggest that neither the rendering style of virtual hands nor the consistency of the rendering style between the virtual hand and the environment influenced the sense of body ownership. Nevertheless, the more photorealistically the virtual environment was rendered, the stronger the presence. Our results indicate that different dimensions of virtual body realism affect the sense of body ownership differently, thereby highlighting the importance of applying a suitable classification of realism to understand the body ownership illusion for virtual bodies more in depth.

</details>

### [44] [ReCon: A Resource-Constrained Benchmark for LLM-Based Cybersecurity Compliance Across Ingestion and Retrieval Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22885)
*Rohit Negi,Rishik Jain,Soumyo V Chakarborty,Amit Negi,Sandeep K Shukla*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks resource-constrained Large Language Models (LLMs) that don't require GPUs or high memory for automating cybersecurity policy compliance checks against standards like ISO 27002:2022, demonstrating that low-resource LLMs can achieve good accuracy for smaller organizations lacking expensive compute infrastructure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cybersecurity governance failures often stem from incomplete policy documents that don't fully comply with standards/regulations. Manual gap analysis between policy controls and regulatory requirements is labor-intensive. While LLMs and Agentic AI have been applied to automate compliance checking, existing research relies on high-resource environments (expensive GPUs, high-memory servers) inaccessible to smaller organizations.

Method: The authors benchmark compliance checking tasks using resource-constrained LLMs that operate without GPU acceleration or high memory requirements. They evaluate these low-resource models on the task of checking policy documents against ISO 27002:2022 controls, measuring agreement/accuracy across multiple policy documents.

Result: Low-resource LLMs demonstrated good agreement/accuracy in compliance checking of policy documents against ISO 27002:2022 controls, suggesting they are viable alternatives to high-resource models for smaller organizations.

Conclusion: Resource-constrained LLMs are effective for automated cybersecurity policy compliance checking, making AI-assisted governance accessible to organizations that cannot afford expensive GPU infrastructure.

Abstract: With the increasingly aggressive cyber threat landscape for governments, businesses, and institutions, as information and/or cybersecurity implementations are increasingly under scrutiny by regulators, it has been pointed out that governance failure is one of the major reasons for a weakened cybersecurity posture. A major component of Cyber/information security governance is the development, adoption, and implementation of a comprehensive information and/or cyber security policy document. The policy document must be in compliance with international or national standards and, if possible, with regulatory guidelines. However, it is often observed that policy documents are often incomplete with respect to industry standards or regulations and require revision when subjected to a thorough audit. Identifying the gaps between the controls and processes documented in the policy and those required in the regulations or standards necessitates extensive manual effort. The advent of Generative AI tools such as Large Language Models (LLMs) led to use of LLMs and Agentic AI tools to automate such compliance checks, as seen in a few research publications in recent times. However, such reported use of LLMs are experimented with high resource environments such as expensive GPUs and memory based servers. For smaller organizations such expensive compute platform may not be easily available. In this article, we benchmark the compliance checking tasks on LLMs that do not require GPU and high memory usage and the effectiveness of such resource constrained LLMs in compliance checking. Our experiments demonstrated that the low resource LLMs can provide good agreement/accuracy in compliance checking of policy documents against standards by experimenting with ISO 27002:2022 controls against multiple policy documents.

</details>


### [45] [A Modular Cyber Range Platform for Smart Energy Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22943)
*Vasilis Ieropoulos,Theofanis Eleftheriadis,Kyriakos Christou,Philippos Isaia,Maria Michalopoulou,Angelos K. Marnerides,Christos Laoudias,Maria K. Michael*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A lightweight, modular Cyber Range using FastAPI and QEMU/KVM virtualization that auto-deploys IT/OT environments via YAML. Demonstrated through a multi-stage IoT-to-military-power-outage scenario in a smart city, with native Modbus/TCP and MQTT support plus a Green Team human-performance evaluation framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critical infrastructure needs realistic training tools that connect digital vulnerabilities to physical consequences. Existing solutions are often heavyweight and lack integrated IT/OT fidelity for crisis scenario training.

Method: Lightweight CR architecture built on FastAPI backend and QEMU/KVM virtualization; YAML-based engine for automated complex network deployment on commodity hardware; native industrial protocol simulation (Modbus/TCP, MQTT); Green Team framework for human performance assessment during crises.

Result: Successfully demonstrated a Photovoltaic Plant scenario in a fictional smart city where a home IoT compromise escalates to a military base power outage, validating the platform's ability to simulate multi-stage, cross-domain attacks with physical consequences.

Conclusion: The proposed Cyber Range offers an effective, low-overhead platform for realistic IT/OT security training, bridging digital attack vectors and physical infrastructure impact, with extensible scenario design and built-in human performance evaluation.

Abstract: Modern critical infrastructure requires realistic training tools that bridge the gap between digital vulnerabilities and physical consequences. This paper introduces a lightweight, modular Cyber Range (CR) designed to simulate Information Technology (IT) and Operational Technology (OT) environments with high fidelity. Using the FastAPI framework as backend and Quick Emulator (QEMU)/Kernel-based Virtual Machine (KVM) virtualisation, the platform employs a YAML-based engine to automatically deploy complex networks on standard hardware with minimal overhead. To demonstrate its capabilities, we present a Photovoltaic (PV) Plant scenario set in a fictional smart city. The scenario follows a multi-stage attack that begins with the compromise of a home Internet of Things (IoT) device and escalates into a major power outage at a military base. The proposed CR, natively supports industrial protocols such as Modbus/TCP and Message Queuing Telemetry Transport (MQTT) and includes a "Green Team" framework for evaluating human performance during crisis situations. This paper describes the system architecture, automated setup, protocol-simulation capabilities, and scenario design.

</details>


### [46] [Practical Post-Quantum Cryptography for Bandwidth Constrained or Non-Terrestrial Networks, and Power Constrained Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23007)
*Elliot Eichen,Sylvia Llosa,Yueqi Chen,Sangtae Ha*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper proposes replacing PQ digital certificates with a shared secret key (SSK) framework combining KDC with preshared-key PQ handshakes and ephemeral key establishment, significantly reducing bandwidth, memory, computation, and energy overhead in constrained environments like NTNs and IoT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-quantum cryptographic algorithms impose severe resource burdens (larger certificates, signatures, keys) that are particularly prohibitive in Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTNs) and constrained terrestrial/IoT environments, limiting achievable PQ security levels and potentially preventing ambient-powered devices from supporting PQ authentication altogether.

Method: The paper investigates an alternative framework leveraging shared-secret ecosystems (e.g., 5G/6G) that combines a Key Distribution Center (KDC, e.g., Kerberos) with a preshared-key PQ handshake (e.g., DTLS-PSK) and ephemeral PQ key establishment (e.g., ML-KEM), replacing certificate-based PQ authentication (e.g., ML-DSA).

Result: Compared with certificate-based PQ authentication, the SSK-based approach reduces handshake bandwidth, endpoint RAM, computation time, and energy consumption while preserving PQ-secure AEAD, forward secrecy, and replay resistance.

Conclusion: The proposed SSK-based framework enables practical PQ security in severely constrained environments, with applications spanning NTN key management, UAV command-and-control, embedded medical sensors, and supply-chain/asset-tracking platforms.

Abstract: Post-quantum (PQ) cryptographic algorithms, particularly for authentication, are more complex than classical algorithms and require larger certificates, signatures, and keys. Establishing a PQ-secure network connection increases bandwidth, memory, computation time, and energy consumption. These costs are especially severe in Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTNs), where long propagation delays, intermittent connectivity, constrained link budgets, satellite handovers, limited terminal resources, and bandwidth-constrained satellite-to-ground links amplify the overhead of certificate-based PQ authentication. Consequently, applications such as key rotation and key management may be unable to achieve acceptable handshake reliability or support NIST PQ Security Categories above Category 1. Similar limitations affect low-power IoT devices and bandwidth- or energy-constrained terrestrial networks, where PQ authentication may restrict devices to Category 1 security or prevent ambient-powered endpoints from supporting PQ authentication altogether.
  This paper investigates an alternative cryptographic framework that replaces PQ digital certificates with shared secret keys (SSKs). The framework leverages shared-secret ecosystems that do not rely on asymmetric key distribution, such as 5G/6G, and combines a Key Distribution Center (KDC) (e.g., Kerberos) with a preshared-key PQ handshake (e.g., DTLS-PSK) and ephemeral PQ key establishment (e.g., ML-KEM). Compared with certificate-based PQ authentication (e.g., ML-DSA), the proposed approach reduces handshake bandwidth, endpoint RAM, computation time, and energy use while preserving PQ-secure AEAD, including forward secrecy and replay resistance. Applications include NTN-based key rotation and management, uncrewed aerial vehicle (UAV) command-and-control systems, embedded medical sensors, and supply-chain monitoring and asset-tracking platforms.

</details>


### [47] [Mask2Shield: Strengthening LLM Safety against Neuron-Pruning Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23015)
*Ying JinCheng,Minghui Xu,Yinhao Xiao,Xiuzhen Cheng,Wencheng Yang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Mask2Shield (M2S) is a masked-forward alignment method that trains LLMs under functional pruning conditions, forcing the model to distribute safety representations more broadly. This reduces vulnerability to neuron-level pruning attacks from 80–279 successful attacks down to 1–44 out of 313 prompts, while preserving general capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs, despite safety alignment, remain vulnerable to neuron-level pruning attacks where removing a small set of safety-critical units disables refusal behavior while leaving general functionality intact. This demonstrates an over-reliance on a sparse, removable safety-neuron set, motivating a defense that robustifies safety representations against such targeted removal.

Method: Mask2Shield employs a teacher-student knowledge distillation framework. During training, the student model is subjected to functional pruning (masking forward passes) and must recover safe refusal through remaining computation. A frozen, unmasked teacher provides complete benign responses to prevent capability drift. The method is evaluated against recomputed pruning attacks and TwinBreak (a distinct neuron-selection rule with iterative pruning).

Result: Across ten model configurations, M2S reduces successful recomputed pruning attacks from 80–279 to 1–44 out of 313 harmful prompts. Four capability benchmarks are generally preserved. M2S also proves effective against TwinBreak's alternative pruning strategy, demonstrating that the defense generalizes beyond a single attack type.

Conclusion: M2S effectively mitigates neuron-level pruning attacks by reducing the model's reliance on a small, removable set of safety neurons. The method makes targeted pruning substantially less effective while maintaining general model capabilities, offering a practical defense against this class of structural vulnerabilities in safety-aligned LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are safety-aligned before deployment to reduce harmful content generation.
