Yet neuron-level pruning attacks show that refusal can depend on a small set of removable units: disabling them can remove safety behavior while leaving much of the model usable. To address this problem, we introduce Mask2Shield (M2S), a masked-forward alignment method that trains a model under this functional pruning. The masked student must recover a safe refusal through the remaining computation, while a frozen, unmasked teacher supplies complete benign answers to limit capability drift. Across ten model configurations, M2S reduces successful recomputed pruning attacks from 80--279 to 1--44 out of 313 prompts while generally preserving four capability benchmarks. We also evaluate M2S with TwinBreak, which uses a different neuron-selection rule and iterative pruning procedure. Together, these results show that M2S makes targeted pruning less effective by reducing reliance on a small, removable safety-neuron set.

</details>


### [48] [GLST: Defending Confidence-Driven V2X Collaborative Perception Against Stealthy Multi-Attacker Feature Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23059)
*Ji He,Ying Wang,Lijie Zheng,Xinghui Zhu,Yulong Shen,Xiaohong Jiang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A new attack called "Pretend Benign" exploits the spatial-confidence mechanism in collaborative perception frameworks like Where2comm, injecting stealthy perturbations into uncertain but perception-critical regions. The authors propose GLST, a multi-perspective trust defense using global-local structural consistency, which significantly outperforms single-signal defenses, especially against multi-attacker scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Confidence-driven sparse communication in collaborative perception (CP) reduces V2X bandwidth but introduces a security vulnerability: compromised collaborators can inject malicious features into high-confidence or ego-uncertain regions, which get preferentially selected and amplified during fusion. Existing trust-based defenses relying on a single consistency signal fail under multi-attacker collusion (pseudo-consensus).

Method: 1) **Pretend Benign Attack**: Targets Where2comm's spatial-confidence mechanism by injecting stealthy perturbations into ego-uncertain yet perception-critical regions, maintaining benign-like feature characteristics. 2) **GLST Defense**: A lightweight trust assessment framework with three complementary signals — global feature consistency, multi-scale local residual consistency, and structural consistency with ego-side semantic topology. Trust scores guide fusion to suppress unreliable collaborators.

Result: GLST achieves competitive defense against single-attacker Pretend Benign attacks and substantially stronger robustness under multi-attacker settings. Under a four-attacker Pretend Benign attack, GLST maintains 0.69 AP@0.3 while single-signal defenses degrade severely. GLST also remains effective against gradient-based PGD attacks.

Conclusion: Multi-level trust modeling is essential for securing confidence-driven collaborative perception. Single-signal trust defenses are fundamentally vulnerable to multi-attacker pseudo-consensus, and the GLST framework's three-perspective approach provides robust, generalizable protection across diverse attack types.

Abstract: Collaborative perception (CP) improves autonomous-driving perception by enabling connected vehicles to exchange intermediate features via V2X. Confidence-driven sparse communication reduces bandwidth by transmitting only perception-critical spatial regions, but creates a security risk: once a collaborator is compromised, malicious features in high-confidence or ego-uncertain regions may be preferentially selected and amplified during fusion. Using Where2comm as a representative framework, we show that the proposed Pretend Benign attack exploits its spatial-confidence mechanism by injecting stealthy perturbations into uncertain yet perception-critical regions, substantially degrading 3D object detection while preserving benign-like feature characteristics. Beyond this attack-framework pair, we identify a broader weakness of existing trust-based defenses: their reliance primarily on a single consistency signal leaves them vulnerable when multiple attackers form a pseudo-consensus that biases trust estimation. We therefore propose Global-Local Structural Trust (GLST), a lightweight defense that assesses collaborator reliability through three complementary perspectives: global feature consistency, multi-scale local residual consistency, and structural consistency with ego-side semantic topology. The resulting trust scores guide feature fusion to suppress unreliable collaborators. Experiments on OPV2V show that GLST achieves competitive performance against single-attacker Pretend Benign attacks and substantially stronger robustness in multi-attacker settings. Under a four-attacker Pretend Benign attack, GLST maintains 0.69 AP@0.3, whereas existing single-signal defenses degrade severely. GLST also remains effective against gradient-based attacks such as PGD, indicating that multi-level trust modeling is essential for securing confidence-driven CP.

</details>


### [49] [Traceable LLM Reasoning for Fake-Order Fraud Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23075)
*Siqi You,Bingsong Xu,Zhixian Zheng,Xinjian Peng,Yang Xie,Ying Wang,Jiarong Xu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: DeepScrub is an LLM-based reinforcement learning framework for fake-order fraud detection that converts heterogeneous risk signals into text, uses domain-specific continued pre-training with joint task rewards, and employs a SUggest-REflect (SURE) mechanism for iterative reasoning refinement. It achieves 85.3% macro-F1, surpasses larger models via domain adaptation, and in a live pilot reduced manual review workload by 94% while saving nearly one million RMB annually.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing fake-order fraud detection on O2O platforms relies on expert-designed features, produces black-box decisions, and lacks interpretability, making it difficult to scale effectively while maintaining transparency for risk-review workflows.

Method: DeepScrub consists of three core components: (1) a semantic unification module that converts heterogeneous risk signals into LLM-readable textual descriptions; (2) continued pre-training on risk-control corpora with task rewards that jointly evaluate prediction correctness and reasoning quality; and (3) the SUggest-REflect (SURE) mechanism that incorporates expert feedback and model self-checking to iteratively refine reasoning paths within a reinforcement learning framework.

Result: On a real-world dataset, DeepScrub achieves 85.3% macro-F1, outperforming the best baseline by 2.7 percentage points. The task-optimized 8B model surpasses a 32B model, demonstrating that domain adaptation outweighs model scale. In a four-week live pilot, it achieved 91.8% precision and 88.5% recall, improving over human reviewers by 16.6 and 38.8 points, reduced first-stage manual review workload by 94%, and saved nearly one million RMB annually.

Conclusion: DeepScrub demonstrates that LLMs augmented with domain-specific pre-training and reinforcement learning can deliver both high accuracy and traceable reasoning for fraud detection, improving review efficiency, reducing manual workload, and providing interpretable evidence suitable for production risk-review pipelines.

Abstract: Detecting fake-order fraud at scale remains a critical challenge for large online-to-offline (O2O) service platforms, as existing approaches often rely on expert-designed features, produce black-box decisions, and provide limited interpretability. To address these limitations, we propose DeepScrub, a reinforcement learning framework built upon large language models (LLMs) for fake-order fraud detection with traceable reasoning. DeepScrub introduces three innovations. First, a semantic unification module converts heterogeneous risk signals into textual descriptions that LLMs can understand. Second, continued pre-training on risk-control corpora injects domain knowledge, and task rewards jointly evaluate prediction correctness and reasoning quality. Third, the SUggest-REflect (SURE) mechanism incorporates expert feedback and model self-checking to iteratively refine reasoning paths. On a real-world fake-order fraud detection dataset, DeepScrub achieves a macro-F1 score of 85.3%, outperforming the best baseline by 2.7 percentage points. Our task-optimized 8B model further surpasses a 32B model, showing that domain adaptation can matter more than model scale in this setting. In a four-week live pilot, DeepScrub achieved 91.8% precision and 88.5% recall, improving over first-stage human reviewers by 16.6 and 38.8 percentage points. It reduced first-stage manual review workload by 94% and saved nearly one million RMB annually. These results show that DeepScrub improves fraud review accuracy, reduces first-stage review workload, and provides traceable evidence for production risk-review workflows.

</details>


### [50] [Poster: Rethinking Security in LLM Code Generation through Real-World Risk Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23088)
*Lixun Ma,Ruolong Ma,Bei Wang,Feng Wei,Zhenguang Liu,Lorenzo Cavallaro,Wentao Chen*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A developer-centric benchmark of 2,700 test cases across three risk scenarios reveals that state-of-the-art LLMs exhibit average vulnerability rates exceeding 56% in code generation, though security-aware prompting can reduce risks by up to 45%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM security benchmarks rely on explicitly specified security requirements and fail to capture realistic development workflows where prompts are ambiguous or incomplete. The authors aim to evaluate LLM security behavior under authentic, developer-facing conditions.

Method: The authors adopt a developer-centric perspective and define three representative risk scenarios: (1) Ambiguous Requirements, (2) Under-Specified Operational Context, and (3) Security--Functionality Conflict. Based on these, they construct a large-scale benchmark of 2,700 test cases and evaluate eight state-of-the-art LLMs under these realistic conditions.

Result: All eight evaluated LLMs exhibit average vulnerability rates exceeding 56% across the three risk scenarios. Security-aware prompting substantially mitigates these risks, achieving up to a 45% improvement.

Conclusion: LLMs remain highly susceptible to generating vulnerable code under realistic, ambiguous, or under-specified development scenarios. However, security-aware prompting strategies offer a promising and substantial avenue for mitigating these risks.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are widely used for code generation, yet their security behavior in realistic development workflows remains underexplored. Existing benchmarks often rely on explicitly specified security requirements, failing to capture real-world scenarios where prompts are frequently ambiguous or incomplete. In this paper, we adopt a developer-centric perspective and identify three representative risk scenarios that commonly lead to security vulnerabilities in LLM-generated code: Ambiguous Requirements, Under-Specified Operational Context, and Security--Functionality Conflict. Based on these scenarios, we construct a large-scale benchmark comprising 2,700 test cases, enabling fine-grained evaluation of LLM security under realistic conditions. Extensive evaluation of eight state-of-the-art LLMs reveals that all models exhibit average vulnerability rates exceeding 56\% across risk scenarios. We further demonstrate that security-aware prompting can substantially mitigate these risks, achieving up to 45\% improvement.

</details>


### [51] [False Prophets: On the Security of World Models in Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23147)
*Erik Imgrund,Anna Wimbauer,Klim Kireev,Konrad Rieck*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper exposes security vulnerabilities in world models used by LLM-powered autonomous agents, demonstrating that attackers can manipulate these models to induce harmful actions (malicious code execution, data extraction) with up to 95% success rate, and provides a benchmark dataset plus mitigation recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While world models enhance LLM agents' predictive capabilities for multi-step tasks, their security implications remain unexplored. The authors aim to systematically identify and demonstrate how world models can be exploited to mislead agents into executing harmful actions, raising awareness of intrinsic risks in approximate world modeling for agentic systems.

Method: The authors: (1) systematically discover and categorize world model-specific vulnerabilities in terminal-based agentic systems; (2) construct a security benchmark dataset tailored for evaluating text-based world models; (3) empirically demonstrate attack vectors that induce mispredictions in agentic pipelines, measuring success rates across different attack types including unintended command execution, denial of service, financial drainage, and private information extraction.

Result: Attackers can induce mispredictions with up to 95% success rate. Demonstrated consequences include unintended command execution, denial of service, wallet drainage, and private information extraction. The paper also establishes that some vulnerabilities are intrinsic to approximate world modeling rather than mere implementation flaws.

Conclusion: World models introduce significant and partly intrinsic security risks to agentic systems. The authors provide a benchmark for future research and offer practical hardening recommendations for practitioners deploying world model-enhanced agents.

Abstract: Large language models now power autonomous agents capable of complex, multi-step tasks in different environments. Accurate and reliable execution of these tasks requires the agent to predict the results of its actions. Recent research proposes to enhance predictive capabilities via specially trained environment simulators-world models. While world models can improve performance, they can also mislead agents into executing harmful actions, creating significant security and privacy risks. In this paper, we raise security concerns regarding the usage of world models in agentic systems. We discover a range of world model specific vulnerabilities, which can be exploited in terminal-based agents to execute malicious code or extract sensitive data. To facilitate future development, we introduce a security benchmark dataset designed for text-based world models. We argue that some risks are intrinsic to approximate world modeling, and show that attackers can induce mispredictions in agentic pipelines with up to 95% success rate, possibly resulting in unintended command execution, denial of service, drainage of wallet and private information extraction. Finally, we provide practical recommendations for practitioners to mitigate the discovered harms and harden agentic systems.

</details>


### [52] [SPARC: Automated Root-Cause Analysis of Pre-Silicon Power Side-Channel Leakage in the Processor Design Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23218)
*Andrija Nešković,Christian Ewert,Mladen Berekovic,Saleh Mulhem*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: SPARC is an automated pre-silicon framework that combines macro-cell-level Information Flow Tracking with enhanced shadow logic to detect power-side-channel leakage, attribute it to specific hardware signals and software instructions, and achieve an 8x simulation speedup over prior approaches on RISC-V CPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pre-silicon PSCL evaluation frameworks suffer from either poor simulation scalability or an inability to attribute leakage to the correct hardware signals and software instructions, hindering comprehensive root-cause analysis. A scalable, precise end-to-end solution is needed to mitigate PSCL early in the hardware design flow.

Method: SPARC leverages macro-cell-level Information Flow Tracking (IFT) augmented with enhanced shadow logic that tags switching activity from secret-dependent data. It isolates this tagged activity, applies statistical leakage tests (e.g., TVLA) to detect PSCL, attributes leakage to specific hardware signals, and maps those signals back to corresponding software instructions for full hardware-software root-cause analysis.

Result: SPARC was validated on multiple open-source RISC-V CPUs (32-bit and 64-bit, in-order and out-of-order) across cryptographic workloads including masked/unmasked AES and ML-KEM (Kyber-512). It recovered known leakage sources as a sanity check, identified specific microarchitectural leakage sources, and achieved an 8x per-trace simulation speedup over previously shown approaches on comparable designs.

Conclusion: SPARC provides a practical, precise, and scalable pre-silicon framework for PSCL evaluation and root-cause analysis, enabling mitigation of side-channel leakage early in the design flow and ultimately strengthening the security of future processors.

Abstract: Power-Side-Channel Leakage (PSCL) originates from architectural and micro-architectural artifacts in a processor and poses a severe threat to the confidentiality of cryptographic software. Consequently, pre-silicon PSCL evaluation is indispensable for secure hardware design. Existing frameworks are either limited by poor simulation scalability or fail to attribute leakage to the correct hardware signals and software instructions, thereby impeding a comprehensive root-cause analysis. This paper presents SPARC, an automated framework for pre-silicon PSCL evaluation and root-cause analysis. SPARC leverages macro-cell-level Information Flow Tracking (IFT) augmented with enhanced shadow logic that tags switching activity originating from secret-dependent data. By isolating this activity, SPARC applies statistical leakage tests to detect PSCL, while simultaneously attributing the leakage to specific hardware signals and mapping those signals to the corresponding software instructions. This approach thus delivers a full end-to-end leakage evaluation and root-cause analysis for both hardware and software. To demonstrate and validate SPARC, PSCL of multiple open-source RISC-V CPUs, encompassing 32-bit and 64-bit cores with both in-order and out-of-order pipelines, is evaluated across a range of cryptographic workloads, including masked and unmasked AES and ML-KEM (CRYSTALS-Kyber-512). SPARC recovers known leakage sources as a sanity check and identifies specific microarchitectural leakage sources, achieving an 8x per-trace simulation speedup over previously shown approaches on comparable designs. By enabling precise and scalable root-cause analysis at the pre-silicon stage, this work provides a practical framework to mitigate PSCL early in the design flow, thereby strengthening the security of future processors.

</details>


### [53] [From Signals to Behaviors: Evidence-Based Android Malware Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23272)
*Shiwen Song,Yiheng Xiong,Sen Chen,Xiaofei Xie*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Praxis reframes Android malware detection as a behavior-oriented, three-stage pipeline (hypothesize, confirm, judge) that recovers and evaluates an app's actual malicious behaviors in context, rather than relying on surface proxies. It achieves 87.4% F1 for detection, drastically reduces false positives on high-permission benign apps, and recovers fine-grained malicious behaviors at 87.3% F1, substantially outperforming prior baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Android malware detectors rely on proxies for behavior (learned features, local code slices) and flag deviations as malicious. This is fundamentally flawed: unusual benign apps are over-flagged, and evolving malware that appears ordinary evades detection. The authors argue detection should instead be behavior-oriented — recovering what an app actually does and judging maliciousness in context (user awareness, functional context, attack composition).

Method: Praxis implements a hypothesize-confirm-judge pipeline: (1) hypothesize candidate malicious behaviors from coarse static signals; (2) confirm each hypothesis by grounding it in code evidence verified through program analysis; (3) judge confirmed behaviors by considering the user's awareness, the app's functional context, and how behaviors compose into an attack. The final output includes both a verdict and the supported malicious behaviors.

Result: Praxis achieves 87.4% F1 for overall detection, outperforming seven baselines by 18.6–34.8 percentage points. On high-permission benign apps, false-positive rate drops to 13.0% (a reduction of 41.1–67.0 pp). For fine-grained behavior recovery, it reaches 87.3% F1, surpassing prior behavior-level approaches by 56.5–73.4 pp. Ablation studies confirm each pipeline stage contributes meaningfully to performance.

Conclusion: Behavior-oriented detection via the hypothesize-confirm-judge pipeline is a more principled and effective approach to Android malware detection. By recovering and contextually judging actual behaviors rather than relying on surface proxies, Praxis substantially improves detection accuracy, reduces false positives, and provides fine-grained, interpretable malicious behavior identification.

Abstract: Android malware remains a persistent threat, and detecting it accurately is a long-standing open problem. Whether an app is malicious depends on what it actually does and the context in which it does it, not on the surface signals it happens to exhibit. Existing detectors instead reason about proxies for behavior, such as learned features or local code slices, and flag whatever deviates from these proxies as malicious. But deviation is not maliciousness: benign apps that merely look unusual are over-flagged, evolving malware that looks ordinary slips through. We argue that detection should be behavior-oriented: recover an app's potentially malicious behaviors and judge which are truly malicious. To realize this, we present Praxis, which structures detection as a hypothesize-confirm-judge pipeline: it hypothesizes candidate behaviors from coarse static signals, confirms each by grounding it in code evidence verified with program analysis, and judges the confirmed behaviors in context: the user's awareness, the app's functional context, and how they compose into an attack. For a malicious app, Praxis returns a verdict and the supported behaviors. We evaluate Praxis against seven baselines across three challenging settings. It achieves the best overall detection performance (87.4% F1), outperforming the baselines by 18.6-34.8 percentage points. On high-permission benign apps, it reduces the false-positive rate to 13.0%, a reduction of 41.1-67.0 percentage points compared with the baselines. Beyond binary detection, Praxis recovers fine-grained malicious behaviors at 87.3% F1, outperforming prior behavior-level approaches by 56.5-73.4 percentage points. Ablation studies show that each stage of the pipeline contributes to the final performance.

</details>


### [54] [Hiding in Plain Sight: An Effective Physical Adversarial Patch Attack against Visual-Infrared Fused Face Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23292)
*Qiucheng Yu,Tao Ni,Yihe Zhou,Jiayimei Wang,Qingchuan Zhao*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: VIPatch introduces a physical adversarial patch attack against visual-infrared fused face detection models, using gradient-color masks and band-aid stickers jointly optimized across both modalities to achieve >90% attack success rates while maintaining a natural, inconspicuous appearance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing adversarial patch attacks against face detection target either visual or infrared images alone in the digital domain, making them ineffective against fused models in physical settings. Moreover, prior patches are easily noticeable due to unnatural patterns. There is a need for a physically realizable, cross-modal attack that evades both visual and infrared detection while appearing realistic.

Method: VIPatch jointly crafts two components—a gradient-color mask and a band-aid sticker—across both visual and infrared facial images. These elements are co-optimized to attack visual-infrared fused face detection models. The optimized digital patches are then used to guide the fabrication of physical adversarial patches for real-world deployment.

Result: VIPatch achieves attack success rates exceeding 90% in both digital and physical domains against visual-infrared fused face detection models, while the patches remain unobtrusive and natural-looking to human observers.

Conclusion: VIPatch demonstrates that physically realizable, inconspicuous adversarial patches can effectively attack multi-modal (visual-infrared) face detection systems, highlighting a significant vulnerability in fused sensing models deployed in real-world applications.

Abstract: Deep learning-based visual-infrared fused face detection models are increasingly deployed across a wide range of applications, yet they remain susceptible to adversarial patch attacks. Most prior attacks target either the visual or the infrared image alone in the digital domain, which renders them ineffective against fused models in the physical world. Moreover, many of these methods are readily noticeable, as their patch patterns deviate substantially from those seen in the real world. In this paper, we introduce VIPatch (Visual-Infrared Patch), a novel physical adversarial patch attack that produces inconspicuous, realistic, and natural-looking patches for facial images. Specifically, VIPatch crafts a gradient-color mask together with a band-aid sticker across both the visual and infrared images, and jointly optimizes these two elements; the resulting digital patches further guide the fabrication of their physical counterparts. Experimental results show that VIPatch achieves competitive attack success rates (over 90%) in both the digital and physical domains, while keeping the patches unobtrusive to human observers.

</details>


### [55] [The Illusion of Secure LLM Code: Closing the Security Gap via Iterative Reprompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23710)
*Ishpuneet Singh,Shreyas Mahajan,Gurjot Singh,Maninder Singh*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Evaluates five AI coding assistants on generating secure authentication code. Finds that single-shot prompts (even with NIST context) produce structurally insecure code lacking brute-force resistance, session management, and password handling. Only iterative reprompting (contextual self-auditing loops) achieves defense-in-depth. Concludes LLMs are not secure-by-default; enterprises need continuous standards-driven verification pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly embedded in software development workflows, but their capacity to autonomously produce secure authentication code remains unverified. The study is motivated by the risk that developers relying on AI coding assistants may inadvertently deploy systems with critical security vulnerabilities, particularly in authentication—a high-stakes domain where failures directly compromise user data and system integrity.

Method: A bi-modal assessment framework combining (1) static code analysis and (2) dynamic penetration testing, with findings mapped to NIST SP 800-63B guidelines. Five prominent AI coding assistants are evaluated across four prompting strategies: Basic (functional only), Secure (generic security requests), NIST-Based (explicit standards context), and Reprompting (iterative self-auditing loops that force models to revise their own output against security criteria).

Result: Basic and Secure prompts consistently omitted critical protections for brute-force resistance, session management, and robust password handling. Explicit single-shot NIST context improved compliance but remained structurally inadequate for defense-in-depth. Iterative reprompting—forcing models into a contextual self-auditing loop—was strictly necessary to achieve comprehensive security architecture. No model produced secure-by-default code under non-iterative conditions.

Conclusion: Current AI coding assistants do not produce secure-by-default applications. Enterprise deployments must transition from single-shot prompt engineering to continuous, standards-driven verification pipelines. Iterative, self-auditing prompt strategies are not merely beneficial but structurally required for achieving defense-in-depth in LLM-generated authentication systems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into software development workflows, yet their ability to autonomously generate secure authentication code remains uncertain. This paper evaluates the security architecture of authentication systems generated by five prominent AI coding assistants through a bi-modal assessment framework combining static code analysis and dynamic penetration testing, mapped to NIST SP 800-63B guidelines. The study examines model behavior across four prompting strategies Basic, Secure, NIST-Based, and Reprompting to reflect varying levels of developer guidance. Empirical results demonstrate that code generated from functional or generically secure prompts consistently omits critical protections, particularly concerning brute-force resistance, session management, and robust password handling. While providing explicit, single-shot NIST context significantly improves compliance, the findings reveal that this remains structurally inadequate. Instead, iterative Reprompting: forcing models into a contextual self-auditing loop is strictly required to achieve a comprehensive, defense-in-depth security architecture. Ultimately, this study proves that current AI coding assistants do not produce secure-by-default applications, dictating that enterprise deployments must transition from single-shot prompt engineering to continuous, standards-driven verification pipelines.

</details>


### [56] [A Structured Cyber Threat Intelligence Dataset Using STIX 2.1 Entities and MITRE ATT&CK Mappings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23312)
*Dipshikha Das,Arnab Banik,Md. Shariful Islam,Md Rayhanur Rahman*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A manually curated dataset of 150 CTI reports represented as STIX 2.1 graphs was constructed, featuring 4,777 entities and 5,817 relationships mapped to 269 MITRE ATT&CK techniques. Evaluated on a gold-standard subset, Qwen3.6:27B achieved a 0.803 kappa and >92% micro-F1, demonstrating LLMs can assist annotation consistency checking, though expert validation remains essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CTI extraction tools and datasets lack report-level graph representations that preserve complex entity relationships and normalized adversarial behaviors. There is a need for a benchmark dataset with STIX 2.1–based graphs, MITRE ATT&CK alignments, and expert-validated annotations to support both CTI analytics and evaluation of automated extraction methods.

Method: The authors manually constructed a dataset of 150 English-language CTI reports, modeling each as STIX 2.1 graphs containing entities, relationships, and attack-pattern objects mapped to MITRE ATT&CK Enterprise techniques/sub-techniques. Two cybersecurity researchers independently annotated a random sample of 25 reports; inter-rater agreement was measured, disagreements were adjudicated to produce a gold-standard reference. Four locally deployed open-source LLMs were then evaluated as automated judges on this adjudicated subset using kappa, micro-F1, and false-positive rate metrics.

Result: The full dataset comprises 4,777 STIX entities, 5,817 relationships, and 1,273 attack-pattern entities covering 269 unique MITRE ATT&CK techniques/sub-techniques. Inter-rater agreement on the 25-report sample was substantial. Among LLMs evaluated, Qwen3.6:27B performed best: kappa of 0.803, micro-F1 >92%, false-positive rate <5%.

Conclusion: The STIX 2.1 graph dataset provides a valuable benchmark for CTI information extraction, knowledge-graph construction, incident analysis, and threat attribution. Locally deployed LLMs can aid human reviewers in flagging annotation inconsistencies, but expert human validation remains indispensable for high-quality CTI annotation.

Abstract: Cyber threat intelligence (CTI) reports are typically written in unstructured formats, which complicates the extraction and analysis of important entities and adversarial behaviors. Although existing CTI research provides extraction tools, knowledge-graph frameworks, and MITRE ATT&CK mapped datasets, curated report-level datasets that preserve complex entity relationships and normalized adversarial behaviors remain limited. To address this limitation, this study presents a manually constructed dataset of 150 English-language CTI reports, each represented as STIX 2.1 based graphs, which includes 4,777 STIX entities, 5,817 STIX relationships in total, and 1,273 STIX attack-pattern entities (adversarial behaviors) mapped to 269 unique MITRE ATT&CK Enterprise techniques and sub-techniques. Twenty five randomly sampled reports were independently assessed by two cybersecurity researchers, which shows substantial inter-rater agreement. Disagreements were subsequently adjudicated to establish a gold-standard reference dataset. Four locally deployed open-source LLMs were evaluated as automated judges against this adjudicated reference sample. Qwen3.6:27B achieved the strongest overall performance, with a maximum kappa score of 0.803, micro-F1 scores exceeding 92%, and false-positive rates below 5%. The dataset provides a benchmark for CTI information extraction, knowledge-graph construction, incident analysis, and threat attribution. The findings further indicate that locally deployed LLMs can support human reviewers in identifying annotation inconsistencies, but expert validation remains essential.

</details>


### [57] [Exploring the OODA Loop as a Systematic Way of Thinking in Coping with Conflicts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23342)
*Ethan Anderson,Shouhuai Xu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper revisits Boyd's OODA (Observe, Orient, Decide, Act) loop as a systematic framework for reasoning about conflicts in uncertain, adaptive, adversarial environments. It extends the OODA loop beyond its original air warfare context into two domains—cyber conflicts and cognitive conflicts—arguing that conflict is fundamentally a contest over the ability to generate, protect, and leverage superior observation, orientation, and decision-making while degrading the adversary's.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conflicts in modern dynamic environments require systematic thinking frameworks. The authors identify a gap in applying Boyd's OODA loop beyond its military aviation origins and seek to validate its broader utility for reasoning about conflicts characterized by uncertainty, adaptation, and adversarial interference, particularly in emerging domains like cyber and cognitive conflict.

Method: The paper conducts a conceptual exploration and domain-specific application of the OODA loop framework. It maps the OODA loop's core mechanisms—particularly the relationship between information, understanding, choice, and action—onto two distinct application domains: cyber conflicts and cognitive conflicts. Situational awareness is emphasized as a critical mechanism within the orientation phase, and observations are analyzed in terms of perception, comprehension, projection, and integration with mental models, objectives, doctrine, trust, and experience.

Result: Across both domains, the OODA loop proves useful as a systematic reasoning tool. The analysis reveals that situational awareness is critical to the orientation function, and that observations gain utility only through layered cognitive processing. Most significantly, the paper finds that conflict is not merely a contest of actions or effects, but a contest over the ability to generate, protect, and leverage one's own superior observation, orientation, and decision-making while simultaneously degrading and exploiting the adversary's corresponding capabilities.

Conclusion: The OODA loop offers a robust, transferable framework for reasoning about conflicts across diverse domains. The fundamental nature of conflict is recharacterized as a competition over cognitive and informational superiority—the capacity to observe, orient, and decide better than an adversary while undermining their ability to do the same—rather than a contest of kinetic effects alone.

Abstract: When conflicts emerge, we need systematic ways of thinking to deal with them. This paper revisits Boyd's Observe, Orient, Decide, Act (OODA) loop and explores its usefulness as a systematic way of thinking for reasoning about conflicts in dynamic environments characterized by uncertainty, adaptation, and adversarial interference. We explore the OODA loop beyond its origin in air warfare where it focuses attention on the relationship between information, understanding, choice, and action. Our exploration is conducted in two application domains: cyber conflicts and cognitive conflicts. Across both domains, we emphasize situational awareness as a critical mechanism of orientation, while noting that observations become useful only when they are perceived, comprehended, projected into possible futures, and integrated with mental models, objectives, doctrine, trust, and experience. Our exploration suggests that conflict is not merely a contest of actions or effects, but a contest over the ability to generate, protect, and leverage one's own superior observation, orientation, and decision, while degrading and exploiting adversary's observation, orientation, and decision.

</details>


### [58] [Rendering on Real Silicon: GPU Render-Timing as a Passive, AI-Resistant CAPTCHA Signal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23389)
*David Noever,Forrest McKee*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper explores using GPU render-timing dynamics under WebGL workloads as a bot-detection signal, avoiding persistent device fingerprinting. In a pilot study, automated (headless) clients showed ~5x slower mean render times for software rendering and distinct timing signatures (75–106% differences) even on real hardware versus human-driven browsers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional CAPTCHAs are increasingly solvable by AI, while behavioral and cryptographic-attestation defenses impose privacy or enrollment burdens. The authors seek an orthogonal, privacy-preserving signal—GPU timing behavior—that classifies automated vs. human clients without leaking a persistent identifier.

Method: A 12-hour passive deployment characterized real-world adversaries (207 unsolicited requests). Labeled GPU-timing samples were then collected via: (1) a public endpoint exercised by real browsers with 13 distinct GPUs (positive class), and (2) keyed headless automation across a render-backend matrix (negative class). Metrics included mean render time, frame jitter, timer-quantization ratio, and coefficient of variation, with confound-controlled comparisons (same GPU family, same browser engine, headless vs. interactive).

Result: 86% of unsolicited requests were automated; 85% of browser-claiming clients failed HTTP header-consistency checks. Software-rendered automation showed ~5x mean render-time separation from real GPUs. Headless automation on real hardware differed from human samples by 75–106% on frame jitter, timer-quantization ratio, and coefficient of variation, even under confound-controlled conditions.

Conclusion: GPU render-timing dynamics represent a promising, privacy-preserving bot-detection signal. However, these are pilot-scale findings limited to a single GPU architecture; cross-architecture data collection is needed to establish generalization.

Abstract: Conventional CAPTCHAs pose puzzles that modern AI systems increasingly solve, while behavioral and cryptographic-attestation defenses carry privacy or enrollment costs. We investigate an orthogonal signal: the physical timing behavior of a client's GPU under a controlled WebGL rendering workload. Unlike WebGL fingerprinting, which hashes pixel output into a static device identifier, we measure render-timing dynamics to classify rather than identify, leaking no persistent identifier. We characterize the in-the-wild adversary with a 12-hour passive deployment (207 unsolicited requests; 86% automated; 85% of browser-claiming clients failed HTTP header-consistency checks). We then collect labeled GPU-timing samples through a single public endpoint exercised by real browsers (positive class, 13 distinct GPUs) and by keyed headless automation across a render-backend matrix (negative class). Software-rendered automation -- empirically the dominant real-world adversary -- separates from genuine GPUs by roughly 5x in mean render time. On a confound-controlled comparison (identical GPU family and browser engine, differing only in headless vs. interactive execution), headless automation on real hardware still exhibits a distinct timing signature, separating from human samples by 75-106% on frame jitter, timer-quantization ratio, and coefficient of variation. We report these as pilot-scale findings on a single GPU architecture and outline the cross-architecture collection required to establish generalization.

</details>


### [59] [Early Detection of Hardware Trojans Using Neural Controlled Differential Equations and Analysis of Power Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23417)
*Hasala Senevirathne,Rahul Vishwakarma,Amin Rezaei*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A novel hardware Trojan detection method using Neural Controlled Differential Equations (NCDEs) trained on Trojan-free power traces, combined with LDA classification, to detect both dormant and active Trojans before activation — outperforming traditional ML approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evolving Hardware Trojans exhibit stealthy, adaptive behavior that evades traditional detection methods. Even state-of-the-art ML-based approaches only detect Trojans after activation, leaving a vulnerable window for security breaches. There is a critical need for pre-activation (dormant) Trojan detection.

Method: The approach uses an NCDE model trained exclusively on Trojan-free power trace data to learn nominal power consumption behavior. A sliding window processes side-channel measurements to capture subtle deviations. A Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA) classifier, calibrated on labeled data, distinguishes among three classes: no Trojan, dormant Trojan, and active Trojan.

Result: The NCDE-based method achieves superior accuracy compared to traditional machine learning approaches. It successfully handles dormant Trojans above a sensitivity threshold. Validation on standard hardware Trojan benchmarks demonstrates robust detection and prediction performance.

Conclusion: NCDEs combined with power side-channel analysis provide an effective solution for hardware Trojan detection, crucially enabling the identification of dormant Trojans before activation — closing a critical security gap left by prior methods.

Abstract: Evolving Hardware Trojans pose a serious threat to modern digital systems by evading traditional detection through stealthy, adaptive behavior. Even recent methods that leverage advances in machine learning can only detect them after activation, leaving a critical window for potential security breaches. To address this gap, we propose a novel approach for hardware Trojan detection and prediction using Neural Controlled Differential Equations (NCDEs) and analysis of power traces. Our method leverages an NCDE model trained exclusively on Trojan-free data to learn nominal power behavior, combined with a Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA) classifier calibrated on labeled data, to distinguish between three scenarios: no Trojan, dormant Trojan, and active Trojan. Our method uses a sliding window to process side-channel measurements, enabling detection of subtle power consumption deviations that indicate Trojan presence, even when dormant. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed NCDE-based method achieves superior accuracy compared to traditional machine learning approaches, with the additional advantage of handling dormant Trojans above a sensitivity threshold. We validate our approach on standard hardware Trojan benchmarks, showing robust detection and prediction performance.

</details>


### [60] [TroPUF: Evaluating Hardware Trojan Insertion in Delay-Based Physical Unclonable Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23418)
*Marissa Marcarelli,Amin Rezaei*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents a simulation framework to evaluate stealthy hardware Trojan insertion in delay-based PUFs, showing that dormant Trojans evade conventional detection until activated, exposing a critical gap in PUF security assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Delay-based PUFs rely on manufacturing-induced delay variations for authentication and key generation, but these same non-deterministic variations can mask malicious hardware Trojan behavior. The security risk of embedding Trojans directly inside PUF primitives is not well understood, motivating a systematic evaluation of this coupled threat.

Method: A unified simulation framework is developed to assess stealthy hardware Trojan insertion across multiple delay-based PUF architectures and Trojan types. The framework evaluates functional metrics, hardware overhead, and resistance to machine learning modeling attacks in parallel, comparing PUF behavior before and after Trojan activation.

Result: Dormant Trojans preserve expected PUF behavior, structural characteristics, and machine learning modeling resistance, making them indistinguishable from benign PUFs using conventional validation techniques. Detectable degradation in these metrics appears only after Trojan payload execution.

Conclusion: Conventional PUF validation techniques are insufficient for detecting embedded hardware Trojans prior to activation. This exposes a fundamental gap in current PUF security assumptions and demonstrates the necessity of evaluating PUFs and hardware Trojans as a coupled, rather than independent, security problem.

Abstract: Delay-based Physical Unclonable Functions (PUFs) are commonly used for device authentication and key generation due to the fact that they rely on manufacturing induced delay variations. However, these same variations make PUFs inherently non-deterministic, which can allow malicious logic to blend in with normal circuit behavior. As a result, the act of embedding hardware Trojans directly inside the PUF primitive presents a unique security risk that is not yet well understood. This work presents a unified simulation framework for evaluating stealthy hardware Trojan insertion across multiple delay-based PUF architectures and Trojan types. Functional metrics, hardware overhead, and resistance to machine learning modeling are assessed in parallel. Results show that dormant Trojans preserve expected PUF behavior, structural characteristics, and modeling resistance. Detectable degradation appears only after activation, indicating that conventional validation techniques fail to identify embedded Trojans prior to payload execution. These findings expose a gap in current PUF security assumptions, and highlight the need to evaluate PUFs and hardware Trojans as a coupled security problem.

</details>


### [61] [PATCH-FFT: Unmasking Dormant Hardware Trojans with Patch-Based Frequency-Domain Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23421)
*Hasala Senevirathne,Amin Rezaei*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A patch-based Transformer using frequency-domain (rFFT) analysis of power traces achieves 90.94% detection accuracy for hardware Trojans, notably outperforming prior methods in detecting dormant Trojans that time-domain approaches miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dormant hardware Trojans that leak sensitive information via power side channels are undetectable by time-domain methods before trigger activation, yet detection must occur in the dormant state to prevent data exfiltration. Existing approaches fail to address this critical dormant-state detection gap.

Method: The authors propose a patch-based Transformer architecture that first converts time-domain power measurements into frequency-domain representations using real Fast Fourier Transform (rFFT), then applies a Transformer model to these spectral patches to identify Trojan signatures across both dormant and active states.

Result: The method achieves 90.94% average detection accuracy across dormant and active Trojan scenarios, surpassing state-of-the-art approaches, with particular gains in dormant Trojan detection where prior time-domain methods are ineffective.

Conclusion: Frequency-domain analysis via a patch-based Transformer is a promising direction for hardware Trojan detection, especially for the challenging dormant-state scenario that remains unaddressed by conventional time-domain techniques.

Abstract: Hardware Trojans embedded by malicious entities in integrated circuits can covertly leak sensitive information through power side channels, often remaining undetected in their dormant state until specific trigger conditions activate their malicious behavior. For information-leaking Trojans, detection in the dormant state is critical, as once triggered, the secret data is already exfiltrated. This paper introduces a patch-based Transformer architecture for detecting dormant hardware Trojans through frequency-domain analysis of power traces. Our approach converts time-domain power measurements into frequency-domain representations using real Fast Fourier Transform (rFFT), revealing spectral signatures hidden in conventional time-series analysis. Experimental results demonstrate that our method achieves 90.94% average detection accuracy across dormant and active Trojan scenarios, outperforming state-of-the-art approaches particularly in detecting dormant Trojans that prior time-domain methods do not address.

</details>


### [62] [Renting the Cracking Machine with a Cost-and-Time Analysis of Exhaustive DES-56 Key Search in the Cloud](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23443)
*Gonzalo Sharif Curi Martínez,Rodrigo Ramele*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A distributed brute-force system on AWS EC2 (37 c6i.2xlarge instances) achieves 108M keys/s aggregate throughput against DES. Partial key searches cost as little as $0.41; full 56-bit exhaustion would cost ~$1.2M and could complete in ~1 day with sufficient cloud capacity, demonstrating DES is practically broken even on commodity cloud infrastructure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite DES being considered cryptographically broken since 1998, no recent literature provides concrete, reproducible measurements of the actual cost and time required for exhaustive key search using modern commodity cloud infrastructure. The authors aim to fill this gap with empirical data.

Method: A distributed brute-force system was deployed on AWS EC2 using 37 c6i.2xlarge instances, each running a C/OpenMP worker. The 2^56 keyspace was partitioned across instances. Fifteen independent trials were conducted at keyspace offsets ranging from 10^6 to 1.5×10^10 keys, measuring wall-clock time and monetary cost per trial.

Result: Per-instance throughput: 2.91M keys/s; aggregate: ~108M keys/s. For small offsets (≤10^8), total time dominated by ~90s instance boot latency, yielding mean 116.8±20.1s at $0.41/attack. Larger offsets scale linearly: offset 1.5×10^10 required ~87 minutes and $18. Full 2^56 exhaustion with 37 instances would take ~21 years; extrapolated cost for complete exhaustion is ~$1.2M, completable in ~1 day with elastic scaling.

Conclusion: The system is practical for bounded-subspace attacks at negligible cost. Full DES exhaustion, while expensive at ~$1.2M, is firmly within reach of well-funded attackers using only commodity cloud resources — confirming DES's insecurity in practical, measurable terms.

Abstract: The Data Encryption Standard (DES), with its 56-bit key, has been considered cryptographically broken since 1998. However, a concrete, reproducible measurement of the cost and time required to perform an exhaustive key search using today's commodity cloud infrastructure has not been widely reported in recent literature. In this paper we present a distributed brute-force system built on AWS EC2 that partitions the $2^{56}$ keyspace across 37 \texttt{c6i.2xlarge} instances running a C/OpenMP worker, achieving a measured throughput of $2.91$\,M\,keys/s per instance (${\sim}108 \times 10^6$ keys/s aggregate). We conduct 15 independent trials covering keyspace offsets from $10^6$ to $1.5 \times 10^{10}$ keys, measuring wall-clock time and monetary cost per trial. For small offsets ($\leq 10^8$), total time is dominated by AWS instance boot latency (${\approx}\;90$\,s), yielding a mean of $116.8\pm20.1$\,s at \$0.41 per attack. For larger offsets the search time dominates and grows linearly: a key at offset $1.5{\times}10^{10}$ requires ~${\approx} \; 87$ minutes and \$18. At the measured aggregate throughput of 108\,M\,keys/s, exhausting the full $2^{56}$ keyspace with these 37 instances would take ${\approx} \; 21$ years; however, because the workload is embarrassingly parallel and cloud capacity is elastic, the same search can be traded for money almost linearly. Extrapolating our measured cost, a complete exhaustive search would cost ${\approx} \; \$1.2$M and, with a sufficiently large fleet, could be completed in about one day. The system is thus practical for bounded-subspace attacks at negligible cost, and full DES exhaustion, while expensive, is firmly within reach of a well-funded attacker using only commodity cloud resources.

</details>


### [63] [Isolated but Exposed: Persistence-Based Memory Extraction Attack on LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23444)
*Xinyu Gao,Wenyu Chen,Xiangtao Meng,Li Wang,Chuanchao Zang,Jianing Wang,Zheng Li,Shanqing Guo*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: SPORE is a novel attack that exfiltrates private data from LLM agents' isolated long-term memory by exploiting the tool interface. It achieves 80% extraction with unlimited triggers and 47% with only 20 triggers, demonstrating that memory isolation alone is insufficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing defenses for LLM agent long-term memory rely on user-level isolation (binding LTM to unique identifiers), which has blocked known shared-storage attacks. However, the authors identify the tool interface as an overlooked attack surface: agents embed LTM-retrieved data into tool call parameters, allowing a malicious tool to exfiltrate private memory without violating isolation. Naive extraction techniques fail due to semantic interference and tool-call budget limits.

Method: SPORE (1) decouples adversarial commands from retrieval anchors by persisting commands in short-term memory and emitting semantically pure anchors in tool responses, restoring retrieval precision; (2) uses geometric coverage optimization over the embedding space to systematically steer anchors toward unexplored memory regions; and (3) persists reactivation payloads in memory that automatically resume extraction across sessions without additional user triggers.

Result: SPORE achieves an 80.0% record extraction rate with unlimited triggers and 47.0% with only 20 triggers. In multi-user deployments, attackers can link extracted records to user identities, enabling targeted surveillance.

Conclusion: Memory isolation alone is insufficient to protect user privacy in LLM-based agents. The tool interface constitutes a critical attack surface, necessitating a reexamination of tool-side trust boundaries in agent architectures.

Abstract: LLM-based agents extend large language models with long-term memory (LTM) that persists privacy-sensitive user data across sessions. Production systems mitigate extraction risks through memory isolation, binding each user's LTM to a unique identifier. This defense has blocked known attacks on shared storage, fostering the assumption that isolated LTM is secure. We identify the tool interface as an overlooked attack surface. Agents routinely embed LTM-retrieved data in tool invocation parameters, enabling a malicious tool to exfiltrate private memory without violating user-level isolation. Naive adaptations of user-side extraction techniques fail because the adversarial command's semantics interfere with retrieval precision, and platform-imposed tool-call limits constrain the extraction budget per trigger. We present SPORE, the first extraction attack designed for this threat model. SPORE decouples the adversarial command from retrieval anchors by persisting the command in short-term memory and emitting semantically pure anchors in tool responses. The restored retrieval precision enables a geometric coverage optimization over the embedding space that systematically steers anchors toward unexplored memory regions. To sustain extraction beyond tool-call limits, SPORE persists reactivation payloads in memory that automatically resume the attack within and across sessions without additional user triggers. SPORE achieves an 80.0% record extraction rate with unlimited triggers and 47.0% with only 20 triggers. In multi-user deployments, attackers can link extracted records to user identities, enabling targeted surveillance. These results demonstrate that memory isolation alone is insufficient and call for reexamining tool-side trust boundaries in agent architectures.

</details>


### [64] [ATLAS: Automated Approximation of Transformers for Efficient Homomorphic Inference in One Hour](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23478)
*Jianhang Xie,Sicheng Tan,Vishnu Naresh Boddeti,Zhichao Lu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: ATLAS automates per-layer approximation configuration for transformer models under FHE, using two-stage multi-objective optimization with surrogate models to navigate an enormous search space (up to 10^225 configurations) while balancing latency and accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying transformers under fully homomorphic encryption (FHE/CKKS) requires replacing non-linear operations (softmax, normalization, activation) with polynomial approximations. Current frameworks apply uniform approximation hyperparameters across all layers, which is overly rigid — different layers tolerate different approximation errors, creating an opportunity to reduce latency. However, per-layer configuration leads to an intractable search space (10^84 to 10^225 configurations), making manual optimization impossible.

Method: ATLAS formulates per-layer approximation configuration as a multi-objective optimization over latency and predictive accuracy. It addresses three core challenges: (1) a large decision space (120-320 variables), (2) expensive evaluations (70-1000s per configuration), and (3) sparse optimization signals (35-50% invalid configurations). The solution uses a two-stage optimization strategy that progressively relaxes layer-wise constraints, combined with surrogate models to accelerate evaluation.

Result: Not explicitly quantified in the abstract, but the framework is presented as successfully navigating the massive search space (up to 10^225 for LLaMA3-32 layers) and handling the 35-50% invalid configuration rate to produce valid per-layer configurations that balance latency and accuracy.

Conclusion: Automated per-layer approximation configuration via multi-objective optimization is both feasible and necessary to make transformer inference practical under FHE, overcoming the limitations of rigid uniform-configuration approaches.

Abstract: Fully homomorphic encryption (FHE) provides strong cryptographic guarantees for private inference, but deploying transformer models under FHE remains prohibitively expensive. A key bottleneck is that non-linear operations such as softmax, normalization, and activation must be replaced with polynomial approximations compatible with the CKKS scheme, and the multiplicative depth consumed by these approximations dominates inference cost. Recent frameworks have advanced approximation techniques, yet all rely on manually configured approximation hyperparameters (e.g., number of iterations, polynomial degree), applied uniformly across all layers. While convenient, this uniform-configuration approach is overly rigid: different layers can tolerate different levels of approximation error without degrading predictive accuracy, and uniform configurations cannot exploit this variability to reduce latency. Allowing each layer to adopt its own configuration, however, causes the search space to explode with model depth, reaching roughly $10^{84}$ configurations for BERT/ViT (12 layers) and $10^{225}$ for LLaMA3 (32 layers), rendering manual exploration practically impossible. We present ATLAS, an automated framework that configures per-layer approximation settings by formulating the problem as a multi-objective optimization over latency and predictive accuracy. The resulting problem is inherently difficult: 1) competing objectives over a large decision space (120 or 320 variables for BERT/ViT or LLaMA3); 2) expensive evaluation, as each configuration takes 70-1,000 seconds even in cleartext; and 3) sparse optimization signals, as 35-50% of candidate configurations yield numerically invalid solutions. ATLAS addresses these challenges through a two-stage optimization strategy that progressively relaxes layer-wise constraints, combined with surrogate models to accelerate evaluation.

</details>


### [65] [Mission-Level Runtime Assurance for LLM-Assisted ISR Swarms over a Verification-Aware Fabric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23532)
*Nikolaos Kekatos,Stylianos Basagiannis,Panagiotis Katsaros,Alexios Lekidis,Tom Nianios*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A three-tier compositional runtime-verification framework for LLM-assisted autonomous robot swarms that detects mission-level violations invisible to per-platform monitors by aggregating verdicts over a verification-aware messaging fabric with evidence provenance tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Per-platform guardrails in autonomous robot swarms fail to detect mission-level violations that emerge from the composition of individually-compliant actions across multiple platforms (e.g., splitting prohibited tasks across agents, collectively exceeding budgets). Contested communications further enable violations to hide behind lost or delayed evidence, and best-effort central monitors can silently emit false all-clears.

Method: A three-tier (platform/squad/mission) compositional runtime-verification framework that: (1) decomposes mission policy into per-agent and cross-agent aspects; (2) aggregates per-platform verdicts over a verification-aware messaging fabric; (3) fuses verdicts using an evidence-aware, two-axis (security × completeness) algebra that names the platforms jointly triggering violations. The fabric makes evidence loss and silence observable, downgrading unsupported negative verdicts to "unknown" rather than all-clears.

Result: In a simulated ISR mission, an indirect prompt injection causing real LLM planners to split a prohibited collection task across four platforms went undetected by every per-platform monitor but was compositionally detected with full provenance. Under an injected fault campaign, a best-effort central monitor emitted silent false all-clears while the verification-aware fabric emitted none.

Conclusion: Compositional runtime verification with explicit evidence tracking and observable message loss is necessary to detect emergent swarm-level violations that per-platform monitors and best-effort central monitors miss, particularly in contested communication environments.

Abstract: Swarms of LLM-assisted autonomous robots are increasingly proposed for cooperative intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance (ISR) in contested environments. A growing class of their assurance failures arises not within any single platform but across the swarm: individually-compliant actions compose into a mission-level violation: a prohibited objective split across platforms to evade per-platform lim- its, or a collective budget quietly exceeded. Per-platform guardrails miss these by construction, and contested communications let the violation hide behind lost or delayed evidence. We present a three-tier (platfor- m/squad/mission) compositional runtime-verification framework that de- composes a mission policy into per-agent and cross-agent aspects, aggre- gates per-platform verdicts over a verification-aware messaging fabric, and fuses them with an evidence-aware, two-axis (security x complete- ness) algebra whose provenance names the platforms that jointly trig- gered a violation. Because the fabric makes evidence loss and silence observable, unsupported negative verdicts are downgraded to an explicit unknown rather than reported as mission-wide all-clears. On a simulated ISR mission, an indirect prompt injection that causes real LLM planners to split a prohibited collection task across four platforms is invisible to every per-platform monitor yet detected compositionally with full prove- nance; under an injected fault campaign a best-effort central monitor emits silent false all-clears while the verification-aware fabric emits none

</details>


### [66] [Collusion-Resistant Image-Agnostic Watermarking for Multi-Screen Shooting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23553)
*Mingyue Chen,Xin Liao,Yufeng Wu,Han Fang,Xiaoshuai Wu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: CoMSMark is a collusion-resistant screen-shooting watermarking framework that uses screen-specific residuals via style modulation, a collusion suppression loss, and image-agnostic encoding to resist multi-screen collusion attacks (both removal and forgery), maintaining >90% watermark accuracy under attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing screen-shooting watermarking methods embed the same copyright watermark across different screens, producing highly similar and estimable watermark patterns. These shared patterns are vulnerable to multi-screen collusion attacks, where adversaries exploit multiple watermarked images to remove or forge watermarks. No prior work adequately addresses this collusion threat.

Method: CoMSMark has three core components: (1) a style modulation mechanism that incorporates screen ID to generate screen-specific watermark residuals for source attribution; (2) a collusion suppression loss that minimizes shared residual components across screens and promotes high-entropy predictions on forged samples; (3) an image-agnostic encoding paradigm that generates watermark residuals independently of image content for efficient large-scale distribution.

Result: CoMSMark achieves average watermark accuracy above 90% under collusion-based removal attacks while keeping forged-watermark accuracy near 50% (random-guess level). It also demonstrates competitive robustness under diverse screen-shooting conditions including varying capture distances and angles.

Conclusion: CoMSMark effectively mitigates the multi-screen collusion attack threat through screen-specific residual generation and collusion suppression, offering a practical solution for copyright protection in screen-shooting scenarios with strong resistance to both removal and forgery attacks.

Abstract: Screen-shooting poses a significant threat to confidential information protection. While existing screen-shooting watermarking methods enable copyright verification, the copyrighted images carrying the same copyright watermark across different screens often exhibit highly similar and estimable watermark patterns. These shared patterns can be exploited for watermark removal and forgery, a threat we term the multi-screen collusion attack. To mitigate this threat, we propose CoMSMark, a collusion-resistant image-agnostic watermarking framework for multi-screen shooting, which reduces shared residual components across screens to resist multi-screen collusion attacks. Specifically, we incorporate screen ID through a style modulation mechanism, enabling the encoder to generate screen-specific watermark residuals for reliable source attribution. We further introduce a collusion suppression loss that reduces shared residual components and encourages high-entropy predictions for forged samples, improving resistance to collusion attacks. Finally, to enable efficient large-scale distribution, CoMSMark employs an image-agnostic encoding paradigm that generates watermark residuals independently of image content. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CoMSMark effectively resists both collusion-based watermark removal and forgery. It maintains an average watermark accuracy above 90% under removal attacks while keeping forged-watermark accuracy near 50%. Moreover, CoMSMark achieves competitive robustness under diverse screen-shooting conditions, including varying capture distances and angles.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [67] [StateAct: Program State, before Pixels, for Long-Horizon Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22798)
*Yan Yang,Xiangru Jian,Ziyang Luo,Zirui Zhao,Yutong Dai,Ziji Shi,Hanshu Yan,Jun Hao Liew,Silvio Savarese,Junnan Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: StateAct proposes a code-first, multi-agent architecture where the main agent operates directly on program state via code, delegating only necessary GUI interactions to a dedicated subagent. This state-grounding approach improves performance (26.9% vs. 20.6% binary success on OSWorld 2.0), reduces cost ~9x, and shifts the bottleneck from perception to reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional computer-use agents rely on screenshots (pixels) for perception and action, but screenshots are lossy renderings of underlying program state. Different states can yield identical pixels, and purely visual interaction misses the richer, manipulable state (files, DOM, application backends). The authors argue that grounding action, verification, and memory in program state—rather than pixels—fundamentally shifts the agent's bottleneck from perception to reasoning.

Method: StateAct is a multi-agent harness with two key components: (1) a main agent that operates code-first, directly reading and manipulating program state (files, DOM, application backends) through code execution, and (2) a dedicated GUI subagent invoked only when screenshot-and-click interaction is necessary (used in just 28/108 tasks, 1.1% of main-agent steps). An independent "finish gate" verifies outputs for structural failures (missing, unsaved, or misplaced results). The main agent delegates subgoals to fresh subagents to maintain focused context over long horizons.

Result: On OSWorld 2.0, StateAct improves Claude Opus 4.8 from 20.6% to 26.9% on binary success and from 54.8% to 61.6% on partial success, at approximately 9× lower cost per task than the screenshot-only baseline. A code-only variant (no GUI subagent) achieved only 45.9% partial success, below the screenshot-based baseline's 54.8%, demonstrating that minimal GUI interaction remains necessary for some tasks.

Conclusion: State-grounding—anchoring action, verification, and memory in program state rather than pixels—shifts the primary bottleneck in computer-use agents from perception to reasoning, making failures depend more on what the agent thinks than on what it sees. The code-first approach with selective GUI delegation offers a more efficient and effective paradigm.

Abstract: Computer-use agents are usually improved by strengthening perception: better models for reading a screenshot and choosing where to click. Yet a screenshot is only a lossy rendering of the underlying program state, e.g., the files, application backends, and DOM that hold the task data. Different states can produce the same pixels, while code can inspect and modify that state directly. StateAct is a code-first, multi-agent harness built around this distinction. Its main agent works directly with program state by using code, while a dedicated GUI subagent handles screenshot-and-click interaction on the few subgoals that need it, just 28 of 108 tasks and 1.1% of main-agent steps. The same direct access to program state also supports verification: an independent finish gate double-checks the saved result for structural failures, e.g., output that is missing, unsaved, or written to the wrong path. To stay on track over hundreds of steps, the main agent hands subgoals to fresh subagents, keeping its own context focused. On OSWorld 2.0, StateAct lifts Claude Opus 4.8 from 20.6% to 26.9% on binary success, and from 54.8% to 61.6% on partial success, at ~ 9x lower cost per task than the same model driven by screenshots alone; a code-only variant with no GUI subagent reaches only 45.9% partial, below that screenshot-based baseline's 54.8%. In general, grounding action, verification, and memory in state, what we call state-grounding, shifts the main bottleneck from perception toward reasoning: failures depend more on what the agent thinks than on what it sees.

</details>


### [68] [The Best Programming Language for Tokenmaxxing: An Investigation of Coding Agent Behavior Across Programming Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22807)
*Zixuan Wu,Carolyn Jane Anderson,Arjun Guha*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Coding agents consume vastly different amounts of tokens depending on the programming language used, even when problem difficulty is controlled. The paper analyzes agent trajectories to uncover behavioral patterns—such as repeated non-compiling solutions, unnecessary revisions, and prototyping in Python—that explain these cost discrepancies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While multilingual coding agents are increasingly capable, no prior work has systematically examined how the choice of programming language affects token consumption (cost). Understanding this is critical for both benchmarking fairness and practical deployment of coding agents.

Method: The authors evaluate five recent models on programming problems in Python, Java, Rust, and OCaml while controlling for problem difficulty. They analyze trajectories through two lenses: (1) structural analysis—re-executing every intermediate solution, representing trajectories as sequences of test-outcome vectors, and labeling the work between successive solutions; (2) content analysis—examining trajectory text for planning comments, test distrust behaviors, and cross-language prototyping patterns.

Result: Token consumption varies starkly and consistently across languages and models. Structural analysis reveals agents repeatedly produce non-compiling solutions in unfamiliar languages and revise solutions that already pass tests. Content analysis shows agents plan in code comments, distrust provided tests in favor of self-invented inputs, and sidestep unfamiliar languages by prototyping in Python first.

Conclusion: By-language token efficiency should be a standard metric in multilingual agent benchmarking and development. It also serves as a practical guide for cost-conscious users to identify the most expensive languages to work with.

Abstract: Although coding agents are now very effective in a variety of programming languages, this paper first shows that the cost (in tokens) can very significantly by programming language. We evaluate five recent models on programming problems in Python, Java, Rust, and OCaml. We carefully control for problem difficulty, and show that there can be stark variation in token consumption that is consistent across models. To understand why, we analyze both the structure and content of agent trajectories. First, we re-execute every intermediate solution and abstract each trajectory as a sequence of test-outcome vectors, then label the work between successive solutions. This reveals agents repeatedly producing noncompiling solutions in unfamiliar languages and revising solutions that already pass. Second, we analyze trajectory text, finding that agents plan solutions in code comments, distrust the provided tests in favor of inputs they invent, and sidestep unfamiliar target languages by prototyping in Python.
  Our results show that by-language token efficiency is a metric that should be considered when benchmarking and developing multilingual agents, and, for the tokenmaxxer, a guide to the most expensive language to work in.

</details>


### [69] [Do Coverage and Mutation Scores of LLM-Generated Test Suites Correlate with Their Effectiveness? (Replicability Study)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22880)
*Junda Zhao,Shurui Zhou,Eldan Cohen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A large-scale replication study examining whether code coverage and mutation score reliably predict real-bug detection for LLM-generated tests. The authors find that prior negative conclusions about proxy metrics do not fully carry over: in regression settings (bug-free code assumed), coverage and mutation provide meaningful signals across models; in buggy-code settings, they fail. Test suite size is not a dominant confounder for LLM-generated tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work by Inozemtseva et al. and Papadakis et al. demonstrated that correlations between coverage/mutation score and real-bug detection largely vanish once test suite size is controlled — but these findings were established using human-written tests. Given that LLM-based test generation workflows differ substantially (e.g., whole-suite generation, different failure modes), it is unclear whether these conclusions generalize. This motivates a large-scale replication to re-examine these relationships specifically for LLM-generated tests.

Method: The authors conduct a large-scale replication study using test suites generated by a diverse set of LLMs. They systematically re-examine the relationships among coverage, mutation score, and real-bug detection effectiveness under two distinct settings: (1) a regression-style setting where the code provided to the LLM can be assumed bug-free, and (2) a setting where the code-under-test is already buggy and the goal is to expose that bug. They also specifically test whether test suite size acts as a dominant confounder, as prior work claimed for human-written tests.

Result: Findings diverge substantially from prior results. In regression-style settings with presumably bug-free code, coverage and mutation metrics provide meaningful signals when comparing across LLMs. In scenarios where the code-under-test may already contain bugs, these metrics are not reliable indicators of real-bug detection. Little evidence is found that test suite size is a dominant confounder for LLM-generated tests, unlike the pattern observed with human-written tests.

Conclusion: The usefulness of coverage and mutation score for evaluating LLM-generated tests is highly context-dependent. Researchers and practitioners should distinguish between regression-style evaluation (where proxy metrics can be useful) and bug-exposure scenarios (where they are unreliable). The paper provides actionable guidance for interpreting prior results and for evaluating LLM-based test generation going forward.
