Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have driven growing interest in using LLMs to automate test generation. Prior work commonly evaluates generated test suites using proxy metrics such as code coverage and mutation score. However, studies by Inozemtseva et al. and Papadakis et al. show that, for human-written tests, correlations among coverage, mutation, and real-bug detection can largely vanish once test suite size is controlled, raising concerns about the validity of evaluations based on proxy metrics. It also remains unclear whether these conclusions carry over to LLM-generated tests, given that prevailing LLM-based test-generation workflows differ substantially from traditional approaches.
  In this paper, we conduct a large-scale replication study of these two prior works using a wide range of test suites generated by a diverse set of LLMs, and re-examine the relationships among coverage, mutation, and real-bug detection effectiveness. Our findings diverge substantially from prior results. We show that the usefulness of coverage and mutation is highly context-dependent: in regression-style settings where the code provided to the LLM can be reasonably assumed bug-free, these metrics can provide meaningful signals when comparing across models; in another common scenario where the code-under-test may already be buggy and the goal is to expose the bug within the code-under-test, they no longer serve as reliable indicators. We also find little evidence that test suite size is a dominant confounder for correlations among coverage, mutation, and real-bug detection for LLM-generated tests. Based on these findings, we discuss how to interpret results from prior studies and provide actionable guidance for evaluating LLM-based test generation.

</details>


### [70] [Evaluating and Mitigating the Misguidance Effect of Buggy Code in LLM-Generated Unit Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22883)
*Junda Zhao,Shurui Zhou,Eldan Cohen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Buggy code prompts cause LLMs to generate tests that validate incorrect behavior instead of catching bugs ("misguidance effect"). The authors introduce a metric to quantify this, reveal its dual impact, and propose a specification-based prompting paradigm that replaces buggy code with LLM-generated docstrings to mitigate the problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs show promise for automated unit test generation, but recent work suggests buggy code in prompts degrades test quality. No prior work has quantitatively measured this "misguidance effect" or its internal mechanisms, nor proposed effective countermeasures.

Method: The authors (1) define a new metric to quantify the misguidance effect; (2) perform empirical analysis measuring how buggy code prompts increase misguided tests and suppress bug-finding tests; (3) analyze model-internal preference shifts; and (4) propose and validate a specification-based paradigm that substitutes the code under test with an LLM-generated specification docstring in the prompt.

Result: Buggy code prompts produce a severe twofold impact: significantly more misguided tests that assert wrong behavior and fewer effective bug-finding tests. Model-internal analysis confirms buggy code skews LLM preference toward erroneous behavior-asserting tests. The specification-based prompting paradigm effectively reduces misguided tests, increases effective tests, improves multi-round feedback-driven pipelines, and works for both buggy and bug-free code.

Conclusion: Specification-based prompting is a promising strategy for mitigating the misguidance effect, enabling more reliable LLM-driven unit test generation even in the presence of buggy code under test.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) show great promise for automating unit test generation, recent studies suggest that the quality of generated tests can be negatively impacted when models are prompted with buggy code. This paper presents a new metric to quantitatively measure the "misguidance effect," a phenomenon where buggy code steers LLMs toward generating tests that validate its erroneous behavior rather than expose it. Our analysis reveals that prompting LLMs with buggy code has a severe, twofold impact: it significantly increases "misguided tests" that assert incorrect behavior while simultaneously suppressing the generation of effective, bug-finding tests. We further corroborate this effect from a model-internal perspective, showing that buggy code skews LLMs' preference toward tests that assert the same erroneous behavior. To counter this, we introduce and validate a specification-based unit test generation paradigm that replaces the code under test in the prompt with an LLM-generated specification docstring. Our results show that this paradigm effectively reduces misguided tests while substantially increasing effective tests, improves multi-round, feedback-driven test generation pipelines, and remains applicable to both buggy and bug-free code. Overall, these results suggest that specification-based prompting is a promising strategy for mitigating misguidance from buggy code in LLM-generated unit tests.

</details>


### [71] [AssumptionMiner: Extracting, Tracing, and Revising Implicit Assumptions in LLM Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22898)
*Jie "JW" Wu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: AssumptionMiner extracts implicit assumptions from LLM-generated code as explicit, inspectable artifacts. It uses an AST-based dependency graph to enable targeted code regeneration when assumptions are revised. Evaluated on a new benchmark of 180 ambiguous tasks with 676 annotated assumptions, a confidence-weighted ensemble achieves F1=0.816 for extraction, and AST-guided localization outperforms baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world programming prompts are ambiguous and incomplete — they rarely specify input formats, error-handling strategies, or design decisions. LLMs silently fill these gaps with implicit assumptions that are hidden from developers, risking code that passes tests but violates developer intent. Making these assumptions explicit is necessary for transparency, trust, and controllability in LLM-based code generation.

Method: AssumptionMiner is a framework that (1) extracts implicit assumptions from LLM-generated code into a structured "assumption layer" that developers can inspect, confirm, or revise; (2) builds an AST-based dependency graph linking assumptions to affected code regions; and (3) enables targeted regeneration — only code affected by a revised assumption is regenerated. Evaluation uses a newly introduced benchmark of 180 ambiguous programming tasks with 676 annotated assumptions (including a human-verified subset for localization). A confidence-weighted ensemble of open-source LLMs is used for assumption extraction.

Result: The confidence-weighted ensemble achieves an F1 score of 0.816 for assumption extraction, a 3.6× improvement over the strongest offline baseline. AST-guided localization identifies more precise code regions than keyword-based and whole-file baselines on the human-verified benchmark. Targeted regeneration modifies less code than non-targeted alternatives, though challenges with cascading edits remain.

Conclusion: Making implicit assumptions explicit as a first-class artifact significantly improves the transparency and controllability of LLM-based code generation. The AssumptionMiner framework demonstrates that assumption extraction, AST-based localization, and targeted regeneration are viable techniques, while also highlighting open challenges in handling cascading code edits.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) generate code from natural-language prompts, yet real-world prompts rarely provide complete specifications. When prompts leave input formats, error handling, or design decisions unspecified, LLMs fill these gaps with implicit assumptions that shape the generated code's behavior and correctness. Because these assumptions remain hidden, generated code may satisfy tests while violating developer intent. We present AssumptionMiner, a framework that makes implicit assumptions a first-class artifact of LLM-based code generation. In addition to code, AssumptionMiner produces an explicit assumption layer, a structured representation of inferred constraints and design decisions that developers can inspect, confirm, or revise. An AST-based dependency graph enables targeted regeneration of only the code affected by a revised assumption. We also introduce a benchmark of 180 ambiguous programming tasks with 676 annotated assumptions, including a human-verified subset for evaluating code localization. We evaluate assumption extraction, code localization, and assumption-guided regeneration. Across open-source LLMs, a confidence-weighted ensemble achieves an F1 score of 0.816 for assumption extraction, improving on the strongest offline baseline by 3.6x. On the human-verified localization benchmark, AST-guided localization identifies more precise code regions than keyword-based and whole-file baselines. During assumption revision, targeted regeneration modifies less code than non-targeted alternatives while exposing challenges in handling cascading edits. These results demonstrate that making assumptions explicit improves the transparency and controllability of LLM-based code generation.

</details>


### [72] [Metamorphic Testing for Clinical ML Models: A Framework Proposal and Pilot Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22984)
*Jie JW Wu,Feiyu E,Bo Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Clinical ML models achieve high AUROC but can violate medical common sense. This paper proposes metamorphic testing (MT) with 12 clinically-grounded metamorphic relations to evaluate behavioral correctness without ground-truth labels, demonstrating on heart disease data that strong AUROC models still exhibit high MT violation rates (27-87%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AUROC, the dominant metric for clinical prediction models, measures ranking performance but fails to capture behavioral correctness — a model can rank patients correctly yet produce predictions that contradict established clinical knowledge (e.g., predicting lower mortality when SOFA score worsens). Ground-truth labels are also often unavailable or unreliable for individual predictions in clinical settings.

Method: The authors apply metamorphic testing (MT) to clinical ML models: (1) they design 12 candidate metamorphic relations (MRs) for three ICU prediction tasks (in-hospital mortality, sepsis onset, etc.) using MIMIC-III/IV, with each MR grounded in authoritative clinical guidelines; (2) they propose a five-layer validation strategy to ensure MRs are clinically sound before deployment; (3) as a feasibility study, they evaluate three models on the UCI Heart Disease dataset using five pilot MRs, and conduct an injected-fault experiment (sign-negation on blood pressure feature) to test MR sensitivity versus AUROC.

Result: Three clinical models achieved AUROC of 0.849–0.900 on the UCI Heart Disease dataset, yet exhibited MT violation rates of 27%–87% across five pilot MRs. In the injected-fault experiment, a sign-negation error in a blood pressure feature went undetected by AUROC but increased MT violation rates by 31–67 percentage points.

Conclusion: Metamorphic testing provides a valuable complement to conventional performance metrics like AUROC for assessing behavioral correctness in clinical prediction models. High AUROC does not guarantee clinically sensible predictions, and MT-based evaluation can detect issues invisible to traditional metrics.

Abstract: Machine learning models for clinical prediction tasks, such as in-hospital mortality and sepsis onset, routinely achieve high AUROC scores. However, AUROC measures ranking performance rather than clinical sensibility. A model may rank patients correctly overall while predicting a lower mortality risk when a patient's SOFA score worsens, contradicting established medical knowledge.
  This paper proposes applying metamorphic testing (MT) to clinical machine learning models to evaluate behavioral correctness without requiring ground-truth labels for individual predictions. We design a catalog of 12 candidate metamorphic relations (MRs) for three ICU prediction tasks using the MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV datasets, with each MR grounded in an authoritative clinical guideline. We further propose a five-layer validation strategy to ensure that MRs are clinically sound before deployment.
  As a feasibility study, we evaluate the approach on the UCI Heart Disease dataset. Although the three clinical models achieve strong predictive performance (AUROC = 0.849-0.900), they exhibit MT violation rates ranging from 27% to 87% across five pilot MRs. An injected-fault experiment further shows that a sign-negation error in a blood pressure feature remains undetected by AUROC but increases the MT violation rate by 31-67 percentage points. These findings suggest that metamorphic testing provides a valuable complement to conventional performance metrics for assessing the behavioral correctness of clinical prediction models.

</details>


### [73] [Adversarial Test-Hardening for AI-Written Code: An Instrument Autopsy and a Pre-Registered Causal Estimate of the Critic Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23002)
*Jeff Otterson*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates an adversarial test-hardening loop where a Tester model generates tests, mutation testing identifies surviving mutants, and a Critic model writes tests to kill those mutants. It reveals a prior cross-lineage effect was an artifact caused by output truncation, and a corrected pre-registered experiment shows same-lineage Critic rounds kill 78% of surviving mutants, with cross-provider comparisons confounded by operational asymmetries in the testing harness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs increasingly generate both code and tests, coverage metrics only indicate what code was executed, not what was verified. The authors aim to rigorously evaluate whether an adversarial test-hardening loop improves test quality under a fully mechanical oracle, while also correcting a prior erroneous finding caused by an instrument artifact (output truncation) that went undetected until adversarial review.

Method: A three-agent pipeline: a Tester model generates test suites, a mutation testing framework mechanically identifies surviving mutants (injected defects), and a Critic model generates tests targeting those survivors. Two experiments are conducted: Experiment 1 on five Python subjects, and Experiment 2 uses a pre-registered frozen-shared-round-0 design with four subjects, five replicates, and pre-committed seeds. Causal inference uses within-replicate comparisons; uncertainty is quantified via cluster-bootstrap intervals.

Result: Experiment 1 found the loop killed 105 mutants missed by one-shot generation with no losses, but a prior-reported cross-lineage effect (p = 9.5e-66) was revealed as an instrument artifact from output truncation. Experiment 2 showed same-lineage Critic rounds killed 78% of surviving mutants (mean incremental kill rate 0.783, 95% CI [0.592, 0.935]). The cross-provider configuration showed a rate gap of 0.178 (95% CI [0.039, 0.347]), partly attributable to one configuration's operational failures including truncation recurrences.

Conclusion: Adversarial test-hardening loops with mechanical oracles improve test suite quality beyond one-shot generation. However, cross-model comparisons are fragile: they can inherit asymmetries from the harness itself (e.g., output truncation, operational failures), which must be detected, scored, and reported rather than silently laundered. The authors advocate for adversarial review of completed analyses and release all protocols, receipts, and code.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly write both code and the tests meant to check it; coverage records what ran, not what was verified. We study an adversarial test-hardening loop under a mechanical oracle: a Tester model writes tests, mutation testing names surviving injected defects, and a Critic model writes tests to kill exactly those, with every verdict decided mechanically, so no model judges another's output. In Experiment 1, on five Python subjects (one same-lineage-loop cell could not be scored), the loop killed 105 mutants that one-shot generation missed and lost none, and the cross-lineage-Critic question returned a pre-declared null. The central finding was an autopsy: an earlier analysis reported a cross-lineage effect at p = 9.5e-66 that was an instrument artifact, an output cap silently truncating the verbose model, caught only by adversarial review of the completed analysis. Review then found a further confound, each arm resampling its own initial suite; Experiment 2 removes it. Under a pre-registered frozen-shared-round-0 design (five replicates on each of four subjects, seeds committed in advance), same-lineage Critic rounds killed 78% of the survivors the frozen initial suite left standing (mean incremental kill rate 0.783, 95% cluster-bootstrap interval [0.592, 0.935]), a within-replicate causal estimate; the cross-provider configuration showed a positive pilot difference (rate gap 0.178, 95% interval [0.039, 0.347]; magnitude dominated by a single replicate) at 5.5x lower arm cost. This compares two named model-provider-harness configurations, not an isolated lineage effect: part of the gap is one configuration's receipted operational failures, including truncation recurrences, now detected and scored rather than laundered. Cross-model comparisons can inherit the asymmetries of the harness that runs them. We release both protocols, all receipts, and the analysis code.

</details>


### [74] [Bifrost: Empowering Pretrained Language Model with Fallibility Representation for Log-Based Fault Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23169)
*Minghua He,Tong Jia,Lingzhe Zhang,Chiming Duan,Xinlong Zhao,Leyi Pan,Cheng Wang,Kangjin Wang,Yinghao Yu,Liping Zhang,Yifan Wu,Ying Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Bifrost is a self-supervised contrastive learning method that learns multi-level "fallibility representations" from system logs, outperforming pre-trained language models (PLMs) by significant margins across anomaly detection, root cause localization, and fault identification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing log-based fault diagnosis methods rely on PLMs pre-trained on natural language, which cannot adequately capture the multi-level fault information inherent in system log structures. This gap motivates the need for representations that explicitly encode fallibility — the multi-level signals reflecting system faults.

Method: Bifrost employs self-supervised contrastive learning with strategies inspired by Site Reliability Engineer (SRE) log analysis practices. These strategies are designed to learn fallibility representations that capture fault information across multiple levels of log structure, rather than relying on natural language semantics alone.

Result: On three public systems and one industrial ML-as-a-Service system, Bifrost outperforms existing PLMs by: 9.83% in F1 for anomaly detection, 18.28% in HR@k for root cause localization, and 20.88% in Macro-F1 for fault identification.

Conclusion: Bifrost demonstrates that explicitly modeling multi-level fallibility representations through contrastive learning yields substantially better log representations for fault diagnosis compared to general-purpose PLMs pre-trained on natural language.

Abstract: Log-based fault diagnosis is crucial for runtime debugging and maintenance. Existing fault diagnosis methods use language models pre-trained on natural language (PLMs) for log representation. However, system faults are reflected in the multi-level structure of system logs. PLMs pre-trained on natural language struggle to comprehensively capture multi-level fault information, failing to meet the requirements of fault diagnosis. We refer to this information as fallibility representations. To address this problem, we propose a novel log representation learning method, Bifrost. It draws inspiration from the log analysis experience of Site Reliability Engineers and meticulously designs strategies based on self-supervised contrastive learning to learn the fallibility representations of logs. Across three public systems and one industrial ML-as-a-Service system, the log representations produced by Bifrost outperform existing PLMs by average margins of 9.83% in F1 for anomaly detection, 18.28% in HR@k for root cause localization, and 20.88% in Macro-F1 for fault identification.

</details>


### [75] [Backend-Aware Graph Learning for Denoising Outcome Distributions in Quantum Program Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23211)
*Ning Ma,Jun Dai,Heng Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Q-BRIDGE is a graph learning-based noise mitigation framework for quantum program testing that uses a graph transformer with FiLM conditioning to denoise NISQ backend outputs, achieving 93.97–94.90% precision and 82.50–83.51% recall in detecting bug-induced test failures across 23 IBM backends.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Testing quantum programs on NISQ backends is fundamentally compromised by hardware noise, which distorts output distributions and leads to unreliable pass/fail decisions. Existing noise mitigation techniques are insufficient for the demands of oracle-based verification, where accurate outcome distributions are critical for detecting logical bugs distinct from stochastic noise.

Method: Q-BRIDGE employs a graph transformer architecture that encodes transpiled quantum circuits by jointly modeling gate characteristics and circuit connectivity (logical structure) alongside physical backend information. A FiLM-based conditioning layer integrates this structural encoding with raw noisy observations to produce denoised output distributions. Two training regimes are evaluated: (1) per-backend models and (2) a single general model across all backends.

Result: Evaluated on 23 IBM noise backends and 6 representative circuit families, Q-BRIDGE substantially outperforms the state-of-the-art baseline in noise mitigation. In bug-detection scenarios under noisy execution, it achieves 93.97–94.90% precision and 82.50–83.51% recall, significantly exceeding baseline performance in both the per-backend and shared-model settings.

Conclusion: Jointly modeling the graph structure of transpiled circuits and the physical characteristics of target quantum backends is a practical and effective strategy for reliable noise-aware quantum program testing, enabling high-fidelity oracle-based verification on contemporary NISQ hardware.

Abstract: Testing quantum programs on NISQ (Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum) backends is challenging because the noise disturbs outcome distributions and can affect pass/fail decisions. We present Q-BRIDGE, a graph learning-based approach that converts noisy observations into denoised distributions suitable for oracle-based verification. Q-BRIDGE uses a graph transformer architecture to encode a transpiled quantum circuit, capturing the characteristics of its gates and their connectivity; the physical backend information is encoded together with the logical structure of the circuit. An additional conditioning layer, based on FiLM (Feature-Wise Linear Modulation), takes the encoding as input and integrates noisy observations to produce denoised outcomes. We evaluate Q-BRIDGE on 23 IBM noise backends and 6 circuit families representative of practical workloads. In the first setting, we train a separate Q-BRIDGE model for each backend; in the second setting, we train a single general model shared across all backends. Across both settings, Q-BRIDGE outperforms the state-of-the-art baseline in noise mitigation by a large margin. In testing scenarios with noisy executions, Q-BRIDGE achieves 93.97%-94.90% precision and 82.50%-83.51% recall in detecting bug-induced test failures, significantly outperforming the state-of-the-art baseline. These results indicate that considering the graph structure of the transpiled circuits and the physical characteristics of specific quantum backends is a practical route to more reliable noise-aware quantum program testing.

</details>


### [76] [Towards LLM-assisted High-Quality Property Generation for Solidity Smart Contracts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23308)
*Muhammad Wahid,Shahzaib Khan,Mashhood Ali,Muhammad Hassan,Muhammad Naiman Jalil,Affan Rauf*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper explores using LLMs to automatically generate security properties for fuzzing smart contracts, finding that Gemini Pro 1.5 with prompt chaining can approach human-expert quality, achieving 25.99% average mutation score vs. the human benchmark of 31.75%, with some individual contracts matching expert-level performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smart contracts are immutable post-deployment, making pre-deployment vulnerability detection critical. Property-based fuzzing is effective but traditionally requires human experts to manually write system properties, which is time-consuming and expensive. LLMs' ability to understand code semantics offers a potential solution for automating high-quality property generation.

Method: The authors employ state-of-the-art LLMs (including Gemini Pro 1.5) to generate properties for Solidity smart contracts using various prompting strategies: zero-shot, few-shot, and prompt chaining. Quality is measured via mutation testing — artificially injecting faults and measuring how well generated properties detect them (mutation score). Results are benchmarked against human-written properties.

Result: Gemini Pro 1.5 with prompt chaining achieves the highest average mutation score of 25.99%, compared to the human-written baseline of 31.75%. Per-contract analysis shows high variance: the LibBit contract reached 74.34% (on par with human-written 74.83%), demonstrating LLMs can match expert-level property generation in specific cases despite lower average performance.

Conclusion: LLMs show strong potential for generating high-quality security properties for smart contract fuzzing. While average performance trails human experts, individual contract-level results prove that LLMs can achieve expert-parity in certain scenarios. Prompt chaining emerges as the most effective prompting strategy.

Abstract: The immutable nature of smart contracts makes it challenging to fix and patch bugs once they are deployed to a blockchain. This implies that security vulnerabilities may be exposed to possible exploitation for a longer period, necessitating comprehensive pre-deployment testing. Property-based testing combined with fuzzing has proven itself as a promising technique for uncovering vulnerabilities. Traditionally, system properties are written by human experts, which is time-consuming and consequently expensive.With the recent advancement in Large Language Models (LLMs) and their ability to 'understand' natural language and code semantics, it may be possible to generate effective properties. This study, leverages state-of-the-art LLMs to generate high-quality properties for Soliditybased smart contracts. We measure the quality of the generated properties using mutation testing. Our results show that LLMs have the potential to generate high-quality properties that are close to those written by human experts. We extensively evaluate LLMs using various prompting techniques (e.g., zero shot, few shot, and prompt chaining). Overall, we find that Gemini Pro 1.5, when combined with prompt chaining, achieves the highest average mutation score of 25.99% among all studied configurations, closely approaching the human written benchmark of 31.75%. However, our per contract analysis reveals notable variance, particularly for the LibBit contract, where Gemini Pro 1.5 under prompt chaining achieves a mutation score of 74.34%, which is on par with human written properties (74.83%). This highlights that while average performance is informative, individual contract level results demonstrate that LLMs can, in some cases, match expert level property generation.

</details>


### [77] [Enhancing Code Understanding for Impact Analysis by Combining Transformers and Program Dependence Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23355)
*Yanfu Yan,Nathan Cooper,Kevin Moran,Gabriele Bavota,Denys Poshyvanyk,Steve Rich*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Athena is a novel impact analysis approach combining program dependence graphs with deep representation learning-based conceptual coupling. Tested on Alexandria, a new large-scale benchmark of 25 projects using fine-grained bug-fix commits, Athena achieves mRR 60.32%, mAP 35.19%, and HIT@10 81.48%, significantly outperforming baselines without requiring change histories or execution traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated impact analysis approaches rely on coupling metrics derived from static, dynamic, or evolutionary information. These heuristics are brittle, require expensive execution analysis, or depend on large co-change histories. Furthermore, prior IA benchmarks are small (fewer than 10 projects) and suffer from tangled commits, compromising evaluation accuracy.

Method: The authors introduce Athena, which fuses a software system's dependence graph information with conceptual coupling derived from deep representation learning for code. This combination captures both structural dependencies and semantic similarities without needing change histories or execution information. They also construct Alexandria, a large-scale IA benchmark comprising 25 open-source projects with fine-grained commit data from bug fixes to enable rigorous evaluation.

Result: Athena's best configuration achieves mRR of 60.32%, mAP of 35.19%, and HIT@10 of 81.48% on the Alexandria benchmark. Ablation studies and qualitative analyses demonstrate that the combination of program dependence graphs and conceptual coupling yields statistically significant improvements of 10.34% (mRR), 9.55% (mAP), and 11.68% (HIT@10) over a simpler baseline.

Conclusion: Athena demonstrates that combining structural dependence graph information with learned conceptual coupling representations is an effective, practical approach to impact analysis that avoids the limitations of history- and execution-dependent methods. The Alexandria benchmark provides a more robust evaluation framework for future IA research.

Abstract: Impact analysis (IA) is a critical software maintenance task that identifies the effects of a given set of code changes on a larger software project with the intention of avoiding potential adverse effects. IA is a cognitively challenging task that involves reasoning about the abstract relationships between various code constructs. Given its difficulty, researchers have worked to automate IA with approaches that primarily use coupling metrics as a measure of the "connectedness" of different parts of a software project. Many of these coupling metrics rely on static, dynamic, or evolutionary information and are based on heuristics that tend to be brittle, require expensive execution analysis, or large histories of co-changes to accurately estimate impact sets.
  In this paper, we introduce a novel IA approach, called Athena, that combines a software system's dependence graph information with a conceptual coupling approach that uses advances in deep representation learning for code without the need for change histories and execution information. Previous IA benchmarks are small, containing fewer than ten software projects, and suffer from tangled commits, making it difficult to measure accurate results. Therefore, we constructed a large-scale IA benchmark, called Alexandria, from 25 open-source software projects, that utilizes fine-grained commit information from bug fixes. On this new benchmark, our best-performing approach configuration achieves mRR, mAP, and HIT@10 scores of 60.32%, 35.19%, and 81.48%, respectively. Through various ablations and qualitative analyses, we show that Athena's novel combination of program dependence graphs and conceptual coupling information leads it to outperform a simpler baseline by 10.34%, 9.55%, and 11.68% with statistical significance.

</details>


### [78] [On AI Safety and Security Technical Debt in Engineering AI-Enabled Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23365)
*Muhammad Tukur,Hayatullahi B. Adeyemo,Tao Chen,Nour Ali,Anis Zarrad,Rick Kazman,Marco Agus,Rami Bahsoon*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper systematically reviews 60 studies to identify 31 types of AI Technical Debt (AITD), organizing them into a 7-class root-cause taxonomy. It maps these debts to 18 trust-related concerns (6 safety hazards, 12 security vulnerabilities), synthesizes 34 mitigation guidelines, and introduces AITD-MAP — an integrated framework for risk-aware AI engineering across the full AI lifecycle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems deployed in high-stakes domains introduce novel forms of engineering liability — AI Technical Debts (AITDs) — that differ fundamentally from conventional technical debt. These debts are latent, propagate across tightly coupled AI pipelines, and degrade reliability, safety, and security. Existing approaches lack a unified framework linking root causes, trustworthiness impacts, and mitigation strategies, motivating this systematic review and framework development grounded in AI TRiSM principles.

Method: The authors conducted a systematic literature review of 60 primary studies. They identified and classified 31 distinct AITD types into a root-cause-oriented taxonomy of 7 classes. The debts were mapped to 18 trust-related concerns — 6 safety hazards and 12 security vulnerabilities. From the literature, 34 actionable mitigation guidelines (8 safety-focused, 26 security-focused) were synthesized. These components were then integrated into AITD-MAP, a unified framework spanning the AI lifecycle.

Result: A taxonomy of 31 AITD types across 7 root-cause classes (data governance, model implementation, algorithm design, architectural decisions, operational processes, documentation practices, testing adequacy). Mapping to 18 trust concerns (6 safety, 12 security). A catalog of 34 actionable mitigation guidelines. The AITD-MAP framework integrates the taxonomy, quality/risk impacts, and mitigation strategies for lifecycle-wide application.

Conclusion: AITDs represent a critical but underexamined dimension of AI engineering risk. By systematically taxonomizing these debts, mapping their safety and security implications, and providing actionable mitigation guidelines within the AITD-MAP framework, this work equips AI software engineers to make technical debts visible, trace their root causes, and reduce their presence throughout the AI lifecycle.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) systems are increasingly deployed in high-stakes domains such as healthcare, autonomous driving, finance, and education. While these systems offer powerful data-driven and adaptive capabilities, their complexity, rapid evolution, and dependence on dynamic data pipelines introduce new forms of engineering liability collectively referred to as AI Technical Debts (AITDs). AITDs arise from root causes spanning data governance, model implementation, algorithm design, architectural decisions, operational processes, documentation practices, and testing adequacy. Unlike conventional technical debt, many AITDs are latent and propagate across tightly coupled AI pipelines, leading to maintenance challenges, reliability degradation, and heightened safety or security risks. Guided by the principles of AI Trust, Risk, and Security Management (AI TRiSM), this study reinterprets technical debt through the interconnected dimensions of trustworthiness, focusing on AI safety and security technical debts. We conduct a systematic review of 60 primary studies and identify 31 distinct types of AITD, which are organized into a root-cause-oriented taxonomy comprising seven classes. The analysis examines how these debts map to 18 trust-related concerns, including 6 safety hazards and 12 security vulnerabilities. To support mitigation, the review synthesizes 34 actionable guidelines (8 safety and 26 security) targeting the prevention, detection, and reduction of AITDs across the AI lifecycle. Building on these findings, we introduce AITD-MAP, an integrated framework that connects the AITD taxonomy, quality and risk impacts, and mitigation strategies into a unified structure for risk-aware AI engineering. The framework aims to assist AI software engineers in making AI safety and security technical debts visible, understanding their root causes, and mitigating their presence.

</details>


### [79] [TLA$^{+}$-Bench: An Execution-Grounded Benchmark and Dataset for Natural-Language to TLA+ Specification Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23425)
*Arslan Bisharat,Eric Spencer,Brian Ortiz,Khushboo Bhadauria,Mujtaba Nazari,Beatriz Santos,Anisa Ramos,TaiNing Wang,George K. Thiruvathukal,Konstantin Läufer,Mohammed Abuhamad*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TLA$^{+}$-Bench introduces a benchmark for formal specification generation that grades by model-checked execution correctness rather than syntactic similarity, revealing that correctness rates vary sixfold to elevenfold depending on grading choices—a range called the "correctness envelope"—with even the strongest model achieving only 16-26% correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of LLM-generated TLA$^{+}$ specifications relies on surface-level metrics (resemblance to a reference or parse success), which fail to capture whether the specification is semantically correct. There is a need for an execution-grounded benchmark with a definitive correctness oracle.

Method: The authors construct TLA$^{+}$-Bench, a dataset of 403 model-checked gold and 897 parse-only silver specifications from 13 public repositories, each paired with a TLA$^{+}$ model-checker configuration that exhaustively verifies named properties over the full reachable state space. Four model-written descriptions (two styles × two providers) accompany each problem, with difficulty and category labels. They systematically vary grading choices to measure the "correctness envelope."

Result: Correctness rates swing dramatically under different grading choices: from 10.0% to 1.7% (sixfold) on a fixed output set; adding interface-supply widens this to 18.7%–1.7% (elevenfold). The strongest model achieves 16% correct by default and 26% with interface names; open models achieve at most 1%. Correctness falls sharply with problem difficulty, and every model writes valid (parseable) TLA$^{+}$ far more often than correct TLA$^{+}$.

Conclusion: Rigorous measurement of formal specification generation requires an execution-based oracle, but even with one, grading choices produce a wide correctness envelope that must be explicitly reported. LLMs remain far from reliable TLA$^{+}$ generation, especially on harder problems, and open models lag significantly behind proprietary ones.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly write TLA$^{+}$ formal specifications from natural-language descriptions, but progress is hard to measure: existing resources grade by resemblance to a reference or by whether the output parses, neither of which shows correctness. We present TLA$^{+}$-Bench, a dataset and benchmark that grades by execution. Every gold specification ships a configuration the TLA$^{+}$ model checker runs over the full reachable state space, deciding exactly whether the specification holds the properties that configuration names. The dataset holds 403 model-checked gold and 897 parse-only silver specifications from 13 public repositories, subsumes prior TLA$^{+}$ generation data, and carries four model-written descriptions in two styles from two providers, with difficulty and category labels. Our main finding is about measurement itself: an exact oracle gives not one correctness number but a range. Varying only the grading choices earlier benchmarks leave unstated, on one fixed set of model outputs, the correct rate moves sixfold, from 10.0\% to 1.7\%; adding the interface-supply choice, where the model is told the configuration's names, widens the range to elevenfold, from 18.7\% to 1.7\%. We call this range the correctness envelope and measure each of its bounds. The findings inside it are stable. Every model writes valid TLA$^{+}$ far more often than correct TLA$^{+}$: the strongest is correct 16\% of the time by default and 26\% when given the interface names, open models at most 1\%, and correctness falls sharply with difficulty.

</details>


### [80] [LinkRank: A Learning-to-Rank Framework for One-to-Many Issue-Commit Traceability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23610)
*Abhishek Kumar,Tuhin Mondal,Partha Pratim Das,Partha Pratim Chakrabarti*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LinkRank frames one-to-many issue–commit traceability as an issue-centric learning-to-rank problem with an iterative pick–remove–renormalize strategy, outperforming pairwise classification baselines on a new six-repo GitHub dataset (74.54% F1 under Known-K, 68.84% under Unknown-K).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing issue–commit traceability methods assume one-to-one links, but real-world issues are often resolved by multiple commits. This mismatch causes incomplete traceability, motivating a framework that explicitly models the one-to-many nature of issue resolution.

Method: LinkRank is a learning-to-rank framework that treats each issue's candidate commits as a ranked list. It employs an iterative pick–remove–renormalize strategy: select the top-ranked commit, remove it from the candidate pool, renormalize the remaining scores, and repeat until a stopping criterion is met. Two stopping rules (ABS and REL) handle the Unknown-K setting. A new benchmark dataset is curated from six open-source GitHub repositories for realistic evaluation.

Result: Under Known-K (true number of linked commits given), LinkRank achieves an average F1 of 74.54% across six projects, vs. 48.39% for the best baseline. Under Unknown-K with automatic stopping, LinkRank attains 68.84% F1 (ABS) and 67.02% F1 (REL), outperforming the strongest baselines in both settings.

Conclusion: One-to-many issue–commit traceability is more effectively framed as an issue-centric ranking and iterative selection problem rather than as independent pairwise classification. LinkRank's strong performance across both Known-K and Unknown-K settings supports this conclusion.

Abstract: Recovering traceability links between issues and commits is important for software maintenance, debugging, impact analysis, and project understanding. However, most existing approaches assume a one-to-one relationship, where each issue is linked to a single commit. In practice, many issues are resolved through multiple commits, and ignoring this one-to-many nature can lead to incomplete traceability. This paper presents LinkRank, a learning-to-rank framework for recovering one-to-many issue--commit links. Unlike existing methods that mainly judge issue--commit pairs independently, LinkRank considers the set of candidate commits for an issue and identifies the commits that are most likely to contribute to its resolution. To support realistic evaluation, we construct a new dataset from six open-source GitHub repositories. LinkRank follows an iterative pick--remove--renormalize strategy: it selects the highest-ranked commit, removes it from the candidate pool, renormalizes the remaining scores, and repeats the process until the stopping criterion is met. We evaluate LinkRank under two settings: Known-K, where the true number of linked commits is available, and Unknown-K, where the model must infer when to stop selecting commits using ABS and REL stopping rules. Across six projects, LinkRank achieves an average Known-K F1 score of 74.54%, compared with 48.39% for the strongest baseline. In the Unknown-K setting, LinkRank achieves 68.84% F1 with ABS and 67.02% F1 with REL, outperforming the strongest baselines under both automatic stopping rules. Overall, the findings suggest that one-to-many issue--commit traceability is better addressed as an issue-centric ranking and iterative selection problem than as independent pairwise classification.

</details>


### [81] [Where Is the Cost of Third-Party API Routers in Agentic Software Development?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23624)
*Donghao Fu,Jingxin Li,Xue Jiang,Yihong Dong*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper empirically investigates how third-party API routers in LLM coding-agent workflows can inject malicious modifications into provider outputs, and finds that existing client-side defenses are largely ineffective against such router-side injection attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Third-party API routers sit on the trusted path between coding agents and LLM providers, inspecting and modifying every request/response. Despite high-autonomy coding agents relying on these routers, no mechanism verifies alignment between provider outputs and the repository-level actions ultimately executed. The real-world impact of this control gap on software development tasks remains unmeasured.

Method: The authors conduct an empirical study of router-side injection across four intervention levels: Response Substitution (L1), Response Append (L2), LLM-Polished Injection (L3), and LLM-Polished with Distribution Alignment (L4). They develop SIDEL, a framework for trace recording, replay, injection, and defense evaluation using a curated dataset of 400 samples. Four representative coding agents are evaluated, along with whitelist-based execution control and LLM review defenses.

Result: Router-side intervention substantially alters repository-level actions and evades existing client-side safeguards. All evaluated agents achieved a 0% defense success rate across all injection levels without additional mitigations. Client-side mitigations and reactive reviews improve resistance but fail to fully restore end-to-end control.

Conclusion: The control gap introduced by third-party API routers is real and exploitable. Current client-side defenses are insufficient, motivating the need for provider-side output-integrity guarantees to restore trust in LLM coding-agent workflows.

Abstract: Third-party API routers have become a common layer that unifies access across increasingly diverse LLM providers. In coding-agent workflows, high-autonomy operation is widely adopted because it reduces interaction overhead. As a result, a third-party API router, which sits between the agent and the upstream provider, inevitably occupies the trusted path. It can inspect and modify every request and response, yet no mechanism verifies alignment between the provider's output and the repository-level actions ultimately executed by the agent. Consequently, client-side permission mechanisms may become ineffective in practice. Whether this control gap produces real, hard-to-detect effects on software development tasks remains empirically unmeasured. In this paper, we conduct an empirical study of router-side injection in coding agents, examining four intervention levels of increasing subtlety: Response Substitution (L1), Response Append (L2), LLM-Polished Injection (L3), and LLM-Polished with Distribution Alignment Injection (L4). Moreover, we develop SIDEL, a framework for trace recording, replay, injection, and defense evaluation, with a curated dataset of 400 samples. We evaluate four representative coding agents, and further evaluate whitelist-based execution control and LLM review. Router-side intervention substantially alters repository-level actions and remains difficult for existing client-side safeguards to detect. Without additional mitigations, all evaluated agents achieved a defense success rate of 0 percent across all injection levels. Client-side mitigations and reactive reviews improve resistance but do not fully restore end-to-end control, motivating provider-side output-integrity guarantees. Our code is available at https://github.com/Riyasushin/SIDE.

</details>


### [82] [Multi-level Code Optimization via Mixture of Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23665)
*Yun Peng,Jun Wan,Jiakun Liu,Shuzheng Gao,David Lo,Xiaoxue Ren*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Optimo is a multi-level LLM-based code optimization framework using a Mixture-of-Prompts architecture that identifies performance bottlenecks via differential profiling and applies targeted optimizations across four abstraction levels, achieving up to 57.48% optimization success rate and 3.97× speedup on human-written code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional code optimization methods (IR-based, compilation-dependent) fail for dynamic languages that lack compilation. Existing LLM-based approaches for direct source-code optimization suffer from two key limitations: (1) inability to identify suitable optimization targets, and (2) incomprehensive single-level optimization that misses opportunities beyond statement-level improvements.

Method: The authors propose Optimo, built on a novel Mixture-of-Prompts (MoP) architecture. It first identifies time-critical code structures as performance bottlenecks using differential profiling. These identified structures are then routed to specialized optimization strategies (analogous to expert models in Mixture-of-Experts), each tailored to specific code patterns. Critically, Optimo operates at four levels of abstraction: from coarse-grained algorithmic improvements down to fine-grained API-usage optimizations, enabling multi-level optimization beyond the statement-level focus of prior work.

Result: On the COFFE and Effibench benchmarks, Optimo achieves up to 57.48% opt% (percentage of optimized programs that are correct and ≥10% faster), up to 3.97× speedup on human-written code, and outperforms the best baseline by up to 96.51% in opt%. For LLM-generated code, it achieves up to 42.42% opt% and up to 13.51× speedup.

Conclusion: Optimo demonstrates that a multi-level, prompt-routing-based LLM approach with differential profiling can effectively and comprehensively optimize code in dynamic languages, significantly outperforming existing single-level LLM-based methods.

Abstract: Runtime efficiency is a critical factor that impacts both software quality and user satisfaction. There are many approaches proposed for code optimization to improve runtime efficiency. Traditional code optimization methods operate on intermediate representations (IRs) during compilation for static languages. They are effective but struggle to handle dynamic languages that do not require compilation. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have been leveraged to directly optimize source code in dynamic languages. However, these methods fail to identify suitable optimization targets and usually conduct incomprehensive single-level optimization.
  To address these challenges, we propose Optimo, a multi-level LLM-based code optimization approach built on a novel Mixture-of-Prompts (MoP) architecture. In the MoP architecture, Optimo identifies time-critical code structures as performance bottlenecks via differential profiling. These structures are then routed to some optimization strategies, akin to expert models in MoE, each tailored to optimize specific code patterns. Unlike traditional approaches that focus only on statement-level optimizations, Optimo operates at four levels of abstraction, ranging from coarse-grained algorithmic improvements to fine-grained optimizations in API usage. We evaluate Optimo on two code efficiency benchmarks, COFFE and Effibench. Our results demonstrate that Optimo achieves an up to 57.48% opt%, i.e., the percentage of optimized programs that are correct and at least 10% faster than the original programs, and an up to 3.97x speedup when optimizing human-written code, and it consistently outperforms the best baseline by up to 96.51% in terms of opt%. Furthermore, Optimo achieves an up to 42.42% opt% and an up to 13.51x speedup when optimizing LLM-generated code.

</details>


### [83] [Semantic-Enhanced Automatic Refinement of Architecture Recovery Results Using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23774)
*Yiran Zhang,Chengwei Liu,Yuqiang Sun,Zhengzi Xu,Weisong Sun,Wenke Li,Wuxia Jin,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Semref is a framework that combines LLMs with dependency analysis to automatically refine software architecture recovery results, achieving significant accuracy improvements (17.72%–43.35% normalized gains, with over 100% relative improvement on MoJoFM and a2a_adj metrics) across 9 projects and 10 recovery tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrepancies between designed and implemented software architectures create significant risks. Existing automatic architecture recovery tools have limited accuracy, forcing architects to spend substantial manual effort refining results. There is a need to reduce this manual refinement burden.

Method: Semref combines large language models (LLMs) with dependency analysis to automatically refine architectures recovered by existing tools. It leverages semantic understanding from LLMs and integrates structural dependency information to improve both accuracy and comprehension of recovered architectures. Evaluation was conducted on 9 projects with ground-truth architectures and 10 state-of-the-art recovery tools, using 5 commonly used metrics.

Result: Semref improved accuracy across all metrics, with normalized gains ranging from 17.72% to 43.35%. For MoJoFM, relative improvement reached 118.57%, and for a2a_adj, 100.41%.

Conclusion: Integrating LLMs with structural dependency analysis is an effective approach for refining automatically recovered software architectures, substantially reducing the manual effort required from architects while significantly boosting accuracy.

Abstract: Understanding the architecture is crucial for effectively maintaining and managing large software systems. However, discrepancies often exist between the designed and implemented architectures, which can pose significant risks. To identify these discrepancies, architects need to extract the architecture from the system implementation, which is both time-consuming and error-prone. To simplify this procedure, many automatic architecture recovery techniques have been developed. Yet, their accuracy is often limited. Architects must still invest significant effort in refining recovery results to ensure they accurately reflect the implemented architecture.
  To reduce such manual effort, we introduce Semref, a framework that combines LLMs with dependency analysis to automatically refine architectures recovered by existing architecture recovery tools. By leveraging the LLM's semantic understanding capabilities and integrating structural dependencies, Semref enhances both the accuracy and the comprehension of recovered architectures. To evaluate Semref, we tested on 9 projects with published ground-truth architectures and 10 state-of-the-art architecture recovery tools. 5 commonly used metrics are adopted to evaluate the effectiveness of Semref. The results show that Semref improves accuracy across various metrics, with normalized gains ranges from 17.72\% to 43.35\%. Specifically, for MoJoFM and $a2a_{adj}$ metrics, Semref achieves relative improvements of 118.57\% and 100.41\%, respectively.

</details>


### [84] [The Influence of Fraudulent AI-Generated Responses on Software Engineering Surveys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23805)
*Ronnie de Souza Santos,Italo Santos,Maria Teresa Baldassarre,Cleyton Magalhaes,Mairieli Wessel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study examines how AI-assisted or fraudulent responses affect the validity of software engineering survey findings. Through secondary analysis of four survey datasets, the authors found that quantitative results remained relatively stable after removing suspicious responses, while qualitative findings were more substantially influenced, particularly in contextual framing and evidence interpretation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The emergence of LLMs raises concerns about fraudulent or AI-assisted participation in software engineering surveys, potentially threatening result validity. The study aims to empirically assess the extent and nature of this threat to survey-based research.

Method: Secondary analysis of four existing software engineering survey datasets, employing manual identification of suspicious responses, automated AI-generated text detection, descriptive statistical analysis, and thematic analysis. The authors compared findings from original datasets with those from manually cleaned datasets.

Result: Quantitative findings remained largely stable after filtering suspicious responses, though some demographic and analytical variables exhibited moderate variation. Qualitative findings were more strongly affected, with shifts in contextual framing, code prominence, and the nature of evidential support, altering how participant experiences and study contexts were characterized.

Conclusion: AI-assisted participation differentially impacts survey findings based on analysis type. Researchers should combine multiple validation procedures, especially in studies relying on open-ended responses.

Abstract: Background: Large Language Models (LLMs) introduce new concerns regarding fraudulent or AI assisted participation in software engineering surveys. Aims: This study investigates how suspicious or potentially AI assisted responses may affect the validity of software engineering survey findings. Method: We conducted a secondary analysis of four software engineering survey datasets using manual identification of suspicious responses, automated AI generated text detection, descriptive statistical analysis, and thematic analysis. We compared findings obtained from the original and manually cleaned datasets. Results: Quantitative findings generally remained stable after filtering suspicious responses, although some demographic and analytical variables showed moderate variation, affecting the interpretation of specific participant groups and contextual characteristics. In contrast, qualitative findings were more strongly influenced by changes in contextual framing, code prominence, and the nature of the evidence supporting interpretation, shaping how participants' experiences and study contexts were interpreted and characterized. Conclusions: AI assisted participation may influence software engineering survey findings differently depending on the type of analysis being conducted. The findings reinforce the importance of combining multiple validation procedures, particularly in studies relying on open ended responses.

</details>


### [85] [RESTOR: Automated Test Oracle Generation for RESTful APIs via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23963)
*Xun Zhou,Zhen Dong,Mingyu Ren,Qiang Li,JunJie Li,Sifan Wang,Xiaolong Yu,Chaofeng Sha,Xin Peng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Restor is a black-box framework that generates executable test assertions from a single REST API request-response pair using a GRPO-fine-tuned lightweight LLM, achieving 85.42% F1 in key field identification and boosting automated test case adoption from 74.1% to over 96% in production at ByteDance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern REST API testing in agile industrial settings suffers from missing/outdated formal specifications (e.g., OpenAPI) and unavailable historical execution logs for new endpoints, making reliable test oracle generation extremely challenging. Existing approaches either depend on rule-based templates (brittle) or require massive training logs (impractical for new services).

Method: Restor uses a novel data augmentation pipeline to fine-tune a lightweight LLM via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). The reward function jointly optimizes for: (i) selecting stable, semantically meaningful fields while avoiding dynamic noise (timestamps, trace IDs), and (ii) generating robust assertions resilient to logic variations. The framework operates in a black-box setting requiring only a single request-response pair.

Result: Evaluated on 2,300+ API traces across 246 real-world services, Restor achieves 85.42% F1 in key field identification, significantly outperforms prompt-engineered baselines and generalist models, and produces a higher proportion of semantically accurate assertions. In ByteDance's production CI/CD pipeline, it raised automated test case adoption from 74.1% to over 96%.

Conclusion: Restor demonstrates that fine-tuning a lightweight LLM with GRPO and a carefully designed multi-objective reward function enables practical, high-quality test oracle generation from minimal input, substantially reducing manual QA effort while maintaining execution stability in industrial settings.

Abstract: Modern REST API testing faces a critical challenge in defining reliable test oracles, particularly in agile industrial environments where formal specifications (e.g., OpenAPI) are frequently missing or outdated, and historical execution logs are unavailable for newly deployed endpoints. In this paper, we present Restor (Reinforcement Enhanced Single-Traffic Oracle generator for REST APIs), a framework that generates executable test assertions from a single observed request-response pair in a black-box setting. Unlike existing approaches that rely on rule-based templates or massive training logs, Restor utilizes a novel data augmentation pipeline to fine-tune a lightweight Large Language Model (LLM) via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). This training process enables the model to internalize testing "common sense" by optimizing a reward function that jointly encourages: (i) the selection of stable, semantically meaningful fields for validation and the avoidance of dynamic noise (e.g., timestamps or trace IDs); (ii) the generation of robust assertions that withstand logic variations. We evaluate Restor on an industrial dataset comprising over 2,300 API traces across 246 real-world services. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that Restor significantly outperforms prompt-engineered baselines and generalist models, achieving a superior $F_1$ score of 85.42% in key field identification and increasing the proportion of semantically accurate assertions. Furthermore, deployment in a production CI/CD workflow at ByteDance confirms its practical value: the system raised the adoption rate of automatically generated test cases from 74.1% to over 96%, substantially reducing manual Quality Assurance (QA) effort while ensuring high execution stability.

</details>


### [86] [Industrial Practice of LLM-Based Test Case Carving and Assertion Generation (Experience Paper)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24000)
*Haozhen You,Zhen Dong,Jingjing Wang,Qiang Li,Xin Peng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: NL2Test is an end-to-end tool that generates executable API regression tests from natural-language scenario descriptions and captured traffic, combining LLM-based semantic interpretation with deterministic guardrails to achieve 82.4% exact-match accuracy on industrial scenarios and 85.4% code adoption in production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise microservice regression testing suffers from incomplete/outdated documentation, forcing QA engineers to manually reconstruct test scenarios from raw traffic — a labor-intensive, error-prone process. There is a need to automate the conversion of traffic captures into replayable, assertion-equipped regression tests.

Method: NL2Test combines two complementary tasks: (1) Test case carving — extracting minimal replayable request sequences from traffic captures and reconstructing data dependencies so dynamic values are bound from responses rather than hard-coded; (2) Assertion generation — producing business-aligned assertions while avoiding non-deterministic fields and hallucinated paths. The system uses LLMs for semantic interpretation and constrained code synthesis, paired with deterministic algorithms for request filtering, dependency confirmation via value consistency, and assertion-path validation.

Result: On 51 industrial regression scenarios from a large consumer-facing Internet company, NL2Test achieved 82.4% (42/51) exact-match rate and produced functionally usable drafts in 98.0% (50/51) of scenarios with minor post-edits. Over a 9-month production deployment (starting March 2025), it generated 3,196 test cases with an 85.4% overall code adoption rate.

Conclusion: Traffic-grounded test generation with deterministic guardrails significantly reduces manual effort and improves regression automation in complex microservice environments, demonstrating practical viability at scale.

Abstract: Enterprise regression testing for microservice systems is often constrained by incomplete or outdated documentation. In practice, QA engineers frequently rely on real execution traffic to reconstruct business scenarios; however, turning raw traffic into replayable regression tests with stable validation logic remains labor-intensive and error-prone.
  This paper presents NL2Test, an end-to-end approach and tool that generates executable API regression tests from (i) a natural-language scenario description and (ii) a traffic capture recorded while executing the scenario. NL2Test addresses two coupled tasks: test case carving, which extracts a minimal replayable request sequence and reconstructs data dependencies so that dynamic values are bound from their responses rather than hard-coded; and assertion generation, which produces assertions aligned with business intent while avoiding non-deterministic fields and hallucinated paths. To improve reliability, NL2Test uses LLMs for semantic interpretation and constrained code synthesis, and uses deterministic algorithms for request filtering, dependency confirmation via value consistency, and assertion-path validation.
  We evaluate NL2Test on 51 industrial regression scenarios extracted from a large consumer-facing Internet company. NL2Test achieves an exact-match rate of 82.4% (42/51), and produces a functionally usable draft in 98.0% (50/51) of scenarios when allowing minor post-edits. In a 9-month production deployment starting in March 2025, NL2Test generated 3,196 test cases with an overall code adoption rate of 85.4%. These results indicate that traffic-grounded generation with deterministic guardrails can substantially reduce manual effort while improving regression automation in complex microservice environments.

</details>


### [87] [Four Years of GenAI: How Educators and Industry Adapted Their Assessment Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24246)
*May Mahmoud,Nisa Shahid,Izah Sohail,Gulshan Sharma,Hanan Salam,Sarah Nadi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study surveys 56 educators and 24 hiring professionals to examine how GenAI is reshaping entry-level software engineering skills and their assessment. While industry has near-universal GenAI adoption governed by organizational policy, educators focus policies on academic integrity. Both groups agree on the importance of critical evaluation of AI output, responsible GenAI use, and independent learning, but disagree on graduate readiness, with hiring professionals reporting larger skill gaps, particularly in foundational, non-GenAI-specific skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GenAI is transforming software engineering tasks, raising questions about what constitutes an entry-level software engineer today. The authors aim to investigate whether core skills and their assessment have shifted, and to identify gaps between educational preparation and industry expectations.

Method: Two surveys were conducted with 56 educators and 24 hiring professionals across diverse geographic regions. The surveys explored GenAI policies, skill valuation, assessment practices, and perceptions of graduate readiness, enabling comparison between educational and hiring perspectives.

Result: Key findings: (1) Educators frame GenAI policies around academic integrity, while industry adoption is near-universal with organizational governance; (2) Interview policies on GenAI are still emerging, with split preferences on candidates demonstrating GenAI skills; (3) Both groups are shifting toward GenAI-resistant assessments using real-time, observable, higher-order tasks; (4) Both agree on top skills but disagree on graduate readiness — hiring professionals report larger gaps, especially in foundational, non-GenAI-specific skills.

Conclusion: The core skills for entry-level engineers have not fundamentally changed, but how they are assessed must evolve. The misalignment between educators and industry on graduate readiness, particularly in foundational skills, signals a need for curricula and hiring practices to adapt to a GenAI-augmented landscape.

Abstract: GenAI's ability to solve a wide range of software engineering tasks is reshaping the software industry, raising the question of what an entry-level software engineer looks like today. In this work, we investigate whether the core skills for entry-level engineers have changed with GenAI and how the assessment of those skills has changed. Through two surveys, we consider both the educational and hiring perspectives, drawing on responses from 56 educators and 24 hiring professionals across diverse geographic regions to identify gaps and misaligned expectations. We find that educators frame their GenAI policies primarily around academic integrity, whereas GenAI use in industry is near-universal, with organizational policy governing how it should be used rather than whether to use it. Policies on GenAI use during interviews are still emerging, and respondents are split on whether they prefer candidates who demonstrate GenAI skills. Both populations are moving toward GenAI-resistant assessments built on observable real-time interactions and higher-order tasks, and both agree on which skills matter most, rating critical evaluation of AI-generated output, responsible and effective use of GenAI tools, and the ability to learn and adapt independently highly. However, they disagree on graduate readiness in these skills: hiring professionals report larger gaps, with the disagreement concentrated on foundational, non-GenAI-specific skills.

</details>


### [88] [Alternative UX Extensions and Their Trade-offs for Code Completion in Pharo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24253)
*Mehdi Elkolei,Omar Abedelkader,Stéphane Ducasse*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Complishon, Pharo's AST-based code completion engine, is extended with four features — typo tolerance, implicit prefix expansion, grouped completion entries, and camel-case matching — to overcome practical limitations of strict prefix matching while preserving its modular architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complishon's strict prefix matching causes several practical usability problems: sensitivity to typos, redundant framework prefix typing, large unscannable completion menus, and lack of support for camel-case abbreviations. These limitations hinder developer productivity despite the engine's strong semantic foundations.

Method: Four extensions are implemented and integrated into Complishon's existing filter-based architecture: (1) typo tolerance using edit-distance thresholding, (2) implicit prefix expansion to infer framework prefixes automatically, (3) grouped completion entries to improve menu scannability, and (4) camel-case matching to support abbreviation-based filtering. Alternative designs and trade-offs are discussed for each.

Result: All four extensions are successfully integrated into Complishon without compromising its modular architecture or predictable behavior. The paper demonstrates that these quality-of-life improvements are achievable within the existing AST-based, filter-driven design paradigm.

Conclusion: Semantic code completion engines can be meaningfully enhanced with practical usability features — typo tolerance, implicit prefixes, grouping, and camel-case matching — without requiring architectural overhauls, as demonstrated by their clean integration into Complishon's modular design.

Abstract: Complishon is Pharo's context-aware code completion engine, built on AST analysis, lazy candidate generation, and filter-based candidate selection. Its existing design already provides strong semantic completion, but several practical limits remain. Strict prefix matching is sensitive to small typing errors, framework prefixes often force redundant input, large completion menus are difficult to scan, and prefix-only matching does not support common camel-case abbreviations. This paper presents four extensions that address these limits: typo tolerance, implicit prefix expansion, grouped completion entries, and camel-case matching. For each extension, we describe the implementation, discuss alternative designs, and explain the trade-offs involved. The main contribution of the paper is to show that these improvements can be integrated into Complishon while preserving its modular architecture and predictable behavior.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [89] [Language-Routed RAG and Direct Option Scoring for Multilingual Financial QA: DS@GT at FinMMEval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22841)
*Justice Ayela,Kabir Sahni*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A submission to FinMMEval 2026 Task 1 using retrieval-augmented direct scoring (RADS) with language-aware model routing and multilingual retrieval for financial exam QA across five languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial certification exams (CFA, EFPA, CPA) require structured domain reasoning that standard NLP benchmarks fail to capture. This challenge is exacerbated in multilingual settings where retrieval and representation infrastructure is underdeveloped, particularly for low-resource languages.

Method: A LangGraph-based pipeline that: (1) detects query language; (2) retrieves semantically relevant exemplars from a 30,209-entry multilingual knowledge base using BGE-M3 embeddings and FAISS; (3) scores answers via Retrieval-Augmented Direct Scoring (RADS), reading next-token log-probabilities over candidate option letters; (4) fuses per-language and cross-lingual retrieval indices via weighted Reciprocal Rank Fusion for low-resource languages; and (5) routes models by language: Qwen3-14B (Arabic, Chinese, Hindi), Qwen2.5-14B (English), Llama-3.1-8B (Greek).

Result: Chain-of-thought prompting degrades Greek accuracy from 90.7% to 20.9%. Qwen3's default thinking mode collapses Arabic RADS performance to near-chance levels. Language-asymmetric performance gaps were revealed through ablation studies, motivating the language-routed model selection strategy.

Conclusion: Effective multilingual financial reasoning requires three key components: language-aware retrieval, model routing based on per-language performance characteristics, and deliberate scoring strategy selection (avoiding chain-of-thought and thinking modes where they prove detrimental).

Abstract: We present DS@GT's submission to FinMMEval 2026 Task 1, a multilingual financial exam question answering benchmark spanning English, Spanish, Greek, Chinese, and Hindi. Financial certification exams such as the CFA, EFPA, and CPA demand structured domain reasoning that standard NLP benchmarks do not capture, and this challenge compounds across languages where retrieval and representation infrastructure is underdeveloped. We build a retrieval-augmented pipeline on LangGraph that detects query language and retrieves semantically relevant exemplars from a 30,209-entry multilingual knowledge base using BGE-M3 embeddings and FAISS indexing. The system then scores answers via Retrieval-Augmented Direct Scoring (RADS), reading next-token log-probabilities over candidate option letters rather than generating free-form output. For low-resource languages, we fuse per-language and cross-lingual retrieval indices using weighted Reciprocal Rank Fusion. Model selection is language-routed: Qwen3-14B for Arabic, Chinese, and Hindi; Qwen2.5-14B for English; and Llama-3.1-8B for Greek, a routing derived from empirical ablations that reveal substantial language-asymmetric performance gaps. Notably, chain-of-thought prompting significantly degrades Greek accuracy (90.7% to 20.9%), and enabling Qwen3's default thinking mode collapses Arabic RADS performance to near-chance levels. Our results indicate that effective multilingual financial reasoning requires language-aware retrieval, model routing, and deliberate scoring strategy selection.

</details>


### [90] [Beyond Exact Match: How Evaluation Methodology Dominates Model Choice in LLM-Based Product Attribute Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22949)
*Ayush Dwivedi,Ashvi Soni*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A controlled study across two LLMs and four prompting strategies on the MAVE benchmark reveals that evaluation methodology and ground truth data quality dominate F1 variance—by factors of 23× and 5× over model choice and prompting strategy, respectively. The benchmark itself exhibits a 23.2% noise rate against modern LLM outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners report widely varying performance in structured product attribute extraction depending on model, dataset, and prompting strategy. This paper aims to systematically quantify the relative impact of model selection, prompting strategy, and—critically—evaluation methodology on extraction performance, isolating the dominant source of variance.

Method: The authors conduct a controlled empirical comparison of four prompting strategies (zero-shot, few-shot, schema-guided, definition-augmented) across two LLMs (GPT-4o-mini, Gemini 2.5 Flash) on the MAVE benchmark, generating 6,400 attribute-level predictions. They evaluate using both exact and fuzzy string matching, perform a noise audit of ground truth labels, and formally decompose F1 variance across four experimental factors. Statistical significance is assessed via paired permutation tests (B=10,000) and Cohen's kappa for inter-protocol agreement.

Result: Evaluation methodology produces F1 variance approximately 23× larger than model choice and 5× larger than prompting strategy choice. The MAVE benchmark exhibits a 23.2% ground truth noise rate against modern LLM outputs. The inter-protocol F1 gap is highly significant (p<0.0001), with Cohen's kappa of 0.769 indicating substantial agreement between protocols.

Conclusion: For production attribute extraction pipelines, evaluation methodology and data quality dominate the impact of model selection and prompt engineering. Investments in cleaner ground truth and robust evaluation protocols yield greater practical gains than choosing between models or tuning prompts.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have become a default choice for structured product attribute extraction in e-commerce pipelines, with practitioners reporting widely varying performance across models, datasets, and prompting strategies. This paper presents a controlled empirical study comparing four prompting strategies -- zero-shot, few-shot, schema-guided, and definition-augmented -- across two production-grade LLMs (GPT-4o-mini and Gemini 2.5 Flash) on the MAVE benchmark.
