We evaluate 6,400 attribute-level predictions using both exact and fuzzy string matching, and conduct a rigorous noise audit of the ground truth labels. We formally decompose F1 variance across four experimental factors and find that evaluation methodology produces variance approximately 23 times larger than model choice and 5 times larger than prompting strategy choice. We further establish that the MAVE benchmark exhibits a 23.2% ground truth noise rate against modern LLM outputs. Paired permutation tests (B=10,000) confirm that the inter-protocol F1 gap is highly significant (p<0.0001) and Cohen's kappa of 0.769 between protocols indicates substantial agreement. We conclude that for production attribute extraction pipelines, evaluation methodology and data quality dominate the impact of model selection and prompt engineering.

</details>


### [91] [When Does Few-Shot Prompting Help? A Systematic Empirical Study of Shot-Count Effects Across Model Scale, Architecture, and Output Parsing Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22969)
*Ayush Dwivedi,Ashvi Soni*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A controlled study across five LLMs and six shot-count configurations reveals four distinct behavioral regimes in few-shot classification, demonstrating that the shot-performance relationship is non-monotonic, non-universal, and unpredictable from model scale alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite widespread adoption of few-shot prompting, little systematic work examines how shot count interacts with model scale, architecture, and output format compliance in classification. This paper addresses that gap with a rigorously controlled experimental design.

Method: Five LLMs (Gemini Flash Lite, GPT-4o-mini, Llama 3.1 8B, Llama 3.3 70B, Llama 4 Scout 17B) were evaluated on the AG News 4-class benchmark (n=200) across k ∈ {0,1,2,3,5,8} shots. Metrics include macro-averaged F1 with 95% bootstrap CIs (B=10,000), permutation-test p-values, and Cohen's d effect sizes across all 30 configurations.

Result: Four behavioral regimes emerged: (1) modest, insignificant gains for well-calibrated zero-shot models; (2) catastrophic zero-shot failure with dramatic single-shot recovery (Llama 3.1 8B, d=10.98, p<0.0001); (3) monotonic degradation from optimal zero-shot (Llama 4 Scout); (4) U-shaped curve (Llama 3.3 70B: F1=0.907 at 0-shot, 0.635 at 2-shot, 0.785 at 5-shot). A parsing artifact deflated Llama 3.3 70B performance by up to 206%.

Conclusion: Shot count and classification performance exhibit no monotonic, universal, or scale-predictable relationship. Evaluation practices must account for parsing artifacts and model-specific few-shot dynamics.

Abstract: Few-shot prompting, the practice of prepending a small number of input-output demonstration pairs to a query before presenting it to a large language model (LLM), is among the most widely adopted inference-time techniques in NLP. Yet little systematic work investigates how shot count interacts with model scale, architecture, and output format compliance in determining classification performance. This paper presents a controlled study of five LLMs across six shot-count configurations (k in {0,1,2,3,5,8}) on the AG News four-class benchmark (n=200). Our models span proprietary and open-source families: Gemini Flash Lite, GPT-4o-mini, Llama 3.1 8B, Llama 3.3 70B, and Llama 4 Scout 17B. We report macro-averaged F1 with 95% bootstrap confidence intervals (B=10,000), permutation-test p-values, and Cohen's d effect sizes across all 30 configurations. Our findings reveal four qualitatively distinct behavioral regimes: (1) models already well-calibrated at zero-shot that show modest, statistically insignificant gains (Gemini, GPT-4o-mini); (2) models that undergo catastrophic zero-shot failure but recover dramatically with a single example (Llama 3.1 8B, d=10.98, p<0.0001); (3) models optimal at zero-shot that degrade monotonically with additional examples (Llama 4 Scout); and (4) models exhibiting a U-shaped curve (Llama 3.3 70B: 0-shot F1=0.907, 2-shot F1=0.635, 5-shot F1=0.785 with parser corrected). We additionally identify, diagnose, and correct a systematic parsing artifact that artificially deflated Llama 3.3 70B performance by up to 206%, constituting a methodological contribution to LLM evaluation practice. Our results demonstrate that the relationship between shot count and classification performance is not monotonic, not universal, and not predictable from model scale alone.

</details>


### [92] [VecTree-RAG: An Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation Framework Combining Vector and Tree Retrieval for Efficiency and Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23006)
*Xinyan Zhong,Yuwei Shi,Yuqi Wei,Chen Shen,Tianhang Zhou,Zhenghao Wu*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: VecTree-RAG combines vector search for corpus-level document ranking with reasoning-guided tree navigation for evidence localization within papers, outperforming Dense RAG, RAPTOR, and Search-o1 on QASPER, LitQA2, and MOSAIC benchmarks while providing structurally traceable retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional RAG systems address scientific QA by similarity search over fixed-length passages, which flattens document structure and separates scientific claims from their methodological/argumentative context. Two distinct retrieval problems—identifying relevant papers and locating supporting evidence within them—require specialized solutions rather than a single retrieval mechanism.

Method: VecTree-RAG is an agentic framework with three components: (1) vector search for ranking compact document and section representations across the corpus, (2) reasoning-guided traversal of source-verified section trees to localize evidence within shortlisted papers, and (3) a page store that retains full text exposed progressively only after structural localization.

Result: On QASPER (300 questions): 0.800 LLM-judge correctness, evidence-page precision 0.274 (vs. 0.046–0.071 baselines). On LitQA2 (54 questions): 0.925 accuracy. On MOSAIC (49 multi-document questions): 0.547 composite score. LitQA2 ablations showed the full vector–tree architecture required fewer inference tokens than variants without tree navigation or corpus-level vector routing.

Conclusion: Vector retrieval narrows the corpus-level search space while tree navigation concentrates reading on structurally relevant evidence. VecTree-RAG provides a structure-aware, traceable architecture for scientific literature QA, though multi-turn inference remains more expensive than single-call retrieval.

Abstract: Scientific question answering requires a retrieval system to solve two distinct problems: identifying which papers are relevant and locating the supporting evidence within those papers. Conventional retrieval-augmented generation typically addresses both through similarity search over fixed-length passages, flattening document structure and separating scientific claims from their methodological and argumentative context. We present VecTree-RAG, an agentic framework that assigns these tasks to complementary retrieval mechanisms. Vector search ranks compact document and section representations across the corpus, whereas reasoning-guided traversal of source-verified section trees localizes evidence within shortlisted papers. Full text is retained in a page store and exposed progressively only after structural localization. We evaluate VecTree-RAG on 300 QASPER questions, an open-access subset of 54 LitQA2 questions, and 49 multi-document MOSAIC questions. Compared with Dense RAG, reranked Dense RAG, RAPTOR, and Search-o1, VecTree-RAG obtained the highest observed answer score on all three benchmarks, reaching 0.800 LLM-judge correctness on QASPER, 0.925 accuracy on LitQA2, and a 0.547 composite score on MOSAIC. On QASPER, its evidence-page precision was 0.274, compared with 0.046--0.071 for the baselines. LitQA2 ablations further showed that the complete vector--tree architecture required fewer inference tokens than variants without tree navigation or corpus-level vector routing. These results indicate that vector retrieval narrows the corpus-level search space and tree navigation concentrates reading on structurally relevant evidence. Although multi-turn inference remains more expensive than single-call retrieval, VecTree-RAG provides a structure-aware and traceable architecture for scientific literature question answering.

</details>


### [93] [EGR: Embedding-Native Generative Retrieval with a Shared LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23038)
*Xiaodong Liu,Congfei Zhang,Hsiang-wei Chao,Siman Wang,Tong Zhao,Xiao Bai,Vincent Zhang,Jingxiao Ma,Zhe Liu,Wenfeng Zhuo,Zichu Li,Jitin Krishnan,Yunzhi Zhou,Yajun Wang,Jinchao Li,Yu Zhang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: EGR is an embedding-native generative retrieval framework that uses a single shared LLM to jointly learn item and user representations in one dense embedding space, outperforming semantic-ID and two-tower baselines with a +2.91% production conversion lift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative retrieval methods for recommendation and advertising suffer from two key limitations: (1) semantic-ID approaches require quantization, mutable codebooks, and heuristic token-to-item grounding; (2) embedding-based pipelines train the item encoder separately from the query generator, leading to suboptimal user-item alignment. A unified framework is needed to simplify system design while improving retrieval quality.

Method: EGR employs a single shared LLM to encode both items (from metadata) and users (from interaction histories) into the same dense embedding space. Items are directly indexed as dense vectors; user histories are encoded as dense retrieval queries. Training uses joint contrastive learning that groups related items together and aligns user queries with their target items, all within one unified model.

Result: EGR outperforms published baselines on Amazon Reviews; on Snap DPA, it scales with increasing data volume, effectively handles cold-start items, and benefits from multimodal input features. In live production deployment, EGR achieves a +2.91% conversion-rate lift.

Conclusion: EGR demonstrates that a unified embedding-native generative retrieval approach simplifies system architecture while simultaneously improving both retrieval quality and downstream advertising performance in real-world, large-scale deployments.

Abstract: Generative retrieval is increasingly popular in large-scale recommendation and advertising systems, yet current methods introduce practical complications. Semantic-ID methods rely on quantization, mutable identifier vocabularies, and token-to-item grounding; embedding-based pipelines train the item encoder separately from the query generator, which limits user-item alignment. We propose EGR, an Embedding-Native Generative Retrieval framework for recommendation and advertising. EGR uses a single shared LLM to learn item representations from item metadata and user representations from interaction histories in one embedding space. Items are indexed directly as dense vectors, and user histories are encoded as dense retrieval queries. Joint contrastive training groups related items and aligns queries with their target items. We evaluate EGR on public benchmarks, industrial data, and live deployment. EGR outperforms published baselines on Amazon Reviews; on Snap DPA, it scales with data, handles cold-start items, and benefits from multimodal input. In production, EGR delivers a +2.91% conversion-rate lift, simplifying system design while improving retrieval quality and ad performance.

</details>


### [94] [SMART: LLM-Augmented Hybrid Retrieval for Dynamic Product Ads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23121)
*Congfei Zhang,Jingxiao Ma,Xiaodong Liu,Hsiang-wei Chao,Siman Wang,Ge Liu,Shantanu Aggarwal,Vincent Zhang,Meghana Missula,Rachel Liao,Zichu Li,Xiao Bai,Yunzhi Zhou,Yajun Wang,Zhe Liu,Jinchao Li,Yu Zhang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: SMART is a retrieval system for Dynamic Product Ads that decomposes retrieval into rule-based keyword queries for retargeting and LLM-generated queries for prospecting, using a lightweight quality gate to route only ~10% of users to the costly LLM path, achieving a 90% cost reduction and +27.6% ad conversion improvement at Snap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic Product Ads require balancing retargeting (known interests) and prospecting (new categories) from massive catalogs. LLMs capture semantic intent well but are too expensive for large-scale deployment and suffer from lexical mismatch. The key insight is that these two objectives have fundamentally different retrieval requirements, suggesting a decomposed approach.

Method: SMART (SeMantic-aware Adaptive ReTrieval) decomposes retrieval into two paths: (1) rule-generated queries on a lexical BM25 index for retargeting, and (2) LLM-generated queries on a dense ANN index for prospecting. A lightweight quality gate detects coverage gaps in the initial keyword results and adaptively routes only ~10% of users to the LLM path, controlling inference costs while preserving semantic gains.

Result: Offline evaluation showed the gated approach captures most semantic prospecting gains in Relevance Score while maintaining competitive retargeting performance at 90% LLM cost reduction. A 2-week online A/B test at Snap demonstrated a +27.6% improvement in ad conversion rate over a strong embedding-based baseline.

Conclusion: The decomposition of retrieval into rule-based retargeting and LLM-based prospecting, combined with adaptive gating, provides a cost-effective and high-performing solution for large-scale Dynamic Product Ads. The approach is validated both offline and in production at Snap.

Abstract: Dynamic Product Ads (DPA) require retrieving relevant items from multi-million product catalogs, balancing two competing objectives: retargeting (re-surfacing known interests) and prospecting (discovering new categories). While Large Language Models (LLMs) capture semantic intent better than traditional embedding models, deploying them at scale introduces prohibitive inference costs and lexical mismatch issues. Through controlled experiments on millions of users, we demonstrate a critical retrieval decomposition: rule-generated queries excel at retargeting on a lexical BM25 index, while LLM-generated queries excel at prospecting on a dense ANN index. Building on this, we propose SMART (SeMantic-aware Adaptive ReTrieval). To manage costs, a lightweight quality gate identifies coverage gaps in initial keyword results, adaptively routing only the ~10% of users who benefit from semantic prospecting to the LLM path. Offline evaluation demonstrates that this gated approach captures the bulk of semantic prospecting gains in Relevance Score while maintaining competitive re-targeting performance at a 90% reduction in LLM costs. Finally, in a 2-week online A/B test at Snap, SMART improved the ad conversion rate by +27.6% over a strong embedding-based baseline.

</details>


### [95] [Statistically Supported LLM Ingredient and Recipe Data Collection in Computational Nutrition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23273)
*James Izzard,Hassan Eshkiki,Fabio Caraffini*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A quality-controlled LLM pipeline is proposed for acquiring structured ingredient data, using repeated sampling, robust statistical estimation, invariant checks, and a web-fetch fallback to achieve high accuracy (98.4% exact match on nutrient flags, 10.1% median absolute percentage error on ratios) at ~$1/ingredient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current nutritional databases are incomplete, inconsistent, and designed for human lookup rather than automated computation. LLMs could fill gaps but single-pass outputs are unreliable and introduce silent errors downstream.

Method: The pipeline treats repeated LLM queries as samples from a model-induced answer distribution and applies robust point estimators with normalized confidence scores across five data types (numerical, Boolean, multiple-choice, open categorical, optional integer). An invariant guard layer enforces nutritional and logical self-consistency. Minor numeric violations are reconciled via a linear program minimizing worst-case percentage deviation while preserving semantic zeros; major violations trigger a web-evidence-grounded repair step, escalating to human review only as a last resort. A Heap's Law fit on 233 recipes demonstrates sub-linear unique-ingredient growth.

Result: On a curated 30-ingredient reference set, the pipeline achieved 98.4% exact match on nutrient flags and reduced median absolute percentage error on nutrient ratios from 31.9% (median-aggregated baseline) to 10.1% — a 21.8 percentage-point improvement — at approximately $1 per ingredient in API cost.

Conclusion: LLM-assisted database construction can be framed as a controlled data-engineering workflow where uncertainty is made operational rather than discarded, yielding high-accuracy structured ingredient data suitable for computational nutrition.

Abstract: Computational nutrition needs precise ingredient data, but current databases are incomplete, inconsistent, and built for human reference rather than automated reasoning. LLMs could help fill these gaps, but single-pass outputs are unreliable and can introduce silent errors into downstream computation. We present a quality-controlled LLM pipeline for ingredient data acquisition that combines robust statistical estimation, domain-specific invariant checks, and a web-fetch fallback. An illustrative Heap's Law fit to 233 recipes suggests that unique-ingredient growth is sub-linear and front-loaded: the projected ratio of unique ingredients to recipes falls from 1.74 at 100 recipes to 0.19 at 5,000. For each ingredient attribute, repeated LLM queries are treated as samples from a model-induced answer distribution, and we apply robust point estimators and normalised confidence scores across numerical, Boolean, multiple-choice, open categorical, and optional integer types. An invariant guard layer enforces nutritional and logical self-consistency within each ingredient record. Minor numeric inconsistencies are reconciled via a linear program that minimises worst-case percentage deviation while preserving semantic zeros, and major violations are escalated to web-evidence-grounded repair, then human review only if that fails. On a curated 30-ingredient reference set, the pipeline achieves 98.4% exact match on nutrient flags and cuts median absolute percentage error on nutrient ratios from 31.9% for the median-aggregated baseline to 10.1%, a reduction of 21.8 percentage points, at an API cost of about $1 per ingredient. This frames LLM-assisted database construction as a controlled data-engineering workflow that makes uncertainty operational rather than discarding it.

</details>


### [96] [Choosing a Text Embedding Model: A Practical Benchmarking and Decision Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23507)
*Madhav S Baidya*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This report presents a practical framework for selecting text embedding models, benchmarking a commercial API model (T3EM) against open-source alternatives across diverse retrieval and MTEB tasks, while also addressing indexing, chunking, and deployment considerations beyond raw scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embedding model selection is critical yet often under-examined in retrieval system design. Leaderboard rankings rarely reflect real-world suitability, and practitioners lack a holistic framework that accounts for the full retrieval pipeline — including indexing, chunking, latency, cost, and deployment constraints — rather than evaluating models in isolation.

Method: The authors conduct a benchmarking study comparing T3EM (a commercial API-based embedding model) against a broad set of open-source alternatives on English retrieval tasks. Results are contextualized within the MTEB landscape (classification, clustering, semantic similarity, reranking, pair classification, bitext mining, summarization). The study further traces the entire retrieval pipeline: embedding production, indexing/scaling, and document chunking strategy, to assess how each component influences retrieval quality.

Result: The report yields a consolidated set of practical, evidence-based recommendations for embedding model selection tailored to specific task requirements, latency budgets, cost profiles, and deployment scenarios — demonstrating that optimal model choice depends on pipeline-wide considerations rather than benchmark scores alone.

Conclusion: Embedding model selection must be a reasoned, pipeline-aware decision. Practitioners should evaluate models not solely on leaderboard performance but within the context of chunking strategy, indexing architecture, inference latency, operational cost, and deployment constraints to achieve optimal retrieval outcomes.

Abstract: Choosing the right text embedding model is one of the most consequential -- and most frequently under-examined -- decisions in building a retrieval or search system, yet the model that tops a leaderboard is rarely the best choice for a given deployment. This report develops a practical, evidence-based framework for embedding model selection, built on a benchmarking study that evaluates T3EM (Text 3 Embedding Model), a commercial API-based embedding model, against a broad set of open-source alternatives on English-language retrieval tasks, and situates these findings within the wider Massive Text Embedding Benchmark (MTEB) landscape spanning classification, clustering, semantic similarity, reranking, pair classification, bitext mining, and summarization. Beyond raw benchmark scores, the report traces the full path from embedding model to retrieved result -- how embeddings are produced, how they are indexed and searched at scale, and how document chunking strategy shapes retrieval quality -- so that model choice can be reasoned about as one decision within a complete retrieval pipeline rather than in isolation. The result is a consolidated set of practical recommendations for selecting an embedding model according to task, latency, cost, and deployment constraints.

</details>


### [97] [Towards a Relevance Posterior in Neural Information Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23561)
*Andrew Parry,Emmanouil Georgios Lionis,Debasis Ganguly,Sean MacAvaney*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper argues for decomposing relevance into query-independent document priors and query-dependent likelihoods, framing modern retrieval systems as approximate posterior inference. This decomposition enables shifting computation offline, and experiments show that a learned document prior improves both first-stage retrieval and downstream LLM re-ranking via simple score fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current retrieval systems overwhelmingly treat relevance as a monolithic query-time score, ignoring the long-standing probabilistic tradition of document and query priors. As neural rankers grow computationally expensive and pipelines expand to multi-stage ranking, recommendation, and RAG, this single-pass query-time scoring view becomes impractical and conceptually limiting.

Method: The authors extend classical probabilistic retrieval formalisms (e.g., Robertson-Spärck Jones, language modeling) to contemporary learned systems, proposing a likelihood-prior decomposition where relevance emerges from combining query-dependent likelihoods with query-independent document priors. Empirically, they train a learned document prior and integrate it via score fusion with existing first-stage retrievers and re-rankers (including RankZephyr), without any architecture changes or additional query-time computation beyond a linear combination.

Result: The learned prior improves first-stage retrieval through rank fusion: up to 0.046 nDCG@10 on TREC DL-2019 and 0.029 nDCG@10 on TREC DL-2020. Downstream re-ranking also benefits, with the largest gains for the LLM re-ranker RankZephyr (up to 0.054 nDCG@10 on TREC DL-2020).

Conclusion: Explicitly decomposing relevance into priors and likelihoods offers both practical efficiency benefits (shifting computation offline) and improved effectiveness. The authors call for retrieval systems that deliberately allocate modeling capacity between offline document priors and online query-dependent interaction, connecting this perspective to broader information access problems.

Abstract: Modern information retrieval systems typically operationalise relevance as a query-conditional score computed at inference time. This design choice has become dominant such that alternative decompositions of relevance are rarely discussed, despite the long history of document and query priors in probabilistic retrieval and large-scale search. As neural ranking models grow more computationally expensive and retrieval pipelines expand to include multi-stage ranking, recommendation, and retrieval-augmented generation, this monolithic view of query-time scoring becomes increasingly limiting. We argue that modern information access systems are more naturally understood as performing approximate posterior inference, in which relevance is refined through a staged combination of query-dependent likelihoods and query-independent priors. We extend classical probabilistic retrieval formalisms to contemporary learned systems and show how explicit likelihood-prior decomposition exposes new opportunities to shift computation offline while disentangling document-level and interaction-level beliefs. We present empirical evidence that incorporating query-independent document utility can complement existing rankers and improve effectiveness with minimal query-time computation (solely score fusion). Concretely, a learned prior improves first-stage retrieval through rank fusion (up to 0.046 nDCG@10 on TREC DL-2019 and 0.029 nDCG@10 on TREC DL-2020) and also improves downstream re-ranking, with the largest gains observed for the LLM re-ranker RankZephyr (up to 0.054 nDCG@10 on TREC DL-2020). Finally, we discuss how this decomposition connects to broader information access and outline research directions for designing retrieval systems that explicitly allocate modelling capacity between offline priors and online interaction.

</details>


### [98] [Melo: A Production LLM-Powered Music Recommendation Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23718)
*Shijia Wang,Da Guo,Qiang Xiao,Fanghui Bi,Weisheng Li,Dongjing Wang,Chuanjiang Luo*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Melo is a production LLM-powered music recommendation agent on NetEase Cloud Music that uses a deterministic state graph with entity grounding and reflective retry mechanisms to handle hallucination and long-tail degradation, achieving significant lifts in playlist retention and engagement metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-powered recommendation agents at industrial scale suffer from two critical failure modes: entity hallucination (where the agent commits to catalog-unsupported interpretations) and long-tail degradation (where over-constrained requests collapse to generic popular fallbacks). The core insight is that the bottleneck is not the intelligence of the LLM itself, but the system's ability to detect and recover from the LLM's mistakes.

Method: Melo is structured as a deterministic five-node state graph over heterogeneous tools, orchestrated by prompts and a state machine rather than a fine-tuned controller. Two complementary mechanisms are introduced: (1) Inference-time entity grounding, which repurposes the production search index as a verification primitive to gate entity decisions before they propagate downstream; (2) Reflective retry, which verbalizes failure reasons from broken tool chains and feeds them back into the next planning step, enabling constraint relaxation or revision instead of blind fallback.

Result: A one-month online A/B test on NetEase Cloud Music's playlist surfaces showed over 2 pp lift in a primary playlist retention metric and over one minute lift in a core playlist engagement metric. Offline ablation revealed a 7.8 pp reduction in entity misidentification from the three-layer grounding stack, and triggered-session analysis showed reflective retry firing on 5.8% of sessions with 59% process-level recovery.

Conclusion: Progress on LLM-powered music recommendation at industrial scale depends as much on named, ablatable runtime machinery that catches and corrects the LLM's mistakes as on the LLM itself. The authors offer this as a hypothesis for the community to test.

Abstract: We describe Melo, an LLM-powered music recommendation agent deployed on NetEase Cloud Music. Melo is structured as a deterministic five-node state graph over heterogeneous tools, with a prompt- and state-machine-driven orchestration policy rather than a fine-tuned controller. At industrial scale, the bottleneck is not how smart the brain is but how the system detects and recovers from the mistakes that brain makes. Two production failure modes drove the design: entity hallucination, where the agent commits to interpretations unsupported by the live catalog or user-behavior index, and long-tail degradation, where over-constrained requests collapse to generic popular fallbacks. We address them with two complementary mechanisms. Inference-time entity grounding repurposes the production search index as a verification primitive that gates entity decisions before they propagate downstream. Reflective retry verbalizes failure reasons from a broken tool chain and feeds them into the next planning step, so the system can relax or revise constraints rather than fall back blindly. A one-month online A/B test across NetEase Cloud Music's playlist surfaces reports an over 2 pp lift in a primary playlist retention metric and a lift of over one minute in a core playlist engagement metric. Offline ablation isolates a 7.8 pp reduction in entity misidentification from the three-layer grounding stack on our evaluation set, and a triggered-session analysis on our evaluation set shows reflective retry firing on 5.8% of sessions with 59% process-level recovery. Our deployment experience suggests that progress on LLM-powered music recommendation at this scale depends as much on the named, ablatable runtime machinery that catches and corrects the brain's mistakes as on the brain itself: a hypothesis we offer for the community to test.

</details>


### [99] [Breaking the Loop: An Empirical Comparison of Strategies for Novelty and Freshness in YouTube Music](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23749)
*Srivaths Ranganathan,Zihuan Diao,Bernardo Cunha,Joshua L. Moore,Robin Dumas,Murat Goksedef,Yanwei Song,Mukai Lu,Gergo Varady,Tracy Pesin*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: YouTube Music tested six debiasing interventions across four layers (serving, training, architecture, exploration) via online A/B tests. Serving-time fixes are neutralized by continuous training feedback loops; architectural debiasing improves diversity but not discovery; SNGP-based uncertainty exploration yields the best new-release lift, though with engagement tradeoffs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuously trained music ranking models suffer from feedback loops where past consumption dominates future recommendations, suppressing both temporal freshness (new releases) and novelty (unlistened catalog items). While many academic interventions exist, live industrial systems introduce challenges—continuous content ingestion, interconnected components, and practical constraints—that undermine academic findings. The authors aim to empirically evaluate which intervention layers are effective under real-world conditions.

Method: The authors conducted off-policy online A/B tests on the YouTube Music homepage, evaluating six individual interventions and one combination experiment across four conceptual layers: (1) serving-time heuristics, (2) training-data reweighting, (3) architectural debiasing, and (4) uncertainty-driven exploration via a Spectral-normalized Neural Gaussian Process (SNGP) head. Candidate generation and upstream components remained fixed; only the ranking model or serving layer consuming its scores was modified.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Serving-time interventions are neutralized over time by the continuous training feedback loop. (2) Architectural debiasing reduces popularity dominance and improves diversity metrics but does not drive meaningful discovery; it also carries hidden integration costs. (3) SNGP-based uncertainty exploration produced the largest lift in new-release recommendations, though with a measurable tradeoff in engagement or diversity.

Conclusion: The authors recommend selecting intervention layers based on specific objectives, with each layer carrying distinct hidden costs. They advise against relying on serving-time fixes in continuously trained systems, caution that architectural debiasing alone is insufficient for discovery, and endorse uncertainty-driven exploration for boosting new-release exposure despite its engagement tradeoffs.

Abstract: Continuously trained ranking models in music recommenders fall into feedback loops where previously consumed items dominate recommendations. This suppresses two distinct content classes: new releases (temporal freshness) and unlistened catalog items (novelty). Industry practitioners have a wide menu of interventions available, ranging from serving-time heuristics, training-data reweighting, architectural debiasing, to uncertainty-driven exploration, each of which are well understood in academic settings. But live systems offer challenges with continuously ingested content, interconnected components, and practical limitations that counteract the findings from academic research.
  We report results from off-policy online A/B tests for six interventions and a combination experiment across four conceptual layers (serving, training, architecture, exploration) on the YouTube Music homepage. All interventions modify the ranking model or the serving layer that consumes its scores; candidate generation and other upstream components are held fixed. We discuss key takeaways from our results: first, serving-time interventions on continuously trained systems are neutralized by the learning loop. Second, architectural debiasing reduces popularity dominance and improves diversity but does not create discovery, while carrying hidden integration costs. Finally, uncertainty-driven exploration interventions with a Spectral-normalized Neural Gaussian Process (SNGP) head produce the largest new-release lift, though they come with a measurable engagement or diversity tradeoff. We close with recommendations on which layer to intervene at, and the hidden costs of each choice.

</details>


### [100] [Escaping the Euclidean Void: Manifold-Informed Flow Matching for Sequential Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23762)
*Dengzhao Fang,Jingtong Gao,Yu Li,Xiangyu Zhao,Yi Chang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: MIRAGE addresses the "Euclidean void" problem in flow-matching-based sequential recommendation, where straight-line interpolation paths cross regions devoid of valid item semantics. It uses an item co-occurrence graph to rectify the embedding geometry during training, aligning path states with local anchors while preserving the original probability path for efficient one-step inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow-matching generative recommenders synthesize items via continuous trajectories, but item catalogs are discrete and sparse. Straight Euclidean paths between noise and target embeddings can traverse regions with no valid item support—the "Euclidean void"—degrading generation quality. Existing methods lack mechanisms to ensure interpolated states remain semantically grounded.

Method: MIRAGE introduces a Manifold-Informed Rectification framework that uses an item co-occurrence graph as a proxy for the semantic manifold. During training, it aligns interpolated path states with local graph anchors, reorganizing the embedding space to keep trajectories within valid item support. Crucially, it retains the original straight probability path and applies the graph only at training time, enabling accurate one-step inference without graph dependency.

Result: Extensive experiments on four real-world datasets show MIRAGE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, particularly boosting performance on sparsely observed target items while maintaining robust overall accuracy.

Conclusion: MIRAGE effectively resolves the Euclidean void by rectifying embedding geometry via manifold-informed training, delivering superior sequential recommendation performance with efficient one-step generation.

Abstract: Conventional recommenders capture users' preferences by optimizing observed user-item relations, whereas continuous generative recommendation additionally learns the trajectory of synthesizing a target item. Flow matching drives this process by gradually shaping initial noise into a definitive next-item representation through intermediate states in a continuous embedding space. However, item catalogs are discrete and sparsely supported, meaning even a straight Euclidean path can cross continuous regions that contain little evidence of valid item semantics. Formalizing this failure as the Euclidean void, we propose MIRAGE, a Manifold-Informed Rectification framework for Accelerated Generation of Embeddings in sequential recommendation, which rectifies the learned embedding geometry around an unchanged straight probability path. By leveraging an item co-occurrence graph as a proxy for the underlying semantic manifold, MIRAGE aligns interpolated path states with local anchors, reorganizing the embedding space to ground the trajectory in valid item support. MIRAGE retains the original probability path and uses the graph only during training, thereby enabling accurate and efficient one-step inference. Extensive experiments on four real-world datasets reveal that MIRAGE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, effectively boosting performance on sparsely observed targets while achieving robust overall accuracy. Our code will be made publicly available upon publication.

</details>


### [101] [ClawRec: A Claw-Native Recommender System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23779)
*Chenghao Wu,Kesha Ou,Xiaolei Wang,Bowen Zheng,Bingqian Li,Enze Liu,Wayne Xin Zhao,Weitao Li,Long Zhang,Sheng Chen,Ji-Rong Wen*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: ClawRec introduces a new recommender system paradigm where recommendations span multiple platforms via a persistent personal agent ("claw"), producing unified, non-redundant slates. It outperforms baselines significantly on a new benchmark, ClawRec-SimBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing recommender systems are confined to single platforms with isolated interaction traces and candidate spaces, making them unsuitable for real-world tasks that involve cross-platform searches, content consumption, and comparisons. The emergence of claw-style personal agents with persistent cross-platform access creates an opportunity for user-centric, multi-source recommendation.

Method: ClawRec maintains an evidence-linked, temporally structured user state that connects cross-platform behaviors with cross-source recommendations. Retrieval is organized around functional source roles, and candidates are selected by marginal utility to produce non-redundant slates aligned with the user's active task. Evaluation uses ClawRec-SimBench, a benchmark built from concrete life events and cross-platform behavior trajectories.

Result: ClawRec achieves NDCG@20 of 0.6134 (+0.1126 over baselines) and Hit@20 of 0.6944 (+0.0854), while also improving user state quality and temporal alignment.

Conclusion: The claw-native recommender system paradigm, instantiated by ClawRec, demonstrates substantial gains over platform-local approaches, validating the benefits of cross-platform, user-centric recommendation with temporally structured state and marginal utility-based slate construction.

Abstract: Recommender systems have become integral to navigating the modern digital ecosystem. Yet most deployed systems remain confined within single-platform boundaries, observing localized interaction traces and ranking items from isolated candidate spaces. This design is poorly suited to real-world tasks that unfold through searches, content consumption, and comparisons across multiple information sources. Claw-style personal agents, with persistent access to authorized cross-platform context, create an opportunity for recommendation to operate around the user rather than any single platform. In this paper, we introduce Claw-native recommender systems, a new paradigm that moves beyond platform-local ranking to produce unified, complementary recommendation slates spanning diverse sources and content forms. To instantiate this paradigm, we present ClawRec, the first recommender system designed to operate natively in this environment. ClawRec maintains an evidence-linked, temporally structured user state that connects cross-platform behaviors with cross-source recommendations. It organizes retrieval around functional source roles and selects candidates according to their marginal utility, producing non-redundant slates aligned with the user's active task. To enable rigorous evaluation, we introduce ClawRec-SimBench, a benchmark constructed from sequences of concrete life events and cross-platform behavior trajectories. Experiments show that ClawRec outperforms the strongest baselines, achieving an NDCG@20 of 0.6134 (+0.1126) and a Hit@20 of 0.6944 (+0.0854), while also improving user state quality and temporal alignment. Our code and dataset are available at https://github.com/RUCAIBox/ClawRec.

</details>


### [102] [Who Gets Named: Citation Type Predicts Individual Naming by Grounded Language Models, and a Roster Instrument Captures 0.5% of It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23893)
*Dmitrij Żatuchin*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether AI models recommend individual professionals (e.g., real estate agents, insurance brokers) rather than firms, using 2,400 API calls across four models, four European markets, and five languages. Models named individuals in 25.8% of responses, with substantial variation by category, model, and language—suggesting roster-based measurement of AI visibility captures only a small, unrepresentative slice of model behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior AI brand visibility research measures firm-level recommendations and reputational tracking. This study shifts the granularity one level down to individual professionals in person-selection categories (real estate, car dealerships, insurance), addressing the gap in understanding whether and how AI models surface named individuals rather than companies.

Method: The study issued 2,400 grounded API calls during a single two-hour window (24 July 2026) using 120 buyer-intent prompts across four models (GPT-5.6 Sol, Gemini 3.6 Flash, Perplexity Sonar Pro, Grok 4.5), five iterations each, spanning four European markets and five query languages. Responses were coded for individual naming via a rule cascade (never consulting a roster), with geo-disambiguation to exclude same-named American cities (precision 96.9%, recall 61.7%). Inference corrects for within-prompt clustering (ICC 0.258, effective n=407). A 939-person LinkedIn roster was used for comparison.

Result: Models named an individual in 25.8% of responses. Category dominated variance: real estate 35.4%, car dealerships 32.9%, insurance 9.1% (χ²=159.3, p=5.8e-8). Model rates varied four-fold (Grok 38.0% to Gemini 9.3%). Citation type predicted naming: named responses cited the individual's own site 2.6 points more (95% CI +1.4 to +3.9) and category portals 4.3 points more, while firm-owned page citation was equivalent. English prompts named individuals at 36.7% vs. 15.6% for local-language equivalents (OR=3.14, clustered p=0.074). Roster matching captured only 0.47% of name-shaped mentions (128/27,293), covering 26 of 939 people, yielding roster-derived rates of 0.0%–25.4%.

Conclusion: Individual AI visibility varies dramatically by category, model, and language. Roster-based measurement captures a tiny and unrepresentative fraction of model naming behavior, rendering it inadequate for measuring individual professional visibility in AI systems. The lower-bound nature of all reported rates (due to imperfect recall) and the clear directionality of language effects are key methodological takeaways.

Abstract: Prior work on AI brand visibility measures the firm: does a model recommend a company, and does that track its reputation. This study asks the question one level down, in categories where the buyer picks a person. It issued 2,400 grounded API calls in one two-hour window on 24 July 2026: 120 buyer-intent prompts, four models (GPT-5.6 Sol, Gemini 3.6 Flash, Perplexity Sonar Pro, Grok 4.5), five iterations each, four European markets and five query languages. Every response was coded for whether it named an individual professional, by a rule cascade that never consults a roster and that drops detections resolving to a same-named American city (precision 96.9%, recall 61.7%, so every rate below is a lower bound). All inference corrects for clustering within prompt: intraclass correlation 0.258, effective n 407 against a nominal 2,400. Models named an individual in 25.8% of responses. Category dominates: real estate 35.4% and car dealerships 32.9% against insurance 9.1% (chi-square 159.3, p = 5.8e-8 after correction). Models differ four-fold, from Grok 38.0% to Gemini 9.3%. Citation type predicts naming and citation volume does not: naming responses cite the individual's own site 2.6 points more often (95% CI +1.4 to +3.9) and category portals 4.3 points more often, and cite firm-owned pages at the same rate (44.1% against 45.5%). On nine matched translation pairs, English prompts named an individual in 36.7% of responses against 15.6% for the same question in the local language (OR 3.14, clustered p = 0.074, so the direction is clear and the design cannot close it). A 939-person roster built from public LinkedIn search matched 128 of 27,293 name-shaped mentions (0.47%), 26 of the 939 people were ever named, and the roster-derived rates of 0.0% to 25.4% measure that overlap. Roster-based measurement of individual AI visibility sees a small and unrepresentative slice of what models do.

</details>


### [103] [Energy Constrained Hierarchical Underwater Monitoring via Local Multi-Agent RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24313)
*Mohamed Amine Janati,Laurent Gautier,Stéphane Barbot*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A hierarchical underwater monitoring architecture pairs always-on ultra-low-power microcontrollers (MAX78000/MAX78002) with an on-demand NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX for local multimodal AI processing, embedding-based indexing, and automated reporting — minimizing energy and communication costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Marine life monitoring is constrained by strict energy budgets, poor underwater connectivity, and prohibitive transmission costs for raw multimodal data. Existing solutions lack an integrated approach that balances continuous low-power sensing with selective, high-performance local reasoning.

Method: A master–satellite hierarchical architecture: always-on MAX78000/MAX78002 MCUs perform continuous visual/acoustic sensing; an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX wakes for scheduled, event-driven, or researcher-triggered processing. The Jetson runs a fully local pipeline — data ingestion, visual extraction, BioCLIP/OpenCLIP embedding generation, ChromaDB-based indexing of mission data and references, a dedicated identification layer (visual similarity search, centroid classification, supervised classifiers), and a LangChain multi-agent framework for query routing, analysis, energy management, reconfiguration, and report generation.

Result: The architecture was evaluated through visual and acoustic monitoring case studies, demonstrating structured, researcher-ready knowledge output while compressing local data for flexible transmission (acoustic, optical, satellite).

Conclusion: The proposed system effectively bridges ultra-low-power continuous sensing with local multimodal intelligence, enabling underwater stations to produce actionable knowledge with significantly reduced energy use and communication overhead.

Abstract: Marine life monitoring is limited by strict energy constraints, poor underwater connectivity, and the high cost of transmitting raw multimodal data from remote deployments. This paper proposes a low-consumption underwater monitoring architecture that combines always-on edge sensing with selective high-performance local reasoning. The system follows a hierarchical master--satellite design in which ultra-low-power MAX78000/MAX78002 microcontrollers continuously monitor visual and acoustic signals, while an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX is activated only for scheduled processing, event-driven analysis, or researcher interaction. Once active, the Jetson executes a fully local multimodal pipeline for data ingestion, visual target extraction, embedding-based indexing, species identification, retrieval-augmented reasoning, and automated reporting. BioCLIP/OpenCLIP embeddings are used to organize mission data, marine taxonomic references, scientific documents, and operational metadata in local ChromaDB collections. A dedicated identification layer combines visual similarity search, centroid-based classification, and supervised classifiers to support adaptive species recognition. A LangChain-based multi-agent framework coordinates query routing, structured analysis, energy management, hardware reconfiguration, and report generation. The architecture is evaluated through visual and acoustic monitoring case studies. The proposed system bridges ultra-low-power continuous sensing with local multimodal intelligence, enabling underwater stations to produce structured, researcher-ready knowledge while compressing local data for flexible acoustic, optical, or satellite transmission, minimizing both energy use and communication overhead.

</details>


### [104] [MEMOIR: Temporal Behavioral Memory for Recommendation Across the Preference-Drift Spectrum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23986)
*Younggue Bae*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: MEMOIR generates LLM-based semantic behavioral memories from temporal windows of user interaction history. While statistically tied with UniSRec on aggregate metrics (NDCG@10 of 0.0643 vs 0.0641), the key finding is a drift-stratified performance pattern: MEMOIR excels at ranking quality (NDCG@10, MRR) for users with high and low preference drift, while UniSRec dominates middle-drift users and volume-oriented metrics across all strata.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sequential recommenders struggle to capture evolving user preferences over time. The authors aim to leverage LLMs to construct temporally-aware, semantically rich user representations that model preference evolution and future intent, rather than relying solely on interaction sequences.

Method: MEMOIR segments user interaction histories into temporal windows, uses an LLM to generate semantic behavioral memory for each period, and aggregates current state, evolution direction, and predicted future into a unified user representation. The training objective includes an evolution-preserving contrastive loss with a directional-consistency term. Evaluation is conducted on two Amazon Reviews 2023 categories against baselines including SASRec and UniSRec, with ablation of all three architectural components and stratified analysis by a composite preference-drift score.

Result: MEMOIR achieves ~18% relative gain over ID-based SASRec. Against UniSRec, aggregate results are statistically tied (NDCG@10: 0.0643 vs 0.0641), splitting four metrics 2-2. No single ablated component explains the gain — all ablations land within 2% of the full model. The critical finding emerges from drift stratification: MEMOIR leads ranking-quality metrics at high- and low-drift extremes, while UniSRec leads HR@10/HR@20 across all strata and ranking quality in the middle band.

Conclusion: The drift-stratified performance pattern is presented as MEMOIR's most substantive and reproducible finding, with aggregate near-ties and ablation results being inconclusive. Why this pattern holds — MEMOIR's advantage at drift extremes but not the middle — is left as an open question for future work.

Abstract: We propose MEMOIR, a framework that segments user interaction histories into temporal windows, generates semantic behavioral memory for each period using an LLM, and aggregates current state, evolution direction, and predicted future into a single user representation. On the Electronics and Clothing_Shoes_and_Jewelry categories of Amazon Reviews 2023, MEMOIR is statistically tied with UniSRec, the strongest baseline, on aggregate NDCG@10 (0.0643 vs. 0.0641), splitting the four reported metrics 2-2: MEMOIR leads NDCG@10 and MRR, UniSRec leads HR@10 and HR@20. An ablation study finds that no single architectural component - the evolution-preserving contrastive loss, its directional-consistency term, or temporal window segmentation itself - individually explains much of MEMOIR's approximately 18% relative gain over ID-based SASRec; all four ablations land within 2% of the full model on aggregate NDCG@10. Stratifying test performance by a composite preference-drift score instead reveals where the gain concentrates: MEMOIR leads on ranking-quality metrics (NDCG@10, MRR) specifically among users at the high- and low-drift extremes of the distribution, while UniSRec leads the volume-oriented HR@10/HR@20 metrics across all drift strata and edges out MEMOIR on ranking quality in the middle band. We report this drift-stratified pattern, rather than the near-tied aggregate numbers or any single ablated component, as MEMOIR's most substantive and reproducible finding, and surface why it holds as an open question for future work.

</details>


### [105] [Mosaic: A Fleet of User Embedding Specialists for Recommendation at Meta](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24015)
*John Zhiyuan Zheng,Xian Sun,Xiangyang Mou,Yujunrong Ma,Christina You,Michael Jiayuan He,Hrishikesh Paranjape,Aakarsha Agarwal,Hong Li*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Mosaic is a foundational user modeling platform that uses a fleet of architecturally diverse specialist models—each capturing distinct facets of user behavior—combined with novel multi-task training techniques (MRM, CRL) and a logging-free evaluation framework (CoEval, User Tower Zero-Out) to achieve consistent offline and online gains in industrial recommendation systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior industrial user representation approaches rely on either a single model or a shared backbone with task-specific heads, which limits the diversity and complementarity of user behavior signals captured. The authors identify an opportunity to maximize marginal information contribution by employing multiple architecturally heterogeneous specialists, each optimized for a distinct behavioral facet, and to improve evaluation velocity through logging-free embedding assessment.

Method: Mosaic employs a fleet of four specialist model families: memorization-driven, dense-heavy, sequential-based, and CoTrain models. Two key training techniques are introduced: MRM (Multi-task Relations Mining) and CRL (Cosine Redundancy Loss), designed to maximize each specialist's marginal information contribution and minimize representational overlap. For evaluation, CoEval and User Tower Zero-Out provide a logging-free embedding evaluation framework. The serving infrastructure uses a hybrid CPU/GPU online-and-offline stack, allowing each specialist to independently select its serving strategy based on freshness, latency, and compute constraints.

Result: Mosaic delivers consistent and significant offline NE (Normalized Entropy) improvements, along with measurable online gains in production recommendation systems.

Conclusion: A fleet-of-specialists approach to user modeling, guided by techniques that explicitly manage inter-model redundancy and evaluated via logging-free methods, offers a scalable and high-leverage path for improving industrial recommendation performance across multiple downstream tasks.

Abstract: User representation is one of the highest-leverage modeling problems in industrial recommendation systems: a single advancement in how users are encoded can propagate across retrieval, ranking, and integrity tasks at platform scale. Prior industrial user representation work builds either a single user model that emits one or more embedding vectors or a shared backbone with task-specific adaptation. In this paper, we present Mosaic, a foundational user modeling platform that employs a fleet of specialists to learn user embeddings. The fleet comprises four architecturally diverse model families - memorization-driven, dense-heavy, sequential-based, and CoTrain models - each focusing on a distinct facet of user behavior. We developed MRM (Multi-task Relations Mining) and CRL (Cosine Redundancy Loss) techniques to maximize the marginal information contribution of each new specialist. We also introduce CoEval and User Tower Zero-Out, new logging-free embedding evaluation framework that improves development velocity while preserving downstream-aligned accuracy. Our hybrid CPU/GPU, online-and-offline serving stack allows each specialist to choose the adequate serving strategy to meet the freshness, latency, and computational requirements. Mosaic delivers consistent and significant offline NE improvements in addition to online gains.

</details>


### [106] [SpecFormer: Mitigating Embedding and Attention Collapse via Spectral-Aware Transformer for Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24025)
*Yu Cui,Yi Xu,Jiahao Wang,Hao Zhang,Yu Zhang,Xiaoyi Zeng,Can Wang,Jinxin Hu,Jiawei Chen*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Standard Transformers underperform in recommendation due to spectral collapse from heterogeneous, long-tail data. SpecFormer mitigates this via learnable spectral softening, spectrum-softened attention, and spectral residual position encoding, achieving SOTA results and successful industrial deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Transformer self-attention often yields suboptimal performance in recommendation systems, sometimes worse than simpler models. The authors identify the root cause as severe embedding and attention collapse driven by the heterogeneity and long-tail nature of recommendation data, which creates a vicious cycle in forward/backward propagation that limits scaling with depth.

Method: SpecFormer introduces three components: (1) a Learnable Spectral Softening module that dynamically smooths the singular value distribution of input token embeddings; (2) a Spectrum-softened Attention mechanism that models feature interactions under a more uniform spectral distribution; and (3) Spectral Residual Position Encoding based on Taylor expansion of singular values to inject spectral inductive bias.

Result: Extensive experiments on one industrial and two public datasets show SpecFormer significantly outperforms SOTA baselines. It has been deployed in a real-world commercial recommender system and demonstrates exceptional scaling: stacking more layers actively improves attention effective rank and recommendation performance.

Conclusion: The spectral collapse problem fundamentally limits Transformers in recommendation. SpecFormer effectively addresses this by operating in the spectral domain, breaking the vicious cycle, and enabling deeper architectures that improve rather than degrade performance.

Abstract: Transformer architectures have achieved remarkable success across diverse domains; however, directly applying their standard self-attention mechanism to recommendation often yields suboptimal performance, sometimes even trailing behind well-designed simple recommendation models. In this paper, we reveal that this performance bottleneck stems from severe embedding and attention collapse unique to recommendation scenarios. The heterogeneity and long-tail nature of recommendation data lead to a severe spectral collapse dominated by a few principal singular values. We further theoretically demonstrate that this triggers a vicious cycle in recommendation model's forward and backward propagation, which accelerates embedding and attention collapse and limits the model's scaling capability with increased depth. To address these issues, we propose SpecFormer, a novel Spectral-Aware Transformer designed for mitigating embedding and attention collapse in recommendation. Specifically, SpecFormer introduces 1) a Learnable Spectral Softening module to dynamically smooth the singular values distribution of the input token embeddings; 2) a Spectrum-softened Attention mechanism to model feature interaction under a more uniform spectral distribution space; 3) a Spectral Residual Position Encoding via Taylor expansion of singular values, explicitly providing a spectral inductive bias for feature interactions. Extensive experiments on one industrial and two public datasets demonstrate that SpecFormer significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. Notably, SpecFormer has been successfully deployed in a real-world commercial recommender system and exhibits exceptional scaling capabilities: stacking SpecFormer layers actively improves the attention effective rank and recommendation performance.

</details>


### [107] [ConAlign: Conditional Alignment Framework for Balancing Biased and Unbiased Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24092)
*Jingcheng Zhang,Yihan Wang,Qi Song,Liyin Hong*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: ConAlign is a conditional debiasing framework for industrial recommender systems that uses a discrete gating mechanism to selectively transfer knowledge from a biased tower to an unbiased tower, balancing factual accuracy with unbiased preference estimation while supporting streaming adaptation. It is the first streaming debiasing framework successfully deployed at scale, validated via offline experiments and online A/B testing on Kuaishou.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing debiasing methods for industry recommender systems, which rely on unbiased uniform data, suffer from two key limitations: (1) they neglect factual (biased) recommendation performance, and (2) they incur substantial computational overhead. These shortcomings prevent practical industrial deployment and fail to address the core problem of filter bubbles that collapse user interests and degrade long-term engagement.

Method: ConAlign introduces a discrete gating-based conditional alignment mechanism operating between two towers: a biased tower (trained on observational data) and an unbiased tower (trained on a small fraction of uniform random traffic). Rather than universally correcting all predictions, the gating mechanism selectively intervenes, transferring knowledge from the biased tower to the unbiased tower only when beneficial. This selective intervention paradigm balances factual accuracy and unbiased preference estimation while enabling real-time streaming adaptation.

Result: Offline experiments on three real-world datasets rigorously validate the framework's effectiveness. Large-scale online A/B testing on Kuaishou demonstrates significant improvements in long-term user engagement and interest diversity, all while incurring negligible latency overhead.

Conclusion: ConAlign is the first streaming debiasing recommendation framework successfully deployed in a large-scale industrial system. It demonstrates that conditional, selective alignment — rather than universal debiasing — is a practical and effective strategy for mitigating filter bubbles, improving long-term engagement, and maintaining interest diversity in industrial recommender systems.

Abstract: Industry recommender systems trained on observational data suffer from various biases that create filter bubbles, causing user interests to collapse into narrow categories and severely degrading long-term engagement. While utilizing unbiased uniform data for debiasing has shown promise, existing methods remain impractical for industrial deployment due to limitations such as neglect of factual (biased) recommendation performance and the substantial computational overhead. To overcome these limitations, we propose ConAlign (Conditional Alignment Framework), a conditional debiasing approach for industrial deployment. The key innovation of ConAlign lies in a discrete gating-based conditional alignment mechanism that selectively transfers knowledge from the biased tower to the unbiased tower. Following a selective intervention paradigm rather than universal correction, it seamlessly balances factual accuracy and unbiased preference estimation while supporting real-time streaming adaptation. To the best of our knowledge, ConAlign is the first streaming debiasing recommendation framework successfully deployed in a large-scale industrial recommendation system that utilizes a small fraction of unbiased random traffic for debiasing. Extensive offline experiments on three real-world datasets rigorously validate the effectiveness of our proposed framework. Furthermore, large-scale online A/B testing on Kuaishou demonstrates significant improvements in long-term user engagement and interest diversity, with negligible latency overhead.

</details>


### [108] [Strategy-Aware Parameter-Efficient Adaptation for LLM-based Auto-Bidding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24232)
*Songyue Cai,Lianyu Wang,Shan Gu,Ziru Xu,Jian Xu,Xiaofeng Zhu,Bo Zheng*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: SAGE is a parameter-efficient framework that aligns trajectory and text modalities using LLMs for auto-bidding, achieving superior performance while fine-tuning under 10% of parameters compared to full fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based auto-bidding methods suffer from shallow trajectory-text interactions and require costly full fine-tuning, limiting efficient use of pretrained knowledge under diverse bidding constraints.

Method: SAGE uses three components: (i) position augmentation with temporal-semantic positional embeddings for capturing dynamics; (ii) text alignment via gated cross-attention for multi-modal fusion while reducing long-trajectory overhead; (iii) constraint-gated LoRA that routes constraints to activate sparse expert subsets, enabling efficient adaptation of a frozen LLM.

Result: SAGE achieves superior performance on large-scale auto-bidding benchmarks while tuning less than 10% of the trainable parameters required by full fine-tuning. Ablation studies confirm each component's critical contribution.

Conclusion: SAGE effectively addresses the efficiency and alignment limitations of prior LLM-based auto-bidding approaches through its parameter-efficient multi-modal alignment framework, demonstrating both strong performance and practical deployability.

Abstract: Advertising bidding has evolved from manual strategies to auto-bidding systems better adapted for large-scale, dynamic auction environments. While recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer strong reasoning for auto-bidding, existing methods suffer from shallow trajectory-text interactions and require costly fine-tuning, hindering the efficient use of pretrained knowledge under diverse constraints. To address these challenges, we propose SAGE, a novel Strategy-aware Auto-bidding framework Guided by LLMs for Efficient bidding. SAGE introduces a parameter-efficient multi-modal alignment framework for constrained auto-bidding with LLMs. Specifically, SAGE comprises three key components: (i) the position augmentation module adopts temporal-semantic positional embeddings to effectively capture the intrinsic dynamics and semantic structures; (ii) the text alignment module leverages gated cross-attention to align the embedding spaces of trajectory and text modalities, enabling effective multi-modal fusion while alleviating the computational overhead caused by long trajectories; (iii) the constraint-gated LoRA module employs constraints as routing signals, activating only a small subset of experts to adapt the behavior of a frozen LLM efficiently. Extensive experiments on large-scale auto-bidding benchmark demonstrate that SAGE consistently achieves superior performance while tuning less than 10% of the trainable parameters required by full fine-tuning. Ablation studies further validate the critical contribution of each component to the framework's overall performance.

</details>


### [109] [A Model-Driven Pipeline for Data Quality Specification and Operationalization: A No-Code Approach for Domain Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24245)
*Arno Kesper,Lukas Sebastian Hofmann,Markus Matoni,Gabriele Taentzer*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper presents a pipeline and supporting tools (QPM metamodel and Constrainify web application) that enable domain experts to formalize and operationalize data quality constraints into reusable, repeatable, domain-specific quality analyses, addressing the gap between domain knowledge and technical implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-quality data is critical, especially in domains like cultural heritage where manual curation leads to inconsistencies. Domain experts understand quality expectations but lack the technical skills to formalize them into executable quality analyses, creating a dependency on data engineers that is time-consuming and technically demanding.

Method: The authors propose a pipeline for formalizing and operationalizing data quality constraints, supported by QPM (a metamodel for defining reusable quality analysis templates) and the Constrainify web application, which allows tailoring templates to conceptual requirements and translating them into executable analyses via model-driven engineering subpipelines.

Result: The approach produces a set of reusable, repeatable, and domain-specific quality analyses, enabling domain experts to participate more directly in data quality assessment without deep technical expertise.

Conclusion: By bridging the gap between domain experts' conceptual understanding and the technical execution of quality analyses, the proposed pipeline and tools reduce dependencies on data engineers, streamline the quality analysis process, and improve data quality in domains like cultural heritage.

Abstract: High-quality data is essential for reliable analysis, decision-making, and research across domains. This is especially relevant in areas such as cultural heritage, where data is collected and curated manually, making it prone to quality issues like inconsistencies. To improve data quality, the data must be analyzed regularly using systematic quality analyses. Quality analyses validate the conformance of data to domain-specific expectations. These expectations are best understood by domain experts, who can express them using natural language. However, they rarely possess the technical expertise to formalize these expectations into executable quality analyses. Consequently, this process requires domain experts and data engineers, making it time-consuming and technically demanding. The required technical expertise and the resulting dependencies pose a significant challenge. To address this challenge, we present a pipeline for formalizing and operationalizing data quality constraints. We support this pipeline using QPM, a metamodel for defining templates for reusable quality analyses. The web application Constrainify enables tailoring templates to specific conceptual requirements and translating them into executable quality analyses via a tool-chain based on model-driven engineering subpipelines. The result is a set of reusable, repeatable, and domain-specific quality analyses.

</details>


### [110] [OxygenREC-v2: Internalizing Discrimination into Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24255)
*Guo Tang,Hanye Wu,Changjiang Han,Qingyang Li,Ming Zhang,Xiangyu Qian,Yanchen Qiao,Huanjie Wang,Zhi Ma,Zhen Li,Yaqiang Zang,Pinghua Gong*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: OxygenREC-v2 is a generative recommendation model that internalizes behavior signals (clicks, cart additions, orders) into a single unified backbone using behavior-conditioned generation and entropy-aware trajectory optimization, eliminating the need for separate discriminative objectives or reward models. Deployed as a 3B-parameter MoE on JD.com, it achieved 1.6–4.4% UCTCVR lift and 2.8–6.8% GMV lift over its predecessor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative recommendation systems struggle to reliably incorporate multi-behavior signals (clicks, add-to-cart, orders). Jointly optimizing generative and discriminative objectives requires delicate trade-offs, while using a separate ranker as a post-hoc RL reward risks out-of-distribution scoring and reward misalignment. A method is needed that integrates discriminative behavior signals directly into the generative process without auxiliary objectives or external reward models.

Method: OxygenREC-v2 introduces IDGR (Internalizing Discrimination into Generative Recommendation) with two training stages: (1) Pre-training with behavior instructions that condition autoregressive SID generation on target behavior types. (2) Post-training via entropy-aware trajectory optimization self-distillation, where future interaction behaviors serve as privileged knowledge to guide policy optimization without a reward model. Both stages share a single unified backbone, implemented as a 3B-parameter Mixture-of-Experts with 1B activated parameters.

Result: In online A/B tests on JD.com's e-commerce platform, OxygenREC-v2 outperformed OxygenREC-v1 with a 1.6–4.4% improvement in User Click-Through Conversion Rate (UCTCVR) and a 2.8–6.8% improvement in Gross Merchandise Volume (GMV).

Conclusion: OxygenREC-v2 demonstrates that internalizing discrimination directly into generative recommendation—via behavior-conditioned generation and reward-model-free policy optimization—is both feasible and effective at large scale, yielding significant real-world business metric improvements while maintaining architectural simplicity with a single unified backbone.

Abstract: Generative recommendation unifies retrieval and ranking within a single model by autoregressively decoding semantic identifier (SID) sequences. Yet reliably incorporating behavior signals from clicks, cart additions, and orders remains challenging. Existing approaches either jointly optimize generative and discriminative objectives, requiring delicate trade-offs, or use a separate ranker as a post-hoc reinforcement-learning reward, risking out-of-distribution scoring and reward misalignment. We propose OxygenREC-v2, a generative recommender that Internalizes Discrimination into Generative Recommendation (IDGR). Rather than adding a separate discriminative objective, OxygenREC-v2 uses logged behavior to condition generation and supervise training. During pre-training, a behavior instruction conditions generation on the target behavior. During post-training, future interaction behaviors are exploited as privileged knowledge in our entropy-aware trajectory optimization self-distillation framework, enabling reward-model-free policy optimization. Throughout both training stages, OxygenREC-v2 maintains a single unified backbone. We implement OxygenREC-v2 as a 3B-parameter, 1B-activated MoE and deploy it on JD.com's large-scale e-commerce platform. Across multiple online A/B tests, OxygenREC-v2 improves user click-through conversion rate (UCTCVR) by 1.6--4.4% and GMV by 2.8--6.8% over OxygenREC-v1.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [111] [Spectral Dynamics of Semantic Drift in Clinical Multi-Agent Language Model Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22758)
*Amritesh Banerjee*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper mathematically disproves the assumption that scale-free and small-world networks (BA, WS) are efficient for multi-agent LLM diagnostic systems, showing they cause catastrophic semantic drift, hallucination amplification, and irreversible entropy saturation. It proposes spectral graph monitoring to enforce topological stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Iterative LLM-based multi-agent diagnostic systems commonly adopt scale-free or small-world communication topologies under the untested assumption of optimal efficiency. The authors seek to rigorously evaluate whether these network structures actually preserve semantic fidelity in medical contexts, where patient safety is paramount.

Method: The authors map multi-agent communication uncertainty trajectories into a 768-dimensional Bio_ClinicalBERT embedding space using an analytical isotropic variance proxy. They simulate message passing over Barabási–Albert (scale-free) and Watts–Strogatz (small-world) networks, compute phase transition matrices, and derive terminal entropy saturation (H∞ ≈ 5.947), cosine similarity degradation (53.29%), and variance amplification ratios (ρ). They contrast these against Erdős–Rényi random graphs and propose dynamic spectral monitoring via continuous eigen-decomposition of the graph Laplacian, enforcing a strict lower bound λ₂ₘᵢₙ at O(N³) complexity.

Result: Key quantitative findings: (1) localized dense cliques trap hallucinated data, preventing global consensus and driving entropy to a permanent saturation of H∞ ≈ 5.947; (2) terminal cosine similarity degrades by 53.29%, obliterating the original ground-truth; (3) highly clustered architectures exhibit variance amplification ρ = 1.5181 (51.81% increase) versus ρ = 1.0766 for Erdős–Rényi; (4) hub-centric systems autonomously compound rather than reduce localized hallucinations.

Conclusion: Topological stability is a non-negotiable quantitative imperative for autonomous medical diagnostics. Rather than improving reliability, hub-centric networks inherently amplify errors. The proposed spectral monitoring framework—enforcing a lower bound on algebraic connectivity—provides a mathematically rigorous safeguard to ensure global state diffusion and diagnostic safety.

Abstract: The integration of iterative LLMs within multi-agent diagnostic frameworks requires a rigorous quantitative reevaluation of underlying communication topologies. Frequently used architectural paradigms depend on scale-free or small-world networks, assuming optimal communication efficiency. Our study mathematically dismantles that assumption for semantic data. By mapping multi-agent communication uncertainty trajectories onto a 768-dimensional Bio_ClinicalBERT embedding space via an analytical isotropic variance proxy using Barab'asi--Albert (BA) and Watts--Strogatz (WS) networks, we prove that structural bottlenecks compromise diagnostic safety. Our phase transition matrices illustrate that localized dense cliques confine hallucinated data, preventing global consensus and forcing the system toward a permanent entropy saturation threshold of $H_{\infty} \approx 5.947$.
