As a result, we measure a severe terminal cosine similarity degradation of 53.29%, completely overwriting the original ground-truth. Moreover, the terminal semantic drift reveals a catastrophic variance amplification of 51.81% ($ρ= 1.5181$) in highly clustered architectures, proving total system unpredictability when compared to Erdős--R'enyi configurations ($ρ= 1.0766$). Instead of reducing errors, hub-centric systems autonomously compound localized hallucinations. By introducing dynamic spectral monitoring operating at an $\mathcal{O}(N^3)$ time complexity and imposing a strict lower bound on algebraic connectivity ($λ_{2_{min}}$) via the continuous eigen-decomposition of the graph Laplacian, we present a mathematically rigorous technique to ensure global state diffusion. Securing the reliability of autonomous medical diagnostics necessitates treating topological stability as a non-negotiable quantitative imperative.

</details>


### [112] [Emergent Behaviour in Financial Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23311)
*Omar Inverso,Emilio Tuosto,Dragisa Zunic*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper identifies and structures sources of complexity in emergent systems, using electronic financial markets as a case study, and outlines a research program for formal specification and automated analysis of market mechanisms without prematurely committing to specific technical frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emergent behavior in complex/collective systems poses significant challenges for automated reasoning. The formal methods community has growing interest in tackling these systems, but the diverse sources of complexity are not well-structured. Financial markets serve as a concrete, high-stakes domain where computational support for analyzing emergent phenomena is urgently needed.

Method: The authors adopt a problem-structuring approach rather than a solution-evaluating one. They identify, categorize, and structure the sources of complexity inherent in electronic financial markets that must be addressed for automated analysis. They then survey and discuss possible alternatives for handling key technical aspects while maintaining domain fidelity, deliberately avoiding simplifying assumptions that would distort the phenomenon under study.

Result: The paper produces a structured taxonomy of complexity sources relevant to formal analysis of emergent market behavior, and maps out a systematic research agenda covering specification and analysis challenges. It does not endorse specific tools but highlights trade-offs and directions for the formal methods community.

Conclusion: A systematic, domain-grounded approach is needed for the formal methods community to meaningfully engage with emergent phenomena in financial markets. Rather than rushing to apply existing tools with oversimplifying assumptions, researchers should first understand the structural sources of complexity and then develop or adapt techniques accordingly.

Abstract: Some properties of so-called complex or collective systems can be observed to emerge from the interactions of elementary agents. This phenomenon, known as emergent behaviour, has long since been studied in the most diverse disciplines, with recent growing awareness from the formal methods community about the opportunity of opening up to seemingly distant disciplines with appropriate technology for computer-aided reasoning. Different peculiar elements of complexity make automated reasoning on these systems particularly challenging. We consider electronic financial markets to drive our discussion. We identify and structure the sources of complexity to tackle in order to provide computational support for the analysis of emergent phenomena. We refrain from evaluating the suitability of specific technical solutions or frameworks of preference, which would as usual require simplifying assumptions and divert from the actual phenomenon of interest. Rather, we elaborate on possible alternatives to handle some of the main technical aspects involved in automated analysis, while retaining a solid and concrete interpretation of the domain, and in doing so outline a more systematic research program for the formal specification and analysis of market mechanisms.

</details>


### [113] [MulRobBench: A Decision-Level Benchmark for Safe and Security-Policy-Compliant Multimodal UAV Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23870)
*Belal S. Alsinglawi,Weizheng Wang,Junyi Wu,Yi Jiang,Lianhai Lin,Merouane Debbah,Izzat Alsmadi*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: MulRobBench is a benchmark for evaluating Vision-Language-Action (VLA) UAV agents in smart-city environments, integrating multimodal observations, security policies, and cyber-physical safety. Across 17 models, best scores are low (0.5141 semantic, 0.1599 strict accuracy), revealing decision instability from modality trust, constraint extraction, glare, missing data, and operator shorthand.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing UAV and multimodal benchmarks evaluate perception, navigation, collaboration, and reasoning in isolation, but fail to assess whether physical evidence, protocol constraints, and action risk remain coupled during critical operational decisions. Smart-city UAVs must act as cyber-physical decision makers under degraded observations and ambiguous language, requiring evaluation that integrates protocol-level security policies with action-level safety.

Method: The authors introduce MulRobBench, an offline, protocol-conditioned benchmark comprising 3,024 samples across 17 task taxonomy nodes and 12 scoring dimensions. Evaluation spans four stages: operational context understanding, multimodal evidence arbitration, degradation-aware reasoning, and risk-aware action planning. Scoring combines semantic scoring with structural diagnostics (policy compliance, format compliance, unsafe actions, parsing failures, dimension-level validity). A controlled 20-anchor modality-ablation study isolates the influence of visual versus textual inputs on action selection.

Result: Across 17 multimodal models, the best semantic protocol-decision score is 0.5141 and the best strict mean scoring-dimension accuracy is 0.1599. The ablation study changed 4–15 action selections per model, confirming both visual and textual inputs influence decisions. Primary causes of decision instability identified: modality-trust selection, constraint extraction failures, glare, missing data, and operator shorthand.

Conclusion: MulRobBench provides a reproducible benchmark for trustworthy multimodal UAV decision making under realistic operational constraints, highlighting significant performance gaps in current VLA models and identifying key failure modes that must be addressed for safe autonomous UAV operation in smart-city airspace.

Abstract: Smart-city airspace is transforming Uncrewed Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) from passive sensing platforms into cyber-physical decision makers that must follow operational rules under degraded observations and ambiguous language. Existing UAV and multimodal benchmarks evaluate perception, navigation, collaboration, and reasoning, but few assess whether physical evidence, protocol constraints, and action risk remain coupled during critical decisions. We introduce MulRobBench, an offline, protocol-conditioned benchmark for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) UAV agents in smart-city environments. MulRobBench integrates real UAV multimodal observations, protocol-level security policies, and action-level cyber-physical safety into a unified evaluation framework. The benchmark contains 3,024 samples spanning 17 task taxonomy nodes and 12 scoring dimensions across four stages: operational context understanding, multimodal evidence arbitration, degradation-aware reasoning, and risk-aware action planning. Evaluation combines semantic scoring with structural diagnostics, including policy compliance, format compliance, unsafe actions, parsing failures, and dimension-level validity. Across 17 multimodal models, the best semantic protocol-decision score reaches only 0.5141, while the best strict mean scoring-dimension accuracy is 0.1599. A controlled 20-anchor modality-ablation study changes 4-15 action selections per model, confirming that both visual and textual inputs influence decisions. Analysis identifies modality-trust selection, constraint extraction, glare, missing data, and operator shorthand as the primary causes of decision instability. MulRobBench provides a reproducible benchmark for trustworthy multimodal UAV decision making under realistic operational constraints.

</details>


### [114] [A Comparative Study of MCP and A2A for Inter-Agent Coordination in LLM-Based Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23884)
*Ionut Predoaia,Tuong Manh Vu,Konstantinos Barmpis,Dimitris Kolovos,Antonio García-Domínguez*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A comparative implementation study of MCP and A2A protocols for LLM-based multi-agent coordination reveals MCP offers lower complexity but requires application-layer state management, while A2A provides richer native abstractions for stateful coordination at the cost of greater implementation complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid emergence of heterogeneous, tool- and LLM-mediated agentic systems in industry has surfaced practical questions about inter-agent coordination and protocol design, with no clear empirical guidance on the trade-offs between emerging protocols like MCP and A2A.

Method: The authors conduct an implementation-grounded comparison by building both an MCP-based and an A2A-based multi-agent system for the same software engineering task, then evaluate them against a requirement set (agent discoverability, multi-part messaging, multi-turn conversations, asynchronous communication, observability, interoperability, access control) derived from literature and industry partner discussions.

Result: MCP supports inter-agent coordination with lower coordination complexity and a lightweight implementation model, but requires explicit application-layer implementation for conversational state and task lifecycle management. A2A offers richer native support for stateful, multi-turn coordination via protocol-level task/lifecycle abstractions, but incurs substantially greater implementation and coordination complexity.

Conclusion: The findings are framed as design observations from an experience report rather than general claims of protocol superiority. The paper highlights how protocol abstractions shape the distribution of coordination responsibilities in agentic systems, emphasizing trade-offs over broad recommendations.

Abstract: Recent industry practice has seen the rapid emergence of agentic systems composed of heterogeneous, tool- and LLM-mediated agent components, raising practical questions about inter-agent coordination and protocol design. This paper presents an implementation-grounded comparison of the Model Context Protocol (MCP) and the Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol, from a multi-agent systems engineering perspective, using an inter-agent coordination scenario involving LLM-based agents. We evaluate an MCP-based and an A2A-based multi-agent implementation of the same software engineering task against a set of requirements derived from prior literature and discussions with industry partners, including agent discoverability, multi-part messaging, multi-turn conversations, asynchronous communication, observability, interoperability, and access control. The results evidence that MCP can support inter-agent coordination in constrained LLM-based systems through a comparatively lightweight implementation model with lower coordination complexity, although coordination concerns such as conversational state management and task lifecycle handling must be implemented explicitly at the application layer. In contrast, A2A provides richer native support for stateful, multi-turn coordination through protocol-level abstractions for tasks and lifecycle management, but this comes with substantially greater implementation and coordination complexity. Given the narrow scope of the evaluated coordination pattern, these findings are presented as design observations from an empirical experience report rather than general claims of protocol suitability or superiority across broader classes of MAS, highlighting trade-offs and how protocol abstractions shape the distribution of coordination responsibilities in contemporary agentic systems.

</details>


### [115] [Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23982)
*Dane Malenfant*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Dialogue Moral Hazard Game, a text-based testbed for studying cooperative behavior in language agents under hidden-action conditions. Evaluating seven open-weight models, the authors find that base models rarely engage in costly, socially beneficial information sharing. Diagnostic fine-tuning and prompt optimization produce heterogeneous effects, sometimes improving team success without recovering the intended cooperative mechanism, highlighting the need for mechanism-level evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperation often fails when effort is costly, weakly observable, and benefits others more than oneself. Language agents increasingly deployed in multi-agent settings face analogous hidden-action problems, yet existing evaluations focus on aggregate outcomes rather than whether agents faithfully execute cooperative mechanisms. The authors aim to bridge this gap by operationalizing Holmström's team moral-hazard framework as a controlled language-agent testbed.

Method: The authors design the Dialogue Moral Hazard Game, where an agent chooses between preserving a local reward or paying a query cost to reveal hidden safety information that aids another agent's decision. They evaluate seven open-weight language models across six behavioral metrics: query use, realized information transfer, local-reward preservation, unsafe choice, format validity, and team success. Four update mechanisms are applied diagnostically: supervised fine-tuning (SFT), RLOO, sequential SFT+RLOO, and GEPA prompt optimization.

Result: Base models predominantly preserve local reward without achieving team success, or query without communicating decision-relevant information. Among update mechanisms, OLMo-7B exhibits the clearest mechanism-consistent weight-level improvement. GEPA prompt optimization sometimes increases team success while paradoxically reducing or eliminating costly queries, indicating that optimization can shift aggregate reward without recovering the intended cooperative mechanism.

Conclusion: Aggregate team success alone is an insufficient metric for evaluating cooperative behavior in language agents. Optimization can produce surface-level improvements that bypass rather than restore the intended cooperative mechanism. The authors advocate for mechanism-level behavioral reporting in multi-agent language model evaluations to ensure that cooperation is genuine rather than accidental.

Abstract: Cooperation can fail when socially valuable effort is costly, weakly observable, and mainly benefits others. Drawing on Holmström's team moral-hazard model, we introduce the Dialogue Moral Hazard Game, a controlled textual game that operationalizes this hidden-action structure for language agents. In each episode, an agent can preserve an immediate local reward or pay a query cost to reveal a hidden safety fact that primarily helps another agent's downstream decision. We evaluate seven open-weight language models and decompose behavior into query use, realized information transfer, local-reward preservation, unsafe choice, format validity, and team success. Base models commonly preserve local reward without team success or query without communicating information that changes the final decision. We then use supervised fine-tuning, RLOO, sequential SFT+RLOO, and GEPA prompt optimization as diagnostic update mechanisms. Their effects are heterogeneous: OLMo-7B shows the clearest mechanism-consistent weight-level improvement, whereas GEPA sometimes improves team success while reducing or eliminating costly queries. Thus, optimization can shift aggregate reward without recovering the intended cooperative mechanism, motivating evaluations that report mechanism-level behavior rather than team success alone.

</details>


### [116] [Decentralised Consensus Learning Networks: SME Rotation Without Centralised Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24416)
*Florin Neagu*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A decentralised multi-agent learning framework replaces centralised reward with peer-validated consensus, dynamically assigning SME status by competence rank. Across 84 simulations (30–10,000 agents), expertise rotation is robust and scale-invariant, while high-dimensional vector beliefs trigger regime shifts culminating in expertise concentration on a single agent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Centralised reward signals impose an external, monolithic definition of what constitutes correct or valuable knowledge. The authors seek to replace this paradigm with a decentralised system where expertise and knowledge validation emerge endogenously from peer consensus rather than prescribed rewards.

Method: A consensus-based multi-agent learning framework where agents update beliefs via weighted social consensus. Trust is allocated based on peer-consistency-derived competence (not ground truth). Subject-matter expert status is dynamically conferred as a top-percentile competence rank. The framework is tested across 84 simulation runs varying agent count (30–10,000), graph topologies, belief representations (scalar/vector), dimensionality (D=1–500), and includes multi-seed robustness and sensitivity analyses.

Result: Phase 1: 90–100% of agents attain SME status; SME rotation is robust, topology- and scale-invariant; most expertise turnover occurs post-convergence. Phases 2–3: Vector beliefs produce heterogeneous convergence with cascade dynamics and five distinct dynamical regimes as dimensionality increases. At D=150–200, stable partial consensus emerges while expertise concentrates on a single agent. ETA sensitivity analysis confirms dimensionality, not noise, drives this concentration.

Conclusion: In complex, high-dimensional consensus spaces, the agent most consistently aligned with the collective belief naturally emerges as the recognised expert — an emergent property of decentralised learning rather than an externally designated role.

Abstract: Centralised reward signals dominate modern AI learning systems, but they impose a single external definition of correct or valuable knowledge. We present a decentralised, consensus-based multi-agent learning framework in which expertise emerges through peer validation rather than prescribed reward. Agents update beliefs via weighted social consensus, while trust is allocated according to competence inferred from peer consistency instead of ground truth. Subject-matter expert (SME) status is assigned dynamically as a top-percentile competence rank rather than a fixed label.
  We evaluate the framework across 84 simulation runs spanning 30 to 10,000 agents, multiple graph topologies, sparse large-scale networks, scalar and vector belief representations, dimensionality sweeps (D=1-500), multi-seed robustness tests, and parameter sensitivity analyses. Phase 1 shows that SME rotation is robust, persistent, topology-invariant, and scale-invariant: 90-100% of agents attain SME status, with most expertise turnover occurring after belief convergence and increasing with network size. Phases 2 and 3 show that vector beliefs introduce heterogeneous convergence with cascade dynamics and reveal five distinct dynamical regimes as belief dimensionality increases. At high dimensionality (D=150-200), the network reaches stable partial consensus while expertise becomes increasingly concentrated in a single agent. ETA sensitivity analysis demonstrates that this concentration is driven by belief dimensionality rather than stochastic noise. We interpret this behaviour as an emergent property of decentralised learning: in complex high-dimensional consensus spaces, the agent most consistently aligned with the collective belief naturally emerges as the recognised expert.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [117] [Semalith v1.4: A Calibrated 184M Safety Classifier Achieving State-of-the-Art Prompt-Injection Detection at 44x Fewer Parameters than Llama-Guard-3-8B](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22545)
*Tejasvi C. Addagada*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Semalith v1.4 is a compact 184M-parameter DeBERTa-v3 classifier that performs simultaneous three-axis safety classification (prompt injection, general harm, and financial regulatory compliance) in a single forward pass, outperforming Llama-Guard-3-8B on prompt injection and achieving zero false positives on benign agentic prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing open guardrails cannot simultaneously handle prompt injection, regulatory compliance, and general harm detection in a single inference pass — a critical gap for deploying LLMs in financial-services and agentic settings.

Method: A 184M-parameter DeBERTa-v3-base model with a 22-class head (BENIGN, 9 prompt-injection subtypes, general-harm, 11 BFSI labels) and a 4-class auxiliary super-category head trained under jointly weighted loss. The training corpus comprises 76,204 rows from 49 public sources, with SHA-1 deduplication against all held-out evaluation sets to ensure near-zero contamination (21/22 benchmarks at 0%, max 0.22%).

Result: Semalith v1.4 wins all 7/7 prompt-injection evaluations and 11/18 benchmarks overall against Llama-Guard-3-8B, with 44x fewer parameters. It achieves FPR=0.000 on 208 benign agentic prompts (vs. 0.063 for Llama-Guard-3). Llama-Guard-3 leads on general-harm benchmarks (WildGuardMix, HEx-PHI, HarmBench). Six weak spots are disclosed.

Conclusion: The paper recommends v1.3 for conversational moderation (ToxicChat F1 0.624) and v1.4 when BFSI label coverage or zero-FPR on benign agentic prompts is prioritized, with the complementary strengths versus Llama-Guard-3 suggesting potential ensemble deployment.

Abstract: Deploying large language models in financial-services and agentic settings requires safety classifiers that simultaneously handle prompt injection, regulatory compliance, and general harm, a combination no existing open guardrail addresses in a single inference pass.
  Semalith v1.4 is a 184M-parameter DeBERTa-v3-base classifier performing simultaneous three-axis safety classification including prompt injection, general harm, and financial-services regulatory compliance, in a single forward pass. Its 22-class head (BENIGN, nine prompt-injection sub-types, general-harm, eleven BFSI labels) is trained with a 4-class auxiliary super-category head under jointly weighted loss, on a 76,204-row corpus mined from 49 public sources with SHA-1 deduplication against every held-out evaluation set, with 21 of 22 benchmarks at zero contamination (max 0.22%).
  Against Llama-Guard-3-8B on 22 held-out benchmarks, Semalith v1.4 wins every prompt-injection evaluation (7/7) and 11 of 18 benchmarks overall at 44x fewer parameters, with FPR = 0.000 on 208 benign agentic prompts vs 0.063 for Llama-Guard-3-8B. On general-harm benchmarks (WildGuardMix, HEx-PHI, HarmBench), Llama-Guard-3 leads; this complementary split is documented in Section 4. Six measured weak spots are disclosed in Section 6.
  Deployment guidance: v1.3 is recommended for conversational moderation deployments (ToxicChat F1 0.624); v1.4 is recommended when BFSI label coverage or zero-FPR on benign agentic prompts is the priority.

</details>


### [118] [CORVUS: Context Optimization and Reduction Via Underlying Synchronization for LLM Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22711)
*Mingwei Zheng,David OBrien,Siwei Cui,Pardis Pashakhanloo,Rajdeep Mukherjee,Myeongsoo Kim,Sachit Kuhar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CORVUS proposes a trajectory architecture for LLM coding agents that decouples file-read actions from their observations by maintaining a synchronized file registry, injecting current file contents at each reasoning cycle. This eliminates stale snapshots and redundant re-reads, reducing token usage by 9-50%, shortening prompts by 15-32%, and cutting reasoning cycles by up to 37% while maintaining pass rates on SWE-bench evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional append-only trajectory architectures tightly couple file-read actions with their observations as fixed snapshots in chronological history. As files are modified (by the agent or concurrently by humans), these snapshots become stale, leading to reasoning errors and forcing redundant re-reads that bloat the trajectory with duplicate copies. The authors identify this as a fundamental structural inefficiency in existing LLM coding agent designs.

Method: CORVUS decouples file-read actions from their observations by maintaining a synchronized registry of all relevant files. Instead of storing file contents as historical snapshots, the architecture injects only the current contents of each file at every reasoning cycle. This ensures the trajectory remains lightweight and synchronized with the actual codebase state by construction, eliminating both stale snapshots and redundant file copies.

Result: Evaluated on SWE-POLYBENCH_VERIFIED and SWE-BENCH PRO across four LLMs, CORVUS achieved: 9-50% reduction in average input tokens per task, 15-32% shorter final prompts, up to 37% fewer reasoning cycles, while maintaining comparable pass rates to conventional append-only trajectories.

Conclusion: Decoupling file observations from read actions and injecting synchronized current file contents at each reasoning cycle is an effective architectural improvement for LLM coding agents. CORVUS significantly reduces token consumption and reasoning cycles without degrading task performance, addressing a key scalability and correctness limitation in multi-step agent trajectories.

Abstract: LLM coding agents operate by constructing trajectories that accumulate reasoning, tool calls, and results to enable multi-step decision-making. However, the conventional append-only trajectory architecture found in practice tightly couples file-read actions with their observations, capturing snapshots that become permanently fixed in the chronological history. As files change through agent edits or concurrent human modifications, these snapshots become stale, causing reasoning errors and causing agents to redundantly re-read files, with each re-read appending yet another copy to the trajectory. To mitigate this, we propose CORVUS, a novel trajectory architecture that decouples file-read actions from their observations by maintaining a synchronized registry of relevant files and injecting only their current contents at each reasoning cycle. This structural change produces significantly lighter-weight trajectories that remain synchronized with the actual codebase state by construction, eliminating redundant file copies and stale snapshots that bloat conventional trajectories. We evaluated CORVUS on SWE- POLYBENCH_VERIFIED and SWE-BENCH PRO across four LLMs, achieving 9-50% reduction in average input tokens per task, 15-32% shorter final prompts, and up to 37% fewer reasoning cycles while maintaining comparable pass rates.

</details>


### [119] [CausalGate: Causal Importance Distillation for Transformer Module Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22720)
*Kiran Nair,Smriti Regmi,Rodrigue Rizk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CausalGate introduces an intervention-based framework that uses causal analysis (zeroing out sub-layers and measuring semantic damage via KL divergence) to determine which transformer modules to prune, then distills this into static scalar gates for zero-overhead inference — outperforming dynamic routing baselines on models up to 8B parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing adaptive inference methods for LLMs use correlation-based heuristics (e.g., hidden-state similarity, activation magnitudes) to drop redundant modules, but these metrics fail to capture subtle, non-linear structural computations critical for semantic accuracy. A causally grounded method is needed.

Method: CausalGate operates in two phases: (1) a calibration phase where individual Attention and MLP sub-layers are intervened upon (outputs zeroed out) and semantic damage is quantified via KL divergence on final logit distributions, establishing a structural importance hierarchy; (2) a distillation phase where this hierarchy is compressed into static, lightweight scalar gates using an Exponential Moving Average smoothing objective combined with a differentiable pairwise ranking loss, eliminating runtime routing overhead.

Result: Evaluated on TinyLlama-1.1B, Qwen2.5-3B, and Llama-3.1-8B across language modeling and commonsense reasoning benchmarks, CausalGate consistently outperforms prominent dynamic routing and layer-skipping baselines, translating theoretical compute savings into concrete hardware latency reductions with zero operational overhead.

Conclusion: CausalGate demonstrates that intervention-guided structural importance, distilled into static gates, provides a more accurate and efficient approach to adaptive transformer inference than correlation-based heuristics, achieving both superior semantic fidelity and measurable hardware speedups.

Abstract: Existing adaptive inference methods for Large Language Models rely on observational heuristics, such as hidden-state similarity or activation magnitudes, to drop redundant modules. However, these correlation-based metrics often fail to capture subtle, non-linear structural computations vital for semantic accuracy. We introduce CausalGate, an intervention-guided framework for compute-efficient transformer inference. During a calibration phase, CausalGate isolates individual Attention and MLP sub-layers, zeros out their respective outputs, and measures the exact semantic damage via the Kullback-Leibler divergence of the final logit distribution. To eliminate runtime routing overhead, this structural importance hierarchy is distilled into a global set of static, lightweight scalar gates using an Exponential Moving Average smoothing objective paired with a differentiable pairwise ranking loss. Evaluated on TinyLlama-1.1B, Qwen2.5-3B, and Llama-3.1-8B across language modeling and commonsense reasoning benchmarks, CausalGate consistently outperforms prominent dynamic routing and layer-skipping baselines, translating theoretical compute savings into concrete hardware latency reductions with zero operational overhead.

</details>


### [120] [Progress-conditioned Group Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22724)
*Kaibing Yang,Guangfeng Cai,Shengtian Yang,Shuo He,Yu Li,Mengyi Liu,Pengwei Chen,Jun Xu,Lei Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProGPO addresses the "credit trap" in group-based policy optimization for LLM agents, where repeated low-effect actions dominate sampling and zero-reward groups provide no learning signal. It breaks this loop by using first-visit observation coverage as a proxy reward when all trajectories in a group fail, prioritizing steps that visit new states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In long-horizon tasks, group-based policy optimization suffers from sampling imbalance: repeated or low-effect actions dominate high-probability policy regions, while state-changing actions remain under-sampled. This creates a self-reinforcing "credit trap" where failure-dominated sampling yields all-failed rollout groups with zero outcome rewards, providing no corrective signal, allowing poor actions to persist.

Method: ProGPO (Progress-conditioned Group Policy Optimization) augments group-based policy optimization with a fallback reward mechanism. When all samples in a group receive zero outcome reward, ProGPO assigns higher relative advantages to trajectories or steps that visit more new states, based on first-visit observation coverage. This leverages the intuition that reaching novel observations is a necessary prerequisite for eventual task success.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks using Qwen2.5-1.5B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct models show that ProGPO consistently improves over group-based baselines, with particularly large performance gains on hard tasks.

Conclusion: Progress-conditioned rewards via observation coverage effectively break the credit trap in group-based policy optimization, yielding substantial improvements on difficult long-horizon agentic tasks without requiring dense reward signals.

Abstract: Group-based policy optimization has been increasingly used to train large language model (LLM) agents from sparse outcome rewards by comparing trajectories or steps within a group. However, on difficult long-horizon tasks, this comparison can suffer from a sampling imbalance: repeated or low-effect actions dominate the high-probability region of the policy while useful state-changing actions remain under-sampled. This imbalance produces many all-failed rollout groups, where outcome rewards provide no direction for correcting the policy. Together, these effects can form a self-reinforcing credit trap: failure-dominated sampling yields no outcome-based correction, allowing repeated low-effect actions to persist. To break this loop, we propose Progress-conditioned Group Policy Optimization (ProGPO), which uses first-visit observation coverage only when all samples in a group receive zero outcome reward. Specifically, within such groups, ProGPO assigns higher relative advantages to trajectories or steps that visit more new states since reaching new observations is a prerequisite for task success. Experiments on two challenging agentic benchmarks, ALFWorld and WebShop with Qwen2.5-1.5/7B-Instruct, show that ProGPO consistently improves over group-based baselines, with particularly large gains on hard tasks.

</details>


### [121] [QFedPolyp: A Communication- and Inference-Efficient Federated Learning Framework for Polyp Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22743)
*Madan Baduwal,Priyanka Paudel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QFedPolyp is a federated learning framework that uses quantization-aware training and low-precision (8-bit) model communication to enable privacy-preserving polyp segmentation with ~4× reduced transmission costs and up to 1.5× faster inference, while maintaining Dice scores of 0.910–0.930.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Centralized deep learning for polyp segmentation requires sharing sensitive patient data across hospitals. Standard federated learning addresses privacy but incurs high communication costs from repeatedly transmitting full-precision (32-bit) model parameters. There is a need for a communication-efficient, privacy-preserving, and inference-efficient solution suitable for real-time clinical deployment.

Method: QFedPolyp combines quantization-aware training (QAT) with federated averaging. Each hospital client locally trains a lightweight U-Net on private data while simulating quantization during training. Clients then transmit low-precision (uniform 8-bit) quantized model parameters to a central server, where parameters are reconstructed and aggregated via Federated Averaging. Evaluation uses four datasets: Kvasir-SEG, CVC-ClinicVideoDB, PolypGen, and BKAI-IGH NeoPolyp.

Result: Full-precision federated training achieves Dice scores of 0.910 (Kvasir-SEG) and 0.930 (CVC-ClinicVideoDB). Uniform 8-bit quantization reduces transmission cost by approximately 4× while preserving competitive segmentation accuracy. Quantized models achieve up to 1.5× faster inference compared to full-precision models.

Conclusion: QFedPolyp successfully enables privacy-preserving collaborative polyp segmentation with significantly reduced communication overhead and faster inference. The resulting lightweight quantized models are well-suited for real-time clinical deployment in colonoscopy settings.

Abstract: Background and Objective: Automatic polyp segmentation supports computer-aided diagnosis and early colorectal cancer detec- tion. Centralized deep learning requires hospitals to share sensitive medical data, while federated learning preserves privacy but introduces high communication costs through repeated transmission of full-precision model parameters. We propose QFedPolyp, a communication- and inference-efficient federated learning framework for collaborative polyp segmentation.
  Methods: QFedPolyp combines quantization-aware training with low-precision model communication. Each hospital locally trains a lightweight U-Net on private data while simulating quantization during training. Clients transmit quantized model parameters to a central server, where they are reconstructed and aggregated using Federated Averaging. Evaluation is performed on Kvasir-SEG, CVC-ClinicVideoDB, PolypGen, and BKAI-IGH NeoPolyp.
  Results: Full-precision federated training achieves Dice scores of 0.910 on Kvasir-SEG and 0.930 on CVC-ClinicVideoDB. Uni- form 8-bit communication reduces transmission cost by approximately 4 times while preserving competitive segmentation accuracy. Quantized models also achieve up to 1.5 times faster inference than full-precision models.
  Conclusions: QFedPolyp enables privacy-preserving collaborative polyp segmentation with reduced communication overhead and faster inference. The resulting lightweight models are suitable for real-time clinical deployment.

</details>


### [122] [Learning to Access Computation: Accessibility Plasticity as a Principle of Adaptive Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22748)
*Zhaowen Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces "Accessibility Plasticity," a principle where neural systems adapt by reorganizing which existing computations can interact, rather than only modifying parameters. The authors formalize a reuse-first hierarchy prioritizing accessibility changes over structural ones, and demonstrate that accessibility adaptation reduces the need for parameter modification while maintaining performance on sequential learning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural network adaptation methods—modularity, conditional computation, parameter-efficient fine-tuning—focus on modifying what computations exist, but do not treat computational accessibility (which computations can interact) as a distinct adaptive variable. The authors argue that distinguishing capability from accessibility opens a new, underexplored dimension for adaptation.

Method: The authors formalize Accessibility Plasticity via a relationship-based operational realization, establishing a reuse-first adaptation hierarchy: accessibility modification precedes capability modification, which in turn precedes structural changes. A proof-of-concept evaluation is conducted on sequential learning tasks to validate the framework.

Result: The proof-of-concept shows that accessibility adaptation can reduce the amount of capability (parameter) modification needed while maintaining comparable task performance, supporting accessibility as a viable and distinct adaptive dimension.

Conclusion: Accessibility Plasticity represents a distinct adaptive dimension that complements existing methods. The reuse-first hierarchy provides a foundation for future dynamic neural systems whose computational relationships evolve with changing environments.

Abstract: Modern neural networks primarily adapt through parameter modification within predefined computational structures. While recent methods introduce modularity, conditional computation, and parameter-efficient adaptation, they generally do not distinguish computational capability from computational accessibility as separate adaptive variables. This work introduces Accessibility Plasticity, a principle of adaptive computation in which systems adapt not only by changing what computation exists, but also by reorganizing which existing computations can interact and participate. We formalize Accessibility Plasticity through a relationship-based operational realization and establish a reuse-first hierarchy of adaptation, where accessibility modification precedes more costly capability and structural changes. A proof-of-concept evaluation on sequential learning tasks shows that accessibility adaptation can reduce capability modification while maintaining comparable task performance. These results suggest accessibility as a distinct adaptive dimension and provide a foundation for future dynamic neural systems whose computational relationships evolve with changing environments.

</details>


### [123] [Hierarchical Grading in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22757)
*T. Shaska*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GLLMs introduce an algebraic grading on transformer representation spaces. The optimal grading solves a convex program derived from geometric invariant theory, improving on uniform architectures. Graded models compile to standard transformers with identical inference cost. For level-stratified targets, a minimax separation result shows graded priors exponentially outperform uniform ones over an explicit sample-size window.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard transformers treat all representation dimensions uniformly, lacking a principled mechanism to assign differential importance to subspaces. The authors aim to show that introducing a grading — a weighted scalar action on the representation space — yields strictly better models without increasing inference cost, and that the optimal grading can be determined offline via convex optimization before training.

Method: The framework extends graded neural networks to autoregressive transformers. A grading torus acts on embeddings, self-attention, and the loss. Geometric invariant theory provides the analytic tools: a Kempf–Ness functional defines optimality; a Hilbert–Mumford criterion determines beneficial grade directions via two measurable data/target profiles; optimal grades are the coincidence point of two moment maps in closed form. The uniform transformer is a semistable boundary point. A separate minimax analysis for level-stratified targets quantifies risk separation between graded and uniform estimators.

Result: Beneficial grades form an open convex cone. Optimal grades solve a convex program certifiable offline. For geometrically stratified targets, the risk separation factor between graded and uniform estimators decays exponentially in the number of levels. After training, the grading is absorbed into learned parameters, so GLLMs compile to standard transformers with identical architecture and inference complexity.

Conclusion: GLLMs provide a rigorous algebraic framework showing that grading transformer representation spaces yields provable improvements over uniform architectures. The optimal grading is computable offline via convex optimization, and the resulting model compiles to a standard transformer, making the approach both theoretically sound and practically cost-free at inference time.

Abstract: We introduce Graded Large Language Models (GLLMs), an algebraic framework that equips the representation space of a transformer with a grading and propagates the induced weighted scalar action through embeddings, self-attention, and the training objective. The construction extends the theory of graded neural networks and graded transformers to autoregressive language models while preserving expressive power, asymptotic computational complexity, and inference cost.
  The governing geometric picture is that of geometric invariant theory. The benefit of a grading is expressed by a Kempf--Ness functional on the grading torus; the grades that improve upon the uniform architecture form an open convex cone whose membership is decided by a Hilbert--Mumford-type criterion pairing a grade direction against two measurable profiles of the target and the data; the optimal grades are the coincidence point of two moment maps, given in closed form; and the ordinary transformer appears as a semistable isotropic point on the boundary of the cone: one member of a larger graded family rather than a distinguished optimum.
  Separately, for level-stratified targets we prove a minimax separation between the graded prior and its absence: over all estimators the risks of the graded and uniform target classes separate throughout an explicit window of sample sizes, by a factor that decays exponentially in the number of levels under geometric stratification. Both profiles are estimable offline, so the optimal grades solve a convex program certified before training begins. Because the grading is absorbed into the learned parameters after training, every GLLM compiles to a standard transformer of identical architecture and inference complexity.

</details>


### [124] [An Integrated Deep Learning and Statistical Framework for Whole-Network Gene--Environment Association with Leaf Vascular Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22763)
*Geran Zhao,Yangsheng Wang,Xiaotian Dai,Guifang Fu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes an integrated deep learning and statistical framework for whole-network leaf venation phenotyping, combining EDTER-based edge extraction with Semiparametric Sparse Canonical Correlation Analysis (SSCCA) to model gene–environment associations using high-dimensional image phenotypes, validated through simulations and applied to Populus data to identify significant gene–geography interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing gene–environment association studies reduce leaf venation to low-dimensional summary traits, discarding most structural information in original images. A method is needed to treat the complete leaf vascular architecture as a high-dimensional image phenotype while handling the statistical challenges of zero-inflated edge maps and repeated measures.

Method: The framework has four components: (1) representing leaf vascular architecture as a whole-network image phenotype; (2) fine-tuning EDTER (Edge Detection with Transformers) to extract vascular networks from RGB images using joint local and global features; (3) constructing a new annotated leaf image database merging DiffusionEdge-generated edge maps with BSDS500; (4) applying SSCCA for variable selection between repeatedly measured high-dimensional bivariate image responses and high-dimensional predictors, using a truncated latent Gaussian copula to handle sparse, zero-inflated edge map data.

Result: Two simulation studies validated the framework under increasing complexity. Application to a real Populus dataset identified three significant gene–geography interactions associated with leaf vascular architecture.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides new biological insights into leaf vascular architecture and establishes a broadly applicable methodology for high-dimensional complex image phenotype analysis in gene–environment association studies.

Abstract: Leaf veins exhibit remarkable diversity in architecture and patterning, yet existing gene--environment association studies have primarily quantified leaf venation using a small collection of low-dimensional summary traits, thereby discarding most of the structural information contained in the original images. We propose an integrated deep learning and statistical framework. The proposed framework achieves four methodological advances. First, it represents the complete leaf vascular architecture as a whole-network image phenotype. Second, it fine-tunes the deep learning-based Edge Detection with Transformers (EDTER) model to accurately extract whole-network leaf vascular architecture from RGB images by jointly learning local and global contextual features. Third, it constructs a new annotated leaf image database by integrating edge maps generated by DiffusionEdge with the Berkeley Segmentation Database (BSDS500). Fourth, it applies Semiparametric Sparse Canonical Correlation Analysis (SSCCA) to perform variable selection and model associations between repeatedly measured high-dimensional Bivariate image responses and high-dimensional predictors while simultaneously accommodating sparse, zero-inflated data represented by edge maps through a truncated latent Gaussian copula model. Two simulation studies demonstrate the performance of the proposed framework under increasing levels of complexity. Application to a real \emph{Populus} dataset identifies three significant gene--geography interactions associated with leaf vascular architecture, providing new biological insights and establishing a broadly applicable methodological framework for high-dimensional complex image phenotypes.

</details>


### [125] [Beyond Shapley: An Influence-Based Data Auditing Pipeline for LLM Alignment and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22766)
*Yunting Song,Matthew Watson,Peter Grabowski,Jun Qin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A scalable, inference-only Shapley-value-based data valuation pipeline identifies harmful or mislabeled records in LLM alignment datasets by measuring predictive impact via conditional log-likelihood shifts, reducing manual audit overhead by 99.1% and exposing thousands of hidden safety and helpfulness label errors in benchmarks like HelpSteer2 and HH-RLHF.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM alignment datasets at scale accumulate hidden contradictions, safety risks, and human annotation errors. Existing auditing methods (semantic deduplication, LLM-as-a-judge) fail to capture actual predictive impact or detect deep functional rule clashes, necessitating a more principled data valuation approach.

Method: The authors propose an inference-only pipeline that approximates the Shapley value without model retraining. It constructs a directed graph from semantic k-NN neighborhoods and computes data utility via zero-shot and one-shot conditional log-likelihood shifts from a reference LLM. Predictive influence scores are converted into localized advantage metrics to flag gradient-conflicting records.

Result: On HelpSteer2, the pipeline reduced manual audit search space by 99.1% and uncovered falsely-labeled records across multiple failure modes. On HH-RLHF training and evaluation splits, it identified thousands of hidden safety and factual preference inversions. Notably, the evaluation split audit revealed that capable models are frequently penalized for correct predictions due to flawed human ground-truth labels, exposing severe benchmark integrity vulnerabilities.

Conclusion: The proposed pipeline offers a mathematically grounded, efficient diagnostic for uncovering human label failures, sanitizing evaluation benchmarks, and safeguarding the integrity of LLM alignment data at scale.

Abstract: The alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) is increasingly bottlenecked by data quality. As datasets scale, massive preference and instruction-tuning corpora inevitably accumulate hidden structural contradictions, safety risks, and systemic human annotation errors. Standard dataset auditing methods, such as semantic deduplication or LLM-as-a-judge, struggle to capture the actual predictive impact of individual records and often miss deep functional rule clashes. To address this, we introduce a scalable, inference-only data valuation pipeline that approximates the Shapley value without iterative model retraining. By mapping semantic k-NN neighborhoods into a directed graph, our framework evaluates data utility directly through a reference LLM's probability distribution using zero-shot and one-shot conditional log-likelihood shifts. Our pipeline then translates these predictive influence scores into localized advantage metrics to isolate gradient-conflicting records. We demonstrate the pipeline's efficacy in sanitizing two heavily vetted alignment datasets. First, applying our pipeline to the HelpSteer2 dataset reduced the manual audit search space by 99.1%, successfully uncovering falsely-labeled records across diverse failure modes. Second, applying our automated audit strategy to Anthropic's HH-RLHF training and evaluation splits identified thousands of hidden safety and factual preference inversions. Crucially, by extending this audit to the evaluation split, we expose severe vulnerabilities in current benchmark integrity: highly capable models frequently predict the safer or more helpful response, only to be penalized by objectively flawed human ground-truth labels. Overall, our work provides a mathematically grounded, highly efficient diagnostic tool to uncover human label failures, sanitize evaluation benchmarks, and ensure the integrity of LLM alignment data.

</details>


### [126] [DomainPilot: Domain-Level Loss-Guided Two-Stage Data Mixture Optimization for Efficient Language Model Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22769)
*He Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DomainPilot is a two-stage, domain-level loss-guided framework for optimizing LLM training data mixtures. It uses token-level domain loss monitoring with a patch-based architecture (~30 lines of adapter code) and applies Scaling Law-guided coarse optimization followed by Mixing Law-guided fine optimization to adjust domain proportions, achieving significant benchmark improvements (+2–3.8%) on Qwen3-1.7B SFT without increasing data volume or training cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dynamic data scheduling methods are impractical for industrial-scale LLM training: data selection incurs O(N) costs on terabyte corpora, mixture optimization causes severe I/O bottlenecks or requires auxiliary reference models, and sample-level reweighting relies on noisy loss signals that conflate difficulty, noise, and novelty. A lightweight, cost-effective mixture optimization approach is needed.

Method: DomainPilot introduces: (1) token-level domain loss monitoring that captures per-domain learning dynamics without halting the data pipeline; (2) a Scaling Law-guided coarse optimization stage that fits domain-specific convergence curves to derive a principled mixture prior; (3) a Mixing Law-guided fine optimization stage that models cross-domain interaction effects via controlled sweep experiments. The system is implemented as a patch-based architecture injecting domain-aware loss computation into frameworks like MindSpeed/Megatron-LM with only ~30 lines of adapter code.

Result: Validated on Qwen3-1.7B during SFT, DomainPilot's optimized mixture achieved: +2% on MMLU-Redux, +1.8% on AIME24, +3.8% on LiveCodeBench v5, and +3.6% on BFCL v3, all without increasing total data volume or training cost.

Conclusion: Domain-level training signals provide an effective, lightweight alternative to expensive data selection or auxiliary model training for data mixture optimization in LLM training.

Abstract: The training efficacy of large language models (LLMs) is fundamentally constrained by the quality and composition of training data. Existing dynamic data scheduling methods face critical limitations in industrial-scale pretraining and supervised fine-tuning (SFT): data selection incurs prohibitive O(N) costs on terabyte-scale corpora, mixture optimization schemes introduce severe I/O bottlenecks or require training auxiliary reference models, and sample-level reweighting strategies rely on loss signals that conflate noise, difficulty, and novelty.
  We present DomainPilot, a domain-level loss-guided two-stage data mixture optimization framework. DomainPilot introduces token-level domain loss monitoring to capture per-domain learning dynamics during training without halting the data pipeline. Building on these signals, we propose a Scaling Law guided coarse optimization stage that fits domain-specific convergence curves and derives a principled prior for mixture adjustment. A subsequent Mixing Law guided fine optimization stage refines the mixture by modeling cross-domain interaction effects through controlled sweep experiments. The entire mechanism is realized via a patch-based architecture that injects domain-aware loss computation into existing training frameworks (e.g., MindSpeed/Megatron-LM) with only ~30 lines of framework-specific adapter code.
  We validate DomainPilot on the Qwen3-1.7B model during SFT. Compared to the original data mixture, our optimized mixture achieves improvements of +2% on MMLU-Redux, +1.8% on AIME24, +3.8% on LiveCodeBench v5, and +3.6% on BFCL v3, without increasing total data volume or training cost. These results demonstrate that domain-level training signals provide an effective, lightweight alternative to expensive data selection or auxiliary model training for mixture optimization.

</details>


### [127] [Dementia Etiology Diagnosis via Collaborative Meta Knowledge Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22770)
*Siyuan Du,Mengxi Chen,Xinyang Jiang,Zilong Wang,Jiangchao Yao,Dongsheng Li,Ya Zhang,Lili Qiu,Yanfeng Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes COME, a Collaborative Meta Knowledge Enhancement framework that injects heterogeneity-aware embeddings (acquisition semantics, source identifiers, modality indicators) into a unified Transformer with trust-region constrained optimization to address cross-center data heterogeneity in AI-based dementia etiology diagnosis, achieving state-of-the-art performance and strong out-of-domain generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-based dementia etiology diagnosis suffers from complex overlapping symptoms and inherent data heterogeneity across centers/populations (e.g., site-specific acquisition protocols, modality availability). Simply scaling up with cross-center data introduces conflicts, and conventional multi-task learning fails to leverage meta information to combat this heterogeneity.

Method: The COME framework introduces three key components: (1) heterogeneity-aware embeddings encoding multi-center acquisition semantics, source identifiers, and modality indicators injected into a unified Transformer architecture for scale-up training with explicit heterogeneity modeling; (2) a trust-region constrained optimization scheme that uses a reference model to regularize against spurious correlations during training.

Result: Across seven independent cohorts, COME achieves a mean macro-averaged AUC of 85.62% with a 4.29-point gain over the strongest baseline in-domain, and demonstrates superior out-of-domain generalization under both cross-center and cross-sequence evaluations. Model predictions also align with established biomarkers (amyloid, tau) and clinical severity.

Conclusion: COME enables robust, interpretable dementia diagnostics by explicitly modeling cross-center heterogeneity through meta knowledge enhancement and trust-region optimization, showing strong potential for real-world clinical deployment.

Abstract: Although artificial intelligence (AI) has shown promising performance in several medical tasks, accurate dementia etiology diagnosis with AI remains challenging due to complex overlapping symptoms among diseases. Scaling up the dataset size by combining the cross-center samples may bring a gain in the pursuit of performance, while the inherent data heterogeneity across centers or populations induces the conflict. Conventional multi-task learning paradigms offer a promising framework; however, they fail to consider critical meta information (e.g., site-specific acquisition and modality availability) to combat the heterogeneity. To address this challenge, we propose a Collaborative Meta Knowledge Enhancement (COME) framework for dementia etiology diagnosis, which injects multi-center acquisition semantics, source identifiers, and modality indicators as heterogeneity-aware embeddings into a unified Transformer architecture for scale-up training, enabling explicit modeling of heterogeneity. Besides, a trust-region constrained optimization scheme is designed to regularize the model from spurious correlations during training through a reference model. Across seven independent cohorts, our method achieves state-of-the-art in-domain performance with a mean macro-averaged AUC of 85.62% and a 4.29-point gain over the strongest baseline, while maintaining superior out-of-domain generalization under both cross-center and cross-sequence evaluations. Extensive validation also confirms the alignment between model predictions and established biomarkers (amyloid, tau) and clinical severity, highlighting the potential of COME to enable robust and interpretable dementia diagnostics in real-world settings.

</details>


### [128] [CC-AOS: Cost- and Horizon-Conditioned Amortized Backward Induction for Finite-Horizon Optimal Stopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22774)
*Tianwei Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CC-AOS is an amortized optimal stopping framework that jointly conditions on cost and horizon to solve multiple stopping problems from a single model, encoding monotonicity, concavity, and Lipschitz properties into the architecture, achieving superior cost-risk trade-offs compared to per-point baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing backward-induction methods for finite-horizon optimal stopping solve each cost-horizon operating point independently, requiring retraining and separate models when conditions change. This makes continuous cost adaptation and multi-horizon deployment inefficient. The authors aim to amortize over both cost and horizon in a single model.

Method: CC-AOS learns a shared continuation-value model conditioned on state, absolute time, remaining horizon, and acquisition cost via joint amortized fitted backward induction. The architecture explicitly encodes structural properties: nondecreasing monotonicity in cost, concavity in cost, and horizon-dependent Lipschitz continuity. Residual-based bounds on value and policy errors are derived analytically.

Result: On six unseen FordA cost-horizon pairs, a single CC-AOS checkpoint achieved lower terminal-risk-plus-sampling-cost than independently fitted Convex Function Learning at all six pairs, with an average 15.75% reduction, while matching tuned static thresholds on average.

Conclusion: Amortizing optimal stopping over continuous costs and multiple horizons with structurally constrained architectures is both feasible and effective, enabling efficient deployment across varying operating conditions without retraining.

Abstract: Finite-horizon optimal stopping is a central problem in early time-series classification, where a system must decide at each sequence prefix whether the expected benefit of another observation justifies its acquisition cost. Existing data-driven backward-induction methods typically solve each cost-horizon operating point separately, so changing operating conditions requires repeated optimization and separate model stacks, making continuous cost adaptation and multi-horizon deployment inefficient. We propose CC-AOS (Cost- and Horizon-Conditioned Amortized Optimal Stopping), a structured amortized solver for a family of finite-horizon stopping problems with continuous costs and multiple horizons. CC-AOS learns a shared continuation-value model conditioned on the current state, absolute time, remaining horizon, and acquisition cost through joint amortized fitted backward induction. We establish that the exact value and continuation functions are nondecreasing, concave, and horizon-dependently Lipschitz in cost, encode these properties in the model architecture, and derive residual-based bounds on value and policy errors. Experiments on controlled Gaussian and time-varying non-Gaussian processes and the FordA engine-noise time-series benchmark compare CC-AOS with representative per-operating-point backward-induction solvers and tuned static stopping rules. At six unseen FordA cost-horizon pairs, one CC-AOS checkpoint achieved a lower terminal-risk-plus-sampling-cost objective than independently fitted Convex Function Learning at all six pairs, with an average reduction of 15.75 percent, while matching the tuned static thresholds on average.

</details>


### [129] [Predicting the Outcome of rTMS Depression Therapy using EEG Signals and CNN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22776)
*Wael Korani,Md Fahimul Kabir Chowdhury,Sadam AlQadi,Priyan Malarvizhi kumar,Reza Rostami,Reza Kazemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EEG signals from MDD patients are transformed into images using FBSE-ED and DWT, then classified by a lightweight CNN to predict rTMS treatment response, achieving 93.60% accuracy with FBSE-ED and outperforming both EEG-specific and pretrained deep learning models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable early, accurate prediction of rTMS treatment outcomes for Major Depressive Disorder using EEG signals, thereby supporting targeted clinical decision-making in psychiatric settings.

Method: EEG signals are transformed into 2D time-frequency images via FBSE-ED and DWT. A custom lightweight CNN is trained with 10-fold cross-validation on a private rTMS database. Performance is benchmarked against EEG-specific models (EEGNet, DeepConvNet, SleepEEGNet) and pretrained models (Xception, DenseNet201, MobileNetV2). Generalization is tested on an independent private rTMS database.

Result: FBSE-ED image representation yields 93.60% classification accuracy, surpassing DWT. The proposed CNN outperforms EEG-specific models by 3.62–10.72% and pretrained models by 23.03–27.35%. Robustness is confirmed on a separate test database.

Conclusion: The FBSE-ED + lightweight CNN framework is interpretable, computationally efficient, and clinically deployable, offering a promising tool for early rTMS response prediction in local psychiatric clinics.

Abstract: Repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation (rTMS) is a non invasive therapy for Major Depressive Disorder (MDD). In this study, we generate images using two time frequency methods to represent EEG signals: Fourier-Bessel Series Expansion with Euclidean Distance (FBSE-ED) and Discrete Wavelet Transform (DWT). We propose an efficient deep learning classifier to predict the outcome of rTMS depression therapy. In this study, we use a private rTMS databases to train a lightweight custom Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) using 10-fold cross validation strategy in order to avoid any bias in our results. The results show that the FBSE-ED representation achieves the highest classification accuracy of 93.60\%, outperforming traditional time-frequency technique (DWT). In addition, the proposed architecture with FBSE-ED image representation technique outperforms more complex EEG-Specific deep learning models (EEGNet, DeepConvNet, SleepEEGNet) by 3.62-10.72% and pretrained models (Xception, DenseNet201, and MobileNetV2) by 23.03-27.35%. For more experiments, we utilize another private rTMS database as test database to show the robustness of the proposed model. Our results suggest that integrating advanced signal decomposition with deep learning can facilitate early prediction of rTMS treatment response and support more targeted clinical decision-making. The proposed framework is interpretable, computationally efficient, and well-suited for deployment in real-world local psychiatric clinics.

</details>


### [130] [LC-SEPLM: long-range contact-supervised adaptation for sequence-only protein representation learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22777)
*Chen Wang,Boming Kang,Qinghua Cui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LC-SEPLM enhances ESM2 protein language models by adding long-range residue-pair contact supervision via LoRA, improving eight downstream tasks—especially remote-homology recognition (+6.47 pp macro-F1)—while preserving sequence-only inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard protein language models like ESM2 learn contextual dependencies along amino-acid sequences but are not explicitly constrained to capture three-dimensional residue-residue contacts formed after protein folding. There is a need to inject structural information into sequence representations without sacrificing the convenience of sequence-only inference.

Method: The authors propose LC-SEPLM, which adapts ESM2 using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) with long-range residue-pair contact supervision. Pair-specific queries employ cross-attention over the entire sequence to extract global sequence context linked to long-range spatial contacts. The model is trained on 500,000 AlphaFold-predicted Swiss-Prot protein structures. Critically, the architecture is designed so that downstream inference remains sequence-only.

Result: LC-SEPLM improved performance on all eight protein-level downstream tasks compared to ESM2. The most significant gain was in remote-homology recognition, with macro-F1 rising from 0.6122 to 0.6769 (+0.0647). On the official ESM-S enzyme commission (EC) benchmark, LC-SEPLM outperformed ESM-S by up to 0.1771 absolute gain.

Conclusion: Residue-pair contact supervision is a viable and bounded strategy for incorporating three-dimensional structural information into protein sequence representations, yielding substantial downstream improvements while maintaining the practical advantage of sequence-only inference.

Abstract: Protein language models learn transferable sequence representations. However, because they primarily model contextual dependencies along amino-acid sequences, their training objectives do not explicitly constrain the model to learn three-dimensional residue contacts formed after folding . Here, we introduce LC-SEPLM (Long-range Contact-supervised ESM Protein Language Model), which adapts ESM2 with LoRA and long-range residue-pair contact supervision while retaining sequence-only downstream inference. Pair-specific queries use cross-attention over the complete sequence to extract global sequence context associated with long-range spatial contacts. To expose the model to diverse structural information, we trained LC-SEPLM on 500,000 AlphaFold Swiss-Prot proteins. In downstream evaluation, LC-SEPLM improved all eight protein-level tasks relative to ESM2. The largest gain occurred in remote-homology recognition, where macro-F1 increased from 0.6122 to 0.6769 (+0.0647, or 6.47 percentage points). On the official ESM-S EC benchmark, LC-SEPLM also outperformed ESM-S with a maximum absolute gain of 0.1771. These results support residue-pair contact supervision as a bounded route for introducing structural information into protein sequence representations while preserving sequence-only inference.

</details>


### [131] [Multimodal Surface EMG Hand Gesture Recognition Using Query-Based Transformers for Prosthetic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22779)
*Federico Del Pup,Elisa Tentori,Manfredo Atzori*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EMG-CrossFormer is a hybrid convolutional-transformer architecture that combines local and global features across multimodal physiological signals (sEMG, inertial, eye-tracking) for hand gesture recognition. It outperforms unimodal convolutional baselines, with multimodal fusion boosting accuracy by up to ~18 percentage points across four NinaPro datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current deep learning models for sEMG-based hand gesture recognition rely on low-latency unimodal convolutional architectures that (1) operate locally and fail to capture long-range sequential dependencies, and (2) cannot leverage complementary information from other modalities like inertial and eye-tracking data. Performance degrades as gesture set size increases due to increased statistical complexity.

Method: EMG-CrossFormer is an end-to-end hybrid convolutional-transformer. It employs arbitrary unimodal encoders whose representations are integrated via cascaded cross-attention fusion layers, and decodes fused representations using learnable gesture queries. The architecture jointly models local features (via convolutions) and global sequential patterns (via transformers).

Result: sEMG-only accuracies: 72.33% (DB2), 52.48% (DB3), 79.16% (DB7), 73.49% (DB10). With inertial data: 90.66% (DB2), 80.40% (DB3), 92.79% (DB7), 92.06% (DB10). EMG-CrossFormer outperformed six state-of-the-art models, with multimodal fusion yielding substantial gains across all datasets.

Conclusion: Joint local-global feature modeling improves sEMG-only decoding, and multimodal fusion via cross-attention substantially amplifies this benefit. Both design principles are critical for robust, scalable hand gesture recognition from physiological signals.

Abstract: Hand gesture recognition via surface electromyography (sEMG) is fundamental to prosthetic control. In this field, deep learning approaches have become the gold standard. However, current architectures struggle to scale; model performance typically decreases as the number of hand movements increases. Performance degradation is tied to the increased statistical complexity of decoding expanded gesture sets and compounded by the limitations of state-of-the-art methods, which primarily rely on low-latency unimodal convolutional architectures. Convolutions operate locally, limiting model's ability to capture long-range sequential patterns. Unimodal setups cannot leverage complementary information from coordinated signals characterizing movement execution, such as inertial and eye-tracking data. These limitations motivate architectures that integrate local and global features across multimodal physiological sequences. To bridge this gap, this study introduces EMG-CrossFormer, an end-to-end hybrid convolutional-transformer for seamless multimodal integration. EMG-CrossFormer combines representations from an arbitrary number of unimodal encoders through cascaded cross-attention fusion layers, and decodes the fused representations using learnable gesture queries. EMG-CrossFormer was evaluated on four NinaPro datasets (DB2, DB3, DB7, and DB10) and benchmarked against six state-of-the-art models using an increasing number of modalities. Using only sEMG, EMG-CrossFormer achieved mean accuracies of 72.33%, 52.48%, 79.16%, and 73.49% on DB2, DB3, DB7, and DB10, respectively. Incorporating inertial signals improved performance to 90.66%, 80.40%, 92.79%, and 92.06%. These results show that joint local-global feature modeling improves sEMG-only decoding and that multimodal fusion substantially amplifies this benefit, underscoring the value of both design principles for complex hand gesture recognition.

</details>


### [132] [What Softmax Throws Away: Mass-Aware Attention for Evidence Accumulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22781)
*Minwoo Yu,Young-guk Ha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mass-Aware Attention (MAA) generalizes L1 normalization to an Lp family, making attention representations retain information about the effective number of contributing inputs under repetition, thereby improving hidden representation informativeness and downstream task performance across temporal graphs and other sequence models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention mechanisms lose structurally relevant information due to weighted averaging: when evidence patterns repeat, normalized attention weights produce identical aggregates regardless of how much evidence accumulated. This means models can achieve high task performance (e.g., future-link AUC) while basic graph statistics become unrecoverable from internal representations, revealing a gap between task utility and information accessibility.

Method: MAA replaces standard L1 normalization in attention with Lp normalization, where p≠1 causes numerator and denominator to scale at different rates under input repetition. This retains the effective count of contributing inputs encoded in the representation magnitude. MAA adds no supervision, parameters, hidden dimensions, or explicit count features, and recovers standard attention exactly when p=1.

Result: MAA improves future-link AUC in 11 of 12 model-dataset combinations across four dynamic graph models and three datasets. Linear recovery from hidden representations increases by 4.49% on average; preferential-attachment recovery improves in all 12 cells after family-wise correction. Consistent evidence is observed in marked temporal point processes (NLL improvement), temporal knowledge graphs (ranking preserved), retrieval-augmented generation (additional information doesn't improve diagnostic head), and spatio-temporal point processes (LayerNorm can erase the signal).

Conclusion: MAA serves as a general normalization principle that improves predictor-facing representation informativeness by controlling repetition invariance in standard attention, with the key insight that task utility and information accessibility are distinct properties that MAA helps align.

Abstract: High task performance does not show whether a model retains prediction-relevant structural information in its internal representation. Temporal graph models, for example, can achieve high future-link AUC while basic graph statistics remain difficult to recover from the same representation. We identify one source of this gap in the weighted averaging used by standard attention: when an evidence pattern is repeated, the numerator and denominator grow at the same rate, so inputs with different amounts of accumulated evidence can produce the same aggregate.
  We propose Mass-Aware Attention (MAA), which generalizes standard L1 normalization to an Lp family. Under repetition, MAA makes the numerator and denominator scale at different rates, retaining the effective number of contributing inputs in the representation magnitude. It adds no supervision, parameters, hidden dimensions, or explicit count features, and recovers standard attention at p=1.
  Across four continuous-time dynamic graph models and three datasets, MAA improves future-link AUC in 11 of 12 model-dataset cells. Linear recovery from the same hidden representation increases by 4.49% on average, and preferential-attachment recovery improves in all 12 cells after family-wise correction. We also observe consistent evidence in marked temporal point processes, temporal knowledge graphs, retrieval-augmented generation, and spatio-temporal point processes. Information accessibility and task utility remain distinct: NLL improves in MTPP, ranking is largely preserved in TKG, additional information in RAG does not improve the diagnostic head, and downstream LayerNorm can erase the signal in STPP. These results position MAA as a general normalization principle for improving predictor-facing representation informativeness by controlling repetition invariance in standard attention.

</details>


### [133] [Optimizing Transformer Neural Network for Real-Time Outlier Detection on FPGAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22786)
*Ilia Sobakinskikh,Paul Alexander Bilokon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Explores efficient FPGA-based implementation of Transformer neural networks for real-time anomaly detection in financial time series, with focus on minimizing inference latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial time series contain errors and outliers that degrade downstream processing. The growing volume of data demands cleaning methods that are both accurate and fast. Transformers offer strong anomaly detection capabilities (long-range dependencies), but need hardware acceleration (FPGAs) to achieve real-time performance.
