Method: Exploration of different Transformer architectures for time series modeling, implemented and optimized on a PYNQ-Z2 FPGA board to accelerate inference and minimize latency for anomaly detection in price series.

Result: Demonstrates that Transformer architectures can be efficiently deployed on resource-constrained FPGA hardware (PYNQ-Z2) for real-time financial time series anomaly detection, with code made publicly available on GitHub.

Conclusion: FPGA-based Transformer inference is a viable approach for real-time anomaly detection in financial time series, offering a balance of accuracy and low-latency processing suitable for data cleaning at scale.

Abstract: In this work, we explore how the inference time of a Transformer Neural Network can be efficiently optimized with applications to real-time anomaly detection in financial time series. The financial time series are price series such as asset prices. Unfortunately, the data is often with errors or outliers that make the downstream data processing tasks useless, unstable or even harmful. Moreover, the amount of financial time-series data has been significantly increasing. Hence, there is a need for better data-cleaning methods in terms of accuracy and in terms of processing speed.
  Transformers as a neural network architecture have achieved superior performances in many tasks such as Natural Language Processing and Computer Vision. Time series modelling and especially anomaly detection tasks can benefit from the features of transformers architecture in multiple ways, including the capacity to capture long-range dependencies and interactions.
  Increasingly powerful hardware, such as field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs), have seen increasing usage in recent years due to their reconfigurability and high performance. They can be efficiently utilized to speed up the computations of the Transformer architecture.
  We explore different Transformer architectures for time series modelling and how they can be efficiently implemented on an FPGA board (PYNQ-Z2). In particular, we examine the application of Transformers to detect anomalies in time series and we show how they can be efficiently implemented on an FPGA board to minimize latency.
  The code is available at https://github.com/thxi/icl_thesis

</details>


### [134] [FMOPF: Latent Flow Matching with Constraint-Aware Interaction Priors for AC Optimal Power Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22788)
*Zhilin Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FMOPF introduces a latent flow matching framework that decouples manifold compression from conditional generation for AC OPF, achieving scalable, feasible sampling of near-optimal dispatch distributions. It is the first generative method to scale to several hundred buses while maintaining physical feasibility and low tail risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With deepening renewable penetration, operators need the full distribution of near-optimal OPF solutions — not just a single point — for risk quantification, sensitivity analysis, and multi-objective trade-offs. Supervised neural networks only provide point estimates, while existing diffusion-based generative models operating in raw state space degrade in solution quality and fail to scale beyond medium-sized systems.

Method: FMOPF decouples two conflated tasks: (1) compressing the high-dimensional OPF solution manifold via a variational autoencoder into a compact latent space, and (2) learning the conditional mapping from loads to that latent manifold using flow matching with a Constraint-Aware Interaction Prior Network. The interaction prior explicitly models load-state coupling and functions as a late-stage tail-risk controller. Generation is performed entirely in latent space, then decoded back to feasible OPF solutions.

Result: Experiments on four IEEE test systems show FMOPF provides the most effective Newton-Raphson warm starts, achieves the lowest tail risk among all generative methods, and is the first such method to scale to systems with several hundred buses while preserving full feasibility. Ablation studies confirm that the latent generation pipeline is necessary for physical feasibility and that the interaction prior controls tail risk.

Conclusion: Decoupling manifold compression from conditional generation is essential for scalable, feasible generative OPF. FMOPF demonstrates that latent flow matching with a constraint-aware interaction prior enables reliable distributional modeling of OPF solutions at practical system scales.

Abstract: AC optimal power flow determines the minimum-cost generation dispatch under nonlinear power balance constraints and is solved thousands of times daily in electricity market operations. Learning a direct mapping from load conditions to OPF solutions can accelerate this computation, yet with deepening renewable penetration, a single optimal dispatch is no longer sufficient. Operators require a characterization of the distribution of feasible near-optimal solutions for risk quantification, sensitivity analysis, and multi-objective trade-off assessment. Supervised neural networks provide fast point predictions but cannot capture this conditional distribution. Diffusion-based generative models can sample diverse solutions in principle, yet existing methods operating in the raw state space exhibit degraded solution quality and fail to scale beyond medium-sized systems. We identify the root cause as the conflation of two distinct tasks within a single model. Compressing the high-dimensional OPF solution manifold is one task, and learning the conditional mapping from loads to that manifold is another. This paper presents FMOPF, a framework that resolves this conflation by decoupling compression from generation through latent flow matching and by explicitly modeling load-state coupling through a Constraint-Aware Interaction Prior Network. Experiments on four IEEE test systems demonstrate that FMOPF provides the most effective Newton-Raphson warm starts, achieves the lowest tail risk among generative methods, and is the first such method to scale to systems with several hundred buses while preserving full feasibility. Ablation studies confirm that the latent generation pipeline is a necessary condition for physical feasibility and that the interaction prior functions as a late-stage tail-risk controller.

</details>


### [135] [Multimodal Domain Generalization for Depression Detection: An Attention-Based BiLSTM Network with Domain-Adversarial Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22794)
*Ali Tabaraei,Federico Simonetta,Stavros Ntalampiras*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A patient-independent multimodal (audio + text) depression detection framework using BiLSTM with attention mechanisms and domain generalization (DANN gradient reversal) achieves 93.2% accuracy and 94.2% F1-score on the Androids-Corpus dataset, surpassing all benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current deep learning-based depression detection suffers from limited generalization due to domain shift caused by inter-speaker variability, necessitating a domain-generalized, patient-independent approach.

Method: The framework combines BiLSTM with intra- and cross-modal attention, segment-level fusion, and a DANN-inspired gradient reversal layer to suppress speaker-identifying information. Acoustic (MelSpec) and textual (ItalianBERT) features are extracted over 30-second segments. Evaluated via 5-fold cross-validation on the Androids-Corpus.

Result: Adding domain generalization improved accuracy by 2.5% and F1-score by 3.3%, reaching 93.2% accuracy, 93.2% precision, 96.2% recall, and 94.2% F1-score—outperforming all existing benchmarks. Ablation studies confirmed contributions of multimodal fusion, architectural choices, and DG.

Conclusion: The proposed domain generalization approach effectively mitigates patient-specific bias in multimodal depression detection, yielding robust, generalizable performance that surpasses prior methods.

Abstract: Automatic depression detection with deep learning has shown promise but often suffers from limited generalization due to domain shift arising from inter-speaker variability. To address this critical issue, we present the first patient-independent multimodal depression detection framework that incorporates domain generalization (DG), jointly leveraging both acoustic and textual modalities. The proposed model integrates bidirectional Long Short-Term Memory (BiLSTM) with intra- and cross-modal attention mechanisms, accompanied by segment-level fusion for decision-making. Generalization is further enhanced by applying a gradient reversal layer inspired by Domain-Adversarial Training of Neural Networks (DANN), which promotes domain-invariant representations by adversarially limiting the model's ability to identify individual speakers, effectively reducing patient-specific bias. Conducting experiments on the Androids-Corpus dataset with a 5-fold cross-validation (CV) protocol, various pairings of audio and text feature extractors were evaluated over different segment durations, determining MelSpec and ItalianBERT as the optimal baseline at a 30-second segment duration. The addition of DG to this baseline yields a 2.5% increase in accuracy and 3.3% in F1-score, achieving 93.2% accuracy, 93.2% precision, 96.2% recall, and 94.2% F1-score, surpassing all existing benchmarks. Extensive ablation studies assess the impact of multimodal fusion, deep architectural choices, and DG, highlighting their combined contribution to robust and generalizable depression detection.

</details>


### [136] [Physically Verifiable Evidence and LLM-Based Reporting for Bearing Fault Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22797)
*Yuntong Chen,Jianyu Liu,Guobin Zhao,Ziang Wang,Chao Chen,Ju Huang,Xitian Tian,Lijiang Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DENet extends diagnostic outputs beyond class labels to include verifiable evidence (characteristic frequency, temporal localization) and constrains LLM reporting to translation-only, enabling inference-time validation and reducing hallucination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI-based fault diagnosis produces unverifiable outputs (softmax scores) that cannot be checked against physical reality, while generative LLMs used in maintenance reporting hallucinate content. Both issues undermine trustworthiness in safety-critical mechanical systems.

Method: DENet is an encoder-agnostic multi-task framework that outputs: (1) classification, (2) predicted characteristic frequency (checkable against theoretical values from bearing geometry/shaft speed), and (3) temporal localization of transient impulses (inspectable on raw waveforms). A QLoRA-adapted language model is constrained to translate but not generate diagnostic content. Validation is performed across four encoders and three public bearing fault datasets.

Result: No statistically significant accuracy cost from the evidence outputs. Frequency error ~6 Hz on 1024-point segments where spectral estimation is structurally inapplicable. Frequency deviation detects misclassifications with AUROC 0.970 and 0.871, remaining discriminative in high-confidence regimes where confidence-based detectors fail. LLM unsupported-claim rate reduced from 10-12% to 2%, with fabricated quantities eliminated.

Conclusion: The label-free, inference-time validation signal based on frequency deviation provides a practical mechanism for trustworthy deployment. Coupling structured verifiable evidence with constrained LLM output addresses both the validation gap and hallucination risk in AI-based diagnosis for safety-critical systems.

Abstract: Trustworthy deployment of AI-based diagnosis in safety-critical mechanical systems hinges on validation: whether a prediction can be checked against physical reality before it is acted upon. Current intelligent fault diagnosers fail this standard in two ways. Their standard output, a class label with a softmax confidence score, is an internal statistic of the classifier, offering nothing checkable against independent physical knowledge; and the growing use of generative language models in maintenance reporting adds a second risk: hallucinated content entering reports on which decisions rest. Taking bearing fault diagnosis as the testbed, this work addresses both problems from the output side. The proposed Diagnostic Evidence Network (DENet) is an encoder-agnostic multi-task framework extending the output to a structured evidence record: the classification, a predicted characteristic frequency comparable against the theoretical value determined by bearing geometry and shaft speed, and a temporal localization of transient impulses inspectable on the raw waveform. Across four encoders and three public datasets, this evidence incurs no statistically significant accuracy cost, with a frequency error of about 6 Hz on 1,024-point segments where spectral estimation is structurally inapplicable. Centrally, the deviation between predicted and theoretical frequency constitutes a label-free, inference-time validation signal: it detects misclassifications with AUROC values of 0.970 and 0.871, and remains discriminative in the high-confidence regime where confidence-derived detectors are blind. Finally, a QLoRA-adapted language model is constrained to translate, but never generate, diagnostic content, reducing unsupported-claim rates from 10-12% to 2% and eliminating fabricated quantities.

</details>


### [137] [LithoFormer: A Robust Framework for Stratigraphic Inference via Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22804)
*Shwetha Salimath,Francesca Bugiotti,Sylvain Wlodarczyk,Sohaib Ouzineb*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LithoFormer is a Seq2Seq transformer model using PatchTST with rotary positional embeddings and a geology-informed loss to jointly predict geological zonation and boundary probabilities from full well logs, achieving 90% reduction in boundary error and 80% reduction in manual labor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated geological characterization methods rely on sliding-window classification, which fails to capture broader geological context, leading to misaligned formation layers and stratigraphic inconsistencies. A method that ingests entire well logs holistically is needed for accurate subsurface reservoir characterization in CCS, geothermal, and resource extraction projects.

Method: LithoFormer employs a Seq2Seq transformer with a channel-independent PatchTST backbone enhanced with rotary positional embeddings (RoPE) to capture long-range geological dependencies. A decoupled multi-task head jointly predicts geological zonation and precise boundary probabilities. A geology-informed loss function enforces physical constraints, notably the Law of Superposition.

Result: On three real-world datasets, LithoFormer achieved a 90% reduction in median boundary error, eliminated stratigraphic order violations entirely, and reduced manual expert labor by 80% compared to traditional sliding-window baselines.

Conclusion: LithoFormer provides a scalable, reliable, and physically consistent solution for large-scale subsurface modeling, significantly outperforming sliding-window approaches in both accuracy and efficiency.

Abstract: Accurate geological characterization of subsurface reservoirs from well log data is essential to support projects such as carbon capture and storage (CCS), geothermal development, and extraction of natural resources. Existing automated techniques for geological characterization primarily use sliding-window classification, which limits their ability to understand broader geological contexts, often leading to misaligned formation layers. To overcome these limitations, we introduce LithoFormer, a robust framework for stratigraphic inference using a Seq2Seq transformer model that ingests entire multivariate well logs in a single pass. The framework utilizes a channel-independent PatchTST backbone enhanced with rotary positional embeddings (RoPE) to capture long-range geological dependencies across entire multivariate well logs. A decoupled multi-task head is employed to jointly predict geological zonation and precise boundary probabilities, while a geology-informed loss function enforces physical constraints such as the Law of Superposition. Validated and deployed on three real-world datasets, LithoFormer demonstrates a 90% reduction in median boundary error and eliminates stratigraphic order violations compared to traditional sliding-window baselines. It also achieves a 80% reduction in manual expert labor and eliminates stratigraphic inconsistencies, providing a scalable and reliable solution for large-scale subsurface modeling.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [138] [Concept-based Visual Counterfactual Explanations with Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22544)
*Yassine Oueslati,Daniil Kirilenko,Martin Gjoreski,Marc Langheinrich*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: C-VCE introduces a diffusion-based counterfactual explanation framework that embeds a concept bottleneck layer directly into the generative model, enabling human-interpretable, minimal semantic edits without relying on fragile external classifiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based visual counterfactual methods depend on external classifiers that must be robust to noisy images, making them fragile and difficult to deploy reliably in safety-critical domains like medicine. The authors aim to eliminate this dependency by integrating classification directly into the generative process.

Method: C-VCE integrates a concept bottleneck layer into a diffusion model, allowing users to toggle semantic concepts on/off during sampling. It employs a probabilistic regularizer to balance prediction flipping against proximity to the original image, and a gradient-based mask to confine edits to the most relevant regions while preserving feature correlations.

Result: On CelebA benchmarks, C-VCE matches or improves flip rates while producing counterfactuals that are visually closer to the input and less distorted than baselines relying on separate noisy-image classifiers.

Conclusion: Embedding an internal concept layer into generative models is a promising direction for making them more interpretable and safer, with C-VCE serving as a practical tool for generating trustworthy "what-if" images without requiring additional noise-robust classifiers.

Abstract: Visual counterfactual explanations aim to answer "what minimal change to this image would flip the model's prediction?", and are increasingly important as vision models are deployed in safety-critical domains (e.g., medicine). Existing diffusion-based methods can produce realistic edits, but they rely on external classifiers that must work reliably on noisy images, which makes them fragile and hard to deploy for robust explanations. We introduce C-VCE, a new diffusion framework that builds the classifier directly into the generative model via a concept bottleneck layer, so that counterfactuals are guided by human-interpretable features (concepts) instead of a separate noise robust classifier that works with pixel-level edits. Our model lets users to toggle on/off semantic concepts during sampling, then minimally adjusts relevant image regions, while preserving the rest of the image, respecting feature correlations. To keep edits small and controlled, we add a simple probabilistic regularizer that balances "change the prediction" against "stay close to the original", plus a gradient-based mask that confines modifications to the most relevant regions. On benchmarks such as CelebA, C-VCE matches or improves flip rates while producing counterfactuals that are visually closer to the input and less distorted than baselines that depend on separate noisy-image classifiers. These properties make C-VCE a practical tool for vision systems where users need concrete "what-if" images without having to trust an additional, noise-robust classifier. More broadly, our results suggest that exposing and controlling an internal concept layer is a promising way to make powerful generative models easier to understand and safer to use.

</details>


### [139] [SeT-Diff: Towards Semantic Foundation Models for HPC Telemetry and Time-Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22548)
*Giovanni B. Esposito,Francesco Antici,Daniele Cesarini,Andrea Bartolini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SeT-Diff introduces a diffusion-based foundational model for HPC compute node telemetry that conditions on sensor semantic descriptions, enabling zero-shot permutation stability and multi-task capabilities (imputation, forecasting, virtual sensing) with strong accuracy (MAE 0.047 on reconstruction, 0.033 on thermal inference).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ML models for HPC telemetry rely on static subsets of anonymous, fixed-position sensors and are single-task specific. They become obsolete when tasks change or sensor configurations vary. A flexible, general-purpose model is needed that decouples system dynamics from dataset structure.

Method: SeT-Diff is a diffusion-based generative model that conditions on each sensor's semantic description rather than fixed-position sensor IDs. This decouples the modeling of system dynamics from the dataset structure, enabling a single pre-trained model to handle multiple tasks (reconstruction, imputation, forecasting, virtual sensing) across varying sensor configurations.

Result: On a real-world supercomputer dataset, SeT-Diff achieves 0.0470 MAE on reconstruction, demonstrates zero-shot permutation stability (negligible degradation when sensors are shuffled), 0.033 MAE on thermal inference/virtual sensing, and performs effectively across imputation and forecasting tasks.

Conclusion: SeT-Diff is the first foundational model for compute node telemetry, offering a flexible, semantically-conditioned diffusion approach that serves as an effective data-driven digital twin for HPC systems.

Abstract: Data centers and their compute nodes require accurate and flexible digital twins capable of modeling the complex interplay of workloads, environmental parameters, and physical metrics. Current machine learning approaches for HPC and its telemetry typically rely on a static subset of anonymous, fixed-position sensor variables tailored to single tasks. Consequently, these models become obsolete when target tasks change or sensor metrics vary. We propose SeT-Diff, the first foundational model for compute node telemetry and time-series. Unlike rigid architectures, our diffusion-based approach conditions the generative process on each sensor's semantic description, decoupling the system dynamics from the structure of the dataset. Experiments on a real-world supercomputer dataset demonstrate a Mean Absolute Error (MAE) of 0.0470 on reconstruction tasks. SeT-Diff exhibits zero-shot permutation stability, maintaining accuracy with negligible degradation even when sensors are shuffled. A single pre-trained model effectively performs data imputation, forecasting, and virtual sensing - achieving a 0.033 MAE in thermal inference - making SeT-Diff an effective data-driven digital twin for HPC systems.

</details>


### [140] [QFoldAgent: An Autonomous Quantum Optimization Multi-Agent System for Protein Structure Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22549)
*Winson Chen,Yuqi Zhang,Sixu Chen,Nuo Xu,Qiang Guan,Caiwen Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QFoldAgent is a closed-loop multi-agent framework that iteratively refines Hamiltonian penalty weights for 5-residue tetrahedral-lattice protein folding using VQE under noise. A design agent proposes penalties, a quantum pipeline optimizes, and a feedback agent uses energy-landscape diagnostics plus MolProbity validation to refine across cycles—without exposing RMSD. It reduces median RMSD on QDockBank fragments, raises structural validity on unseen sequences from 87.5% to 98.7%, and recovers 87% of initially invalid cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing lattice-based hybrid quantum-classical workflows for protein structure prediction rely on manually fixed Hamiltonian penalty weights and evaluate only very short fragments, limiting their robustness and scalability. The work aims to automate and improve penalty selection through closed-loop agent-driven refinement.

Method: A closed-loop multi-agent system: (1) a design agent proposes sequence-conditioned penalty weights; (2) a VQE-based quantum-classical pipeline optimizes the resulting Hamiltonian under Qiskit Aer noise; (3) a feedback agent uses energy-landscape diagnostics and MolProbity validation signals to refine penalties across iterative cycles. Ground-truth RMSD is withheld from agents, used only for final evaluation. Tested on two datasets: 55 QDockBank-derived fragments and 100 coverage-optimized unseen sequences.

Result: On QDockBank, median RMSD reduced from 3.64 Å to 3.20 Å, with largest gains on hardest targets. On unseen sequences, structural validity rose from 87.5% to 98.7%, 87% of initially invalid cases were recovered, and the strongest controller improved cycle-3 energy on 87% of sequences while maintaining 96% Ramachandran-favored geometry.

Conclusion: Iterative agent control can systematically improve optimization behavior and reduce failure cases in 5-residue quantum protein folding, demonstrating the viability of closed-loop penalty refinement without ground-truth leakage.

Abstract: Hybrid quantum-classical protein structure prediction depends strongly on Hamiltonian penalty weights, yet existing lattice-based workflows typically fix these coefficients by hand and evaluate only very short fragments in simulation. We present QFoldAgent, a closed-loop multi-agent framework for 5-residue tetrahedral-lattice folding in which a design agent proposes sequence-conditioned penalties, a VQE-based quantum-classical pipeline optimizes the resulting Hamiltonian under Qiskit Aer noise, and a feedback agent uses energy-landscape diagnostics and MolProbity validation signals to refine penalties across cycles. Ground-truth metrics such as RMSD are never exposed to the agents and are used only for evaluation. We study the framework on two complementary datasets: 55 QDockBank-derived fragments with known structures and 100 coverage-optimized unseen sequences. On the QDockBank benchmark, QFoldAgent reduces median RMSD from 3.64 Å to 3.20 Å, with the largest gains on the hardest targets. On unseen sequences, the closed loop raises structural validity from 87.5% to 98.7%, recovers 87% of initially invalid cases, and the strongest controller improves cycle-3 energy on 87% of sequences while maintaining 96% Ramachandran-favored geometry. These results show that iterative agent control can systematically improve optimization behavior and reduce failure cases in a 5-residue quantum setting.

</details>


### [141] [Lexical discovery in unknown environments orchestrated by Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22591)
*Rafael Sendra-Arranz,Iñaki Dellibarda Varela,Eduardo Rocon,Álvaro Gutiérrez,Manuel Cebrian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Autonomous LLM-based agents playing referential games can self-organize a shared lexicon for novel visual entities by anchoring discovered words to natural language via CLIP embeddings, with convergence dynamics modeled analytically (R² > 0.95).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In unknown environments like planetary or deep-sea exploration, autonomous agents encounter entities lacking names in any human language. A method is needed for agent populations to spontaneously develop shared vocabularies for such referents without human intervention.

Method: The Neuro-Symbolic Lexical Discovery (NSLD) framework: populations of agents use a frozen CLIP vision encoder, a private FAISS vector index, and a text-only LLM to play referential games over out-of-distribution visual referents. Words are anchored to natural language via semantic proximity in CLIP embedding space. Simulations involve up to 20 agents and 10 visual referents. Three analytical models characterize convergence dynamics.

Result: Consensus on a shared alien lexicon was achieved across all simulations. Three analytical models of the convergence dynamics achieved R² > 0.95, providing strong predictive fidelity.

Conclusion: NSLD demonstrates that neuro-symbolic architectures combining frozen vision encoders, vector indices, and LLMs enable emergent lexicon formation. The high-fidelity analytical models represent a first step toward pre-deployment planning for autonomous exploration missions.

Abstract: Populations of autonomous agents deployed in unknown environments (e.g. planetary or deep-sea exploration) must develop shared vocabularies to refer to entities that have no name in any human language. We propose the Neuro-Symbolic Lexical Discovery (NSLD) framework, in which a population of LLM-based agents plays a referential game over out-of-distribution visual referents, autonomously self-organising a shared alien lexicon. Each agent combines a frozen CLIP vision encoder with a private FAISS vector index and a text-only LLM. Crucially, discovered alien words are anchored to natural language via semantic proximity in the embedding space, enlarging the human vocabulary with new perceptually grounded words. Consensus is reached in simulations with populations of up to twenty agents and ten visual referents. Convergence dynamics are characterised through three analytical models achieving R^2 > 0.95, representing a first step towards pre-deployment planning in autonomous exploration missions.

</details>


### [142] [Same Question, Different Answers: Evaluating LLM Reliability Beyond Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22554)
*Kazem Faghih,Yize Cheng,Shoumik Saha,Mobina Pournemat,Armin Gerami,Soheil Feizi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs exhibit significant answer instability under meaning-preserving paraphrases, with models frequently flipping between correct and incorrect answers despite modest changes in aggregate accuracy. A self-paraphrasing strategy can partially recover latent knowledge and improve inference-time performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard accuracy benchmarks mask whether LLMs reliably apply knowledge when the same question is rephrased. The authors aim to assess the stability of model outputs under semantically equivalent but syntactically varied prompts.

Method: The authors evaluate 13 models across four benchmarks (factual QA and mathematical reasoning) by comparing model answers on original questions versus meaning-preserving paraphrases. They measure aggregate accuracy shifts, instance-level mismatch/flip rates, and test a self-paraphrasing strategy to recover latent knowledge at inference time.

Result: Aggregate accuracy changes modestly across paraphrases, but instance-level instability is high: mismatch rates exceed 23%, and answer flip rates (conditioned on initially correct answers) are even higher. Models often produce correct answers for at least one paraphrase, indicating knowledge is present but inconsistently retrieved. Self-paraphrasing partially recovers this knowledge, improving performance.

Conclusion: Standard accuracy metrics obscure substantial instability in LLM reasoning. Evaluating consistency across equivalent inputs provides a more faithful picture of reliability. Self-paraphrasing offers a practical mitigation, but the underlying inconsistency remains a fundamental challenge for deploying LLMs in high-stakes settings.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often achieve strong accuracy on benchmarks, yet it remains unclear how reliably they apply this knowledge when the same question is phrased in different but equivalent ways. In this work, we study how model answers change under meaning-preserving paraphrases across factual question answering and mathematical reasoning tasks. Across four benchmarks and 13 models, we find that model outputs frequently depend on the exact wording of the prompt. While overall accuracy typically changes only modestly across paraphrases, instance-level behavior is far less stable: for many questions, models alternate between correct and incorrect answers depending on phrasing, with mismatch rates reaching more than 23%. Conditioning on questions that are answered correctly in their original form reveals even larger failures measured by answer flip rates, showing that single-prompt correctness is often a poor indicator of reliability. At the same time, we find that models often produce a correct answer for at least one paraphrase of a question, suggesting that the underlying knowledge is present but inconsistently retrieved. Building on this observation, we show that a simple self-paraphrasing strategy can partially recover this latent knowledge and improve performance at inference time. Together, these findings suggest that standard accuracy metrics can mask substantial instability, and that evaluating consistency across equivalent inputs provides a clearer picture of LLM reliability.

</details>


### [143] [Decentralized Granular Access Control for Agentic AI Systems in Critical Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22611)
*Arun Malik,Deepal Jayasinghe,Bradley Klemick,Prachi Shah,Nitish Talasu,Vineet Tushar Trivedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A decentralized, multi-layered access control architecture for autonomous AI agents in cloud infrastructure, featuring compound identity, hierarchical permissions, decentralized policy ownership, and progressive trust escalation. Deployed in production across hundreds of datacenters with zero unauthorized writes over eight months.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional RBAC models fail for autonomous AI agents because agents exhibit stochastic (non-deterministic) behavior, making conventional trust models insufficient. As AI agents are deployed in production infrastructure, a new access control paradigm is needed to govern their access to critical systems securely.

Method: The authors propose a framework with four innovations: (1) compound identity binding agent actions to delegated human authority; (2) a five-level hierarchical permission system (global platform → per-parameter); (3) decentralized policy ownership where tool teams independently govern authorization boundaries; and (4) progressive trust escalation with safety interlocks blocking high-risk autonomous operations. The design is grounded in the OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications (2025) threat taxonomy.

Result: Production deployment at a major cloud provider managing 20+ AI agents and 60+ deterministic playbooks across hundreds of datacenters. The system processed thousands of daily operations with zero unauthorized write operations over eight months. Empirical data on access patterns, denial rates, and layered authorization effectiveness against privilege escalation are presented.

Conclusion: The decentralized, multi-layered architecture effectively mitigates security risks unique to agentic AI systems in production, demonstrating that carefully designed access control with progressive trust and safety interlocks can enable safe autonomous operation at scale.

Abstract: The deployment of autonomous AI agents in production infrastructure introduces fundamental security challenges that traditional role-based access control (RBAC) models cannot address. Unlike deterministic automation, AI agents exhibit stochastic behavior, making conventional trust models insufficient for governing their access to critical systems. This paper presents a decentralized, multi-layered access control architecture designed specifically for agentic AI systems operating in critical cloud infrastructure. Our framework introduces four key innovations: (1) a compound identity model that binds agent actions to delegated human authority, (2) a hierarchical permission system spanning five granularity levels from global platform access to per-parameter constraints, (3) a decentralized policy ownership model where tool teams independently govern their authorization boundaries, and (4) progressive trust escalation with safety interlocks that prevent autonomous agents from executing high-risk operations. We ground our design in the OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications (2025) threat taxonomy and demonstrate how each architectural decision mitigates specific attack vectors. Deployed in production at a major cloud provider managing network infrastructure across hundreds of datacenters, the system enforces granular access control for 20+ specialized AI agents and 60+ deterministic playbooks processing thousands of operations daily while maintaining zero unauthorized write operations over eight months of production deployment. We present empirical data on access pattern distributions, denial rates, and the effectiveness of layered authorization in preventing privilege escalation by non-deterministic actors.

</details>


### [144] [DeepLens Diagnosis Agent: Agentic Workflow Design Lets a Small Reasoning Model Compete with Frontier LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22555)
*Mahmood Bayeshi,Veysel Kocaman,Muhammed Ali Naqvi,Yigit Gul,David Talby*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A five-stage diagnostic reasoning pipeline (DeepLens) built around a small 7B medical LLM with RAG achieves 60.14% top-1 accuracy on DiagnosisArena (915 cases), a +36-point gain over the unharnessed model (23.99%), outperforming larger frontier models at 35–45% lower cost, demonstrating that disciplined workflow constraints outweigh raw parameter count in diagnostic reasoning under uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier LLMs exhibit brittle diagnostic reasoning in single-shot prompting despite strong generalist capabilities and high scores on standard medical benchmarks (88.2%). Medical diagnosis is inherently multi-stage (fact extraction, knowledge consultation, differential generation, evidence-based selection), yet single-pass generation collapses these stages, producing unverifiable outputs unsuitable for high-stakes clinical settings where traceability and auditability are essential.

Method: The DeepLens Diagnosis Agent implements a five-stage harnessing pipeline: (1) structured clinical extraction, (2) disciplined retrieval via RAG, (3) constrained candidate generation, (4) explicit evidence triangulation, and (5) an auditable final decision. The pipeline is centered on JSL Medical Small 7B v2, a small medical reasoning model, with workflow constraints enforced at each stage rather than relying on single-shot generation.

Result: On the 915-case DiagnosisArena benchmark, the agent achieved 60.14% top-1 diagnostic accuracy — highest among small and medium-sized models. The unharnessed model scored only 23.99% (+36pp from workflow). The agent costs $0.0072/case (24K tokens, A100, 24s latency), 35–45% cheaper than Claude Sonnet 4.5 ($0.0110) and Gemini 3.1 Pro ($0.0128), while outperforming them by +9.70pp and +9.17pp respectively. Structured intermediate artifacts enable error localization and stage-level inspectability.

Conclusion: Harnessing — combining model capabilities with disciplined process constraints — can substantially elevate small-model diagnostic performance beyond frontier models, at lower cost. Workflow design can outweigh parameter count or API cost, and the structured, auditable intermediate outputs support traceability and reproducibility requirements in high-stakes medical settings.

Abstract: Medical diagnosis is a multi-stage process: extract facts, consult knowledge, generate a differential analysis, and select the best diagnosis with explanations. Frontier LLMs are strong generalists, but single-shot prompting often yields brittle diagnostic reasoning. We present the DeepLens Diagnosis Agent, a five-stage harnessing pipeline (combining model capabilities with disciplined process constraints) centered on a small medical reasoning model (JSL Medical Small 7B v2) and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The pipeline enforces structured clinical extraction, disciplined retrieval, constrained candidate generation, explicit evidence triangulation, and an auditable final decision. On the 915-case DiagnosisArena benchmark, the agent achieved 60.14% top-1 diagnostic accuracy, the highest among small and medium-sized models. The same model without the agent workflow achieved 23.99%, a +36-point gain from workflow design alone, despite 88.2% on standard medical benchmarks, showing that diagnostic reasoning under uncertainty requires more than knowledge recall. The agent costs USD 0.0072 per case (24K tokens on A100) with 24-second latency, 35-45% cheaper than Claude Sonnet 4.5 (USD 0.0110) and Gemini 3.1 Pro (USD 0.0128) while outperforming them by +9.70pp and +9.17pp. Harnessing can also correct frontier model failures; workflow constraints can outweigh parameter count or API cost.
  Beyond aggregate accuracy, the pipeline produces structured intermediate artifacts that make each stage inspectable and support error localization. These properties support high-stakes settings where traceability, reproducibility, and auditable evidence matter alongside benchmark performance.

</details>


### [145] [CRAFT: Learn the Schema, Execute the Plan](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22642)
*Aakash Kolekar,Sahika Genc,Shahriar Shariat,Bunyamin Sisman,Tibor Mezi,Barbara Poblete,Shree Vandana Kachroo,Calvin Chi,Parth Parmar,Ari Singer,Prayaas Jain,Cindy Barker,Benoit Dumoulin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CRAFT is a two-stage post-training method (supervised fine-tuning + reinforcement learning) that enables enterprise coding agents to internalize schema knowledge and tool-use behaviors, eliminating the need for exhaustive prompt-time schema injection while improving consistency, multi-turn coherence, and reducing token usage by ~9x.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current deployment of enterprise coding agents relies on injecting full schema and tool documentation into every prompt, which increases inference costs, complicates schema evolution, and degrades reliability in multi-turn analytical scenarios. The authors investigate whether stable schema knowledge can instead be acquired through post-training.

Method: CRAFT employs a two-stage recipe: (1) Schema-stripped PLAN supervised fine-tuning (SFT) that learns domain-structured plans and executable behaviors from validated trajectories without prompt-time schema injection; (2) Execution-shaped reinforcement learning (RL) that aligns policy for tool selection, code quality, plan-code consistency, and failure recovery. Training data is curated via a Tri-Gate filter combining execution validation, data-integrity checks, and LLM-judge reasoning audits.

Result: Evaluated in an enterprise advertising analytics environment with 25 schema-linked core entities and 30 agentic workflows, CRAFT improved composite Agent Score by +9.6 pp, consistency by +4.1 pp, multi-turn coherence by +4.2 pp, reduced input-token burden by ~9x, and reduced schema-discovery loops by up to 5x compared to a schema-stuffed baseline.

Conclusion: Post-training schema grounding via CRAFT is a viable alternative to prompt-time schema injection for enterprise coding agents, offering significant efficiency and reliability gains. The authors also discuss deployment tradeoffs, reward-shaping limitations, and infrastructure needs for multi-turn tool-use RL in production settings.

Abstract: Enterprise coding agents translate natural-language analytical requests into executable code over proprietary APIs, schemas, and metric definitions. Yet the prevailing deployment pattern injecting exhaustive schema and tool documentation into each prompt increases inference overhead, complicates schema evolution, and undermines reliability in multi-turn analysis. We investigate whether stable schema knowledge and tool-use behavior can instead be acquired through post-training while preserving the consistency required for production-facing analytics. We present CRAFT, a two-stage post-training recipe for schema-grounded coding agents. First, schema-stripped PLAN supervised fine-tuning learns domain-structured plans and executable behaviors from validated trajectories without exhaustive prompt-time schema injection. Second, execution-shaped reinforcement learning aligns the policy for tool selection, code quality, plan-code consistency, and recovery from failed executions. Training trajectories are curated through a Tri-Gate filter combining execution validation, data-integrity checks, and LLM-judge reasoning audit. We evaluate CRAFT for planned rollout in advertising analytics, covering campaign performance analysis, metric drill-downs, entity-level performance analysis, and multi-turn analytical refinement. The enterprise evaluation environment incorporates beta APIs as the agent-facing tool surface and spans 25 schema-linked core entities and 30 agentic workflows. Relative to a schema-stuffed baseline, CRAFT improves composite Agent Score by +9.6 pp, consistency by +4.1 pp, and multi-turn coherence by +4.2 pp, while reducing input-token burden by approximately 9x and schema-discovery loops by up to 5x. We further report deployment tradeoffs, reward-shaping limitations, and training-infrastructure extensions required for multi-turn tool-use reinforcement learning in enterprise settings.

</details>


### [146] [MIITA: Memory-Induced Inference-Time Adaptation for Continual Learning with Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22556)
*Dong Li,Yanchi Liu,Xujiang Zhao,Wei Cheng,Zhengzhang Chen,Xintao Wu,Zhong Chen,Chen Zhao,Haifeng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MIITA is a continual learning framework for small language models that stores compact correction-direction prototypes and applies them at inference time via gated hidden-state adaptation, avoiding parameter updates and mitigating catastrophic forgetting under constrained storage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small language models (SLMs) need continual learning for real-world adaptation but suffer from catastrophic forgetting due to limited parameters. Existing memory-based methods for LLMs assume abundant storage and strong in-context reasoning, which SLMs lack. A new approach is needed that works under constrained storage without relying on backbone updates.

Method: MIITA stores supervised experiences as compact correction-direction prototypes with semantic anchors during training. At inference time, it retrieves relevant prototypes using semantic and uncertainty-based cues, then applies the retrieved directions through gated temporary hidden-state adaptation. This enables non-destructive reuse of past supervision without backbone updates, prompt extensions, or test-time backpropagation. A local theoretical analysis connects the design to first-order loss reduction, uncertainty-guided retrieval, and directional coverage.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse supervised continual learning settings demonstrate that MIITA consistently improves final performance and mitigates forgetting under fixed memory budgets.

Conclusion: MIITA provides an effective solution for continual supervised learning in small language models under storage constraints by decoupling knowledge retention from parameter updates through memory-induced inference-time adaptation.

Abstract: Continual learning (CL) is essential for small language models (SLMs) to adapt to evolving real-world needs in resource-constrained deployments. However, directly updating their limited parameter space causes catastrophic forgetting. While memory-based methods naturally address this by decoupling knowledge retention from parameters, existing approaches designed for large language models (LLMs) rely on abundant storage and strong in-context reasoning that SLMs lack. To address these challenges, we propose MIITA, a Memory-Induced Inference-Time Adaptation framework for supervised CL under constrained storage. MIITA stores supervised experiences as compact correction-direction prototypes with semantic anchors, and retrieves them at inference time using semantic and uncertainty-based cues. The retrieved directions are applied through gated temporary hidden-state adaptation, enabling non-destructive reuse of past supervision without backbone updates, prompt extensions, or test-time backpropagation. A local theoretical analysis links this design to first-order loss reduction, uncertainty-guided retrieval, and directional coverage for retaining old-stage knowledge. Extensive experiments across diverse supervised CL settings show that MIITA consistently improves final performance and mitigates forgetting under fixed memory budgets.

</details>


### [147] [A Vocabulary for Multi-Agent Automated Research Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22682)
*Bardiya Akhbari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a standardized vocabulary for describing and comparing automated research systems (multi-agent or single-agent), decomposing design choices into eight key dimensions. The framework also introduces the concepts of generative taste and evaluative taste to separate two distinct failure modes of such systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated research systems are proliferating with diverse, ad-hoc designs, making them difficult to describe, compare, and systematically improve. There is no shared language to articulate structural design choices (e.g., when agents should communicate, how information persists across runs), and critiques like "lacking taste" conflate distinct problems. A unified vocabulary is needed to turn these structural questions into testable hypotheses.

Method: The authors propose a vocabulary organized around eight dimensions: (1) agent identity, (2) available operations, (3) invocation permissions, (4) inter-agent communication, (5) information visibility within/across runs, (6) action selection mechanisms, (7) initialization, and (8) output evaluation. A trajectory is defined as one run from input task to output artifact, with stochasticity inducing a distribution over trajectories. The framework incorporates the evaluator as an explicit system component and decomposes "taste" into generative taste (rate of proposing novel trajectories before scoring) and evaluative taste (gap between proxy score and true quality). The vocabulary is instantiated on several recent autoresearch systems to demonstrate coverage.

Result: The vocabulary successfully captures and differentiates the design choices of several recent automated research systems spanning diverse architectures, demonstrating broad applicability. The generative/evaluative taste decomposition provides a precise diagnostic framework for two previously conflated failure modes.

Conclusion: The proposed vocabulary provides a common language that transforms opaque design decisions into testable, comparable choices, facilitating more principled progress in automated research system design. Separating generative and evaluative taste clarifies distinct avenues for improvement.

Abstract: We introduce a vocabulary for automated research systems built from one or more agents to make their design choices easier to describe and compare. The vocabulary specifies 1) who the agents are, 2) what operations are available in the system, 3) who may invoke them, 4) how agents communicate, 5) what information is visible within and across runs, 6) how the next action is chosen, 7) how a run begins, and 8) how outputs are evaluated. A trajectory records one run from the input task to the returned artifact. Because agents, operations, and initialization may be stochastic, repeated runs on the same task induce a distribution over trajectories rather than a single behavior.
  Our vocabulary turns structural design questions, such as when agents should communicate, gain or lose a capability, or carry information across runs, into testable choices. It also makes the evaluator a component of the system, since reported gains depend on how closely the proxy score matches true quality. That separation also splits the vague complaint that these systems lack taste into two failures with different solutions. Generative taste is the rate at which a system proposes novel trajectories before any score is observed, and evaluative taste is the gap between the proxy score and the quality it should match. We instantiate the vocabulary on recent autoresearch systems to illustrate that it covers designs that differ widely in structure.

</details>


### [148] [Codifying the Judge: Scalable Evaluation via Program Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22561)
*Tzu-Heng Huang,Shengqi Qiu,Frederic Sala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PAJAMA replaces costly LLM-as-a-judge evaluation with programmatic judges distilled from LLM decision logic, matching 13B LLM performance while eliminating per-sample API costs, improving transparency, and enabling effective reward model training at dramatically lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-a-judge, while standard for automated evaluation, suffers from high API costs, significant latency, and opaque decision-making that undermines scalability and reliability. A cheaper, faster, and more transparent alternative is needed.

Method: The authors propose program distillation: distilling an LLM judge's decision logic into a committee of executable programs that score candidates directly. Their system, PAJAMA, synthesizes these programmatic judges, aggregates their decisions via a joint verdict, and employs a fallback mechanism that selectively escalates low-confidence cases to an LLM for adjudication.

Result: Programmatic judges match the performance of a 13B-parameter LLM judge across five datasets and four model families. Using program outputs as routing signals, PAJAMA improves both accuracy and throughput, advancing the Pareto frontier. On RewardBench, a reward model distilled from program verdicts outperforms one trained on proprietary LLM labels at two orders of magnitude lower API cost.

Conclusion: Program distillation offers a viable, efficient, and transparent alternative to LLM-as-a-judge, with strong empirical performance in both evaluation and reward modeling at a fraction of the cost.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-judge has become the standard for automated evaluation, but it suffers from high cost, significant latency, and opaque decisions -- limitations that undermine its scalability and reliability. We address these with a simple, efficient alternative: program distillation. Instead of prompting an LLM at the evaluation time, we distill its decision logic into a committee of programs that score candidates directly. These programmatic judges offer transparency, are easily inspected or edited, and eliminate per-sample API costs. Building on this notion, we introduce PAJAMA, a system that synthesizes programs as judges, aggregates their decisions into a joint verdict, and incorporates a fallback mechanism to selectively escalate low-confidence cases to an LLM. Across five datasets and four model families, we show that programmatic judges can match the performance of a 13B-size LLM judge. When using program outputs as routing signals, PAJAMA improves both accuracy and throughput and advances the Pareto frontier. Beyond evaluation, programmatic judges produce cheap and effective reward signals: on RewardBench, a reward model distilled from programs' verdicts outperforms one trained on a proprietary LLM's labels at two orders of magnitude lower API cost.

</details>


### [149] [Agent Team Work Zone: An Automated, Persistent Workspace for Long-Lived Coding Agent Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22917)
*Shouren Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ATWZ introduces a filesystem-based operations layer for Claude Code's Agent Teams that treats agents as human employees, preserving their working state in filesystem directories ("workstations") to enable state recovery after compaction or process termination, reduce prompt-writing overhead, and mitigate agentic technical debt.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Claude Code's Agent Teams feature suffers from four key limitations in long-term agentic workflows: (1) team working state is irrecoverable after process termination; (2) conversation compaction erodes detailed working knowledge; (3) decisions and operations become trapped in compacted old chats, creating agentic "technical debt"; and (4) task handoffs require repetitive, heavy prompt writing.

Method: ATWZ implements a filesystem-based operations layer around Claude Code's native Agent Teams. The core design principle is to treat each agent/teammate as a human employee and preserve their working state in files within a dedicated "workstation" directory. This directory also houses skills, hooks, and scripts that maintain these files. Agents can periodically back up working state, send documents to one another, and restore teams with a single command after process termination.

Result: ATWZ enables periodic backup and recovery of agent working state after compaction, single-command team restoration after process termination, mitigation of agentic technical debt by externalizing state from chat history, and significant reduction in prompt-writing effort through agent-to-agent document passing.

Conclusion: By externalizing agent working state to a persistent filesystem layer and modeling agent interactions after human workplace collaboration, ATWZ effectively addresses the fragility, knowledge erosion, and maintenance burdens that undermine long-term LLM coding agent workflows in Claude Code.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents have significantly improved coding and programming workflows. Claude Code, in particular, is one of the most powerful LLM coding agents and is capable of conducting complex coding tasks. However, several drawbacks can undermine long-term agentic workflows. (1) Irrecoverable agent teams: The Agent Teams feature is powerful, but the working state accumulated by each teammate is lost and cannot be resumed once the process stops, for example, when a terminal is closed. (2) Compaction erodes working detail: Compaction condenses the conversation into a summary, causing an agent's working details to become vague. (3) Agentic "technical debt": Over time, a user's decisions and the agents' operations become trapped in compacted old chats, making the project increasingly difficult to maintain and review. (4) Heavy prompt writing: Assigning or handing off tasks requires users to repeatedly write long prompts to achieve the expected agentic performance. We propose ATWZ (Agent Team Work Zone), a filesystem-based operations layer built around Claude Code's native Agent Teams that addresses these problems. Its central design principle is to treat each agent and teammate as a human employee and preserve their important working state in files stored in a dedicated directory called a "workstation," together with the skills, hooks, and scripts that use and maintain these files. With ATWZ, an agent team can periodically back up its working state, allowing an agent's knowledge to be recovered after compaction. After a process ends, the team can be restored with a single command. These features also substantially mitigate the agentic "technical debt" described above. Moreover, within ATWZ, agent "employees" can send documents to one another, greatly reducing the effort required to write prompts.

</details>


### [150] [SF-AMS: Strategic Forgetting for Structured Memory in LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22562)
*Ning Yang,Siqi Li,Miaoxin Shen,Yuan Zhou,Meng Zhang,Tong Li,Haijun Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SF-AMS introduces a utility-driven memory management framework for LLM agents that dynamically scores memory importance using redundancy and temporal signals, creating a hierarchical memory structure that outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on long-context reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context dependencies bottleneck LLM agents because static retrieval and heuristic decay fail to distinguish high-utility information from noise, degrading multi-step reasoning. A mechanism is needed to dynamically model memory importance for compact, high-utility retention.

Method: SF-AMS employs a utility-driven survival mechanism that updates memory importance scores based on usage redundancy and temporal signals, inducing a hierarchical memory structure that retains stable entity-consistent information. A Composite Importance Scoring module integrates semantic and entity-level signals to improve retrieval robustness.

Result: On LoCoMo and LongMemEval-s, SF-AMS achieves consistent gains over LightMem, MemO, and A-Mem. Notable improvements: +9.65 F1 in multi-hop reasoning (Qwen2.5-7B), +6.91 F1 in temporal reasoning (GPT-4o-mini), and +6.53 F1 in open-domain tasks, demonstrating strong cross-backbone generalization.

Conclusion: Modeling memory importance as a dynamic utility signal is critical for reliable long-context reasoning in LLM agents, as evidenced by substantial and consistent improvements across diverse tasks and model backbones.

Abstract: Managing long-context dependencies remains a primary bottleneck in LLM agents, as redundant and irrelevant information can degrade multi-step reasoning. Strategic Forgetting for Agent Memory Systems (SF-AMS) is proposed as a framework for maintaining compact high-utility memory by modeling the long-term importance of memory units. SF-AMS replaces static retrieval and heuristic decay with a utility-driven survival mechanism that updates memory importance from usage redundancy and temporal signals, inducing a hierarchical memory structure that prioritizes stable entity-consistent information while filtering noise. On top of this, Composite Importance Scoring integrates semantic and entity level signals to improve retrieval robustness. Experiments on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-s show consistent gains over strong state of the art baselines including LightMem MemO and A-Mem. The largest improvement appears in multi-hop reasoning under Qwen2.5-7B where SF-AMS achieves plus 9.65 F1 over the strongest baseline followed by temporal reasoning under GPT-4o-mini plus 6.91 F1 and open-domain tasks plus 6.53 F1 demonstrating strong cross backbone generalization. These results show that modeling memory importance as a dynamic utility signal is critical for reliable long-context reasoning.

</details>


### [151] [Let AI Agents Translate Networks, Not Reason About Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22947)
*Hongyu Hè,Maria Apostolaki*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TypoNet uses LLMs to translate network artifacts into verifiable formal models, then relies on a solver for reliable reasoning, challenging the trend of end-to-end autonomous AI agents in network operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production networks lack formal models because manual construction requires rare expertise and is hard to maintain amid frequent changes. While LLMs can translate artifacts into formal logic, trusting them with long-horizon reasoning over complex networks is unreliable. The authors argue for confining AI to verifiable translation and using solvers for reasoning.

Method: TypoNet automatically constructs and validates a symbolic formal model from a network's own artifacts (configurations, topology, routing state) using LLM-based translation. This general model is then specialized for tasks like reachability verification, change-impact analysis, and root-cause analysis. Correctness of translation is formally verifiable, and a solver handles long-horizon reasoning over the model.

Result: Preliminary evaluation on an emulated production-scale WAN shows TypoNet answers operational questions (reachability verification, change-impact analysis) faster, cheaper, and more reliably than an LLM alone. When used as a tool for an AI agent, it boosts fault localization at lower cost.

Conclusion: The paper makes the case for an architecture where AI builds verifiable network models and relies on a solver for reliable reasoning, rather than deploying autonomous end-to-end AI agents for network operations.

Abstract: A formal model enables verifying reachability, localizing an outage, or anticipating the blast radius of a change. Yet, virtually no production network has one, since writing a model by hand demands rare expertise and is hard to keep current as the network changes frequently. At its core, network modeling is a typographical exercise: it translates network artifacts (e.g., configurations, topology, and routing state) into rules in formal logic. Translation of this kind is what large language models (LLMs) nowadays do well. Unlike free-form AI reasoning, such translation can be formally verified.
  Once modeling is no longer the bottleneck, trusting AI to reason over large, complex networks no longer makes sense. Our position therefore cuts against the prevailing race to put autonomous AI agents in charge end-to-end. We instead confine AI to translation and rely on a solver for reliable long-horizon reasoning, building a reusable formal model of general network behavior that can then be specialized to specific tasks, e.g., root-cause analysis (RCA). We build TypoNet that constructs and validates a symbolic model of an emulated production-scale WAN from the network's own artifacts. Our preliminary evaluation shows TypoNet helps in two ways. On its own, TypoNet answers operational questions (e.g., reachability verification and change-impact analysis) faster, more cheaply, and more reliably than an LLM. As a tool for an AI agent, TypoNet boosts fault localization at lower cost. The result makes the case for AI that builds verifiable network models and relies on a solver for reliable long-horizon reasoning.

</details>


### [152] [Synthetic Scenario Generation for Evaluation of Industry 4.0 Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22563)
*Sagar Chethan Kumar,Rohith Kanathur,Dhaval Patel,Kaoutar El Maghraoui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper extends AssetOpsBench with a Smart Grid Transformer asset class and IEC diagnostic tools, and introduces ScenarioGeneratorAgent—a synthetic scenario generation pipeline with hybrid validation-and-repair. Optimizations yield an 8× runtime reduction for 50 scenarios while maintaining quality (74.2 vs. 73.8 composite score).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial agent benchmarks like AssetOpsBench rely on manually authored scenarios with limited asset class coverage, making them costly and narrow. There is a need for scalable, standards-grounded synthetic scenario generation that covers diverse asset classes, telemetry, failure modes, and domain standards without sacrificing quality.

Method: The authors (1) extend AssetOpsBench with a Smart Grid Transformer asset class and four IEC-grounded diagnostic tools (health-index prediction, dissolved-gas analysis, winding-temperature assessment, load-profile assessment). (2) They introduce ScenarioGeneratorAgent, a pipeline that constructs evidence-grounded asset profiles, allocates coverage-aware scenario budgets across operational domains, and generates candidates through a hybrid validation-and-repair loop enforcing schema validity, tool reachability, physical plausibility, standards alignment, and deduplication. (3) Scalability optimizations include two-level caching, parallel focus-group generation, thread-pool offloading, batched LLM calls, and early rejection filtering.

Result: On Smart Grid Transformer scenario generation, the optimized pipeline achieves an 8× end-to-end runtime reduction for 50 scenarios. The composite quality score is 74.2 ± 1.9 for the optimized version versus 73.8 ± 3.0 for the unoptimized baseline, demonstrating that quality is preserved or marginally improved.

Conclusion: Standards-grounded synthetic scenario generation can efficiently expand industrial-agent benchmarks without sacrificing scenario quality. The ScenarioGeneratorAgent pipeline combined with IEC diagnostic tool integration offers a scalable path for broadening benchmark coverage across asset classes.

Abstract: Industrial agent benchmarks require realistic evaluation scenarios that integrate telemetry, failure modes, maintenance records, and domain standards. However, existing benchmarks such as AssetOpsBench rely on manually authored scenarios and cover a limited set of asset classes. We extend AssetOpsBench with a Smart Grid Transformer asset class and four IEC-grounded diagnostic tools for health-index prediction, dissolved-gas analysis, winding-temperature assessment, and load-profile assessment. We further introduce ScenarioGeneratorAgent, a pipeline for synthetic industrial-agent scenario generation. The pipeline constructs evidence-grounded asset profiles, allocates coverage-aware scenario budgets across operational domains, and generates candidates through a hybrid validation-and-repair loop that enforces schema validity, tool reachability, physical plausibility, standards alignment, and deduplication. To improve scalability, we apply two-level caching, parallel focus-group generation, thread-pool offloading, batched LLM calls, and early rejection filtering. On Smart Grid Transformer scenario generation, these optimizations reduce end-to-end runtime by $8\times$ for 50 scenarios while preserving quality, achieving a composite quality score of $74.2 \pm 1.9$ compared with $73.8 \pm 3.0$ for the unoptimized baseline. These results show that standards-grounded synthetic scenario generation can efficiently expand industrial-agent benchmarks without sacrificing scenario quality.

</details>


### [153] [Loss-Aware Feature-Map Pruning in Convolutional Neural Networks Using Multi-Armed Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22564)
*Salem Ameen,Sunil Vadera*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A loss-aware feature-map pruning framework using multi-armed bandits (UCB1, Thompson Sampling) is proposed to remove redundant CNN channels. Bandit algorithms efficiently identify and rank expendable feature maps via temporary masking and loss-change rewards, achieving structured pruning that preserves accuracy close to unpruned models while significantly outperforming greedy and magnitude-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CNNs contain redundant feature maps that inflate storage and inference costs. Prior pruning methods often rely on heuristic importance metrics (e.g., weight magnitude) that are not loss-aware, or require costly per-channel evaluation. A principled, efficient method is needed to identify which feature maps can be safely removed with minimal accuracy degradation.

Method: Each candidate feature map is modeled as an arm in a multi-armed bandit. Over a fixed play budget, one map per play is temporarily masked, a mini-batch is evaluated, and the observed loss change is converted into a safe-removal reward. After the budget is exhausted, maps are ranked by learned scores, and the top-k are permanently pruned along with their filters, biases, and corresponding next-layer input-channel kernels. UCB1 and Thompson Sampling serve as the bandit strategies.

Result: On LeNet/MNIST, CIFAR-10/100, SVHN, CUB-200-2011, and Oxford Flowers 102, UCB1 and Thompson Sampling preserve accuracy close to unpruned baselines while removing feature maps and reducing convolution computation. Friedman and Nemenyi tests show UCB1 achieves the highest mean rank, followed by Thompson Sampling; both significantly outperform greedy and magnitude-based pruning and are statistically comparable to the original unpruned model.

Conclusion: Multi-armed bandits provide an effective, loss-aware approach to structured CNN pruning. UCB1 is particularly well-suited, offering a principled exploration–exploitation trade-off that yields statistically superior rankings over heuristic methods while maintaining near-original accuracy.

Abstract: Convolutional neural networks often contain redundant feature maps that increase storage and inference cost. This paper presents a loss-aware feature-map pruning framework using multi-armed bandits. Feature-map pruning is structured because it removes complete convolutional output channels and their producing filters rather than isolated scalar weights. Each candidate feature map is treated as an arm. At each play time, one map is temporarily masked and evaluated on a sampled mini-batch; the map is then restored and the observed loss change is converted into a safe-removal reward. After a fixed play budget, candidate maps are ranked by learned scores and the top-k maps are permanently removed with their filters, biases and corresponding next-layer input-channel kernels. The study evaluates UCB1 and Thompson Sampling, compares them with direct/oracle-style evaluation on LeNet/MNIST, and extends the evaluation to MNIST, CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, SVHN, CUB-200-2011 and Oxford Flowers 102. Results show that UCB1 and Thompson Sampling preserve accuracy close to unpruned models while removing feature maps and reducing convolutional computation. Friedman and Nemenyi tests show that UCB1 obtains the highest mean rank, followed by Thompson Sampling; both significantly outperform greedy and magnitude-based pruning while remaining statistically comparable to the original unpruned model.

</details>


### [154] [Separating Capability from Permission: A Governance Framework for Agentic AI Autonomy Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23438)
*Haining Zheng,Qian Dong,Rodolfo K. Depena,Jonathan D. Bhatia,Feng Xiao,Peng Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a governance framework that decouples what AI agents are technically capable of doing (Autonomous Capability Levels, ACL) from what they should be permitted to do (Allowed Autonomy Levels, AAL), and demonstrates its application in an enterprise setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems become more agentic, discussions of autonomy conflate technical capability with operational permission, creating governance gaps. The authors aim to provide a clear separation between capability and authorization to enable safer, more accountable deployment of agentic AI.

Method: The authors define two orthogonal constructs: Autonomous Capability Levels (ACL) characterizing inherent technical abilities, and Allowed Autonomy Levels (AAL) defining authorized degrees of autonomy. They propose a structured taxonomy spanning five levels (reactive execution, decision support, supervised action, goal-directed autonomy, delegated operational authority), a risk-aware decision process for assigning AAL, and validate the framework through a case study of a deployed enterprise data engineering agent.

Result: The framework demonstrates that a system assessed at a high ACL can be deliberately constrained to a lower AAL based on risk, reversibility, and organizational readiness. The enterprise data engineering agent case study illustrates how control, reversibility, and accountability mechanisms change across autonomy levels, and how the risk-aware assignment process operates in practice.

Conclusion: Distinguishing authorization from capability provides practical guidance for the design, deployment, and governance of agentic AI systems. The AAL/ACL separation enables organizations to make deliberate, risk-aware decisions about how much autonomy to grant, independent of what the system can technically do.

Abstract: As AI systems increasingly exhibit agentic behavior, discussions of autonomy often conflate what systems are technically capable of doing with what they should be permitted to do in practice. This paper introduces a governance framework that explicitly separates Allowed Autonomy Levels (AAL), which define the degree of autonomy an AI agent is authorized to exercise given risk, oversight, and accountability considerations, from Autonomous Capability Levels (ACL), which characterize an agent's inherent technical abilities. We present a structured set of autonomy levels spanning reactive execution, decision support, supervised action, goal-directed autonomy, and delegated operational authority, and describe how control, reversibility, and accountability change as autonomy increases. To operationalize this framework, we propose a risk-aware decision process for assigning allowed autonomy, analyze how risk and accountability evolve across autonomy levels, and demonstrate its application through a deployed enterprise data engineering agent, illustrating how a system assessed at a high capability level can be deliberately constrained to a lower allowed autonomy based on risk, reversibility, and organizational readiness. By distinguishing authorization from capability, this work provides practical guidance for the design, deployment, and governance of Agentic AI systems.

</details>


### [155] [DSTFView: Multi-View Cloud-Edge Workload Forecasting with Dual-Input Spatio-Temporal-Frequency Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22565)
*Qingzhong Li,Hui Ma,Yajun Zhang,Qingchang Ma,Zhou Long*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DSTFView is a dual-input spatio-temporal-frequency multi-view workload forecasting framework for cloud-edge environments that jointly models closeness and period dependencies across spatial, temporal, and frequency domains, with an adaptive fusion mechanism for handling abrupt changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge platforms increasingly require support for latency-sensitive, highly concurrent, and reliability-critical AI inference workloads. Existing methods fail to balance multidimensional feature modeling with forecasting efficiency in collaborative cloud-edge environments.

Method: DSTFView employs a dual-input architecture that jointly models closeness and period dependencies. It extracts spatial, temporal, and frequency-domain dependencies through a multi-view design. An adaptive fusion mechanism dynamically adjusts the contribution of each view to better capture abrupt workload changes.

Result: Experimental results on CPU and TP (throughput) datasets show that DSTFView consistently outperforms representative baselines across multiple forecasting horizons and evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: DSTFView effectively addresses the workload forecasting challenge in collaborative cloud-edge environments by integrating spatio-temporal-frequency modeling with adaptive multi-view fusion, achieving superior and robust forecasting performance.

Abstract: With the widespread deployment of edge-side AI inference, edge platforms are increasingly required to support latency-sensitive, highly concurrent, and reliability-critical applications. However, existing methods often struggle to balance multidimensional feature modeling and forecasting efficiency in collaborative cloud-edge environments. To address this issue, we propose DSTFView, a dual-input spatio-temporal-frequency multi-view workload forecasting framework for collaborative cloud-edge environments. It jointly models closeness and period dependencies and extracts spatial, temporal, and frequency-domain dependencies. Besides, it designs an adaptive fusion mechanism and adjusts the contribution of each view to capture abrupt changes. Experimental results on the CPU and TP datasets demonstrate that DSTFView consistently outperforms representative baselines across multiple forecasting horizons and evaluation metrics.

</details>
