### [156] [MedLoCoMo: A Long-Context Multi-Session Medical Dialogue Benchmark for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22566)
*Zeyu Zhang,Ziqing Wang,Kaize Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedLoCoMo is a benchmark for evaluating long-context clinical reasoning over multi-admission patient histories, featuring 100 longitudinal patient timelines with synthesized doctor-patient dialogues and QA items across single-admission, cross-admission, and adversarial unanswerable settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medical QA benchmarks focus on short-context knowledge or single-document grounding, failing to test whether LLMs can effectively use, connect, and appropriately abstain when reasoning over longitudinal patient histories across multiple admissions.

Method: The authors construct MedLoCoMo from deidentified MIMIC-IV and MIMIC-IV-Note records by building admission-level clinical packets, synthesizing grounded doctor-patient conversations, and generating evidence-linked QA items in three categories: single-admission (localized evidence), cross-admission (requires connecting information across admissions), and adversarial unanswerable (tests abstention). The benchmark comprises 100 patient timelines.

Result: Cross-admission reasoning is consistently harder than localized evidence use across all evaluated baselines, even for models with long context windows or those employing external memory or retrieval-augmented methods. The average conversation spans 1,669.8 turns across 29.7 sessions with 74,512.2 tokens.

Conclusion: MedLoCoMo reveals a significant gap in current LLMs' ability to perform longitudinal, cross-admission clinical reasoning, highlighting the need for better long-context and multi-hop reasoning capabilities in medical AI systems.

Abstract: MedLoCoMo is a Medical Long-Context Memory benchmark for patient-specific clinical reasoning over multi-admission medical dialogue. Existing medical QA benchmarks largely test short context knowledge or single document grounding, leaving open whether LLMs can use, connect, and abstain over longitudinal patient histories. We build MedLoCoMo from deidentified MIMIC-IV and MIMIC-IV-Note records by constructing admission-level clinical packets, synthesizing grounded doctor-patient conversations, and generating evidence linked QA items over single-admission, cross-admission, and adversarial unanswerable settings. The benchmark contains 100 patient timelines averaging 1,669.8 turns, 29.7 sessions, and 74,512.2 tokens per conversation. Across the evaluated baselines, cross-admission reasoning is consistently harder than localized evidence use, even when models have long context windows or use external memory or retrieval methods. The code and MedLoCoMo benchmark release is available at https://github.com/leozzy13/MedLoCoMo for use and reproducibility.

</details>


### [157] [Grading the Narrators: An Isnad-Rijal Framework for Claim-Level Provenance in Multi-Agent Knowledge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24117)
*Ali Zahid Raja*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Transfers the methodology of classical Islamic hadith science (isnad chains, narrator grading, weakest-link evaluation, corroboration, content criticism) to modern multi-agent AI knowledge systems, providing a formal mapping, relational schema, and decision matrix, validated on 20,000 physics textbook claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern multi-agent systems accumulate knowledge through autonomous transformation chains, but existing provenance solutions only record execution traces without offering an operational framework for graded, per-domain transmitter reliability with completeness semantics, transformation-typed aggregation, decoupled content criticism, and serve/review/quarantine routing.

Method: Formal mapping of hadith-science concepts (isnad, rijal, matn criticism) to multi-agent pipelines; a relational schema implementing claim chains and a graded narrator registry; a decision matrix that combines chain grade with content criticism; evaluation on 20,000 claims from real physics textbooks.

Result: The evaluation validated weakest-link quarantine and independent-chain corroboration; reported a partial failure of the grade-recovery loop (missed the highest-fault narrator); and reported two analyses as inconclusive, including a matched-coverage comparison the framework could not reach with the reference content critic.

Conclusion: The paper explicitly delineates which claims the evidence supports and which it does not, demonstrating both the promise and current limitations of transferring hadith-inspired verification methodology to AI knowledge systems.

Abstract: Modern multi-agent knowledge systems increasingly accumulate knowledge through chains of autonomous transformations rather than direct retrieval. Existing provenance work records what happened - execution traces, tool calls, evidence links - and source-reliability estimation is long established (truth discovery, reputation systems). What is missing is an operational framework that attaches graded, per-domain transmitter reliability to claim-level transmission chains, with completeness semantics, transformation-typed aggregation, decoupled content criticism, and serve/review/quarantine routing.
  Classical Islamic hadith science confronted a structurally similar problem: deciding whether knowledge transmitted through chains of human narrators should be accepted. Over centuries it developed a rigorous methodology - isnad (a complete transmission chain attached to every claim), rijal (systematic grading of each narrator's integrity and precision), weakest-link chain evaluation, corroboration through independent chains, and matn criticism (content evaluated independently of chain quality). This paper transfers that methodology to AI system design.
  We contribute a formal mapping from hadith-science concepts to multi-agent pipelines, a relational schema implementing claim chains and a graded narrator registry, a decision matrix combining chain grade with content criticism, and an evaluation on 20,000 claims from real physics textbooks. The evaluation validates weakest-link quarantine and independent-chain corroboration; reports a partial failure of the grade-recovery loop, which missed the highest-fault narrator; and reports two analyses as inconclusive, including a matched-coverage comparison the framework could not reach with the reference content critic. The paper is explicit throughout about which claims the evidence does and does not yet support.

</details>


### [158] [Keyword Matters: Unveiling the Energy Sensitivity of On-Device LLM Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22568)
*Ruiyi Tao,Xiaolong Tu,Haoxin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper empirically investigates how prompt wording—specifically imperative keywords and instruction structure—affects energy consumption during on-device LLM inference on smartphones, revealing that prompt engineering can serve as a lightweight strategy for energy efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device LLM inference faces high energy consumption on battery-powered hardware. While model compression and runtime acceleration are well-studied, the impact of prompt design on energy efficiency remains underexplored.

Method: Empirical study using real power measurements collected on a smartphone, analyzing how linguistic features (imperative keywords, instruction structure) affect decoding length and total energy consumption.

Result: Consistent energy consumption differences were observed across different verbs and tasks, demonstrating that prompt wording measurably influences energy usage during on-device inference.

Conclusion: Prompt engineering is a viable, lightweight lever for improving the energy efficiency of on-device LLM inference, offering a complementary approach to existing hardware- and model-level optimizations.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed on mobile and embedded devices to improve privacy and reduce network latency. Yet on-device inference faces a fundamental constraint: high energy consumption on battery-powered, resource-limited hardware. While model compression and runtime acceleration have been widely studied, the effect of \emph{prompt design} on energy efficiency remains underexplored. This paper presents an empirical study of the relationship between prompt wording and energy consumption for on-device LLMs. Using real power measurements collected on a smartphone, we quantify how linguistic features, particularly imperative keywords and instruction structure, affect decoding length and total energy. Our results show consistent energy differences across verbs and tasks, indicating that prompt engineering is a lightweight lever for improving energy efficiency.

</details>


### [159] [Execution-Grounded Security Testing for Coding Agents in Software Engineering Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22569)
*Yifei Ge,Weisong Sun,Jinkun Xiao,Yuchen Chen,Yebo Feng,Peizhuo Lv,Xia Feng,Chunrong Fang,Zhihong Zhao,Zhenyu Chen,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a red-team testing framework that evaluates coding agents' security at the execution layer by embedding unsafe operations into routine software engineering tasks, revealing that agents can be induced to perform harmful system modifications at rates up to 73.61% when malicious intent is disguised within plausible workloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding agents are increasingly deployed in system operations with tool-use capabilities that can persistently modify artifacts, environments, and systems. Existing security evaluations focus on what agents say (output filtering), not what they actually do. Unsafe actions—such as inserting hooks into startup scripts—can persist beyond the interaction and abuse delegated privileges, making this a system-level security problem requiring execution-grounded testing.

Method: The authors propose an execution-grounded red-team testing framework that uses observable sandbox evidence (tool invocations, runtime traces, file-system diffs) to probe the execution-layer security boundary. Unsafe operations are embedded into routine software engineering workloads—unit testing, regression testing, crash reproduction, and validation. An execution oracle guides iterative refinement when initial probes are rejected or fail, reformulating attacks as plausible engineering tasks to bypass agent safeguards.

Result: Across multiple agent frameworks and model backbones, the red-team workload reformulation achieved 73.61% verified unsafe execution on code carriers and 53.93% on text carriers. The results demonstrate that task disguise—hiding risky intent inside plausible engineering tasks—effectively induces coding agents to carry out unsafe system actions, revealing persistent insecurity under operational deployment conditions.

Conclusion: Coding agents in system operations remain vulnerable to execution-layer attacks when malicious intent is disguised within legitimate engineering tasks. This highlights the critical need for stronger security testing frameworks that evaluate actual agent behavior (not just outputs) and more robust safeguards at the execution boundary.

Abstract: Coding agents are increasingly integrated into system operations, where their tool use can directly modify project artifacts, execution environments, and the underlying system. For example, if a coding agent inserts a hook into a system startup or configuration script, that change can persist after the interaction, be triggered later, and abuse delegated user or system privileges to modify the system. This makes security testing a system problem: the key question is not only what the agent says, but what it actually does to the surrounding environment. We present an execution-grounded red-team testing framework for probing this execution-layer security boundary using observable sandbox evidence, including tool invocations, runtime traces, and file-system diffs. Our framework embeds target unsafe operations into routine software engineering workloads, including unit testing, regression testing, crash reproduction, and validation, and uses an execution oracle to guide refinement when an initial probe is rejected or fails. Across multiple agent frameworks and model backbones, our red-team workload reformulation substantially increases verified unsafe execution, reaching 73.61% on code carriers and 53.93% on text carriers. These results show that coding agents in system operations remain insecure under task disguise: once risky intent is hidden inside plausible engineering tasks, the agent can be induced to carry out unsafe actions on the surrounding system. More broadly, coding agents in system operations still demand stronger security testing and safeguards.

</details>


### [160] [Reference Feature Atlases for Mechanistic Auditing of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22570)
*Rui Wu,Tong Che*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a "reference feature atlas" — a sparse feature library trained once on a reference panel of models, then reused to audit new target models by fitting only a linear decoder. This creates two complementary views: an atlas channel that maps targets onto already-interpreted features, and a residual channel that captures what the atlas cannot reconstruct, serving as an explicit novelty/audit signal. Experiments on 7-9B instruction-tuned models show the residual channel perfectly recovers and controls planted hidden objectives, outperforming per-target SAE and pairwise crosscoder baselines, and reveals a political-framing cluster in Qwen-2.5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Auditing a new language model typically requires relearning and reinterpreting its internal representations from scratch. The authors aim to create a reusable, stable framework that avoids this repeated effort by leveraging previously interpreted features from a reference panel to audit new models efficiently and to explicitly surface novel or unexpected mechanisms.

Method: They train a sparse feature library (the atlas) once on a panel of five 7-9B instruction-tuned models using a leave-one-out scheme. To audit a held-out target model (Mistral, Qwen), they fit only a linear decoder that maps the target's activations onto the atlas's sparse features. This yields two channels: (1) the atlas channel, which decomposes the target into known, interpreted panel features, and (2) the residual channel, which captures information the atlas cannot reconstruct, flagging it as outside-the-reference-panel. They validate the method via controlled LoRA-injected hidden objectives, residual-channel steering, and comparisons against per-target SAEs and pairwise crosscoders.

Result: On both Mistral and Qwen targets with injected LoRA objectives, the residual channel makes the planted mechanism perfectly controllable at runtime without affecting matched controls and recovers the planted objective as the top-ranked latent. Per-target SAE and pairwise crosscoder baselines, retrained for a head-to-head benchmark on Mistral, both fail to achieve this. On Qwen-2.5, the residual channel additionally reveals a panel-relative political-framing cluster; steering it shifts audited framing metrics while out-of-domain controls remain unchanged.

Conclusion: The reference feature atlas provides a practical and interpretable framework for model auditing, enabling reuse of previously interpreted features, reducing retraining burden, and explicitly surfacing novel mechanisms via the residual channel. It outperforms retrained baselines in detecting and controlling hidden objectives and can discover emergent political biases not present in the reference panel.

Abstract: Auditing a new language model usually means relearning and reinterpreting its internal features from scratch. We propose a reference feature atlas: a sparse feature library trained once on a reference panel and reused for new targets, which attach by fitting only a linear decoder. This yields two complementary views. The atlas channel reads the target on already interpreted panel features, providing a stable coordinate system across models. The residual channel learns features only from what the atlas fails to reconstruct, making "outside the reference panel" an explicit audit signal.
  We train leave-one-out atlases over five 7-9B instruction-tuned models and audit held-out Mistral and Qwen targets. On three controlled LoRA hidden objectives injected into both targets, the residual channel makes the planted mechanism perfectly controllable at runtime while matched controls stay unaffected and recovers the planted objective as the top-ranked latent across both targets; on Mistral, where the per-target SAE and pairwise crosscoder baselines are retrained for a head-to-head benchmark, both baselines fail to do so. On Qwen-2.5, the same channel additionally reveals a panel-relative political-framing cluster; steering it shifts the audited framing metrics while out-of-domain controls remain unchanged.

</details>


### [161] [SCAIR: Schema-Conditioned Agentic Iterative Reasoning for Enterprise Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22571)
*Prateek Chaturvedi,Yuqicheng Zhu,Hongkuan Zhou,Dongzhuoran Zhou,Yunjie He,Steffen Staab,Fei Du,Jie Tang,Evgeny Kharlamov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCAIR is a training-free KG-RAG framework that injects schema-conditioned structural priors and enforces schema-aware traversal for reliable multi-hop reasoning on dense, operationally constrained enterprise KGs, significantly outperforming generic agentic methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic KG-RAG methods perform well on public benchmarks but fail to generalize to real-world enterprise KGs, which are dense, schema-driven, and operationally constrained. There is a need for reasoning approaches that explicitly incorporate domain-specific structural and operational constraints without costly model retraining.

Method: SCAIR (Schema-Conditioned Agentic Iterative Reasoning) is a training-free framework that integrates structured planning with controlled iterative reasoning. It injects schema-conditioned structural priors and enforces schema-aware traversal during multi-hop reasoning over enterprise KGs. The approach aligns agent design with business logic and operational constraints.

Result: Experiments on an enterprise-oriented benchmark built from a real-world Configuration Management Database (CMDB) show that SCAIR substantially improves performance over existing KG-RAG methods, demonstrating that incorporating domain structural and operational constraints yields significant gains without model retraining.

Conclusion: Reliable enterprise graph reasoning cannot rely on generic agentic designs. Instead, reasoning systems must explicitly incorporate the target domain's structural and operational constraints. By aligning agent design with business logic, substantial performance gains can be achieved without costly retraining.

Abstract: Knowledge Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (KG-RAG) enables natural language interaction with structured enterprise knowledge, yet existing agentic approaches that perform well on public benchmarks often fail to generalize to real-world enterprise Knowledge Graphs (KGs), which are dense, schema-driven, and operationally constrained. To address these limitations, we propose SCAIR (Schema-Conditioned Agentic Iterative Reasoning), a training-free framework that integrates structured planning with controlled iterative reasoning by injecting schema-conditioned structural priors and enforcing schema-aware traversal during multi-hop reasoning. Experiments on an enterprise-oriented benchmark constructed from a real-world Configuration Management DataBase (CMDB) demonstrate that SCAIR substantially improves performance over existing KG-RAG methods. Crucially, our study highlights that reliable enterprise graph reasoning cannot rely on generic agentic designs; instead, it must explicitly incorporate the target domain's structural and operational constraints into the reasoning process. We demonstrate that by aligning agent design with business logic, substantial performance gains can be achieved without the need for costly model retraining.

</details>


### [162] [Schema-Aware Localisation (SAL): Live Schema Grounding and Hallucination Validation for Oracle NL2SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22572)
*Sanjay Mishra,Divya Chukkapalli,Ganesh R. Naik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAL is a lightweight middleware for Oracle NL2SQL that eliminates schema hallucination by injecting live catalog metadata into prompts and validating generated SQL against ground-truth schema, achieving 62.6% execution-grounded accuracy vs. 2.2% without grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-generated SQL on enterprise Oracle databases suffers from high execution failure rates (97.6%) due to hallucinated columns, aliases, and dialect-specific syntax errors — primarily caused by missing schema grounding, where the model lacks knowledge of which tables and columns actually exist.

Method: SAL operates as middleware with three components: (1) querying Oracle's USER_TAB_COLUMNS catalog to build a live schema map, selecting relevant table subsets per question; (2) injecting ground-truth schema context into the LLM prompt; (3) a Hallucination Index (Hidx) that validates every alias.column reference against the live catalog, auto-rewrites predictable prefix errors, and triggers structured retries with itemized corrections when needed.

Result: On 500 TPC-H questions against Oracle Autonomous Database 23c with GPT-4o-mini: zero-grounding EGT = 2.2% (12/500); static schema hint EGT = 62.0%; SAL achieves 62.6% EGT (96% simple, 95% medium, 40.7% complex) while reducing execution failures from 97.6% to 2.6%.

Conclusion: SAL demonstrates that a no-retraining, catalog-driven middleware approach can nearly eliminate execution failures in enterprise NL2SQL, matching or exceeding hand-crafted schema hints while requiring zero manual curation. The Hallucination Index and structured retry mechanism are key to bridging the gap between generated SQL and executable, correct queries on real database instances.

Abstract: Large language models can generate fluent SQL from natural language, but on real enterprise Oracle databases they frequently fail at execution time: columns and aliases are hallucinated and dialect-specific syntax is missed, leading to ORA-00904 invalid-identifier errors. In this setting, failures are primarily due to missing schema grounding: the model cannot know which tables and columns actually exist. This paper introduces Schema-Aware Localisation (SAL), a lightweight middleware layer for Oracle NL2SQL that requires no model retraining. SAL queries Oracle's USER_TAB_COLUMNS catalog to build a live schema map, selects a relevant table subset for each question (falling back to the full schema for multi-table queries), and injects this ground-truth context into the LLM prompt. Generated SQL is then checked by the Hallucination Index (Hidx), which validates every alias.column reference against the live catalog, automatically rewrites predictable prefix errors, and otherwise triggers a structured retry with itemised corrections. We evaluate SAL on 500 TPC-H natural language questions executed against a live Oracle Autonomous Database 23c instance using GPT-4o-mini. Without any schema grounding, execution-grounded truth (EGT; executes and matches the reference result set) is 2.2% (12/500). A hand-written static schema hint brings EGT to 62.0%. SAL, with no manual schema curation, achieves 62.6% EGT (96% simple, 95% medium, 40.7% complex) while reducing execution failures from 97.6% to 2.6%.

</details>


### [163] [PhononBench-MP40: a spectrum-resolved benchmark dataset for phonon stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22573)
*Wen-Kao Li,Ze-Feng Gao,Zhong-Yi Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PhononBench-MP40 is a benchmark dataset of 46,899 Materials Project-derived crystals with workflow-defined phonon stability labels and local phonopy YAML spectra, addressing the bottleneck of imaginary phonon modes in computational materials screening.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imaginary phonon modes pose a practical bottleneck in computational materials screening: structurally plausible crystals flagged as dynamically unstable may actually reflect workflow artifacts rather than intrinsic instability. A standardized, auditable benchmark linking stability labels to resolvable phonon spectra is needed.

Method: From 47,969 MP40 workflow tasks, 46,899 completed records were curated with paired stability labels and local phonopy YAML spectra (16,683 Stable; 30,216 completed-phonon unstable). An additional 1,067 relaxation failures are reported separately. Stability labels, lowest sampled frequencies, and threshold-dependent relabeling are all derived from the spectrum, ensuring full provenance.

Result: The dataset provides 46,899 spectrum-resolved records with auditable stability classification. The separation of relaxation failures (1,067) from completed-phonon unstable records (30,216) enables failure-aware triage. All derived labels trace back to the raw YAML spectra.

Conclusion: PhononBench-MP40 establishes an auditable, open-access reference for workflow-defined phonon stability classification, supporting minimum-frequency analysis, threshold studies, and failure-aware screening triage with explicit workflow boundaries.

Abstract: Imaginary phonon modes remain a practical bottleneck in computational materials screening because otherwise plausible structures can be locally dynamically unstable under a chosen workflow. Here we present PhononBench-MP40, a spectrum-resolved benchmark dataset of Materials Project-derived crystals for workflow-defined phonon stability. The dataset starts from 47,969 MP40 workflow tasks and provides 46,899 completed records with paired stability labels and local phonopy YAML spectra, including 16,683 Stable records and 30,216 completed-phonon unstable records. A further 1,067 relaxation failures are reported separately rather than merged into the completed phonon denominator. The release centers on the local YAML spectrum: the stability label, the lowest sampled frequency and any threshold-dependent relabeling are derived from that spectrum. The dataset is openly available through Science Data Bank at https://doi.org/10.57760/sciencedb.38735. A companion GitHub repository provides the calculation code and lightweight access utilities. PhononBench-MP40 provides an auditable reference for workflow-defined stability classification, minimum-frequency analysis, threshold studies and failure-aware triage, while keeping the reference workflow, data schema and interpretation boundaries explicit.

</details>


### [164] [Too much evidence, too little time: From text to actionable recommendations through multi-objective evidence reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22574)
*Adela Bara,Simona-Vasilica Oprea*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCEPTER is a clinical decision-support framework that transforms case descriptions into evidence-based recommendations by combining PubMed retrieval, semantic ranking, LLM-based claim extraction, evidence weighting, contradiction detection, and multi-objective Pareto optimization — reducing 576 papers to 3 recommendations (192:1 compression) while maintaining high evidence diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PubMed searches for complex clinical cases often return hundreds of publications that cannot be manually reviewed under time constraints, creating a bottleneck in evidence-based clinical decision making.

Method: SCEPTER integrates: (1) PubMed retrieval and PubMedBERT semantic ranking, (2) LLM-based claim extraction, (3) evidence-level weighting, (4) contradiction detection and consensus analysis, and (5) multi-objective Pareto claim selection. A Paper Q&A module enables interactive literature exploration. The framework unifies literature support, contradiction analysis, and interactive interrogation into a single pipeline.

Result: Evaluation on 150 case studies: average search space reduced from 576 papers → 53 retained papers → 7 Pareto-optimal claims → 3 final recommendations (192:1 compression ratio). Retained evidence maintained high diversity (entropy=0.901). Ablation study confirmed Pareto-based selection outperformed conventional ranking in both evidence diversity and recommendation utility.

Conclusion: SCEPTER demonstrates that multi-objective reasoning integrating literature support, contradiction analysis, and interactive interrogation can effectively compress large PubMed result sets into actionable, diverse, evidence-grounded clinical recommendations.

Abstract: Evidence-based clinical decision making requires specialists to identify, evaluate and synthesize relevant scientific literature. However, PubMed searches for complex clinical cases often return hundreds of publications that cannot be reviewed manually under time constraints. This study proposes SCEPTER (Single-Case Evidence-driven PubMed-To-rEcommendation Reasoner), a framework for transforming clinical case descriptions into evidence-based recommendations. SCEPTER combines PubMed retrieval, PubMedBERT semantic ranking, large language model (LLM)-based claim extraction, evidence-level weighting, contradiction detection, consensus analysis and multi-objective Pareto claim selection. The framework generates structured evidence syntheses and grounded actionable recommendations. A Paper Q&A module further enables interactive exploration of selected publications. The proposed framework introduces multi-objective reasoning model that integrates literature support, contradiction analysis and interactive literature interrogation into a unified clinical decision-support pipeline. Evaluation on 150 case studies demonstrated that the framework reduced an average search space of 576 papers to 53 retained papers, 7 Pareto-optimal claims and 3 final recommendations, corresponding to an overall compression ratio of 192:1. Despite this reduction, the retained evidence maintained high diversity (entropy=0.901). The ablation study showed that Pareto-based selection increased evidence diversity and recommendation utility compared with conventional ranking approaches.

</details>


### [165] [Temporal Context Reinstatement Drives Episodic-Like Order Memory in Long-Context Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22575)
*Mathis Pink,Vy Ai Vo,Qinyuan Wu,Jianing Mu,Javier Turek,Uri Hasson,Kenneth A. Norman,Sebastian Michelmann,Alexander Huth,Mariya Toneva*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs exhibit human-like temporal distance effects in episodic memory tasks, and mechanistic interpretability reveals this relies on a one-dimensional temporal code reinstated by a single attention head, supporting temporal context reinstatement as a core mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The computational mechanisms underlying human episodic memory retrieval over extended timescales are debated and difficult to access mechanistically in human experiments. The authors propose that long-context LLMs may serve as accessible model systems to reveal plausible computational mechanisms driving episodic-like temporal retrieval.

Method: The authors collected human behavioral data on a temporal order memory task based on a full-length novel, then tested whether LLMs exhibit the same behavioral signatures (distance effect). They applied long-context mechanistic interpretability analyses—specifically attention head analysis—to uncover the internal computations enabling temporal order retrieval in these models.

Result: LLMs exhibited the same characteristic distance effect as humans on the temporal order memory task. Mechanistic interpretability revealed that model performance depends on a one-dimensional temporal code that is reinstated during retrieval by a single dedicated "time-reinstatement" attention head.

Conclusion: Temporal context reinstatement is a key mechanism for episodic-like temporal-order memory in LLMs, providing convergent insights into how temporal aspects of long-term episodic memory may be instantiated in both artificial and biological systems.

Abstract: Human episodic memory supports the retrieval of experiences that unfold over extended timescales, yet the computational mechanisms underlying this ability remain debated due to the limited mechanistic accessibility in long-term memory experiments in humans. Long-context LLMs may offer promising ways to reveal plausible computational mechanisms that drive this type of retrieval. Here, we investigate whether and how LLMs capture the core behavioral signatures of episodic memory via a temporal order memory task. Using a new dataset of human behavior based on memory of a full-length novel, we show that models exhibit the same characteristic distance effect observed in humans on this task. We next apply long-context mechanistic interpretability analyses to uncover how models solve this task, and find that model performance relies on a one-dimensional temporal code that is reinstated during retrieval by a single time-reinstatement attention head. These findings support temporal context reinstatement as an important mechanism for episodic-like temporal-order memory in LLMs, offering new insights into how temporal aspects of long-term episodic memory may be instantiated in both artificial and biological systems.

</details>


### [166] [cMoLLM at Scale: Horizontal Scaling Laws for Mixture-of-LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22577)
*Xin Yang,Yemin Wang,Mingda Liu,Letian Li,Shuaishuai Cao,Zhengxiao He,Ryan Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: cMoLLM reformulates MoE-style mixture layers as variable-kernel dynamic convolutions, enabling end-to-end stream routing across the entire LLM pipeline with fully differentiable dynamic convolution, outperforming ParaScale and AltUp baselines in perplexity and downstream tasks under matched compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling dense Transformers couples capacity with computation (every parameter activated for every token), making costs grow linearly with model size. The authors aim to scale capacity via MoE-style mixture throughout the entire LLM pipeline—not just FFN layers—while overcoming limitations of prior pipeline-level approaches: ParaScale's overhead and routing/gradient issues, and AltUp's limited adaptivity and slow convergence.

Method: The authors establish that MoE-style mixture layers can be reformulated as variable-kernel dynamic convolutions, where each expert is a 1×1 convolutional kernel and routing performs input-conditioned kernel aggregation. Building on this equivalence, they propose cMoLLM (convolutionally gated mixture-of-LLMs), which routes over end-to-end streams using fully differentiable dynamic convolution throughout the LLM pipeline.

Result: On GPT-2-style models trained on FineWeb, cMoLLM achieves improved language modeling perplexity and higher accuracy on GLUE and SQuAD benchmarks under matched compute, with better stream utilization, more stable optimization, and favorable scaling compared to ParaScale and AltUp baselines.

Conclusion: The dynamic convolution reformulation of MoE mixture enables effective pipeline-level mixture routing. cMoLLM demonstrates that fully differentiable, convolutionally gated mixture-of-LLMs can scale capacity more efficiently than prior pipeline-level approaches, with better optimization stability and downstream performance.

Abstract: Scaling large language models (LLMs) has driven their success, yet dense Transformers couple capacity and computation: every parameter is activated for every token, making training and inference costs grow linearly with model size-a critical bottleneck as models approach trillion-parameter regimes. We aim to scale capacity through MoE-style mixture throughout the LLM pipeline rather than only the FFN. Prior pipeline-level approaches include ParaScale, which introduces virtual tokens and parallel streams but incurs substantial overhead and suffers from homogenized routing and gradient collapse, and AltUp, which uses an auxiliary prediction branch but offers limited adaptivity and slow convergence. We establish that MoE-style mixture layers can be reformulated as variable-kernel dynamic convolutions, where each expert corresponds to a $1{\times}1$ convolutional kernel and routing implements input-conditioned kernel aggregation. Building on this equivalence, we introduce cMoLLM: a convolutionally gated mixture-of-LLMs that routes over end-to-end streams through fully differentiable dynamic convolution. In GPT-2-style models trained on FineWeb, cMoLLM improves language modeling perplexity and downstream GLUE and SQuAD accuracy under matched compute, with better stream utilization, more stable optimization, and favorable scaling compared to ParaScale- and AltUp-style baselines.

</details>
